## T: tenure review–totorra

### tenure review
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an exchange of some Crown-leased land for conservation purposes, for freehold title to the remainder of a leased property.

>  It's called "tenure review", an unremarkable term for a remarkable transformation now taking place in our legendary South Island high country. In exchange for giving up the scenic and uneconomic parts of their land for a massive conservation estate, pastoral lease holders get freehold title to the rest of their property. [18] Currently, the main way of achieving it [sc. taking land for the conservation estate]is known as tenure review; a huge land swap inside the pastoral leases that make up 20 percent of the South Island and 10 percent of New Zealand. <i>(Jul 12, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  When Jim Morris of Ben Avon Station entered tenure review, he did it with an open mind, prepared to move with the times and embrace the consequences. <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,17</i>)

>  High-country farmers are quietly opting for tenure review because many are likely to become wealthy from what is [7] one of the best real estate deals in New Zealand in the last 50 years... I suspect that history will judge tenure review as not such a great idea for farmning or the public... What should have happened is a careful review of the pastoral lease system to maintain this unique form of land tenure, with adjustments to assist farmers to diversify and to protect uneconomic land. <i>(Jul 26, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  <b>Relph</b> After six years about two-thirds of the 300 pastoral leases in the South Island have passed through or are undergoing tenure review. <i>(2007,From Tussocks to Tourists,144</i>)

>  Tenure review is a voluntary process for owners of high-country leases who may trade off areas of high conservation value to the conservation estate in return for freeholding the rest of their farms. <i>(Oct 9, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

### terapo
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The natives said it was a bone of the terapo, a very large bird which lived at the top of Hikurangi, and that they made their largest fish-hooks from its bones. <i>(Apr 27, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,16</i>)

### Terawhiti pie
 
 <i>n.</i> Pie made with chops cooked in camp oven including onions and potatoes and topped with Cape Gooseberries.

>  After the bush was cleared, Cape Gooseberries, distributed by birds, sprang up everywhere giving rise to a recipe for "Terawhiti Pie", in Betty's Old Curiosity Recipe Book, with chops in a camp oven, onions and potatoes, topped off with Cape Gooseberries; but the cattle later ate them out. <i>(Jun 1987,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,76/45</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KIRK</b> ON THE NEW ZEALAND TEREDO, OR SHIP-WORM. ... Only one species, <i>Teredo antarctica</i>, Hutton, has at present been described as a native of New Zealand. <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,25</i>)

### teretere
 
 <i>n.</i> brown gecko

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,61</i>) [NOTES: [used in purely maori descr. context so omit]]

### terminal sire
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ram whose progeny are destined only for slaughter.

>  We have used a terminal sire before and it worked very well. <i>(May 4, 1993,NZ Farmer,4</i>)

>  <b>MEADOWS</b> Terminal sire: a ram whose progeny are destined only for slaughter. <i>(1997,Sheep Breeds of New Zealand,90</i>)

>  The 2 th and aged ewes had the service of terminal sires, and the MA ewes to the Romney in the month of February. <i>(Jun 19, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,22</i>)

>  When used with a terminal sire from one of the meat breeds such as a South Suffolk or Dorset Down, this type of ewe will produce lambs of around 16-17 kg carcase weight at 12-16 weeks. <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,Aug/Sept 43</i>)

>  We were changing our sommercial ewe flock to terminal sire. We used various breeds and liked the Suffolk. <i>(May 31, 2002,Dominion,31</i>)

### termination rate
 
 <i>n.</i> the fee a mobile phone company charges other carriers to end calls on their networks

>  The Commerce Commission will investigate whether mobile phone call termination rates - the fees a mobile phone company charge other carriers to end calls on their networks - should be regulated. <i>(Apr 30, 2004,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

### terms
 
 <i>n.</i> have/run sheep on terms [see Acland quotation below (cf thirds; even terms) [check index at ON]

>  Letters to Godley (J. Hamilton, Christchurch) Russell is giving up his sheep on terms at the Upper Station. <i>(1855,Canterbury Papers,2/250</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> I may here explain the ... custom of placing out capital under the care of an experienced manager, to be invested in sheep, or occasionally in cattle also. This is a well-known arrangement in the colonies, under the name of `keeping stock on terms', and is very general, and ... the most advisable [230] plan for persons who cannot conveniently reside on a station of their own at a distance from a town <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),229</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> I may here explain the ... custom of placing out capital under the care of an experienced manager, to be invested in sheep, or occasionally in cattle also. This is a well-known arrangement in the colonies, under the name of `keeping stock on terms', and is very general, and ... the most advisable [230] plan for persons who cannot conveniently reside on a station of their own at a distance from a town <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),229</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Everyone suggests different investments. One says buy sheep and put them out on terms. I will explain to you what this means. I can buy a thousand ewes ..., these I should place in charge of a squatter whose run is not fully stocked ... This person would take my sheep for either three, ... or more years, ... and would allow me yearly <i>2s. 6d.</i> per head in lieu of wool. This would give me <i>2s. 6d</i>. as the yearly interest on <i>25s. Besides this he would allow me 40 per cent. per annum of increase, ...; moreover, the increase would return me 2s. 6d.</i> per head wool money as soon a they became sheep. At the end of the term, my sheep would be returned to me as per agreement, ... sheep of the same age as [any who have died] would have been will be returned in their place. <i>(Oct 2, 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,4/36</i>)

>  [My sheep] are on for one year less 40% less 3% deaths, 2/6 wool money. ... Sheep on terms are not what they used to be ... <i>(Mar 25, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,170,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DUNCAN</b> [In 1862] Mr Switzer bought some few hundred sheep ... which were `running on terms' with Mr Rees. <i>(1888,Wakatipians,64</i>)

>  <i>Sheep on terms</i>. A favourite investment in the fifties and sixties. A man bought ewes and handed them over to a runholder whose run was understocked and who had not money to buy sheep. The runholder allowed the owner of the ewes 2s 6d a head a year for their wool and marked 40 per cent. of lambs for the owner as well; or they shared the increase equally. When the ewe lambs became old enough they were bred from as well, so that by the end of the time arranged (anything from three to seven years) the owner had a much larger mob to sell than he had started with. The practice was so much in vogue that in 1863 C.M. Ollivier published a pamphlet computing the interest and profit of <i>s. o. t.</i> at the various lengths of time. The last of such agreements at Horsley Down ran out in 1870 (H. D. letter book) and these are the latest I have found any record of. `I must confess to be fairly puzzled to know what to do with the money you have sent me. Everyone suggests different investments. One says buy sheep put them out on terms.' --[Butler A First Year in Canterbury Settlement], p. 37, where there is a good description of the business too long to quote. <i>(Nov 25, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <i>Sheep on terms</i>. A favourite investment in the fifties and sixties. A man bought ewes and handed them over to a runholder whose run was understocked and who had not money to buy sheep. The runholder allowed the owner of the ewes 2s 6d a head a year for their wool and marked 40 per cent. of lambs for the owner as well; or they shared the increase equally. When the ewe lambs became old enough they were bred from as well, so that by the end of the time arranged (anything from three to seven years) the owner had a much larger mob to sell than he had started with. The practice was so much in vogue that in 1863 C.M. Ollivier published a pamphlet computing the interest and profit of <i>s. o. t.</i> at the various lengths of time. The last of such agreements at Horsley Down ran out in 1870 (H. D. letter book) and these are the latest I have found any record of. `I must confess to be fairly puzzled to know what to do with the money you have sent me. Everyone suggests different investments. One says buy sheep put them out on terms.' --[Butler A First Year in Canterbury Settlement], p. 37, where there is a good description of the business too long to quote. <i>(Nov 25, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Jim ... evidently must have had some money for he owned sheep which Ritchie kept for him `on terms', that is Ritchie fed and looked after Jim's sheep, keeping half the wool and the [?check] natural increase in return [ca1860s-1870s]. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,146</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Sheep grazed on behalf of another owner under the terms of an agreement by which profits are shared. <i>(1972,Mountain World,310</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [the requirement of attendance and satisfactory completion of set work as a pre-requisite to sitting final degree examinations; usu in phr 'keep terms' or 'fail terms'; transferred from 'term' as division of academic year.]

>  A student must pass the examination and then sign the college declaration before he can keep terms in any subjects. <i>(Feb 22, 1928,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  The donors require candidates to keep terms in one subject only - music, - but the regulations require that terms should be kept in three subjects. <i>(Nov 29, 1889,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  It was resolved that the Board of Examiners in medicine in the first and second professional examinations be instructed that they may, if they see fit, recommend any rejected candidate for admission to a special examination in April, provided that the examiners in the subjects in which he has failed agree to such recommendation, all candidates rejected in the third professional examination to keep terms for one year before they can be re-admitted for examination. <i>(Mar 3, 1893,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  The professorial Board wrote suggesting certain changes in the regulations as to the keeping of terms. <i>(Dec 19, 1901,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  The decision of the university authorities that students for the degree of Bachelor of Commerce must keep terms in accountancy subjects at a university college was criticised at the annual meeting of the New Zealand Society of Accountants. <i>(Mar 1, 1941,Evening Post,7</i>)

### terms sheep
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) sheep that are grazed on specific terms or on thirds.

>  <b>BALFOUR</b> Usually, the grazier took one-half of the annual wool clip of the 'terms' sheep and one-third of the total increase at the end of the period. <i>(1885,Balfour Diaries,75</i>)

### tern
 
 <i>n.</i> a seabird related to the gull, having a slender body, long wings, and graceful flight. [check white-fronted, black-fronted, Antarctic, caspian; sea-swallow, tara, kahawai-bird]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Saw common Albatrosses, ... & some affirmed to have seen Eggbirds or Terns. [ed. fn] Not specifically identifiable but perhaps the White-fronted Tern <i>Sterna striata</i> (Gmelin, 1789). <i>Ibid.</i> 2/289 <i>Capt Furneaux </i>dined with us & I described a new <i>Owl</i> & a new <i>Tern</i>, the former of which was drawn by George. [ed. fn] <i>Sterna antarctica</i> of <i>Descr. Anim</i>., p.107. Now the Black-fronted Tern <i>Chlidonias hybrida albostriatus</i> (Gray, 1845). <i>(Mar 25, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/237</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We saw ... [in s. Indian Ocean] a single tern (<i>sterna</i>) or as the seamen call it an egg-bird, which had a forked tail. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/113</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i>Blue herons, auks, sand larks , sand plovers, terns,</i> ...; as also <i>black divers, rails</i> of various kinds and variegated colours. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/306</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Among these may be noticed the albatross, the molimawk, the Cape pigeon, and several varieties of tern and petrel. The Cape pigeon, which is a beautifully marked black and white bird about the size of a wood pigeon, will follow the vessel to the end of the voyage. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,8</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family STERNIDAE (terns). The graceful white-fronted tern <i>(Sterna striata, ... )</i> is seen in all New Zealand bays ... The somewhat stumpier black-fronted tern (<i>Chlidonias hybrida albostriatus, </i>... ) is common on South Island shingle flats ... The large Caspian tern (<i>Hydroprogne caspia</i>, ... ) is a large tern that includes New Zealand ... in its world-wide breeding range ... The rarest tern breeding in New Zealand is the fairy tern <i>(Sterna nereis,) </i>reported from Lake Ellesmere in 1944, and breeding also, sparingly, in Northland and a few other places. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,267</i>)

>  The graceful terns are some of the most welcome visitors to the Brothers. <i>(May 14, 1910,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  The bird you describe as skimming the water was probably a kind of tern or sea swallow. <i>(Nov 3, 1892,Otago Witness,24</i>)

### ternlet
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>210. Procelsterna cerulea albivitta</i> Bonaparte, 1856 <i>Grey ternlet</i> Subtropical Pacific, including Kermadec, Norfolk and Lord Howe Islands; has also bred in the Bay of Plenty ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,56,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Grey Ternlet Procelsterna cerulea</i> (BENNETT, 1840) ... The grey ternlet breeds in loose colonies on the coasts of Pacific islands. ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Grey noddy, blue-grey noddy, little blue petrel, blue billy. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,235</i>)

### terrace
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  A few yards further on, a party were `terracing'. <i>(Oct 27, 1867,Trip to the Thames Gold-fields,27,1978 ed.</i>)

>  Homann and party, terracing in the main creek, Mahakipawa, obtained 11ozs for four days' work, this week. <i>(Sep 29, 1888,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  With regard to alluvial diggings, a few I hear are doing fairly well "terracing" on the Lower Wilson, getting some rough "shotty" gold. <i>(Jun 28, 1894,Otago Witness,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a horizontal shelf on the side of a hill, or sloping ground [cf. terrace bottom, terrace workings]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The Scenery of the country was bold & worthy of the Pencil of a <i>Salvator Rosa</i>, all the Sides of hills were steep, rocky, & on all the little slopes & small terrasses or Settlements covered with fine shrubs, of various kinds; we saw here a great many <i>Palmtrees,</i> [ed. fn Cordyline sp.] of the same kind as had been found on April Ye 24th. <i>(Apr 27, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/267</i>)

>  [The Rangitata Valley bottom] consisted of a large swamp from which arose terrace after terrace up the mountains. ... The country is, as it were, crumpled up in an extraordinary manner, so that it is full of small ponds in lagoons - sometimes dry - sometimes merely swampy - now as full of water as they could be. <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,36,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> I had to cross the Waimakiriri just after a fresh, ... and I assure you I did not like it. I crossed it first on the plains, where it flows between two very high terraces ... of which the most northern must be ... 300 feet high. <i>Ibid.</i> vii/96 The terraces, which are so abundant all over the back country, and which rise, one behind another, to the number, it may be, of twenty or thirty, with the most unpicturesque regularity (on my run there are fully twenty), are supposed to be elevated sea-beaches. They are to be seen even as high as four or five thousand feet above the level of the sea, and I doubt not that a geologist might find traces of them higher still. <i>(Mar 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,v/62</i>)

>  These old lake beds [in the upper Clutha] are composed of loose drift ... and are generally known by the name of `terraces', although that phrase is more particularly applicable to the remarkable elevations of the Maruherikia and Upper Clutha valleys, which closely resemble military earthworks with glacis-like slopes and surprisingly uniform surfaces. ...[18] ...Terrace workings are in many places proving highly remunerative <i>(1863,AJHR,D/6/16</i>) [NOTES: Pyke Goldfields Report]

>  <b>WALKER</b> (Central Otago) The hill was formed of terraces or steps about 1 or 2 ft high, ... <i>(Jan 19, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> We drove for some miles till we came to the edge of one of the high terraces common to New Zealand scenery: ... <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,27,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> Throughout the Province are to be found numerous remains of <i>former</i> extensive lakes, estuaries, and rivers in the form of `flats' and `terraces' of clay, loam, grass, or sand ... <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,31</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> These questions have reference to gulley workings mainly as I am aware the miner cannot cut a Race on those Runs to work the terraces with ... <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,18</i>) [NOTES: 1870 Nov 30 in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 18]

>  <b>MONEY</b> We saw it in their claim (which was on a terrace some fifty feet above the creek) the morning after its discovery. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,97</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> The scene opens on a rolling plain, flanked by rocky `spurs' of the ever-present mountains; a plain of unequal surface, broken by `terraces', and watercourses, and of varying width - irregular. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/iii/81,1889,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CAIRD</b> The words `terrace' and `flat', so often used, rather aptly describe the general nature of the country. A flat means a plain, but it is a rare thing to gallop along a flat for more than a mile without coming across a terrace, which terrace may be only six feet high, or it may be a hundred ... [and] is certain to be as regular as if it formed part of some ornamental ground. <i>(1874,NZ Sheepfarming,26</i>)

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> One of the men waded up to his knees and shoved off the boat to the edge of the `terrace', where it was caught by the rush of the deeper stream [fording the Waitaki river]. <i>(1877,Colonial Travel,192</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> It is on these Terraces (many of which are of considerable extent), ...that our gold miners pursue their avocations ... <i>(1881,White Hood and Blue Cap,4</i>)

>  RIVER TERRACES [title] ...One of the most remarkable features in the scenery of the South Island ... is the series of river terraces which flank the broad shingle-filled vallies [sic] on either side. <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,511</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton)]

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> I afterwards heard that a party of men drove a tunnel into the terrace ..., and got a pile out of it. <i>(1889,Rhyme or Reason,19</i>)

>  <b>PRINCE</b> The Falls were soon arrived at, and standing ... upon a little terrace of cutty grass, a full ... view was obtained. <i>(1889,Diary of a Trip,11</i>)

>  [West Coast] terrace flats ... covered with Red and Black Pine ... . <i>(1892,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1892 letter to Strauchan in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 175]

>  <b>DOBSON</b> A number of gold-diggers were working in the neighbourhood, some on the beaches and some on the adjoining terraces. <i>(1930,Reminiscences,141</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> On steep hillsides stock will tend to track and contour the land, and the ultimate surface formation of well-stocked country will be a series of broad or narrow terraces with steep slopes between them upon which stock seldom tread. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,254,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> <i>(1963,Coal Flat,156</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,19</i>)

>  Besides, river workings being subject to damage from floods, are much more precarious ventures than terrace workings; and the Kawarau can flood at times, and does so pretty often. <i>(Nov 26, 1887,Otago Daily Times,6</i>)

>  Braemar is situated upon a flat green terrace between the town and the Tasman river bed. <i>(Jan 13, 1898,Otago Witness,25</i>)

>  This pah is pleasantly situate [sic] on the terrace of the cliff, where the hills rise more gradually from its edge. <i>(May 9, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  I walked along these cliffs this evening before sundown, and was charmed at the sight of our pretty town lying snugly in lovely dells and on the slopes of natural terraces, beyond which in the South towered the mountain and the Kaitake Ranges, while Paritutu stood sentinel in the West. <i>(Feb 6, 1907,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> I sunk a shaft on the Larrikin's Terrace there, but it proved a duffer. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,56</i>)

>  On a terrace below the Karitane Home, there is a small but well-equipped laboratory. <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),II(22) : 12</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> "We were having a wonderful party up in Royal Terrace." <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,30</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> They stopped.. where they could look.. across Coal Creek to the terrace where the dredge gouged and scraped with weird screams. They breathed a sense of expansion from the open space over the flat terrace across the Creek. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,156</i>)

### terrace bottom
 
 <i>n.</i> [Cf. bottom n]

>  South of Hokitika, there are no marine deposits on higher levels beyond the existing run of the beaches; the `terrace bottom' (i.e. gravel ^ash) abuts on the lagoons. <i>(1906,Mining Handbook,124</i>)

>  ".. The tunnel is just on the border line, and Mr Morgan is of the opinion that the tunnel is leaving the terrace bottom and coming into wash." <i>(Nov 13, 1883,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  The underlying brown terrace bottom, which is now within 18 inches of the black laths, is more like soft sandstone than the usual gravel conglomerate. <i>(Apr 4, 1916,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  Cumming's claim, adjoining the latter company, is worked by three shifts, night and day, and maintains a very high reputation among those contributing our golden harvest. It is one which has a long time yet in store ere the necessity will arise to sink for the fifth or terrace bottom. <i>(Feb 20, 1871,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  Nature of country sunk through between second and third bottoms, fine gravel with terrace bottom, watery drift and sandy clay, coarse light gravel, and admixture of clay and gravel. <i>(Dec 7, 1899,Otago Witness,19</i>)

>  They intend sinking for the terrace bottom, which they expect to reach at about 200 feet. <i>(Aug 13, 1867,West Coast Times,4</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> trousers [ORIGIN: rhyming slang]

### terrace working
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining, usually plural, occasionally terrace-working]

>  With this invention [sc. Smith's jet pump] we applied to Warden Beetham for a special claim of one mile in length of the Shotover, but as we would not divulge our modus operandi in compliance with the Mining Act, the application for our special claim fell through; when we determined to rest on our oars until our terrace workings were played out. <i>(Jul 20, 1899,Otago Witness,22</i>)

>  Besides, river workings being subject to damage from floods, are much more precarious ventures than terrace workings; and the Kawarau can flood at times, and does so pretty often. <i>(Nov 26, 1887,Otago Daily Times,6</i>)

>  It was resolved .. that alluvial gold-mining was on a different footing from any other branch of industry, and to ensure the success of the schemes for high terrace workings, water-power for pumping purposes should be granted free in all cases affecting alluvial undertakings. <i>(Jul 28, 1909,Bay of Plenty Times,3</i>)

>  At the last sitting of the Warden's Court, applications were granted for 400 acres of a prospecting area, in addition to the scope of ground already held in the immediate locality of the Seddon's Terrace workings. <i>(Mar 19, 1920,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [cf bench]

 <i>n.</i> (See [tarakihi](../T#tarakihi))

 <i>n.</i> New name of map-making section of Lands & Survey

>  Oh, all right, it's Terralink, as in map-making. But it certainly rattled the dags of its board when its practically irreplaceable core specialists demanded the right to take part in a buyout. <i>(May 27, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### terrier
 
 <i>n.</i> a member of the Territorial Army (cf. Terry)

>  ... and to try and prevent the anti-militarists from doing anything foolish concerning same, I dare to give a little advice: `Join the `Terriors,' preach your doctrines, educate, agitate, demonstrate, surely any cause deserves a little sacrifice. <i>(Sep 15, 1911,Maoriland Worker,28/17/2</i>)

>  A jockey named Geo. Jas. Lee, charged before a Feilding Court with being a defaulting `terrier,' produced a doctor's certificate showing he had been undergoing medical attention. <i>(Jan 18, 1913,NZ Truth,395/1/6</i>)

### terrior
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [terrier](../T#terrier))

### Territorial
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>INGRAM</b> Those of us who had received training as Territorials were asked to-day whether we would care for stripes. <i>(Jan 9, 1917,Anzac Diary,10,1987 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The Defence Act of 1909 created The <i>Territorial Force; ... </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/384</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>Territorials</i>: The army RESERVES, weekend warriors, not to mention a fortnight in January. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,102</i>)

>  <b>PUGSLEY</b> The organisation had been outlined by Field Marshal Lord Kitchener on a visit to New Zealand in 1910. All New Zealanders from the ages of 14 to 30 years were liable for part-time military training. Boys from 14 to 18 years served in the senior cadets, and young men from 18 to 25 years served as Territorial soldiers with the regionally affiliated regiments and battalions - hence the name `Territorial'. Service in the reserve was a requirement from the age of 25 to 30, but legislation allowed for this service to be extended up to the age of 55 years in the case of a national emergency. The same legislation allowed the Territorial Force to be mobilised by proclamation for service in New Zealand, and Section 26 (1) of the 1909 Defence Act made provision for members of the Territorial Force to volunteer for special service outside New Zealand. <i>(1984,Gallipoli: the New Zealand Story,38</i>)

### Territorial Customary Right
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [TCR](../T#tcr))

>  <b>A2</b> But there was concern at how a finding in the High Court of a Territorial Customary Right (TCR) would be given effect, and Government MPs were looking at ways to give the courts some guidelines. <i>(Nov 5, 2004,Dominion Post</i>)

### terroirism
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  If terroirism was established here, the result would be rich, engaged regional communities producing food and drink that commanded high prices on international markets, providing handsome returns for farmers for taking care of their land. <i>(Apr 2, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

### terror
 
 <i>n.</i> used for anything surprisingly distinctive or outstanding, either good or bad; a great one for, usu. humorous [thus different in sense or application from P8: something large or forbidding of its kind; something allegedly difficult to handle: contrast <i>Holy Terror</i> or <i>Terror</i>]

>  Owing to the inclemency of the weather - the preceding night being "a terror" for wind and rain, when the wind blew as "'twad blawn its last," and the rain fell in torrents, and Friday being also a cold and stormy day (the dark clouds began to disperse and there was a gleam of sunshine about 4 o'clock) - the attendance was not so large as was anticipated. <i>(Nov 29, 1878,Clutha Leader,6</i>)

>  The son of our next-door neighbour is of an inquisitive turn of mind (writes "Marcia"), and, as his father, says, it "a terror for finding out things," Mr. M_____ tries to get even with the small questioning son sometimes, but doesn't always manage it. <i>(Jun 8, 1910,Colonist (Nelson),1</i>)

>  14 Billy is one of those players who get right into the thick of it, and is a terror for work. <i>(Sep 9, 1922,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  There was a young farmer of Kent, / Such a terror for using strong scent / That he sprayed himself over / With otto de clover, / And bees chased him wherever he went. <i>(Jul 14, 1937,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  <b>KERSHAW</b> .. the roads were terrors, the lake was a terror, some of the women were terrors .. Terror is a New Zealand adjective. Shilling knives are advertised as `perfect terrors'. You can't go far wrong if you call a thing a terror. <i>(1886,Colonial Facts and Fictions,266</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Your uncle was a terror for the drink after you went away. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,24</i>)

>  COMMENT: `I'm a terror for my veges'. <i>(1961,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: programme about Christmas foods]

>  [The plough] would quite often overturn. Having done so, it was a terror of a thing to get upright again. <i>(Oct 30, 1963,Weekly News,[farming page]</i>)

### Terry
 
 <i>n.</i> a member of the Territorial Army (cf. terrier)

>  Trouble for the Terries. <i>(Jun 30, 1941,2NZEF Times,1/1</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He could have gone on to Otago if he'd pushed it but he went into the terries and then overseas when he was twenty-one. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,134</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Talking of tin hats, said Captain Southam. Remember old Bluey when we were in the Terries. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,86</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Terries, the </i>Territorial Army; ref. Partridge and Gordon Slatter; c. 1930. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,112</i>)

>  One feature of New Zealand speech that is often commented on by visitors is our inordinate fondness for abbreviations which end in `ie' or `y'. ... [11] ... Military types often belong to the Terries. <i>(Jan 1989,Pacific Way,10</i>) [NOTES: (Sharp New Zildish)]

### test
 
 <i>n.</i> milk test

>  <b>CONDON</b> Of the quality of milk being received, the test stands high. <i>(1992,Hurleyville,109</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from 1901]

### tester
 
 <i>n.</i> a herd-tester

>  <i>tester</i>: a person who visits farms and tests milk for butter fat content; e.g., `The tester's at Jack's farm tonight.' <i>(1981,NZ Police National Headquarters</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a herd-tester.

>  After much too long a break the tester comes back into the shed during the next week or two. <i>(Apr 11, 1960,NZ Dairy Exporter,43</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Science) a cow, when its milk is being tested for butterfat or milk solid content.

>  The preference for the Jersey is largely due to the fact that the co-operative companies have adopted the system of paying for milk on the butter-fat content, and the Jersey being a high tester appears to return a better profit to the owner than any other breed. <i>(Jul 5, 1923,Hawera Star,3</i>)

>  To improve your average test you would require to either cull your low-testers which is not always advisable, and replace with high testing animals. <i>(Dec 20, 1928,NZ Dairy Exporter,30</i>)

### testing station
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>testing station NZ </i>establishment where motor vehicles are tested for roadworthiness. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,794</i>)

### tete
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Aythya novae seelandiae</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We shot some [several???] Tetes, but could not hit them, because they dived so frequently. . . [fn]Generic name for small ducks of the kind commomly called Teal. The only one of these that dives readily is the Black Teal or Scaup (<i>Aythya novae seelandiae</i>) <i>(1959,Early Travellers,354</i>) [NOTES: 1858 [Jan 7???] S.P. Smith Journey from Taranaki in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 354]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> GREY TEAL <i>Anas gibberifrons Maori name</i>: Tete. ... Like the Grey Duck has pale throat, but noticeably smaller ... <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,88</i>)

>  <i>Grey Teal Anas gibberifrons gracilis</i> BULLER, 1869 ... OTHER NAME: <i>Tete. ... Native to Australia, New Guinea and New Caledonia. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,145</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 100. RHYNCHASPIS VARIEGATA. Gould. Shoveller. Tete <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,37</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> SPATULA \{RHYNCAPSIS\} VARIEGATA. (NEW-ZEALAND SHOVELLER.) ... <i>Native name.</i> Tete, \{Pateke,\} Putaitai, Kuriwhengi, \{Kuruwhengu, Papaungungu,\} Kahoho, and Wetawetangu; `Spoon-bill Duck' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,252,{1888 2nd edn 2/269} ed.</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Querquedula gibberifrons</i>. - Tete, Little Teal. Both Islands; Australia, New Caledonia, Indian Archipelago. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/117</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

### tete-whero
 
 <i>n.</i> brown teal

>  <b>BULLER</b> ANAS CHLOROTIS. (BROWN DUCK.) ... <i>Native names</i>.- Tarawhatu, Patete, \{Tete,\} and Tete-whero. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,248,1888 2nd edn 2/257 ed.</i>)

### teteaweka
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Tete-a-waka .. Olearia angustifolia .. Timber small and of little use <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,15</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>O[learia] angustifolia,</i> Hook.f. ...<i> SOUTH ISLAND ... near Bluff Hill ... STEWART ISLAND: sea-coast south of Paterson's Inlet ... Titi-a-weka. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,281</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>O[learia] angustifolia,</i> Hook.f. ...<i> SOUTH ISLAND ... near Bluff Hill ... STEWART ISLAND: sea-coast south of Paterson's Inlet ... Titi-a-weka. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,281</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> A close relation of <i>O[learia] operina</i>, the teteawaka (<i>O. angustifolia</i>) ... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,76</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> Along the coastal fringes [of Stewart Island] it consists of a characteristic scrub known locally as `Mutton-bird scrub', the principal constituents ... being Puheretaiko (<i>Senecio Rotundifolius</i>), Tete-a-weka (<i>Olearia Angustifolia</i>) and Daisy Tree (<i>Olearia Colensoi</i>). <i>(1940,Rakiura,xvii</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> In the house, big fire of dead tete-a-weka wood going. Two witch's pots (small edition of a whaler's try-pot) and a camp oven swinging above the fire. Titis sizzling in their own kato fat (fat from insides). Piro fat, the fine fat from the intestines, is more precious. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,154</i>) [NOTES: 1952 Apr 20 in 1982 Sansom In the Grip of an Island 154]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The TETE-A-WEKA of Bluff and Stewart Island [<i>Olearia angustifolia</i>] and (<i>O. operina</i>) of the west coast sounds are similer plants. <i>(1961,Flora Mt Cook,200,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>Teteaweka,</i> Olearia angustifolia [title] ... <i>O. angustifolia</i> is a very attractive plant with purple flowers and comes from coastal areas along Foveaux Strait. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/440</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>OLEARIA CHATHAMICA, keketerehe</i>. A few species of <i>Olearia</i> have purple disc florets -<i>O. angustifolia</i>, the teteaweka of southern Southland and Stewart Island, and, in the Chathams, ... <i>O. chathamica</i> on drier ridges. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of Contemporary NZ Poetry,130</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> On one visit we were thrilled to find a Titiaweka in flower. It had a large daisylike flower, the centre of which is ringed with clusters of tiny yellow flowers. <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,83</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Herekopare and Woman's Island are the only two beneficiary muttonbird islands. All the rest are Crown lands. ... I know ... that the fierce nettle also grows on Herekopare. Other lovely ... plants are there too - the Stewart Island yellow senecio, teteaweka of course, ... Cook's health-giving scurvy grass, soft native tatahi grass and punui, the giant-leafed umbrella-like araliad. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,69</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Olearia oporina</i> TEETEEAWEKA ...Shrub or small tree ... Common, windswept coastal scrub and shrubland ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,98</i>)

>  The teteaweka - muttonbird scrub - is in flower; white petals, waxy, serrated leaves, purple-black buds. <i>(Mar 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 56/105</i>)

### tetepango
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> FULIGULA NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (NEW-ZEALAND SCAUP.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Papango, Tetepango, Matapouri, Titiporangi, and Raipo; `Black Teal' and `Widgeon' of the Colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,259,1888 2nd edn 2/273 ed.</i>)

### tether-my-cow
 
 <i>n.</i> humorous alteration of Taramakau (river)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> We have seen the water flowing down the Teramakau (or `Tether-my-cow', as the Europeans call it). <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,67,1915 ed.</i>)

### tetra brik
 
 <i>n.</i> a cardboard carton for milk etc.; cf. tetra pack

>  [He] claims that milk will keep for up to a week in an unopened tetra brik... <i>(Jun 28, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

### Teurian
 
 <i>adj.</i> Geol. classification

>  The lower Teurian age for the vertebrate/lingual horizon makes this locality the most important in New Zealand for providing a record of Lower Paleocene elasmobranchs. <i>(Jul 1981,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,53/37</i>)

>  The Cretaceous-Tertiary boundary in New Zealand is generally placed at the boundary between the Teurian and Haumurian Stages (Hornibrook 1962). <i>(Nov 1978,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,46/13</i>)

### tewhatawhata
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Maori was armed with a <i>tewhatawhata</i>, a wooden weapon shaped like a battleaxe, with a long handle, and a plume of hawk's leathers just below the blade. <i>(Nov 1937,NZ Railways Magazine,19</i>)

### tewhatewha
 
 <i>n.</i> a long axe-like Maori weapon, usually of wood and having both blade and sharpened handle. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The carved weapons of the Maori on which he lavished so much of his artistic skill are well represented in stone and bone mere, taiaha, and tewhatewha. <i>(Oct 16, 1925,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Boys were taught at a very tender age the use of weapons when they indulged in mock battles in which reeds and flax stems were used in practising movements they would later use in earnest when the taiaha and tewhatewha were in their hands. <i>(Apr 1958,Te Ao Hou,22: 45</i>)

>  The <i>tewhatewha</i>, or <i>paiaka</i>, was used in somewhat similar fashion to the <i>taiaha. </i> <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/441</i>)

>  <i>tewhatewha,</i> a weapon with a terminal broad semi-crescent of one side, the other end sharpened for thrusting, the lower side of the crescent pierced with a hole for attachment of a small tuft of feathers, such as pigeon-feathers, the weapon being largely used in oratory. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> ... the <i>taiaha</i>, a kind of two-handed sword made of the hardest wood of the country; and the <i>tewhatewha,</i> a wooden battle-axe, having a sharp-pointed handle to enable it also to answer the purpose of a spear. <i>(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,244</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Another weapon of authority or direction was the battle-axe (<i>tewha-tewha</i> or <i>paiaka</i>) made of bone or hard wood. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,312</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1928,Where the White Man Treads,57, 137</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> Others carried wooden lances or long, hatchet-like weapons called tewhatewha. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,152,1933 ed.</i>)

>  The energy of the paddling - the rhythm of it! The time-giver stood on the thwarts, a feather-tufted <i>tewha tewha</i> in his extended hand; ... the time-giver sang his song ... <i>(1930,NZ Short Stories,30</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Anderson]

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1944,Coming of the Maori, The,2/274</i>)

>  The Constabulary at work in the vicinity of the Gate Pah unearthed a perfect handsome tewhatewha of whalebone, solid, with blade eight inches long. <i>(Jun 10, 1876,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  A Tewhatewha or paiaka, a wooden P-shaped weapon, with hawk-feathers tied below the blade, looks innocent now under the glass, but it figured grimly only in a treacherous murder in the time of William IV., 1832. <i>(Feb 18, 1911,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  The eight natives who had returned from the Front then stepped forward with their arms, seven flint muskets and one tewhatewha. <i>(Apr 2, 1864,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

### text dump
 
 <i>n.</i> ending a relationship by text message

>  She is alarmed by stories of boyfriends breaking up with their girlfriends by text message and vice-versa (a phenomenon one girl labels "the text dump"). <i>(Dec 28, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A11</i>)

### textual intercourse
 
 <i>n.</i> communication by texting

### thanks
 
 <i>exclam.</i> for `please' or `thank(ing) you in anticipation' in usu. shopping contexts as, e.g. shop-person to customer, when a finalisation of a transaction is anticipated: `That will be $1.99 thanks.' [1987 Dec p.c. Professor G.E. Hughes (Wellington) `Noticed over the years, and reminded again recently, that this seems a NZ usage or idiom as distinct from British usage.']

### thar
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This dramatic recovery of native plants... results from a huge reduction in the populations of introduced feral deer, chamois (European antelope) and, in some places, of Himalayan thar (mountain goat). <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,306/2</i>)

### that would be
 
 <i>phr.</i> = that is (hinting for polite agreement with the speaker)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> `I dare say he was pissed and confused.' `That would be the strong of it, `said Sid. <i>(1978,Talkback Man,193</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> `Nah,' said Fred. `... Now that'd be maize over there. Not for Watties. More for meal. ...' <i>(1985,G'day Country,68</i>)

### that'll be the day
 
 <i>phr.</i> expressing doubt, disapproval, etc., of a statement or request; used ironically for something most unlikely to happen. [also that will be the day; (cf. 1867 Lady Barker Station Life (1874) 113 [a cockie speaks] `Ah but it'll be a far day first [before I'll believe that].) ca1935 at least e.g. `I thought you were at church.' `That'll be the day.' `That'll be the day when you see me there.']

>  "That will be der ruddy tag," said the bloke. <i>(Jan 10, 1944,NZEF Times,III/133 : 7</i>)

>  "That'll be the day," chorused half a dozen Kiwis. <i>(Dec 18, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/182 : 5</i>)

>  "THE DAY THE BOOKIES WEPT" .. That WILL be the Day! Under that'll be the day <i>(Feb 7, 1940,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>That'll be the day!</i> used as a cant phrase expressing mild doubt... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,50</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>That'll be the day! </i>`Expressing mild doubt following some boast or claim,' B., 1941: New Zealand: C. 20. See also <i>day, that'll be the. </i> <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1199</i>)

>  <b>HARVEY</b> I asked some Wellington people if they'd like a shine, and all they said was, `You've got a bloody nerve,' or `That'll be the day,' or something mean like that. <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,35</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>That'll be the day,</i> a way of expressing a strong disinclination to do something, was unknown to visiting Americans during the war, one of whom thought it expressed gay anticipation and answered `That'll be the day' when asked to visit a New Zealand family. The expression was worked to death and in its dying agonies produced some contortions as <i>That'll be the frosty Friday</i> etc. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,177</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>That'll be the day</i> c/p expressing doubt or incredulity; NZ since 1930s, widespread elsewhere; also, <i>that'll be the bloody day, boy! </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,112</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> He's a beaut. Wait till I get him. That'll be the day. <i>(1943,Colour Scheme,101</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> `If I've bungled', Alleyn muttered, `I've .. bungled in a big way.' .. Bailey astonished everyone by saying .. `That'll be the day.' `Don't talk Australian', Mr Fox chided. <i>(1951,Opening Night,248</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Onward. "That'll be the Day," we say. "The Day" is getting closer. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,239</i>)

>  "That'll be the day!" she snorted. <i>(Aug 14, 1947,NZ Woman's Weekly: The First 60 Years,17</i>)

>  That will be the Day... Tokyo will get worse punishment that London ever got. "That will be the day." <i>(Sep 7, 1942,NZEF Times,II/62 : 8</i>)

### that'll be the frosty Friday
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> In a Quartermaster's store? That will be the Frosty Friday. <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,131</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>That'll be the day</i>, a way of expressing a strong disinclination to do something, was unknown to visiting Americans during the war, one of whom thought it expressed gay anticipation and answered `That'll be the day' when asked to visit a New Zealand family. The expression was worked to death and in its dying agonies produced some contortions as <i>That'll be the frosty Friday </i>etc. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,177</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>that'll be the frosty Friday/frozen fortnight </i> Kiwi variants of `that'll be the day', which Partridge thinks derives from WWI officer usage. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,112</i>)

### that'll be the sunny afternoon
 
 <i>phr.</i> that'll be the day [ca1949 (Dunedin) fide R. Mason That'll be the Saturday afternoon; or That'll be the sunny Sunday afternoon]

>  There are [from 2NZEF], of course, the eternal variations on the old theme which nearly wrecked New Zealand's slang two or three years ago - `That'll be the day,' `That'll be the sunny afternoon', and so on. <i>(Jan 28, 1944,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>) [NOTES: L R Hobbs]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `That'll be the Sunday afternoon,' says Spandau Craig ... `You're only saying that. ... What have you been up to in these parts? Lashing out on your eight and a bender?' <i>(1970,On the Ball,137</i>)

### that's right
 
 <i>phr.</i> as an affirmation or agreement cf she's right; easy

>  Happily, there is not at present any decided New Zealand accent, but certain very objectionable expressions are current, and by no means only in the <i>sermo plebei.</i> I have heard this, or variants of it, quite frequently:- First decently dressed youth: `Well, how's things?' Second decently dressed youth: `Oh, jest like this, yer know.' First d.d.y.: `That's right!' <i>(Jan 10, 1910,Triad, The,4</i>)

### that's the shot
 
 <i>exclam.</i> an expression of agreement or approbation [AND 1953]

### that's the stuff
 
 <i>phr.</i> Assent or support or agreement.

>  How are you Phil? Quite all right. That's the stuff. <i>(Sep 5, 1998,Radio Pacific,00</i>)

### thatched
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of wool)

>  Add to that their superb mothering ability and a type of thatched wool that keeps out the wet and you have the perfect Taihape sheep, Mr Rowe says. <i>(Jun 20, 2003,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

### thatching
 
 <i>n.</i> a kind of wool

>  The oval lock, or thatching, keeps out water and means the lambs survive storms at lambing and flystrike in summer. <i>(Jun 20, 2003,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

### THC
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Tourist Hotel Corporation

>  After the wars years, [the Chateau] reopened to the public under the control of the government-owned Tourist Hotel Corporation (THC), which also developed ski facilities up the hill. <i>(Oct 8, 2001,Evening Post,20</i>)

### theatre
 
 <i>n.</i> cinema, pictures (check picture theatre [Jim writes: NSOD tags this as <i>now chiefly NAmer Austral. and NZ.</i> Not in DNZE. Though perhaps now less common, the word is still often used in NZ in this sense at an informal level.]

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The</i>)

>  <i>theatre ... 8. Austral. & N.Z</i>. the usual word for cinema. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1213</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 796; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 498]]

>  <b>FRAME</b> There are two theatres opposite each other, the Regent and the Miami. <i>(1960,Owls Do Cry,82</i>)

### thelymitra
 
 <i>n.</i> as a common name from generic for native orchid varieties; <i>Thelymitra uniflora</i>

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>(1908,AJHR,C-14</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Survey Waipoua Kauri Forest]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Report on Dune-areas <i>(1911,AJHR,C-13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Thelymitra longifolia</i>

>  <b>l;AING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

### there's an old boot for every sock
 
 <i>phr.</i> -

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>there's an old boot for every sock</i>: a phrase bearing reassurance for all those seeking spouse or paramour. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,103</i>)

### thermal area
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The bridge will be specially carved in Maori designs to provide a unique entrance to the thermal area. <i>(Oct 7, 1933,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  The discovery of a new thermal area four and a half miles from Rotorua, on the Taupo highway at Waiotapu, was made by engineers engaged on the new Waikiti Valley road from Waiotapu to Ngakuru, states a Rotorua correspondent. <i>(Mar 15, 1940,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>STAFFORD</b> The first "tourist".. in Rotorua... was John Bidwill, the great naturalist, who.. apart from examining the Rotorua thermal areas, acquired some notoriety by.. transgressing this tapu place. <i>(1977,Romantic Past of Rotorua,9</i>)

>  After all, argued Mr. Fox, a good many holes had been bored in the thermal areas of the North Island. <i>(Sep 7, 1955,Evening Post,10</i>)

### thermal district
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Owing, it is believed, to the increase of railway fares, there were fewer visitors to the thermal district during the recent holidays than during the previous Christmas and New Year Season. <i>(Jan 20, 1918,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  But if we are to reap the full benefit from the opening up of communication between Auckland and the Lakes <i>via</i> [sic] Waikato, we must have a speedier means of communication with the Thermal Districts than now exists, and hence this action of our Auckland neighbours should stimulate the people of Tauranga not to let the proposal to construct a railway, <i>via</i> Te Puke, meet the fate that has attended so many promising schemes launched before the public of Tauranga. <i>(Oct 31, 1881,Bay of Plenty Times,2</i>)

>  Much has been written and more has been said concerning the wonders and miraculous healing properties of the SPRINGS AT WAIRAKIE AND ROTORUA, But by far the most wonderful of all the Hot Springs in the Thermal district are the BATHING PUIAS OF TOKAANU. <i>(Jan 2, 1890,Wanganui Herald,3</i>)

### thermal spring
 
 <i>n.</i> [usually plural]

>  Here, again, the chief attraction of the thermal springs which issue close to the margin of the lake. <i>(Apr 6, 1844,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  To the north of this line, the volcanic action appears to have entirely ceased, if we except certain thermal springs which rise in several localities in New Ulster, and which although not situated in volcanic districts, indicate that a channel of communication has been opened with the interior of the earth, at some former period of local convulsion. <i>(May 28, 1852,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  Areas where decomposition and siliceous infiltration have affected the rocks will be recognised by ferruginous springs and by the occurrence of large blocks of the quartz cement, which though non-auriferous in itself yet indicates the former agency of thermal springs. <i>(Oct 31, 1867,Wellington Independent,41</i>)

>  Amongst the manifold blessings bountiful Nature has bestowed upon New Zealand, to which hitherto very little or no attention has been paid, none deserve our consideration more than the thermal springs situated towards the central parts of both islands. <i>(May 11, 1870,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  A Bill to provide for the settlement of the thermal springs districts of the Colony was brought in by the Hon F. WHITAKER, and read a first time. <i>(Aug 27, 1881,Star, The,4</i>)

>  CONFERENCE ON THERMAL SPRINGS. <i>(Sep 21, 1898,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  Bath Houses at Pipiriki Thermal Springs now open. <i>(Dec 21, 1907,Wanganui Herald,1</i>)

>  It repeals the Thermal Springs Act of 1908, which was designed to reserve districts containing thermal springs, and makes other provision in its place. <i>(Feb 3, 1911,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  There are several thermal springs, of which the temperature is variable, ranging from 105 to 124 degrees in the hottest parts. <i>(Jul 31, 1926,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  THERMAL Springs and Pools; Ketetahi Blowholes. <i>(Mar 18, 1938,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Thermal Spring's Level Drops. <i>(Jul 20, 1900,Evening Post,4</i>) [NOTES: Year is incorrect]

### thermal wonderland
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STAFFORD</b> All of this fuelled the desire of a multitude to travel into what was now being described as "The Thermal Wonderland." <i>(1977,Romantic Past of Rotorua,10</i>)

>  From Auckland, tourists would typically head for the thermal wonderland of the central North Island. <i>(Nov 2003,AA Directions,No. 58/55</i>)

>  A LECTURE will be given in St. THOMAS'S SCHOOLROOM .. Subject: "Our Thermal Wonderland." <i>(Jul 3, 1908,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  She decided to go to Waiotapu, a witch's brew of blasting steam, bubbling pools, heaving mud and giddy smells, a storm of the senses in a Thermal Wonderland. <i>(Apr 5, 1993,Evening Post,13</i>)

### thermette
 
 <i>n.</i> [brand name, cf. Benghazi burner]

>  <b>BRIDGES & DOWNS</b> The original Thermette was first sold in 1931 in a blue, green and orange tin, or in tinned copper if you had a few extra bob. <i>(2000,No 8 Wire: The Best of Kiwi Ingenuity,33</i>)

>  [Hoarding on advertising car in photo] THERMETTE 12 cups of tea in 5 minutes. To the farmer, camper, picnicker & motorist "The more wind the quicker it boils". <i>(Jun 10, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>) [NOTES: 1930-1940 vintage]

>  The Thermette, that old water-boiler-in-an-instant without which no New Zealand picnic was once complete is undergoing something of a revival. Thermette buyers.. are mostly middle-aged... For a while the Thermette also sold in Australia as "The Great New Aussie Billie" so named, says Mr Tull heavily, "because the Australians invented everything. <i>(Jan 14, 1999,Dominion,9</i>)

>  I'm not sure whether my useless, but quirky, thing will qualify. It is a thermette. For those who do not know what this is, it is one of the finest inventions to come out of New Zealand. An ingenious means of boiling water. <i>(Oct 6, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,4</i>)

>  Stevens agreed with Orsman in most respects, except that he said Hart got his first patent for the Thermette on October 29. 1930, and the later one was an extension... Both sources agreed that Kiwi soldiers nicknamed it the Benghazi boiler (after a coastal town in Libya), and that every desert unit had one as standard equipment <i>(Sep 18, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  Many a miserable night in the bush as been made more bearable by John Hart's wonderful Thermette. The idea was simple - a jug with a chimney up the middle of it and a built-in fireplace underneath <i>(Nov 30, 1999,Evening Post,27</i>)

>  [Advt.] Complete your Camping-Boating Holdiday by a Thermette. 12 cups of tea or coffee in five minutes with a genuine Thermette. <i>(Dec 1964,Sea Spray,14</i>)

>  The Quick-boiling Picnic Kettle "THERMETTE". <i>(Dec 4, 1930,NZ Truth,22</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> <i>Thermette</i>-An ingenious portable water-heater-fireplace, consisting of a short metal chimney surrounded by a water jacket. Extremely popular on picnic excursions, they will burn anything combustible, and provide a considerable quantity of boiling water in a very short time. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,184</i>)

>  <b>ARMFELT</b> But first Ray had to set up the thermette - a sort of tall cylindrical kettle, with a hollow up the middle in which Ray stuffed some paper and kindling for a fire. The fire was lit and the water in the walls around it boiled in next to no time. `She's a New Zealand invention, the thermette: the servicemen brought it back from the war'. <i>(1971,Catching Up,59</i>)

>  <i>thermette</i>: a genuine NZ word for a Benghazi, or a camp volcano <i>(1981,Rangiora High School 1884-1984</i>) [NOTES: Goldie Brown Collection]

>  Other families had "Thermettes", which I coveted as they involved lighting a real fire. <i>(Nov 5, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

>  How can we ever forget the rabbits and rabbiters, the thermette for the outdoor cuppa, home-made soap, felling trees with a cross-cut saw, splitting the logs with a maul, wedges and blasting gun? <i>(Feb 2004,NZ Memories,No.46/46</i>)

>  Harco Sales..was granted an extension of a patent issued on October 29, 1930, in the name of John Ashley Hart, in respect of an invention for 'means of heating water and cooking operations' called 'Thermette'. <i>(Jun 10, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>) [NOTES: 1930 original use of word]

>  We had to stoke up this water-boiling contraption with small sticks and dry twigs. We stuffed them down a funnel on top of some crumpled newspaper. A strike of the match and, by the time dad had pulled off his waders, the water was boiling for mother to make a cup of tea... It was a Thermette. <i>(Jun 10, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  I am glad to leave them, sprinkling water / Over the embers that heated the Thermette <i>(Sep 1967,Landfall,21(3) No. 83 / 257</i>)

>  In the meantime, no one can tell me if it's permissible to plonk a tartan car rug down on the front lawn of Huka Lodge, light up the Thermette, and tuck in to a good feed of curried egg sandwiches. <i>(May 6, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  [Advt] For a stop on the road, there's little to beat the efficiency of the "Thermette." This wonderful little kettle burns twigs, paper, rubbish, etc., creating its own forced draught. <i>(Sep 4, 1952,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  I learned to stay downwind of the aromatic manuka-fuelled thermette to avoid the sandflies. <i>(Sep 1998,North & South,34</i>)

>  Some old soldiers rang in. They had used the same device during the desert campaign in World War II. But it hadn't been called a Thermette. They knew it as a Benghazi Boiler or chip heater. <i>(Jun 10, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

### they must have captured a sugar boat
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>captured a sugar-boat!, they (occas. we) must have</i>. A cp explaining the issue of a liberal ration of sugar: NZders': in GW. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,127</i>)

### they're off, Mr Cutts
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> In 1875 G.G.Stead (1841-1908), the famous race-horse owner, bought a half-share in Redwood's racing stud. Under the partnership agreement Stead managed the business side while the training was put in the hands of E. Cutts. The horses were raced in the name of one or other of the partners. This arrangement, which proved immensely successful, lasted into the 1890s and was terminated when Stead bought out Redwood's share of the stud. During his lifetime Henry Redwood was known as `the Father of the New Zealand Turf.' <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/58</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> One student [of Canterbury Agricultural College], ... the son of a sporting and well-known racing baronet of North Canterbury, would take Jim into the stables at `Cutts' to see the horses in training; ... <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,60</i>)

>  They're off, Mr Cutts!' At least, they will be about three o'clock today (Saturday), and in less than four minutes afterwards, you will all know whether you have lost your money or not. <i>(Nov 4, 1911,NZ Truth,332/6/4</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> Horse-racing, another popular pastime (including trotting races, or <i>the trots</i>) provides two colloquialisms which need explanation. The exclamation `They're off, Mr Cutts!', announcing that any contest or affair has begun, contains the name of a one-time popular starter at the Auckland races. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,90</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Examples of slang and colloquial expressions of local origin are ... <i>they're off Mr Cutts,</i> for `the performance is about to start',... <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

>  <b>BAKER</b> ...they're off Mr Cutts!, meaning `things have started!' and or `now we're getting down to business!' <i>(1941,NZ Slang,50</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>They're off, Mr Cutts</i>. is a C. 20 New Zealand shape (B., 1941) cf:- [monkey] <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1200</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>they're off, Mr Cutts!</i> the race has begun; Partridge identifies as a variant of `they're off', said the monkey', usually indicating start of a horse race, hence as something that has come loose; often with addition: `when he looked into the lawnmower and carelessly lost his testicles.' This addition may explain Mr Cutts. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,112</i>)

>  "They're off, Mr Cutts!" Heaven only knows where the expression originated, but elderly Ted Cutts, Clifford's trainer, who is the person mentioned, was at Trentham, with his white hair trailing in the breeze. <i>(Jan 25, 1908,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  THEY'RE OFF, MR CUTTS!! What's that left? Kennaquhair? No, that's Kennaquhair out in front, with Poitrel! <i>(May 4, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,1</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> they're off, as the monkey said when he got his balls caught in the chaff-cutter (lawnmower) [ca1935 Havelock (chaffcutter) ca1955 Upper Hutt (lawnmower)]

### Think Big
 
 <i>n.</i> - [attrib. uses (also l.c.)]

>  It was he who saddled New Zealand with a think-big philosophy, which was really, I suppose, an antipodean example of a cargo cult gone mad. <i>(1986,NZ Parliamentary Debates,471/2270</i>)

>  Bill Birch appears to have been landed with much of the blame for the debacle over New Zealand Steel and other "Think Big" projects which have not performed up to expectations. <i>(Feb 15, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> On 29 September 1986, Cabinet invited me to prepare a report on the previous Government's decision-making on the Think Big projects. <i>(1987,Hard Labour,92</i>) [NOTES: 1986 Dec 16 in Moore Hard Labour (1987) 92]

>  The re-financing of the think-big debt has created ... <i>(Feb 12, 1987,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Morning Report National Radio Richard Shallcrass, Treasury Financial Director]

>  <b>WILKINSON</b> The `think big' policy is being mobilised here too. Too large a hospital means difficulties in communications, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 197 The reception given to National's `Think Big' policy had not been favourable....[fn] Significantly, it was later changed to `Think Growth'. <i>(1981,NZ Way, The,138</i>)

>  Birch has carried on. And on. There was Think Big with its billions of dollars of debt...<i>Ibid.</i> 31 There were six Think Big projects pushed through by Bill Birch as Minister of Energy and National Development in the lead-up to the 1981 election. .. In 1988 the Treasury calculated that the country was then $1.3 billion poorer because of Think Big projects, which accounted for 20 percent of all public debt. <i>(Dec 12, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  His most enduring legacy.. was the massive debt burden created by his extravagant national superannuation scheme.. later exacerbated by the Think Big projects. <i>(Aug 5, 1992,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Little changed behind the Think Big facade. <i>(Aug 9, 1992,Dominion Sunday Times,9</i>)

>  The Think Big projects came out of a national energy surplus that developed in the mid 1970s. <i>(Sep 17, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  I believe a story we ran forced the Muldoon government to back off polluting a mussel reef on the Taranaki coast with waste from a think big plant. <i>(2009,Mana,87/68</i>)

>  1983 will go down in history as the year the Think Big projects literally started to take shape on the New Zealand skyline. <i>(Apr 9, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  It was not so much an argument as a monologue - and discussed, like all "think big" disasters before it, as though the alternatives were this or nothing. <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  No to mention Muldoon's Thing Big / Lose Big programmes. <i>(1997,Mana,Autumn : 72</i>)

>  He wants the Council to start following a "Green City" programme aimed at reducing urban unemployment, and over-expenditure on "think big" style projects. <i>(Oct 5, 1992,Salient,9</i>)

>  When the Government sold the country its think-big strategy, it sold more than an economic game-plan - it put in motion forces which will have a profound impact on New Zealand. <i>(Mar 13, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an economic growth strategy developed by the National Government in the early 1980s to promote industrial development from natural resources such as natural gas and oil.

>  If our energy consumption has gone up by over 80% since Bill Birch visited <i>Think Big</i> on us, then aren't we in a bit of trouble? But of course I'm forgetting what <i>Think Big</i> was all about. <i>(Nov 22, 2000,Capital Times,16</i>)

>  But 'think big'.. was a sensible way of dealing with borrowing. <i>(Oct 21, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

>  Is it the member for Franklin, the person who brought us "think big"; is it the member for Rotorua, son of "think big"; is it the member for Waitotara?... <i> Ibid.</i> The Prime Minister may achieve a $5.8 billion debt - as he called it - as a consequence of "think big" and several magnificant projects developed by our former government. <i>(1986,NZ Parliamentary Debates,471/2272</i>)

>  There has been unusual - and healthy - criticism of Mr Quigley's forced resignation and of the apparent stifling of debate about "think big". <i>(Jul 10, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  Think Big now seems quite a noble thing in retrospect. <i>(May 10, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  There is no doubt that Think Big was a flawed means of establishing a gas industry infrastructure but establish one it did. <i>(May 2000,North & South,71</i>)

>  Such will never be the fate of any biographer of Sir Robert Muldoon, the former prime minister of this country who invented Think Big, a best-forgotten big adventure with taxpayers' money. <i>(Sep 1, 2011,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Birch has carried on. And on. There was Think Big with its billions of dollars of debt...<i>Ibid.</i> 31 There were six Think Big projects pushed through by Bill Birch as Minister of Energy and National Development in the lead-up to the 1981 election. .. In 1988 the Treasury calculated that the country was then $1.3 billion poorer because of Think Big projects, which accounted for 20 percent of all public debt. <i>(Dec 12, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  Ian Wedde's 1975 "Pathway to the Sea" was so insightful about development and the environment, I asked him if he revised it in the light of "Think Big." <i>(Jun 5, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  Think Big was the tonne weight that broke the camel's back. <i>(Aug 31, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Instead, we went for financial mysticism with even more disastrous consequences than "think big". <i>(Aug 4, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  This is in contrast to the heady days from 1977 on when the [rural] sector had its own version of Think Big. <i>(Sep 27, 1992,Dominion Sunday Times,18</i>)

### third rail
 
 <i>n.</i> (political use)

>  Over at National Party Central they have developed a code for those, ahem, awkward issues. They call them "third rail policies"... National's "third rail" is not a new policy alternative but a warning to its own politicians. The third rail on a railway track is the live one, the dangerous one. Touch its political equivalent and you risk a nasty electoral shock from the voting public... The politics of the third rail are simple: avoid, downplay or defuse issues for which the majority of the public has no stomach, even if the party and its MPs think they are desirable. <i>(May 13, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  Parallel to the economic message, Dr Brash is likely to continue softening some of the remaining "third rail" issues that national's strategists believe cost it votes at the September election. <i>(Feb 13, 2006,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

### Thirty-Niner
 
 <i>n.</i> Army veteran who enlisted in 1939;(often capital) one who volunteered for the First Echelon at the outbreak of World War II in 1939. often pl a soldier who volunteered for war service in 1939; a First Echelon man [cf. FOUR-FIGURE MAN, GRIM DIG)]

>  I asked the thirtyniner for his counsel on the subject and he replied... "You just pick it up, kickin' around... From the moment.. a thirty-niner guard told a myopic boatman to "stanna, stanna, asib, STANNA", I was filled with a desire to learn Arabic. <i>(Aug 9, 1943,NZEF Times,III/111 : 2</i>)

>  With the exception of the `thirty-niners' and other pioneers, most members of the 2NZEF left New Zealand with a reasonably fitting battledress. <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in the Middle East,3</i>)

>  <b>ST BRUNO</b> Frank Bruno left New Zealand as a machine-gunner with the `Thirty-Niners' (First Echelon) of the N.Z. Division. <i>(1959,Hellbuster, The,[back dust-jacket]</i>)

>  First Echelon reunion, Dunedin, Labour Weekend `Thirty-Niners' and their wives who intend travelling to Dunedin ... <i>(Apr 18, 1964,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  The forgotten legion, the thirty-niners, the four-figure man ...all sound like mysterious titles. [an article on First Echelon] <i>(Sep 18, 1982,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  That's not a Wog, son. That's a Thirty-Niner! <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),3(11) : 22</i>)

>  That's not a Wog, son - that's a Thirty-Niner, my oath. <i>(Oct 19, 1942,NZEF Times,II/69 : 12</i>)

### this side
 
 <i>phr.</i> sc. of the Tasman; New Zealand (a response to THE OTHER SIDE, Australia)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Australian aborigine words which were new to this side. In 1851 a number of Australian `squatters' came over and took up runholding in Canterbury, but, generally speaking, their advent was some years later in Otago. ... They brought over a number of Australian aborigine words that were new to this side, and which were used a certain amount, usually in a joking manner as far as I know. I have heard some of them fairly frequently used by the `old hands,' sometimes oracularly and sometimes as a semi-humorous quotation. When I was a boy I heard sheep called `jumbucks,' an Australian aborigine term for them, but with the decease of ex-Australian stockmen the word has gone out of use now. Another term brought over from Australia was `maimai,' for a rough shelter from the weather. It was said to be a name for a blackfellow's camp, which I have always seen spelt `miamia,' and which is called `gunyah' or `wurley,' etc., by some tribes. We now apply the name to shooting shelters. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,10</i>)

### thistle grubber
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm worker set to dig up noxious thistles.

### thistle money
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [1800s] a development entitlement payment available to bush settlers.

>  <b>H R RICHMOND</b> I write to know whether you wish any claim made in respect of your clearing at Hurworth. You would probably be allowed, thistle money at 30/- per acre and rent at 20/- per acre per annum or perhaps less ... <i>(1863,Letter,33</i>) [NOTES: Letter in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers II]

### thornfish
 
 <i>n.</i>

Both the common names are derived from the sharp spines which puncture the flesh of one's hands when taking the Thornfish from a net. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,299</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> THORNFISH ... <i>Other names: Bovichtus variegatus. </i> <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,266</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The thornfish, <i>Bovichtus variegatus</i>, is a rock-pool fish, up to ten inches long, of Stewart Island and the islands further south. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,224</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> THORNFISH A distinguished-looking, but poor-eating, fish the scaleless Thornfish inhabits our inshore, rocky coasts in cooler waters from about the central North Island southwards. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,260</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>THORNFISH </i>(Horny, dragonet) <i>Bovichtus variegatus (Bovichthys variegatus) </i>... The thornfish is a small, cylindrical fish with scaleless body ... There is a distinctive strong spine directed upward and backward on each gill cover behind the eyes. ... Thornfish are common in tidepools ... from the Wellington area southwards ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,274,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Thornfish Bovichthys variegatus</i> Family Bovichthyidae (thornfishes). Sometimes called horny, or dragonet (which is the name of a similar but unrelated fish). Also occurs off south-east Australia. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,117 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] thornfish 1861-4 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### thorough brace
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The coach used at the time was a type known as a "Thorough Brace," which meant that instead of steel springs the coach was suspended on several thicknesses of buffalo leather, which ran from the front under the body to the back of the coach. These coaches were in common use throughout Australia and New Zealand in the early days of mail coaches. Leather suspension was used because steel springs would not stand up to the rough roads. <i>(1965,Otakeho District & School 80th Jubilee 1884-1964,18</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the breeding of thoroughbred stock, usually horses for the racing industry.

### thrash
 
 <i>v.</i> to overwork (anything mechanical, espec. an engine or vehicle) excessively; to work or drive at an excessive pace hence, to overwork or overexploit soil, country, a trapping block, a fishery, etc.; to take more than a sustained yield espec. as a ppl aj <i>thrashed,</i> `overexploited; ruined, done in (mechanically)' [ca1938 Havelock That car's been pretty well thrashed after only three thousand miles if the rings are gone.]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> I found that deer don't hang about in the open once you start thrashing [sc. overshooting] a place, and it became necessary to go farther and farther along the range. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,24</i>)

>  <b>LAWSON</b> We raced her past the Brothers,/And thrashed her through the Straits,/And where the wild rip smothers ... <i>(1903,Red West Road,17</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to move cattle by use of stockwhip

>  [The cattle] were in two mobs ... We commenced thrashing them about 8 a.m. and by 7 p.m. had verilly thrashed them over some 20 miles. I never [125] thrashed cattle so much before. <i>(Mar 15, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,124,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Wilkinson & James thrashing. <i>(Jul 21, 1866,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> In New Zealand slang a penny is a <i>brown</i>, a threepenny piece is a <i>thray</i> (cf. English<i> tray</i>), a sixpence a <i>sax</i>, presumably from Scottish saxpence. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,91</i>)

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> Threepence [:] thray <i>(1972,From Beads to Banknotes,151</i>)

### thread fern
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Climbing ferns are much less dominant in New Zealand bush than in tropical rainforest, but mokimoki (<i>Microsorum scandens</i>), <i>Rumohra adiantiformis</i> and thread fern (<i>Blechnum filiforme</i>) are common throughout, while jointed fern (<i>Arthropteris tenella</i>) and mangemange (<i>Lygopodium articulatum</i>) are characteristic of the warmer northern forest. The thread fern is an unusual plant, beginning life on the ground where it scrambles around until it finds a trunk to climb. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/76</i>)

### three corner(s)
 
 <i>n.</i> a flatfish

>  <b>WAITE</b> Sand Flounder New Zealand Flounder, Patiki, Three corner, Tinplate <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i> <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/197</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> NEW ZEALAND SAND-FLOUNDER (PATIKI) <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i> (Richardson) ... The common Sand-flounder is known from one end of New Zealand to the other, sometimes by other names in different localities. In Auckland it is known as the Dab.../ Some refer to it as Tinplate or Three Corner; the latter name is derived from its fancied trangular shape but why Tinplate I am at a loss to know. To other than fishermen and those intimately connected with the fishing industry it is just a Flounder. Some say it is Tinplate because when cooked it fills a plate. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,191</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>FLOUNDER</i> (four species) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i> (ii) <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> (iii) <i>Rhombosolea tapirina</i> (iv) <i>Rhombosolea leporina</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) Flounder (ii) Freshwater Flounder (iii) Flounder (iv) Yellowbelly Flounder. (c) Other common names: (i) Square, Dab, Plaice, Diamond or Diamond-back, Tin-plate, Three Corners. (ii) Blacks, Black Flounder (iii) Greenback [25 `Not common but occasionally taken in southern waters.'] (iv) Yellow Flounder. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,24</i>)

>  [species name] sand flounder .. [scientific name] <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i> .. [other name(s)] dab, diamond back, square, tin-plate, three corners <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### throw
 
 <i>v.</i> of humans, to give birth to vicariously (here transf.) (cp. drop)

 <i>n.</i>
