>  <b>McARA</b> When the round of holes was fired each was detonated in sequence from the front and all the broken rock was heaped up at the opposite end of the shaft, this being the `throw' of the holes. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi,179</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> in main sense of cast

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> George had to be shown how to pick up a fleece and how to / throw it. <i>(1965,Skippers Road,93</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> Bill hadn't learned to pick up and throw a fleece, and he felt the anger of a fleeco was directed at him for not getting stuck in when the shearer cried, `Wool away!' ... Fleecos hated this; it made the fleece difficult to throw. The aim was to stand by as the shearer made his last blow, gather the fleece into a compact armful, and throw it like a tablecloth on the wooltable, weather side up. <i>(1981,Sunrise,46</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to fell, cut through, or knock down [(cf. DROP, KNOCK DOWN)]

>  The pines should be thrown last because then they do not cover up the other stuff until it is dry. <i>(Nov 10, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/158</i>) [NOTES: H.A. Atkinson]

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> ... the trees are thrown with the x [sic] cut saw and American axe. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/331</i>)

>  <b>WELCH</b> ... trees thrown with a cross-cut saw and American axe. <i>(1863,NZ Guide,22</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> They could fell a tree in real bushwhackers' style, and could judge best how to `throw' the tree too. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,138</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to put on; perform etc (See [wobbly; sickie; hollywood](../W#wobbly;-sickie;-hollywood))



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [land] to produce feed for stock.

>  This farm 'throws a lot of feed before Christmas,' he says. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,84</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to throw an animal to the ground

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> At Macfarlane's the men saddled fresh horses and rode in some cattle which we had a great bother to yard, ... The yard was divided into three - ... but as there was no crush pen, or drafting race, we had to head rope those requiring brands, and ... legroped and threw them ...[chk] <i>(Dec 18, 1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Dec 18 W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 43]



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> of a crop, to reap [[see DROP (a tree) for quot]]

### throw a mental
 
 <i>phr.</i> have a mad fit, become enraged [1984 17 F E53 Pakuranga Coll. 5 THROWS A MENTAL]

>  <b>Faith Oxenbridge</b> Bea was down for the university break at the time and threw a mental. 'What do you mean, Brian?' Bea said dangerously. <i>(2007,Six Pack 2, The,19</i>)

### throw an ep
 
 <i>phr.</i> put on a demonstration of temper, annoyance, pique, etc. (resembling an epileptic fit); cf bersy. [ca1945-46 Air Training Corps (D. Close Letter May, 1951)]

### throw down
 
 <i>v.</i> to fell a structure

>  <i>(Jul 22, 1911,NZ Truth,317/6/3</i>) [NOTES: He gave instructions to Alexander Davey, head labourer, to take 4 [and-a-half] inches out of the bottom of the wall, and to leave three or four bricks, or `pins' to support the wall, which was the usual method of throwing down a structure of this kind.]

### throw into
 
 <i>v.</i> sc to put runs or blocks into a goldfield

>  <b>EVANS</b> ... as these runs are likely to be wanted for settlement, I should not recommend their being thrown into a gold field till after they have been thrown into Hundreds when the Waste Lands Board could refuse to sell any portion ... considered highly auriferous. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,16</i>)

### throw off at
 
 <i>v.</i> to deride; to tease

>  <i>(1953,Short Stories (various authors)</i>) [NOTES: 1950 Landfall in 1953 Short Stories]

### throw open
 
 <i>v.</i> sc. a block of land, for settlement

>  <b>BELL</b> If I had a couple of hundred acres of good bush land, and a wife like that girl down there would make, I wouldn't call the Queen my aunt; and, by Jove! I mean to have a buck at some of those sections in the Whakatangi Block that'll be thrown open shortly, ... <i>(1899,In the Lee of the Hokonuis,84</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The system of `free selection' enabled a would-be `cockatoo' to choose land in localities `thrown open' to selectors (under certain conditions regarding residence, improvement, and area) and to buy the freehold. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,155</i>)

>  The circumstamces under which they occupied the land had been explained at a former meeting, and it was resolved to remain satisfied with what had been done until the natives could be induced to throw open the Upper Thames. <i>(Oct 19, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  All the pettifogging attempts that have been made to prevent land being thrown open sink into insignificance - collapse like a punctured balloon - when pierced and confronted by plain facts. <i>(Apr 29, 1874,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  Mr. W. W. M'Cardle drew attention to the very large number of persons who had attended the late sale, eager to obtain selections, but who, owing to the limited amount of land thrown open, had failed in their endeavours to secure holdings. <i>(Aug 29, 1889,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  The Lands Department will throw open about 59,000 acres of first and second class land in the Bay of Plenty districts. <i>(Jan 31, 1903,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  Altogether, such a course would throw open about five thousand acres for settlement. <i>(Jul 23, 1912,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that on Monday, June 6, 1842, the township of Wanganui and Country Sections in that district will be thrown open for selection at the Resident Surveyor's Office at Wanganui, at 11 A.M. precisely, and close at 4 P.M.; one half the choices will be made on the first day, and the remainder on the second or following day. <i>(May 4, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to offer or make free land for selection or balloting.

>  <b>WARD</b> We have thought and deemed it proper to throw open all the country ... <i>(1858,BLANK ENTRY,45</i>) [NOTES: in Reeves (1883) Canterbury Rhymes]

>  It is rumored - but we can't vouch for the accuracy of it - that as soon as road lines are cut through the bush country the land will be thrown open for free selection as was done in Canterbury ... <i>(Oct 6, 1880,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  The surveyors are pushing on with the survey of the Waimarino block up the Wanganui River, part of which will shortly be open for application. <i>(Nov 1891,NZ Farmer,471</i>)

>  This is a sample of a large portion of the land shortly to be thrown open by the Crown ... <i>(Jun 1898,NZ Farmer,197</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> By Jove! I mean to have a buck at some of those sections in the Whakatangi Block that'll be thrown open shortly. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,84</i>)

>  In one case, where a section was thrown open by the Government to be ballotted, there were over two hundred applicants. <i>(Mar 1907,NZ Farmer,214</i>)

>  With the idea of giving information to those acquiring land, there will be published in this column full particulars of all Crown, public, and native lands thrown open for settlement. <i>(1908,Weekly News,47</i>)

>  It is interesting to watch the development of an estate such as Starborough or Flaxbourne, which has been purchased by the government and thrown open to settlers. <i>(May 1909,NZ Farmer,394</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Following its destruction, through man's agency by fore and stock, a huge area of virgin soil was, to use a New Zealand political term, 'thrown open' to selection. <i>(1921,Tutira,236</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The system of 'free selection' enabled a would-be 'cockatoo' to choose land in localities 'thrown open' to selectors (under certain conditions regarding residence, improvement and area) and to buy the freehold. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,115</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [light cloth thrown over food to keep off flies etcetera aka (Waikato) tea shower.]

>  These plate throw overs are lightweight nylon edged with different cotton fabrics. Ideal for covering food at barbeques and the like. <i>(2010,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.felt.co.nz/listing/28150/Plate-Throw-Over]

 <i>n.</i> (Sport) an artificial cricket pitch? [no Google evidence found TD May06]

### throw-up
 
 <i>n.</i> [graffiti term]

>  Leaving the ranks of bored adolescents getting quick thrills from quick tags (toys, as the amateurs are called), she has gone on to the more esteemed throwup or bomb (a tag in fat letters with the outline coloured in), then the dub (a throwup with an outer line and bubbles), and finally the piece, short for masterpiece (a dub with filled in letters and a painted background). <i>(Jan 20, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)

### throwaway
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> The guy across from me had a blanket over him and his blue Addidases [check spg???] poking out in the aisle. He drained an L & P, flicked through a throwaway, killed his light. <i>(1985,G'day Country,50</i>)

### throwdown
 
 <i>n.</i> a small banger or modified firework set off by throwing onto a hard surface - [sold pre-Guy Fawkes in NZ in 1940 (DEH), also figurative (see 1907)]

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1896,Little Larrikin,321</i>)

>  "A cold throwdown," says I to myself, and then I turns to him, "I guess she didn't get your wire - she ain't there.' <i>(Aug 3, 1907,Poverty Bay Herald,1</i>)

>  "I was not doing it for mischief," said a youth named James Owen, at Lower Hutt to-day, when charged before Mr. E. Page, S.M., with letting off "throwdowns" in the King George Theatre during a picture performance. <i>(Jan 14, 1920,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  He had stopped at a shop and bought them, a penn'orth of throwdowns. <i>(Dec 23, 1914,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> All along Stout Road kids were exploding throwdowns on the footpaths, making women and girls squeak. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,67,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> ... it was a fireworks thing, called a `throw-down' <i>(1935,Little Country, The,114</i>)

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the worker at a swim or pot sheep dip responsible for encouraging sheep into the dip.

 <i>n.</i>

### thrum
 
 <i>n.</i> short for thrummer an old informal word for a threepenny piece

>  Following are other local money-names...3d.<i> thrum, half-tiz, tray or tray-piece... </i> <i>(Jan 14, 1899,Sydney Bulletin,(Red Page)</i>) [NOTES: Letter from <i>Loafer</i>, Tauranga) [Bill Pearson]]

>  ... a large number of casuals have dropped in [to the pub] to spend a thrum ... . <i>(Sep 30, 1905,NZ Truth,15/1/6</i>)

>  There was ten bob in pennies.. four bob in thrums. <i>(Mar 19, 1904,NZ Observer,16</i>)

>  "Got a duff?"... "Oh, yes. I made two, filled ours with thrums and sent one to mother." <i>(Apr 2, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,71</i>)

>  About 300 names given to good money and coin in slang and Engish by NEMO, Reefton, (Concluded) .. teviss, thrums, time, thrummers ... <i>(Mar 17, 1888,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

### thrummer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I'm single, and jingle / A thrummer and a brown, / Assessors and guessers / Of tax have done me down. <i>(Feb 12, 1937,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> Mother ... what about a thrummer? <i>(1950,Otago Interval,52</i>) [NOTES: ca1950 [?1890s]]

>  About 300 names given to good money and coin in slang and Engish by NEMO, Reefton, (Concluded) .. teviss, thrums, time, thrummers ... <i>(Mar 17, 1888,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  The humble "thrummer" is a big item on a shilling and two bob a day stunt. <i>(May 24, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/44 : 183</i>)

>  He was up about Waimate working fairly night and day, / He'd forked in eighty bushels wheat - that wasn't no child's play - / He'd wallabied right into town to save his blooming fare, / and now if he'd a thrummer left he says he wouldn't care. <i>(Apr 29, 1897,Otago Witness,47</i>)

### thrush
 
 <i>n.</i> any of a family of songbirds, often brown; <i>Turnagara capensis</i>, piopio (See [also piopio](../A#also-piopio),[shrike thrush](../S#shrike-thrush))

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We landed & ... shot a Thrush [[ed. fn] <i>Turdus ochrotarsus</i> of <i>Descr. Anim</i>.,<i>Ibid.</i> 2/269 The male & female of a small Thrush [[ed. fn] <i>Turdus minutus </i>of <i>Descr. Anim</i>. p.83; ... Now the Yellow-breasted Tit ...] was shot, which we hitherto had taken for a variety of the small yellow legged Thrush, commonly called Robin by our Ship's-crew; ... <i>(Mar 27, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/240</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... Pohebirds, Fantails, Thrushes, Mocking birds, Yellowheads & various other birds inhabit these impenetrable Forests. [ed. fn ... the `thrushes' are the Yellow-breasted Tit and South Island Robin] <i>(May 13, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/278</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... the birds seemed to retire from it to a little distance, where the shrill notes of thrushes, the graver pipe of wattle-birds, and the enchanting melody of various creepers resounded on all sides ... <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/148</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The wren and the tomtit and the thrush all are represented quite as nearly or even more so than the robin save that the tomtit is black headed with a yellow breast. <i>(1860,Forest Creek MS,44,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Another ... belonging to the family Turdidae, is the piopio (Turnagra crassirostris), the thrush of the settlers. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,140</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> We have a thrush, but it is rather rare. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,ix/137</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 36. KEROPIA TANAGRA. Schleg. Thrush. Pio-pio. <i>Tumagra Hectori. BULLER </i> <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,14 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> I do not know to what to attribute the scarcity of small birds, ...such as crows, saddlebacks, thrushes, tuis, canaries, wrens, riflebeaks, fantails and many others. <i>(1987,Richard Henry of Resolution Island,88</i>) [NOTES: 1897 Apr 5 E.H. Wilmot Exploration and Topographical Survey in Fiord Country in 1987 Hill Richard Henry of Resolution Island 88]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>This common English bird-name is applied in Australia and New Zealand to four different genera of birds, ... (5) Turnagra</i> (the New Zealand Thrushes), viz. -<i> T. hectori,</i> Buller, North Island Thrush. ... <i>T. crassirostris</i>, Gmel., South Island Thrush. <i>(1898,Austral English,468</i>)

>  It is interesting to note that the Piopio, or Native Thrush (<i>Turnagra crassirostris</i>), and the Long-tailed Cuckoo ... are somewhat [similar] in appearance. <i>(1904,TrNZI,36/124</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 40. COLLURICINCLA CONCINNA. N.S. Shrike Thrush. <i>(1875,Birds of New Zealand,15</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [pipit, snow thrush; one cit]

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

EXTINCT <i>Chatham Island Fernbird B.p. rufescens</i> (BULLER, 1869). OTHER NAMES: <i>Swamp thrush, grass bird, matata.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,274</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

### thunderpants
 
 <i>n.</i>

They are made of cotton and designed to a broad-of-bum brief that delivers satisfaction, as promised by their rather earthly advertising: 'They don't ride up your bum'. <i>(Nov 2003,AA Directions,No. 58/42</i>)

It was the name given to underwear at the boarding school Josie attended... <i>(Jun 19, 2003,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

### thunderplump
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  DELUGED [heading] I am beginning to think that I must be the only ignoramus in Dunedin who has never heard of a thunderplump. Everyone else has known the word since childhood and, as a Buccleugh Street reader neatly put it, I was fairly deluged with thunderplumps yesterday. Other readers wanted to explain that `plumps' were to be found in many dictionaries, but I was aware of that anyway. Most of the people who knew of thunderplump claimed a Scottish origin for it, but it still took me quite a time to locate it in my range of dictionaries. Finally I ran it to earth in a seven-volume Century, published back in 1891, which gave it as `A short violent downpour of rain in connection with a thunder-storm (Rare),' and produced a 19th century quotation. Perhaps we didn't hear it much in Invercargill in my younger days because thunderplumps are pretty uncommon there; but a colleague who came from Winton certainly knew of it; and it was even noised abroad by the Canterbury English. ... <i>(Nov 18, 1977,Otago Daily Times</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

 <i>n.</i> marijuana

### thyme
 
 <i>n.</i> . [citations both 'wild thyme']

>  The soil is consequently dry, and apparently barren enough; but the herbage which it affords is that which peculiarly suits the sheep, and the cocksfoot grass, and the clover (trifolium pretense), and the common thyme, and the wild thyme, and the lavender (lavandula spica), grow amid the flint, affording a pasture nutritive, without one dangerous aqueous property, and of luxuriance that would scarcely be deemed possible. <i>(Dec 21, 1844,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  <b>WELD</b> ... several veronicas of great beauty, wild thyme, dwarf yew, Alpine rose (<i>consanthenum</i>), white violet, a ribes, several heaths, gentianella, harebell, a lily, and several varieties of ranunculus. <i>(Jan 1851,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Jan Weld Letter in 1851 Feb 21 NZGG 34]



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>?Samolus</i> spp.

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> WILD THYME (<i>Thymus vulgaris</i>) ... is a European plant of characteristic smell and taste, and is used for flavouring. It is best known for its dense growth on some hillsides in Central Otago. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,173</i>)

### ti
 
 <i>n.</i> cabbage tree [ORIGIN: Maori, The form <i>ti-tree </i> often leads to confusion with TEA-TREE (=MANUKA) and possibly underlies the spellings <i>ti-tree, titri </i> for <i>tea-tree </i>. (But see TI-SHRUB for distinction from MANUKA] (See [also ti palm](../A#also-ti-palm))

>  The name "cabbage" tree seems to have been applied to the ti because the leaf buds provided for the early settlers a substance that could be cooked and eaten in the place of green vegetables. <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),III/1 : 13</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> (19 January 1847) Collected a quantity of the roots of the ti, or cabbage tree, which we placed in a humu or native oven, for the night. <i>(Jan 19, 1847,Diary</i>) [NOTES: quoted in 1982 Burton Two Hundred Years of NZ Food 19]

>  <i>Ti</i> (t&emacr.), <i>n</i>. [Polynesian.]<i> (Bot.) </i>A liliaceous tree of the genus <i>Cordyline</i>, common in the Polynesian islands and in New Zealand; especially <i>Cordyline ti</i>; - called also <i>titi, ti-tree</i>, and <i>ti-tri.</i> The genus <i>Cordyline</i> comprises twelve species, distributed in the West Indies, the Malay Archipelago, the Pacific Islands, and Brazil. Four species are found in Australia, and six in New Zealand. The name ti is usually confined to <i>C. ti. C. indivisa </i>and <i>C. australis</i> are more commonly called cabbage tree. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>ti</i>: cabbage tree; grows to height of about 30 ft. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,195</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|i, <i>s.</i> The root called Tee, which when baked is very sweet; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,212</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Hattay [crossed out by Markham] or Ettay [<i>heti</i>] is a Tea tree, quite different to the Tea tree bush, a sort of myrtle; as Parr a Fort and Hipparr [<i>he pa</i>] a Fort in Cooke, / is put down The Hip or Hit, is only the Article. Tay, it is useless, it only grows eight to twelve feet and the bark rough, and does not change. It seems very often Solitary. Away from every other Tree. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),33,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... the guardian ... pointed to a small bunch of human hair made fast to a <i>ti</i>, or cabbage palm-tree, denoting the strictness of the tapu. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/67</i>)

>  This last [Mauku] the root of a shrub called by the Mauries T is of extraordinary sweetness when dressed ... . <i>(Apr 1842,Journal of Ensign Best,344,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> We ... turned ... over the downs and hillocks of the coast, which were covered with fern and flax, overshadowed here and there by a picturesque ti (Dracaena australis). <i>Ibid.</i> 2/387 Ti - sweet root of the dragon-tree <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/142</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> This evening, Huruhuru made me a pair of sandals, such as are in common use among these natives. They are called `paraerae', and are made either with the leaves of the flax-[210]plant, or of the `ti' <i>(cordyline Australis)</i>. ... Mine were a pair with double soles, called `torua', ... <i>(Jan 13, 1844,Southern Districts of NZ,209,1851 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> At the first view of this plain [Molyneux], ... I observed a continued line of dry land, indicated by the number of Te trees, and the frequent succession of pine groves. <i>(May 7, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 May 7 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 217]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... cabbage-tree, called ti by the natives. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/58</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The ti (Cordyline australis or Dracaena australis) is found in great abundance. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,435</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> [We found] what was to us quite a new kind of tree, called <i>nei nei</i>. It is something like a diminutive <i>ti. </i> <i>(Jan 8, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 8 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 355]

>  Turned out by waeroas about 4 a.m. ... Eat kumara, ti & kuharu. [fn.] The root of the ti - rather like licorice root. <i>(Dec 20, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/67,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Ti, <i>Col[enso]. Cordyline australis</i> and<i> indivisa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  Ti. Cordyline australis, Hook. f. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/50</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Ti. Cordyline australis, Hook. f. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/50</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth ... (e.g.) the Houhere, <i>Hoheria populnea</i>; ..the Ti, <i>Cordyline australis;</i>.. and, on the sea-coast, ... the Karo, <i>Pittosporum crassifolium</i>; and the truly evergreen Ngaio, <i>Myoporum laetum</i> ... <i>Ibid.</i> Besides which the following roots and plants were often eaten, viz., the roots (cooked) of the Panahi (<i>Calystegia sepium</i>); of the Maikaika (<i>Arthropodium cirrhatum</i>); ... the roots of the little sugary Ti-koraha (<i>Cordyline stricta</i>), of the large Ti, or `Cabbage Tree' (<i>C. australis</i>), and of the large Fern, Para, (<i>Marattia salicina</i>). <i>Ibid.</i> 32 The young inner blanched leaves and heart of the Ti, or `Cabbage-tree' (<i>Cordyline australis</i>), and of the Nikau, or New Zealand Palm, (<i>Areca sapida</i>), were eaten both raw and cooked. <i>Ibid.</i> 36 ... the roots of the Tikoraha (<i>Cordyline stricta</i>), were tied separately for baking ... for their Kao, or prepared sweet potatoes, they used the leaves of the Parataniwha (<i>Elatostemma rugosum</i>); ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/5</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  Tikapu. Cordyline indivisa, Kunth. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/50</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>C[ordyline] australis, Hook.f. ... Ti; Ti-kauka; Ti-rakau; Palm-lily</i>. ... Universally known to New Zealand residents by the inappropriate name of `cabbage-tree'. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,706</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>Ti (tee)</i>: The Cabbage-tree. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,185</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The Kapenga tableland ..., a gully-seamed broken plateau, is covered with a thick growth of <i>manuka</i> and <i>monoao</i> shrubs,<i> tutu</i> and fern, with many <i>ti</i> or cabbage trees ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/399,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <i>ti[i]</i>, a tree (Cordyline spp.), cabbage-tree, palm-lily, the various species being in Maori denoted by the adding of a descriptive suffix: .br;ti[i]-kouka, C. australis (kouka the chief of several suffixes for this species. ti[i]-kapu, C. indivisa. ti[i]-rauriki, C. pumilo (also ti[i]-koraka). ti[i]-ngahere, C. Banksii. ti[i]-parae, C. stricta. ti[i] is the only term in common use, and it has been wonderfully maltreated. see <i>manuka. </i> <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>ti</i>: cabbage tree <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  A Pacific Island and New Zealand (Maori) name for the palm,<i> ti</i> `cabbage-tree'. (See cabbage-tree.) <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),413</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> ... and a greasy little fellow with buck teeth and hair styled like ti was still the rocking fifties, beside him. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,304</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> [Title] Ti (cabbage tree) There are a number of species of cabbage tree (Cordyline) in New Zealand, all of which provide a vegetable in the form of white inner trunk or heart and the fleshy tap-root. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,7</i>)

>  <i>ti n. NZ</i> cabbage-tree; any of several trees related to the cabbage-tree. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,803</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  . - This is the broad-leaved, deep green Ti, with red veins, a single head, and long elegant flowers. It is found plentifully on the West Coast, and merits a place in every collection. Maori, Ti-kapu. <i>(May 28, 1902,Otago Witness,10</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The tropical-looking dragon-trees (dracaena), called ti by the natives, and the karaka-tree, grow up the steep sides of the hills ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/240</i>)

>  At 3 p.m. Charlie arrived with potatoes and ti from the pah. <i>Ibid.</i>72 ti-root as food He [Tarawata's father] went off with all our enakis and ti and my stick - but I pursued him and recovered the latter. <i>(Mar 31, 1849,Torlesse Papers,71,1958 ed.</i>)

>  At first the old hands predicted that when cleared of fern, ti and bush it would blow away. <i>(Feb 7, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/76</i>)

>  Frequent groves of Ti (C. australis) <i>(1851,Canterbury Papers,31</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> TI, in North Island commonly called Whanake<i> (cordyline ti),;a liliaceous plant, resembling in appearance the Xanthorrhaea, or grass-tree of Australia. </i> <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,314</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> ... the Cordyline australis called the `ti' ... has the appearance of a narrow leaved yucca ... <i>(Feb 3, 1853,Traveller under Concern,76,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> ... the sides of the [Wellington] hills ... are almost tropical in their character, from the abundance of the <i>ti</i>, or dragon trees, with which they are covered. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),92</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>Cordyline australis,</i> Endl. ... Nat. name, `Ti',<i> Cunn. </i> ... [Cordyline] Natives of the Tropical islands and South Temperate zone; ... The celebrated Ti of the South Sea Islands (whence the New Zealand name `Ti' for<i> C. australis</i>) belongs to this genus ... <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 257</i>)

>  <b>2/126</b> Cordyline Pumilio Tirauriki <i>(1870,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Ti, or `cabbage-tree palm' <i>(Cordyline Australis)</i>, grows as high as fifty feet. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/192</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Asphodel (Ti) .....<i> O</i>. Liliaceae; <i>G.</i> Neodaphne; <i>S.</i> C. Australis. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/336</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Ti, N.</i> the name of various species of trees of the genus <i>Cordyline, N.O. Liliaceae</i>. It exists in the Pacific Islands as<i> C. Ti,</i> and in New Zealand the species are <i>C. australis</i> and <i>C. indivisa</i>. Is is called in New Zealand the <i>Cabbage-tree </i>(q.v.), and the heart used to be eaten by the settlers. The word is Polynesian. In Hawaiian, the form is<i> Ki</i>; in Maori, <i>Ti</i>. Compare <i>Kanaka</i> (q.v.) and <i>Tangata</i>. By confusion, <i>Tea,</i> in Tea-tree <i>(q.v.)</i>, is frequently spelt <i>Ti</i>, and <i>Tea-tree</i> is sometimes spelt <i>Ti-tri</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,469</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> For in their season he could supplement his dry fare with berries and nuts, with the tender bulbous shoots of the tii (cabbage tree), at heart white and delicate, baked in a haangi; the tender leaves of the nikau - that part between the last ring and where the leaf stems separate, below which there is a huge bulge, clasped firmly, clasp on clasp, with the grip of steel bands. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,16</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>C[ordyline] australis, Hook.f. ... Ti; Ti-kauka; Ti-rakau; Palm-lily.</i> ... Universally known to New Zealand residents by the inappropriate name of `cabbage-tree'. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,706</i>)

>  There were also plenty of sugar ti (draecana terminalis), the true ti of Polynesia. <i>(Sep 8, 1917,NZ Truth,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> obs. elliptical form of ti-tree = tea-tree, manuka

>  <b>POLACK</b> The<i> ti</i> is a useful close grained wood, well adapted for handspikes. It grows to forty feet with crooked branches. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/397</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a finger game among Maoris

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),113</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

 <i>n.</i> [aka tekapu, te-kapu]

>  - This is the broad-leaved, deep green Ti, with red veins, a single head, and long elegant flowers. It is found plentifully on the West Coast, and merits a place in every collection. Maori, Ti-kapu. <i>(May 28, 1902,Otago Witness,10</i>)

>  "Ti-tree," on the other hand, is the cabbage tree, of which there are several species, such as ti-mataku-tai (or toii), ti-kapu, ti-rapo, ti kupenga, &c. <i>(Jan 29, 1887,Wanganui Chronicle,2</i>)

>  Rangiora bloom is over, but ake-piro, ake-rautangi, ti-kapu, harakeke, and manuka are in full flower. <i>(Dec 3, 1895,Wanganui Chronicle,2</i>)

### ti kauka
 
 <i>n.</i> cabbage tree [cf. ti kouka]

>  Kawakawa (pepper tree) leaves for aching joints, bruises and eczema, ti kauka (cabbage tree) leaves for cuts, sores and abrasions, pohutukawa bark for toothache and harakeke (flax) for wounds and skin ulcers are just a few of the huge number of native plants that were used by Maori and early European settlers for treatment of ailments. <i>(Aug 13, 2003,Capital Times,14</i>)

>  Ti Kauka of Whanake is the familiar Cabbage tree (<i>Cordyline australis</i>. <i>(Oct 2000,Growing Today,40</i>)

### ti koraha
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tikoraha, <i>Col[enso]. Cordyline stricta</i>. ...Tikoraha,<i> Geolog. Surv. Cordyline pumilio. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

### ti kouka
 
 <i>n.</i> cabbage tree [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i> Cordyline australis. (Ti-kouka. The Cabbage-Tree or Palm Lily). </i> <i>(1907,Plant Life in Maoriland,93,1940 p. 95 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> Ti-kouka and whanake are varieties of the ti .. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,6</i>)

>  <b>ADCOCK</b> ti-kouka<i> commonly `cabbage tree'</i>, Cordyline australis, <i>with distinctive long narrow leaves growing in tufts </i> <i>(1982,Contemporary NZ Poetry,143</i>)

>  The landscape of rainswept, ragged pasture, remnant stands of natives and brave, lonely ti kouka standing against the rising southerly aren't much like the rich land around Stratford, where I grew up. <i>(Jun 15, 2000,City Voice,14</i>)

>  <b>HUTT</b> One of the early infamous nineteenth-century Southland illegal stills was run by Owen McShane, who converted the sugary pulp of cabbage tree roots (ti kouka) into a concoction similar to brandy or rum... Missionary and Maori alike made beer from ti kouka. <i>(1999,Maori & Alcohol,14</i>) [NOTES: see also Eldred-Grigg <i> "Pleasures of the Flesh..."</i>, p. 73; Riley, <i>Maori Healing & Herbal</i>, p. 18]

>  Niwa climate scientist Darren King said the second flowering of ti kouka was likely to reflect greater variability of recent regional temperatures. <i>(Mar 23, 2006,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

### ti kupenga
 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tikupenga, <i>Lindsay. Cordyline stricta. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

### ti mi
 
 <i>n.</i> ?tainui <i>Pomaderris apetala</i>

>  <b>PAULIN</b> Among the trees Mr. Traill pointed out [on Stewart Island], was what he called a `Ti Mi' tree, and which he said was only found growing in one place in New Zealand, that being Mokai, on the west coast of the North Island. He stated that the Maori tradition about it was this - that the first Maoris who visited New Zealand came in a large canoe built of `Ti Mi' wood, and they landed at Mokaia, where there is a good harbour. canoe got broken up, and some of the planks took root and grew.' Paulin WWC, pp. 33-4. [Andersen notes:] The canoe referred to was the `Tainui', and the tree is said to be the karaka or kopi. White Ancient History Maori (1890), vol. 4, p. 38. Ti Mi is probably intended for tainui: this tree is said to have sprung from the skids of the `Tainui' canoe. Cheeseman Manual NZ Flora (1906), p. 100. <i>(1889,Wild West Coast</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

 <i>n.</i> Cordyline banksii [ORIGIN: Maori, aka ti-ngahere, ti ngaherehere]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tingahere, <i>Geolog. Surv. Cordyline stricta. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Tingahere, or `lancewood' (<i>Cordyline stricta</i>), is another species of the same family. It is of very singular appearance, its head resembling a tuft of copper-coloured hair. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/192</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>C[ordyline] Banksii, Hook.f. ... Ti-ngahere. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,706</i>)

>  C. Banksii (Ti-ngahere): A smaller and more graceful species than C. Australis. <i>(Mar 5, 1917,Ohinemuri Gazette,4</i>)

>  Ti ngaherehere, <i>Cordyline banksii</i>. - A much smaller plant than the [ti]. <i>(1874,TrNZI,6/56</i>)

### ti tawhiti
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Titawhiti, <i>Geolog. Surv. Cordyline</i> sp. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  Among the varieties of Cordyline grown by Maoris was one, variously known as ti tawhiti, ti kowhiti and ti para by tribes of the Taranaki, Wanganui and east coast regions respectively... The lack of a documented history, the unusual dwarf suckering habit, and the observation that it had never been recorded to flower suggested to us that Cordyline 'Thomas Kirk' could be the forgotten ti tawhiti... <i>Ibid.</i> 49 The name ti tawhiti is translated as "the ti from a distant place" or alternatively "the ti from Tahiti". <i>(Jan 1992,NZ Gardener,48</i>)

 <i>n.</i> toi, ti kapu, Cordyline indivisa

### ti-bush
 
 <i>n.</i> manuka

>  We walked afterwards around the farm ... saw the young men cutting Ti-bush, and carting it to the sea to be shipped for Auckland. <i>(1886,Oceana,280 (Ch.XVIII)</i>)

### ti-palm
 
 <i>n.</i> ti, cabbage tree <i>Cordyline australis</i> [incl tipalm, also ti palm] (See [also ti](../A#also-ti),[ti-ti palm](../T#ti-ti-palm))

>  <b>SEWELL</b> The Chapman's house is about half a mile from the River bank, with nothing to mark it but a perfect grove of those Ti-palms. <i>(Mar 12, 1856,Journal,210</i>) [NOTES: in McIntyre (1980) Sewell Journal 1953-1856]

>  <b>POLACK</b> The provisions consisted of about three thousand baskets of potatoes, kumeras, ... steamed kernels of the karaka [a's-acute] maori, tarro [a-acute], ...: the baked roots of the Ti palm, &c. graced the festal scene. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/229</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> A long lane was formed on an extensive level plain, fifty feet in width, by two high walls, formed by perhaps upwards of ten thousand baskets of potatoes, kumeras, Indian corn (<i>Holcus indicus</i>), tarro (<i>arum</i>), water melons, turnips preserved, (<i>kou</i>) steamed kernels of the native fruit, (<i>karaka</i>[as-acute]) baked roots of the <i>ti</i> palm tree, (<i>korou</i>) a quantity of dried cod, eels and other fish, also of shell-fish, bundles of fern-root, onions, peaches, grapes, &c. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/91</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> Walked over a beautiful grass plain [south Canterbury] ... after twelve miles covered with the Ti palm; ... <i>(Jan 18, 1844,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 18 Selwyn Journal in 1851 NZ Part III (Church in the Colonies VIII) 14]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... the indigenous flax-plant and ... the Cabbage-tree or Ti-palm. [fn] <i>Dracaena Australis. </i> <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,45</i>)

>  Country undeviating grass and ti-palm. <i>(Mar 4, 1849,Torlesse Papers,64,1958 ed.</i>)

>  The appearance of Ti palms on the Canterbury plains is very remarkable. They rise amidst the short rich grass with their thick stalks and graceful crest; often when one looks up suddenly from the ground, startling with their likeness to men. One of our wags remarked of these plants, that they looked `like men as trees walking'. A less observant youth having lost his way a few nights ago, in the neighbourhood of the capital, was cheered in the loneliness of the prairie, by the appearance of a man approaching. He took off his hat, (not having yet forgotten the courteous address of the old country,) and accosted the stranger, `I beg your pardon, sir, but can you tell me which is the way to Christchurch.' It was a Ti palm. <i>(Apr 12, 1851,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  <b>ALFRED C BARKER</b> Letter about the New Canterbury <i>Cordyline australis Settlement (And) </i> <i>(Jun 18, 1851,Letter</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> The regular line of enclosures ceases, and it is all open wild land, an unbroken flat - dotted about with Ti palms (a tall ungainly tree like plant with a cross head like a ragged Aloe). . <i>(1853,Journal,1/423,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>(Jan 1856,Edinburgh New Philosophical Journal</i>) [NOTES: Tancred Notes on the Natural History of ... Canterbury (And)]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The distinctive marks which characterise it as not English are the occasional Ti palms, which have a very tropical appearance, and the luxuriance [34] of the Phormium tenax. <i>Ibid.</i> ix/132 The cabbage-tree or ti palm is not a true palm, though it looks like one. It has not the least resemblance to a cabbage. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iii/33-34</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Ti-ti palms are dotted here and there. <i>(1866,Station Days in Maoriland,52,1874 ed.</i>)

>  ... the constantly recurring bush fires have cleared off the stately Ti palms (so fragrant in early spring); ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/49</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  The banks [Thames-Piako] are bare with the exception of small patches of kahikatea and ti palm ... <i>(Jun 23, 1890,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/558,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  A small grove of ti-palms or cabbage-tree. <i>(Dec 22, 1892,Otago Witness,7</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <i>Ti-palm</i>; Ti-tree.- Cabbage-tree <i>(q.v.). ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) </i> <i>(Dec 23, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  Cabbage trees ... which in the old days grew singly or in groups on many parts of the [Canterbury] plains, and up to 1500 feet on the hills. Earliest settlers called [them] ti-tree, ti-palm from [their] Maori name. <i>(1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland]

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,166</i>)

>  Alas! He's gone, / Shattered by death's blow / As broken lies my tall ti-palm, / Shattered by the whirlwind / On the cliffside of Opoura. <i>(Aug 13, 1910,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  Then the ti-palm (cabbage tree), which comes into full bearing only at intervals of three or four years, occasionally supplies the bird with an abundant feast. <i>(Jul 2, 1927,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  <b>McIntyre (ed.)</b> The Naya is as good as the laurel and not unlike it. Plenty of Tree ferns and Tipalms. <i>(1980,Journal of Henry Sewell,I/328</i>) [NOTES: from Journal of HS 1853-7]

>  By an antique sundial, so old that though all then known parts of the world were mapped on it, so that the sun at mid-day should tell the time everywhere, Australasia is not marked, stands a New Zealand Ti-palm, the shadows of its leaves now falling aslant that deluded time-piece! <i>(Apr 1, 1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,21</i>)

### ti-root
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McNEISH</b> In the library he found a Kauffman he had lent hung over a notice advising that the smoking of ti-root and other rank mosses was to be encouraged. <i>(1970,Mackenzie,22</i>)

### ti-shrub
 
 <i>n.</i> =ti(tree), cabbage-tree, cabbage : contrast manuka

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> An unmixed growth [462] of stunted manuka is a bad sign, a quantity of ti-shrubs, commonly called the cabbage-tree, a very good one [sc. of the quality of soils]. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,461</i>)

### ti-ti palm
 
 <i>n.</i> ti, cabbage tree [ti-palm, see] (See [also ti](../A#also-ti))

>  <b>DRURY</b> TREES [MET WITH IN PELORUS]. Ti - Ti palm or Ti bush, common everywhere. Supplejacks - Karea of natives, ... <i>(1855,Sailing Alone,67</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> The inner substance of the Ti-ti palm, which is long and fibrous, contains a good deal of sugar, ... <i>(Oct 1, 1863,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,75,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Nothing seems [25] so wonderful to me as the utter treelessness of the vast Canterbury plains; occasionally you pass a few Ti-ti palms (ordinarily called cabbage-trees), or a large prickly bush which goes by the name of `wild Irishman,' but for miles and miles you see nothing but ... undulating downs of yellow tussocks, the tall native grass. <i>Ibid.</i> 52 Ti-ti palms dotted here and there. <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,24,1870 ed.</i>)

### ti-tree
 
 <i>n.</i> ti, cabbage tree [one cit only]

>  [A] coarse sieve, made of the long ...mid-ribs obtained from the linear leaves of the tii-tree (<i>Cordyline australis</i>). <i>(1881,TrNZI,13/26</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> ti-tree oneself, be ti-treed, to be in a shelter (that is, to take to the scrub)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... <i>ti-tree oneself,</i> to take shelter from artillery, the ti-tree being very common in N.Z., where during the Maori War, the natives had an exasperating habit of retreating to `the bush', wooded country; <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>) [NOTES: 1935 Mitchell and Strong in Partridge Slang Today 286]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Ti-tree oneself; be ti-treed.</i> To take shelter from artillery fire: New Zealand soldiers': in G.W. Ex Maori custom of retreating to the bush at time of the Maori War. See preceding entry. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,881</i>)

>  [Slang of the N.Z.E.F.] ... `to be ti-treed' meant to be in shelter. <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) tea-tree or manuka. [titree, ti tree; includes attibutive use and 'in the ti-tree']

>  His Inspectorship noted a ti-tree whiskered individual during his inspection. <i>(Apr 27, 1912,NZ Free Lance,15</i>)

>  The girl did her evening work gathering ti-tree kindling for the morning porridge. <i>(Oct 20, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  <b>IRELAND</b> [A memory] that has never left me is of Mac out on a far hillside of the nextdoor farm, slashing ti-tree. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,107</i>)

>  [Colin Meads] talks about being a 21-year-old "fresh off the ti-tree", being selected for the All Blacks in 1957. <i>(Dec 12, 1999,Sunday Star Times,B7</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> My horse ... pitched heavily ... sending me [5] head first among the manuka or ti-tree scrub ... I had to stop at an `accommodation house' (or way-side inn) to sponge him down. <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,4</i>)

>  ".. We goes round that ti-tree bush on the bend of the road, and back again " <i>(Sep 28, 1901,New Zealand Free Lance,12</i>)

>  The one great difficulty is the drying process. A good deal of latitude is allowed in this respect, and one may choose from the million and one ti-tree bushes on the hillside, or rig a line with the assistance of two upright sticks, in the vicinity of the tent. <i>(Aug 6, 1918,Wanganui Chronicle,3</i>)

>  At the time the family arrived at Auckland the only habitation of any description was a raupo store depot on the beach, situated on the site of the present Shortland Street Post Office, the rest of the district being covered by dense ti-tree bush. <i>(Aug 2, 1924,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  The Sergeant of Police said the prisoner was a perfect nuisance to the inhabitants, and had no home that he was aware of, excepting a shanty stuck up in the ti-tree. <i>(Jun 9, 1866,Wellington Independent,5</i>) [NOTES: Harry's tea-tree 4b, as in 'the sticks']

>  Ti-tree thicket on a hot summer day. <i>(Nov 4, 1935,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Very heavy rain was encountered during the search, which ended with the men being discovered huddled on cement bags under thick ti-tree. <i>(Sep 5, 1944,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  <i>Ti&#769;.-tree&#769;.</i> (t&emacr.&#769;.tr&emacr.), <i>n. (Bot.) (a) </i>The ti or titi. See TI. (b) An erroneous spelling of TEA TREE. See under TEA. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTONE-SMITH</b> Ti-tree was plentiful but tawa had to be snigged home from quite a distance. <i>(1975,Battles, Buggies and Babies,42</i>)

>  PRICE required for Clearing Half-acre Broom and Ti-tree. <i>(Sep 7, 1929,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  FOR SALE, Ti-tree Stakes, Tomato and bean, approx. 8doz. <i>(Sep 21, 1940,Evening Post</i>)

>  The present writer was asked.. to go and see the hot water away in the ti-tree somewhere. <i>(Mar 5, 1904,NZ Observer,4</i>)

>  Auckland people are glad to get stumpy little bagfuls of ti-tree at half a crown a bag. <i>(Aug 23, 1913,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  A considerable number of settlers and others visited the ground on Saturday to see the trial of Mr Henry Reynolds' new Triumph ti-tree cutter. <i>(Jan 24, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  "The Soul of the Ti-Tree", by Edwinia C. Geach. <i>(Feb 10, 1910,Triad, The,XVII(2) ; 35</i>)

>  FIREWOOD. - Cut and Uncut Titree. CHARCOAL. - Best Titree. <i>(May 8, 1897,Observer (Auckland),20</i>)

>  Orders for Matai or Totara posts, Ti-tree Rails, Shingles, and Kaikatea [sic] Building Timber, executed. <i>(Apr 1, 1873,Waikato Times,1</i>)

>  Over an hour was spent by the Fire Brigade this morning in beating out flames which were licking up the grass and titree on the Rhodes estate at Wadestown at a great rate. <i>(Feb 11, 1915,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  It's a titipounamu, and you often find him in the ti-tree scrub... It is the rifleman, very shy, and a friend of the scrub-cutter. <i>(Feb 5, 1995,NZ Tablet,7</i>)

>  Perchance he's raced tho' the ti-tree bush. <i>(Oct 30, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/5 : 109</i>)

>  FIREWOOD.- Cut and Uncut Titree. CHARCOAL.- Best Titree. <i>(Nov 16, 1901,Observer (Auckland),26</i>)

>  Only a small cartel of particularly hardy plants could survive - titree, gorse, umbrella fern and small red carnivorous sundews are major components <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,112</i>)

### tiaki
 
 <i>n.</i> a bird, the saddleback

 <i>n.</i> a tree

 <i>n.</i>

### tiaweke
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The nearest approach they could make to [Wakefield] was Era Weke, and some wag immediately suggested "Tiaweke," the name of a small bird which is very common in the woods, and known for its chattering propensities. <i>(1845,Chambers Edinburgh Journal,4(84) : 85</i>)

### tick tack
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> One common prank was Knick Knock. In this game, children knocked on someone's door, then ran away, but it could be played more elaborately: In Tick Tack we stuck a pin into the middle sash of a window; a button was suspended from this on a piece of cotton. Then a long length of cotton was attached to the smaller piece and led away to the gate. The cotton was pulled every now and then and the button tapped against the window. It was nighttime, and folks would pull up the blind and peer out. Some more adventurous spirits would come out and investigate and would maybe get entangled with the cotton. Then the culprit would run away to some other house. (1900; Moeraki) <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,90</i>)

### tick tick
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>WATT</b> A warning like the English schoolboys' `cave!'. From J. M. Barrie's <i>Peter Pan</i> where Hook was always warned of the approach of the crocodile because of the ticks of the alarm clock it had swallowed. If a master approaches, the boys warn the rest with `tick tick!'. Also used in the expression `to go tick' - that is, to go on watch for masters and prefects while the rest get into mischief. <i>(1951,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1951 Dilworth School (McWatt letter)]

### ticker
 
 <i>n.</i> spirit

>  There was more ticker shown by these Lions than you'd find in a Swiss clock factory and they deserve a healthy reception at their next home game. <i>(Oct 1, 2001,Evening Post,28</i>)

>  <b>MARRINER</b> I can't condone your actions, but I'd be the first to admit that you've got plenty a ticker. <i>(2001,Stonedogs,328</i>)

>  A night to find out just how much ticker this All Black team has, whether the extraordinary commitment shown in Dunedin against England was a oneoff or a sign of things to come. <i>(Jul 18, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B3</i>)

>  They [Warriors] used to be the sort of team that once you put a few points on them you could score 50 or so. But not now. They've found a bit of ticker. <i>(Apr 9, 2001,Evening Post,25</i>)

>  Caroline's day begins by feeling her pulse rate... Georgina usually passes on the ticker test. <i>(Nov 11, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B18</i>)

>  This season is a great opportunity to show people I do have a little bit of ticker. <i>(Nov 28, 2003,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,3</i>)

### tickerless
 
 <i>adj.</i> lacking heart

>  I fear we have now gone from a team full of Deans; gutsy but talentless Cantabs to a bunch of highly skilled, but tickerless Aucklanders. <i>(Dec 21, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B12</i>)

### ticket
 
 <i>n.</i> Union membership card

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> Bluey had lost his seaman's ticket over his jail blues, and I don't suppose he'll ever get it back. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,127</i>)

>  He was told that Campbell and Liggett had said that if he was not going to pay the levy they [the Union] were going to have his `ticket' and they would have him down the road. That meant they were going to deprive him of his right to work. <i>(Dec 7, 1983,Press, The,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a traffic offence notice; a traffic offence summons, espec. in get a ticket (cf. Bluey, sticker) [Also `ticket' used on English TV show Dec 1984 for parking ticket. 30/3/85 1985 <i>People's Court</i> : US he got a ticket [for riding a moped]]

>  His client had sold his motor-cycle two days after the incident which gained him a ticket ... . <i>(Feb 27, 1953,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  [A] number [of motorists] were detected by traffic officers and collected `tickets'. <i>(Sep 1957,Evening Post</i>)

>  Story of cricketer Cawdrey driving up a one-way street while listening to cricket test, being stopped by policeman. `Colin Cawdrey got a ticking off rather than a ticket.' <i>(Nov 30, 1984,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 2YA Wellington Morning Report]

>  Otahuhu District Court thought that the department would sort out the computer snafu fairly quickly, so it took no further steps towards processing Mr lange's speeding ticket, or any of the other snarled-up cases. <i>(Apr 23, 1988,Dominion,2</i>)

>  [Hdg] Unruly drivers clock up one millon tickets. Close to one million traffic tickets were issued to unruly Kiwi drivers last year... "The minister has said in the past if people don't like receiving tickets, then the message is simple: don't speed,"... <i>(Nov 9, 2003,Sunday Star Times,A3</i>)

### ticket boy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Just by the slaughterhouse office was a place called the Ticket Room, and not surprisingly perhaps, the boys who worked there were called Ticket Boys. <i>(Jan 24, 1981,Evening Post,7</i>) [NOTES: [Clem Goddard on the Gear Meat Co.]]

>  <b>COLIN GORDON</b> The carcases are washed and wiped by a <i>wiper</i>, then a <i>stringer</i>, generally a boy, puts strings on the front legs and necks, and they go over to <i>the clock</i> which counts them. The carcases then go to the <i>pusher-up</i> who pushes them along to the scales to be weighed and graded. A <i>stamper</i> stamps the carcases and a <i>ticket-boy </i>puts a grade ticket on them out at the scales after the <i>grader</i> has graded them for weight and size. <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>)

>  The employees in question embrace such departments of the industry as:- Floormen, boners, cooling chamber hands, general labourers, meat classers, weigh clerks, temporary clerks, trimmers, ticket boys, gut men and gut runners, pressers, vat hands, dryers, crushers, and baggers, etc. <i>(Feb 9, 1912,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Past the meat-inspector and ticket boy / In a line that never fails / Goes your dear wee lamb to the cooling room/ From the grader and his scales./ <i>(1967,Farm & Station Verse,188</i>) [NOTES: From Stronach 1949]

 <i>n.</i>

### tiddling
 
 <i>adj.</i> G.J.Griffiths notes: trivial, ineffectual; probably a manufactured word, and possibly personal to the columnist, manufactured from `piddling' and `tiddler' -

>  When it is noticed how thousands of dollars of Golden Kiwi funds are so often frittered away in tiddling grants to all and sundry for every minor parish project one can imagine, I cannot help feeling that someone up there in the seats of the mighty in Wellington needs a decent shake-up. <i>(Sep 18, 1971,Evening Star,8</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

### tide
 
 <i>n.</i> often used in NZ colloquial (espec. in children's) for `the sea' as in English dialect `without reference to ebb or flow', espec. in localities where low tide leaves mudflats, sandbanks etc. exposed [ca1935 Havelock Where the same mudflat tidal conditions applied.]

>  <b>MICALFE</b> In Whangaroa, Maunganui, in Northland, one always speaks of going for a swim in the tide. These places are silted up with mudflats: one cannot swim etc. when the tide is out, so `tide' and `sea' are identified. <i>(1951,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... and a great Christmas tree in flower reached out far enough to colour the tide. <i>(1977,Never Enough!,11</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [rip](../R#rip))

 <i>n.</i> a ship waiting for the tide

The tide-waiter had come off, and the captain, afraid of his men running away, had put this functionary on guard; he was marching up and down the quarter-deck with the captain's double-barrelled gun on his shoulder, which he ... was swearing to shoot, ... and without the slightest compunction, [15] any man trying to leave the ship. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,14</i>)

G.R.Gray, ... </i>Tieke of the natives of Tasman Bay. <i>Quoy and Gaim</i> Tiaka or Purourou of the natives. <i>Yate. </i> Tira-oua-ke [e-acute] of the natives. <i>Less. Tierawaki</i>, Cook's Straits. <i>Dieffenbach</i>. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/192</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/192]

>  Creadion carunculatus. Tieke. Saddle-back. Not uncommon. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 45. CREADION CARUNCULATUS. Gml. Saddle-back. Tieke. <i>C. Cinereus</i>. BULLER. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,17</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> CREADION CARUNCULATUS. (THE SADDLE-BACK.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Tieke, Tiraweke, \{Tirauweke,\} and Purourou. [151] It is naturally a noisy bird, and when excited or alarmed becomes very clamorous, hurrying through the woods with cries of `tiaki-rere', quickly repeated. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,150, 151</i>) [NOTES: {1888 2nd edn 1/18}]

>  42. <i>Creadion carunculatus</i>, Gml. Saddle-back, Jackbird, Tieke. <i>(Mar 1885,NZ Journal of Science,377</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <i>Creadion carunculatus.</i> - Tieke, Saddle-back. Both Islands. Flight feeble. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/112</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> CREADION CINEREUS. (JACK-BIRD.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Tieke. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/19</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ... to say nothing of Korimako and Pitoitoi ..., we never saw or heard the notes of either the Piopio, the Tieke, or the Hihi, all of which birds were at one time more numerous even than the Huia. The <i>Zosterops</i> was everywhere abundant, also the Grey Warbler and Rifleman, and ... we found the Tomtit comparatively plentiful; the Parakeet chased its mate through the tree-tops with sharp cries of `twenty-eight'; ... and among the tangle of the underwood the ever-present Flycatcher displayed its pretty fan-like tail. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/14</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tieke, n </i>Same as <i>Tiaki</i> (q.v.). <i>(1889,Austral English,469</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> I shot a tieke, and was pleased to notice that this starling had increased in numbers since my earlier visit in 1880. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,107,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <i>tieke</i>, a bird (Creadion carunculatus), saddleback, now a rare bird: its saddle is the mark of the hot hand of Maui. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>saddleback</i> forest bird. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> SADDLEBACK <i> Philesturnus carunculatus Maori name;<i> Tieke </i> <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,233</i>)

>  277. <i>a. Philesturnus carunculatus rufusater</i> (Lesson, 1828) <i>North Island Saddleback [(Tieke)] </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,72,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Saddleback Philesturnus carunculatus</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... <i>North Island Saddleback P.c. rufusater</i> (LESSON, 1828). <i>South Island Saddleback P.c. carunculatus </i>(GMELIN, 1789). ... OTHER NAME: <i>Tieke. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,302</i>)

### tier
 
 <i>n.</i> ? a coating of clay or earth on a ? sod or wattle and daub house; ? or tie-beam

>  Fine weather, Chapman shepherding Wedders, put tier on house &c [24] no tier got on house clay wet [Mar 31] put last tier and wall plates on house. [Apr 2] clayed walls of house put on tie beams [3] finished claying & put on ridge pole & rafters of house. [Apr 4] nailed wattles on roof [Apr 5] commenced thatching house. [Apr 12] put in clay in eaves and put tier on gable and chimney, [14] put tier on gable and chimney & tier of small stove, <i>(Mar 19, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: [Hocken TS]]

### tier-pole
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rack of parallel bars upon which curing-sticks rest in the curing of tobacco.

>  They [curing-sticks] usually carry from eight to ten plants, and are hung in the shed by resting the ends of the sticks on tier-poles. These are strong squared timber, 2in by 4in., lying horizontally, and held in position by bolting them to uprights .. If the distance between curing-sticks on the tier-poles is reckoned at 8 in., it is now possible to work out the accommodation required for curing the crop. <i>(Nov 21, 1927,NZ Journal of Agriculture,340</i>)

>  The stick is placed on tier poles, which run like parallel bars in the curing-shed. <i>(Jan 21, 1929,NZ Journal of Agriculture,59</i>)

### tierawaki
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tiraueke](../T#tiraueke))

### tiffin
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tiffin</i> midmorning cuppa and snack, late C19; from Anglo-Indian word for a light lunch. <i>[Partridge] </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,113</i>)

### tig
 
 <i>n.</i> a fit of temper. a tantrum

>  <b>MARSH</b> [girl speaks] I can't see why you're getting into such a tig over it. <i>(1943,Colour Scheme,217</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the children's game of <i>tag</i>. (from TIG v) [[see Sutton-Smith]]

>  <b>4</b> Steeple-chasing, cricket, ... were varied by a game known as `tig', or `twos and threes', rounders, &c., in some of which the ladies assisted. <i>(Nov 7, 1868,Marlborough Express</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to touch, to tag (espec. in the game of TIG or tag.) [ca1935 Havelock children e.g. Tigged you last. cf. also <i>Tiggy-tiggy-touchwood,</i> a children's game.]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> (occas.) to filch [ca1935 e.g. He tigged an apple from my lunch-tin.]

### tiger
 
 <i>n.</i> an energetic person, a `tiger for work'; a fast, vigorous tramper

>  With the following dawn two tigers stood by for a quick trip back to Maruia Springs while each pack was [40] searched and emptied. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,39</i>)

>  `What about Screamin George? Real bloody tiger. Make two of Donovan.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,205</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Doesn't know if he's punched, bored, or countersunk - A tiger for punishment - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,162</i>)

>  <i>tiger</i>: A shearer who works very hard. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/28</i>) [NOTES: Wallace Shearing Glossary]

>  Aycliffe made his third start on the day and ran well into fourth place - a tiger for work. <i>(Nov 4, 1922,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  A veritable tiger for work was Rufus Kiser. <i>(Jan 13, 1931,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  This man is, I learn, a perfect tiger at work, most strong, most reliable, and no doubt, when times improve, will rapidly grow independent, after all realising the dreams which gladdened the sweet hours of his voyage to these shores. <i>(Nov 6, 1885,NZ Tablet,19</i>)

>  Premier Seddon is a tiger for work. <i>(Jan 23, 1897,Observer (Auckland),4</i>)

>  - That F.G. Bolton's enthusiasm to have the Hutt Park vested in the Wellington City is approved by all the athletic bodies. And isn't F.G.B. a tiger for work? <i>(May 16, 1908,New Zealand Free Lance,22</i>)

>  The men have enlisted to fight and are tigers for work. <i>(Nov 7, 1914,Evening Post,5</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a shark

>  <b>RUHEN</b> tiger shark He was so old a tiger that his stripes had faded, and his colour was a even as a porbeagle's. <i>(1964,Lively Ghosts,38</i>)

### tiger country
 
 <i>n.</i> [figurative, far off the local area, esp in sport]

>  <b>DAVIES</b> Come down to Wellington if you feel that your batteries need to be recharged. I think it's going to be tiger country up there for a permanent union official, but we trust you to do your best. <i>(1984,Bread and Roses,177</i>)

>  Duncan's was the longer ball, but it kicked off the fairway into the "tiger country" to the left. <i>(Nov 11, 1932,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  Goss, after a pulled drive, hooked his second at "Alma" away into the tiger country. <i>(Oct 6, 1923,NZ Truth,9</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) wild, isolated and steep country [usually scrub-covered and difficult to access and traverse.]

>  At the moment the Department [of Lands] is confined to buying areas that can be divided into multiple units. This restricts them to the real 'tiger' country. <i>(Dec 10, 1964,NZ Dairy Exporter,13</i>)

>  Much of the land is 'tiger' country, in gullies and gulches, wide riverbeds and faces leading sharply to broken tops. <i>(May 11, 1968,Napier Daily Telegraph,5</i>)

>  It's tiger country all right. David has 15,380 acres in a longish block without even a track to his far boundary. <i>(Jul 8, 1971,NZ Farmer,32</i>)

>  The Thomas family had farmed happily and well enough on their 760 ha. home block of medium to tiger country for the first 20 years or so. <i>(May 13, 1982,NZ Farmer,14</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> This was the type of steep, grey-walled stuff that on the West Coast they refer to with some pride as 'tiger country.' <i>(1991,Fall Muster,22</i>)

>  Rightly or wrongly, some would call the 520 ha Okoki Valley property farmed by Graeme and Marise Northcott 'Tiger Country.' [big tiger country ] <i>(Nov 18, 1999,NZ Farmer,19</i>)

>  <b>LETHBRIDGE</b> It had been pretty hard `tiger' country in the first place, but when the land had been cleared ... the subsequent pasture was good enough ... to run a dairy herd. <i>(1971,Sunrise on the Hills,126</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> This road . . . winds its way through the harsh `tiger' country which typifies much of that [Marlborough Sounds] locality. <i>(1973,Big Country,32,1977 ed.</i>)

>  Surveyors and geologists use the term `tiger country' ...for the areas of low manuka scrub mixed with second growth native trees in the Rotorua-Taupo district (replacing forest destroyed by erruptions). Geologists say it is country only a tiger could get through, but I have suggested it may derive from the Siberian <i>`taiga'. </i> <i>(1980,NZ Geochemical Group Newsletter,No. 57/12</i>)

>  They come from a mostly fatherless family settling on the Pori Hill's legendary `tiger country', <i>(May 9, 1987,Dominion,11</i>)

### tiger lily
 
 <i>n.</i> (introduced)

>  <i> lily, tiger [s] Lilium tigrinum </i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 79 <i> tiger lily [s] Lillium tigrinum </i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,44</i>)

### tiger shark
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka tiger-shark]

>  A fisherman at Paraparaumu made a haul of twenty-five sharks on one line which had 450 hooks baited for schnapper. None were [sic] less than six feet, and one was a tiger shark nearly 18 feet long which bit a piece out of the boat and made a great fight before it was killed. <i>(Dec 16, 1897,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  While fishing for crayfish at Castlepoint the other day, Mr Southey hauled up a large tiger shark in a crayfish net. <i>(Aug 9, 1900,Otago Witness,44</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... an annual fishery for the tiger-shark is carried on by the natives in Hauraki Gulf, at a certain season of the year. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/295</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The tooth of the tiger-shark, drilled, and the end covered with red wax, is also an esteemed ornament as an ear-drop. <i>(1866,Polynesia,157</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 124. LAMNA GLAUCA. Mull. and Henle. Tiger Shark. Mako. <i>(1872,Fishes,77</i>)

>  <i>Lamna glauca .. Tiger shark. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 117 to 119 TIGER SHARK (<i>Lamna glauca</i>). ... In New South Wales it is called the blue pointer, and, as Mr. Hill tells us, is `not only a very powerful, but perhaps the most bold of any of the large sharks visiting our shores. ... <i>Ibid.</i>118 Although there is some doubt on the subject, <i>Lamna glauca</i> is considered to be the mako of the Maoris, which is mainly found in the neighbourhood of Mayor Island. ... Mango, it should be remembered, seems [119] almost a generic name for shark in the North, as there is the mango-taniwha, a large variety; the mango-pare, the hammerheaded shark; the mango-reremai, the common dog-fish; the mango-pekepeke, and the mango-aruroa. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,115</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tiger S.</i>- Scymnus sp. (<i>Maori name, </i>Mako). <i>(1898,Austral English,412</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Ngutukao, ... lamna cornubica, tiger-shark.</i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,275</i>)

>  <i>Galeocerdo arcticus</i>, Mull & Henle. Tiger Shark <i>(1927,TrNZI,59/Plate 9</i>) [NOTES: (Griffin)]

>  <i>Galeocerda articus:</i> Tiger shark, shovel nose. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,71</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> Toiki (tiger-shark) <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,49</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Mako or Mango - the tiger-shark. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,61</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Tiger Shark <i>Galeocerdo cuvier</i> (Lesueur) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,397</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 397]

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> TIGER SHARK ... <i>Other names: Galeocerdo cuvieri</i>; leopard shark, toiki (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,179</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Tiger shark .. .. toiki .. .. <i>Galeocerdo cuvier </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Tiger shark Galeocerdo cuvier</i> A large shark of all tropical and some warm temperate oceans and coastal waters ... The common name refers to the striped pattern, but this is faint in adults... <i>(1966,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,26</i>)

>  [species name] tiger shark .. [scientific name] <i>Galeocerdo cuvieri .</i>. [other name(s)] <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] shark, tiger 154-6, 245-9, 262 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  An immense tiger shark 15 feet long was captured a few days ago at the same place; the girth of its carcase was as great as that of a horse. <i>(Jun 20, 1872,Wellington Independent,2</i>)

### tiger shell
 
 <i>n.</i> a marine mollusc

>  <b>POWELL</b> TIGER SHELL (<i>Maurea tigris</i>). Grows to 3 inches in diameter and is at once recognised by its delicately tapered spire and conspicuous pattern of zig-zag reddish-brown radiating bands. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,25</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Maurea. Maurea tigris,</i> a univalve mollusc, the tiger shell. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,197</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Tiger shell .. .. matangongore, maurea .. .. <i>Maurea tigris </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  Pink Cameo and Tiger Shell and Enamels set in finest gold. <i>(Jul 11, 1882,Star, The,2</i>)

>  Numerous tiger shells, one of New Zealand's prettiest, nestle between the sponges and anemones. <i>(Nov 1, 2003,Dominion Post,E9</i>)

### tiger weed
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Bidens tripartitus</i> [ca1953 R. Mason citing H.H. Allan: local to Foxton area]

>  <i>tiger weed [=] beggars' ticks: cobblers' pegs</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,79</i>)

### tiger-skin
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He don't like to see others toiling,/ And he proves he's a `tiger-skin',/ When he drinks with a bloke and chats him/ `Why don't you join up and dig in?' <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,69</i>)

### Tigers
 
 <i>n.</i> Christchurch gang name

>  There used to be much more of the push element around Christchurch than there is now, and it frequently occurred that pushes [ie youths belonging to these groups] like the `Tigers,' the `Diehards,' the `Scalpers,' and the [rest of citation???] <i>(Feb 3, 1912,NZ Truth,345/7/3</i>)
