 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>(Dec 1958,NZ Timber Journal,73</i>)

### tigger
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KEENE</b> Because most of us were too small to be of any use in team games, we played hide-and-seek, `tigger', marbles or whipped tops. <i>(1982,Myrtle and Sophia,6</i>) [NOTES: [c1877]]

### tiggy
 
 <i>n.</i> a game out of school [1984 16 F E5 Pakuranga Coll. 21 TIGGIE -->1953 14 M A5 Thames DHS 21 TIGGY [2] -->1984 16 F E7 Pakuranga Coll. 21 TIGGY]

>  In the afternoon we were playing "tiggy" around the flower beds in front of the house, when a naughty bee from the hives came and sat on my head, and it did not forget to sting me before it went away. <i>(Oct 12, 1904,Otago Witness,74</i>)

### tiggy touchwood
 
 <i>n.</i> [see OED, English use from 1845; aka tiggy tiggy touchwood, tiggy-tiggy touchwood]

>  <b>CONDON</b> Other games played by the whole school from time to time included rounders, bedlam, tick-tack, tiggy tiggy touchwood, hide and seek, hares and hounds and cock fights - a form of piggy back <i>(1992,Hurleyville,25</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> Some of the simple games played by the [West Coast] children were rounders, shinty, duckstone, tiggy touchwood, kiss in the ring, ... cockfighting, marbles, fly the garter and leapfrog. <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,38</i>) [NOTES: [c1870s] 1953 Patrick Magee (aged 86) Growing Up at the Diggings in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 38]

>  Here they, to the number of 250, were soon engaged in sport, and the games of "I-spy eye" and "tiggy touchwood" were indulged in with the gusto only to be felt in the early part of one's teens. <i>(May 5, 1866,Wellington Independent,5</i>)

>  BOARD AND MINISTER PLAY AT TIGGY-TIGGY TOUCHWOOD. <i>(Dec 30, 1926,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  I was at a Sunday night concert given by the Derwent Infantry Band recently, and one of the items was Mozart's "Gloria," rendered in what I think fine style, and as the last note died away there was but one solitary attempt at applause, and that was drowned by the shouts of the "dear" children (who are always prominent at our open-air concerts nowadays), playing "Tiggy touchwood," or some other Sabbath pastime, which must be in great favour with the police, for they never seem to interfere, although to the person who goes to hear the music such amusements are rather annoying. <i>(Dec 11, 1907,Otago Witness,74</i>)

### tight
 
 <i>n.</i> ?two-up: prob. merely a play on having little or no money -

>  There is a `tight' in the affairs of man [sic], which taken at the floods leads on to the `rising' - if you haven't 5s. to ante-up. <i>(Jan 18, 1908,NZ Truth,135/1/3</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> of country, difficult to work [1898 [see index and discards]]



 
 <i>adj.</i> close

>  <b>GOLDSMITH</b> At this time, when the Government needed to keep things 'tight' - to use the dominant rugby metaphor of the Bolger administration - Banks threw a series of wild passes. <i>(1997,John Banks : A Biography,194</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of pasture feed] in short supply.

>  .. the other 35-40 cows reared Holstein-Friesian bull calves while feed was tight in spring ... <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,84</i>)

>  Agribusiness consultant Ivan Lines said while feed was a bit tight, most dairy framers would be in a much better situation compared with this time last year. <i>(Aug 6, 2002,Southland Times,3</i>)

### tight as...
 
 <i>phr.</i> very mean (espec. with money), often further elaborated by adding `and that's watertight'; very drunk; (in various expressions of comparison with TIGHT in the senses: (1) close-fisted (P: mostly US: 1828; so WF); and (2) drunk (where NZ English would perhaps prefer FULL AS)) [ca1948 Wellington (Douglas Gray) cf. P: tight as a drum, tick, etc., which are also frequent in NZ.]

>  <b>HILL;IARD</b> Yet in spite of it all he was as tight as a bull's arse going uphill in the fly season. <i>(1969,Green River,38</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tight as a duck's arse</i>, also <i>bull's/gnat's/fish's</i> mean with money; often carrying the response: <i>and that's water tight</i>. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,113</i>)

### tight bush
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bush forest close with undergrowth and heavily wooded.

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> In 'tight' bush, Richard reckoned the calibre and ultra-short barrel was an ideal combination. <i>(1996,Great Hunting Yarns,185</i>)

### tight chains
 
 <i>phr.</i> horses working

>  As a teamster, Pop was required (when working the team) to have 'tight chains' from 8am until 5.30pm. That means that the harness chains had to be tight (horses working), and prior to 8am the horses had to be caught, fed, prepared - and after 5.30pm the opposite applied, so I am not sure just how long his day was. <i>(Feb 2004,NZ Memories,No. 46/41</i>)

### tight five
 
 <i>n.</i> the nucleus of a rugby union scrum, the three front-row forwards and two locks.

>  New Zealand's tight five forwards have been given the big ask by coach Laurie Mains in the lead-up to Saturday's thirds rugby test. <i>(Jul 1, 1993,Evening Post,32</i>)

>  We've put a bit of work into getting the tight five together. <i>(Jul 1, 1993,Evening Post,32</i>)

>  The tight five! As far as I was concerned they were the loose five. <i>(Sep 5, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

>  Everything clicked, especially in the tight five where we set up the platform. <i>(Jun 8, 1992,Dominion,13</i>)

>  In the Wairarapa we have our 'tight five'. Our caterer goes everywhere we go. <i>(Feb 14, 2001,Capital Times,14</i>)

>  The scrum was stronger than the World Cuppers' and should stay intact in the tight five. <i>(Jul 6, 1992,Evening Post,28</i>)

>  He was very happy with the tight five. <i>(Jul 6, 1992,2ZB,Morning Talkback</i>)

>  The All Black tight five was the guilty party. <i>(Jul 23, 1992,Evening Post,27</i>)

>  Our tight five laid the platform and that made it easier for the rest of us. <i>(Aug 6, 1992,Dominion,36</i>)

>  <b>Knight</b> The most obvious of these was the need to bolster the forwards' tight five. <i>(1991,They Led the All Blacks,130</i>)

>  Simply, the tight five, who had slept through the Toulouse test, awoke and that fuelled the loose trio and gave the backs the forward velocity. <i>(Nov 20, 1995,Evening Post,26</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (transferred use, political context)

>  That assurance does not preclude a breakway from NZ First by Mr Henare and the other members of what he yesterday called the "tight five" - the five Maori electorate MPs. <i>(Jul 15, 1998,Dominion,1</i>)

>  He no doubt realised that splitting from the party which took his so-called "tight five" into Parliament would be counterproductive, for them and for Maori. <i>(Jul 15, 1998,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  Mr Kirton said criticism of Mr Peters from the so-called "tight five" of Maori MPs - Mr Henare, Tutekawa Wyllie, Tukoroirangi Morgan, Rana Waitai and Tuariki Delamere - was understandable, but he doubted they would form a breakaway Maori party - at least not yet.<i>Ibid.</i> He disagreed with Mr Henare's notion of the Maori MPs being a "tight five", because although there were strong areas of common interest, there were areas where the MPs disagreed. <i>(Jul 16, 1998,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  The reluctance of the "tight five" to leave a mainstream party is much to be applauded.<i>Ibid.</i> The tight five have something going for them in the Maori constituencies. <i>(Jul 16, 1998,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Mr Delamere was responding to New Zealand First sources who questioned his position in the so called "tight five" of Maori MPs, seen to be backing the deposed Mr Henare. <i>(Jul 18, 1998,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Each of the infamous "tight five" - Maori Affairs Minister Tau Henare, Tukuroirangi Morgan, Tu Wyllie, Rana Waitai and Tuariki John Delamere - was last night despatched to political oblivion. <i>(Nov 28, 1999,Sunday Star Times,A3</i>)

>  That group included a noisy and ambitious group of Maori MPs, labelled "the tight five" and known by their trademark Dirty Dog sunglasses, any one of which probably fancied himself the next leader. <i>(May 21, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Maori voters were not impressed by the antics of the "tight five", especially the suglasses-flashing Mr Henare, who returned to Parliament as a National list MP on Saturday, and Tukuroirangi Morgan, notorious for his expensive wardrobe. <i>(Sep 19, 2005,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

>  To win Tauranga, [Peters] needed to shed the Maori "tight five", and take his core troops.. into opposition. <i>(Aug 29, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  This year, more than a tight five of New Zealand-born artists are continuing to exceed our national rugby team's performance around the world. <i>(Sep 26, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  The audit was instigated after Mr Tamihere sided with the "tight five" of disaffected members against the rest of the board... <i>(Nov 2, 2004,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

### tight stocking
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [pasture management] running of heavy numbers of stock in one area.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A firm believer in tight stocking, he has the welfare of his country at heart. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,53</i>)

### tight-arse
 
 <i>adj.</i> uptight

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> ... Skull worked with the Hories / who were O.K. so long as their tight-arse worries / over land rights and such didn't throw them in flurries / <i>(1982,Rose Ballroom, The,28</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a close-fisted, stingy person; a tight-wad also aj <i> tight-arsed </i> [ca1941 St Patrick's College, Silverstream ca1946 Victoria University]

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> `You know how they choose acountants,' I said. `The tightest arses in the firm,' said Sully. <i>(1984,Day Hemingway Died, The,143</i>) [NOTES: (Sully's Country)]

### tighten up
 
 <i>v.</i> [of pastoralism] to restrict feed to stock

>  We never tighten up the hoggets and they get as much feed as we can give them ... <i>(Oct 2004,Country-Wide,26/3,10 ed.</i>)

### tightie
 
 <i>n.</i> a rugby tight forward

>  The tighties are replaced by the loose forwards while other players arrive for the 11am practice and take turns in physio Glen Muirhead's adjoining room. <i>(Mar 9, 2001,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  And when the ABs have the pill, some hard yards up fat man's track must be gained at scrum time the acid must go on a front row put together by band aids, and the tighties must do their job to clear out and retain the breakdown ball. <i>(Jul 30, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B3</i>)

### tihe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> POGNORNIS CINCTA (STITCH-BIRD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Ihi [omitted 1888], Hihi, Tihe, Kotihe, Kotihewera, \{Tiora, and Tiheora\}; male and female are someties distinguished as Hihi-paka and Hihi-matakiore or Tihe-kiore. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,98,{1888 2nd edn 1/101} ed.</i>)

### tihore
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a variety of farmed native flax, Phormium tenax. [ORIGIN: Maori, aka tihori]

>  The native gave pride of place to 'Tihore', a strong grower, running up to twelve feet in height and liking a moist soil. <i>(Aug 22, 1903,Farmers' Union Advocate,1</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> `The species of <i>Phormium tenax</i> thus cultivated is the tihore, literally the `skinning' flax. This name describes the ease with which it submits to the scraping process.' <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,2/286</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> Purchas and Minnis [????] of Auckland [use the fibre] of that variety or species [of flax] known to the natives [79] as `Tihori,' or `Tihore,' - all other forms yield a coarser fibre. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,78</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maori name for a species of New Zealand flax. Name used specially in the North Island for the best variety of Phormium</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,470</i>)

>  <i>tihore n.</i> New Zealand flax. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12</i>)

>  But above all, samples of flax, the peculiar produce of the colony, - which we yet hope will shortly become one of its staple exports, as soon as the difficulties of preparing it in a cheap and expeditious manner are surmounted, - should be sent carefully prepared, particularly samples of the Tihori, and best kinds of flax. <i>(Jun 22, 1850,New Zealander, The,3</i>) [NOTES: Reprinted from the Wellington Spectator 4 May]

>  With the best kinds (tihore), they simply tore out the fibre, rubbed it together in their hands to open the bundles, and removed the small quantity of tissue that remained by scraping it with their nails. <i>(Dec 2, 1889,Hawke's Bay Herald,6</i>)

>  The noble bush that once yielded tawhari, tihori, kahikatia berries, nikau and fern root, is going down to be replaced with smiling paddocks of English grasses. <i>(May 27, 1919,Thames Star,3</i>)

>  CORRESPONDENCE. NEW ZEALAND FLAX. .. First and foremost is the <i>Tihori.</i> This is the stoutest and best kind. It is known from the others by the extra substance of its leaf, and by a red tinge on the edges. This is the sort the natives select for cultivation, as it is the easiest to clean. <i>(Sep 14, 1844,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,109</i>)

>  The Secretary stated that the subject had engaged his attention, and, during a recent visit to the Waikato, he had procured plants of the true Tihore, the Wairarekei, and other forms, which would be planted in the garden and labelled, as suggested. <i>(May 4, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

### tika
 
 <i>n.</i> right, correct [`among the neighbours was one that a Maori would have classed as not tika'. [Maori]]

>  From lookouts on the hills the ti trees sign / that everything is tika, everything couth; / that the circus is coming, that it will soon be gone. <i>(Mar 16, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|ika, <i>a.</i> Straight, even. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,213</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Tika - just, straight, even <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/387</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> He replied they had thought over my words and yielded to them because they were tika (straight). <i>(Jun 12, 1846,Journal,4/40</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> Only get him to assent that your proposition is `tika' or straight, and you will soon obtain his consent to it. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,135</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> The whole proceeding was objectionable in every respect, and <i>tika</i> (correct). <i>Ibid.</i> 178.. Maoris seldom act against the law, and always try to be able to say what they do is `correct' -<i> (tika). </i> <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,4/73</i>)

>  [On the attack on the Ngati Paoa at Whakatiwai.] The object of this attack was to balance an `utu' account. ... [sic] Ngati Paoa say this account had been previously squared, and the killings which happened at this attack were murders. The other side deny this and say that the balance was against them previously, but the deaths of the three men who fell wound up the account and the whole proceeding was `tika'(correct). <i>(1966,Journal of Ensign Best,421</i>) [NOTES: 1879 J.D. Fenton Important Judgments Delivered in the Compensation Court and Native Land Court, pp. 75-80 in 1966 Best Journal (ed. Taylor) 421]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> A High Chief's conduct had to be `straight' <i>(tika)</i> and justified by their ideas as to the behaviour of a great noble; ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,33</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> A High Chief's conduct had to be `straight' <i>(tika)</i> and justified by their ideas as to the behaviour of a great noble; ... <i>(1906,Old New Zealand,xi</i>) [NOTES: 1906 Hocken Biographical Sketch to ONZ xi]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> ... the writer can see that he himself was in part responsible for what had occurred, that his conduct had not been <i>tika</i>, not been correct. <i>(1926,Tutira,388</i>) [NOTES: see also p. 53]

>  <b>COMBS</b> It was on the other hand `tika'. ...[fn] Maori for `correct'. <i>(1939,Harrowed Toad</i>) [NOTES: [ref. to Auckland country schoolchildren]]

>  <b>LYON</b> Notable among the neighbours was one that even a Maori would have classed as not tika or correct. <i>(1952,Faring South,118</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> All that he did was <i>tika</i>, strictly correct,/ and would have raised grim smiles/ of approbation/ <i>(1963,Sanctuary of Spirits</i>) [NOTES: `Tamihana Te Rauparaha']

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> When your cousin dies you do not continue at your workbench, you attend the funeral rites: that is tika, that is the proper use of time, ... <i>(1969,Green River,32</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Tika, she had said. That was their [sc/ Maoris'] word to justify doing what you like. Behaving according to character, she had said. Doing what you feel the urge to do. If that's the way you are, you do it. <i>(1978,Glory and the Dream, The,191</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> 151. Tika = right, appropriate, correct <i>(1983,Bone People, The,466</i>)

>  "It's a tika [authentic] to be a two ticker", is the Maori Party's mantra. <i>(May 8, 2005,Sunday Star Times,A10</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> It's the right size, tika size, fitting his hand. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,151</i>)

>  The natives shouted a lively chorus, interspersed with cries of "tena! tena!" or "pull away," from the steersmen, and of "ki a tika," or "keep her straight," from the others. <i>(May 16, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

### tikanga
 
 <i>n.</i> (Maori) culture [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [tikanga Maori](../T#tikanga-maori))

>  They're people who have come from a very strong tikanga basis, they have been engaged with Maori people in quite a significantly different way. <i>(Jul 23, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C14</i>)

>  It is debatable whether the amount he waggles his tongue and wiggles is hips is strictly within the bounds of Maori tikanga. <i>(Oct 17, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  I don't have a tikanga Nazi attitude. <i>(Dec 24, 2000,Sunday Star Times,D6</i>)

>  Do you know anything about tikanga? Yes, I do. You don't wear it around your neck. <i>(Jul 2004,TV2</i>) [NOTES: ? Shortland Street; alternatively Facelift on TV1]

>  The greatest reception the Governor has had from natives, the most loyal reverence for the Queen's name and `mana', the most intelligent acquaintance with the English tikanga [fn. Policy.], ... are to be found in the Ngapuhi ... <i>(Mar 21, 1858,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/367,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (F.D. Bell)]

>  <b>HOWE</b> . This term refers to the customs, rules, habits and accepted ways of behaving in each tribal area, and as such may differ from marae to marae. Even within marae. one cannot know all the `tikianga' of that tribe, since aspects of life may only be known by specialists. To find out the tikianga of each marae, one should ask connected tangata whenua or knowledgeable people of the right way to enter and speak and act on that marae. To break `nga tikanga' is to invite (polite) scorn and this means a lowering of `mana'. Tikanga - see `kawa'. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,23</i>)

>  John Clarke is a highly respected professional who enjoys wide support from the Maori community and who has a great depth of knowledge of tikanga (things) Maori. <i>(Dec 24, 1988,Dominion,7</i>)

>  In their rough and ready way of reasoning, the two judgments were paradoxical; no amount of logic could reconcile the two contradictory tikanga. <i>(Feb 2, 1877,Star, The,3</i>)

>  Postgraduate qualifications are essential, and knowledge of te reo Maori me ona tikanga are preferred. <i>(Nov 19, 2005,Dominion Post,H20</i>)

>  <B>KAITA RONGORUA</B> Personal Assistant .. Key qualities for the position are: *Knowledge of teo reo Maori me ona tikanga ... <i>(Nov 11, 2006,Dominion Post,H10</i>)

>  They hadn't had much tikanga and te reo passed on to them, but these sorts of rituals were the most important for them to learn... <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,85</i>)

>  ...tikanga is not tapu. Tikanga is actually the customary usage or behaviours of Maori people, which, if they are to be respected, must be widely known and acknowledged. <i>(Nov 13, 2002,Dominion Post,B9</i>)

>  But a short time ago, a serious altercation arose between the Natives of Orangituapeka and Waimate, three days to the southward of this place: the inhabitants of the former Pa declaring, that they had adopted the bishop's <i>tikanga,</i> and refusing to allow their Missionary, the Rev. J. Skevington (who resides within two miles of the place) to visit them; at the same time using very contemptuous language towards the Natives of Waimate. <i>(Jun 22, 1844,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Basic, simple things - swearing, attitude, personal respect, respect for our audience, respect for our listeners. These are tikanga issues. <i>(May 25, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  Potatau sent a letter to the various tribes this morning, saying that he had not sanctioned the "tikanga reti," viz., - leasing runs to Europeans. <i>(Jun 5, 1860,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

### tikanga Maori
 
 <i>phr.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The problem was, suggested Te Paa, that most of us are absolutely unwilling to challenge what we now glibly define - in the case of the 'parliamentary worriors', it has been defined for us - as 'tikanga Maori' (Maori agenda). <i>(Mar 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  He ... had a `strong loyalty to tikanga maori' (Maori custom) and was more willing to accept whatever outcome a marae sitting might come up with. <i>(May 6, 1989,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Mr Peters was not steeped in tikanga Maori but had taken with him a group of Maori who were, knowing that "cultural facets" would emerge on the trip. <i>(Jun 12, 2006,Dominion Post,A1?</i>)

>  You will need to learn more about tikanga Maori before you criticise what you do not understand. <i>(Oct 31, 2005,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  The perpetrators against whom he advises action are ever seeking the lost fount of "Whakairo" from which the "Tupuna" drew their knowledge, and if "Te Ramanui" has "Tikanga Maori" at heart and knows how to correct the gross misconceptions of would-be Maori carvers and designers, then let him come out on to the "Marae" and let his "great light" ("Te Remanui") so shine before all men that we may see his good works in the making and so profit thereby. <i>(Sep 21, 1933,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  Giving Wellington's cafe patrons a taste of tikanga Maori is among the goals of one of the Capital's newest coffee houses. <i>(May 25, 2000,Contact (Wgtn),1</i>)

>  Maori and Management along with the Maori and Administration programmes are designed to equip students with bi-lingual and bi-cultural management and administration skills for both tikanga Maori and tikanga Pakeha organisations. <i>(Mar 15, 2006,Wairarapa News,13</i>)

>  (4) Te Ture Whenua Maori Act / The Maori Land Act 1993 "Tikanga Maori" means Maori customary values and practices. <i>Ibid.</i> 35 Each person appointed under subsection (1)(b) of this section shall possess knowledge and experience of tikanga Maori. <i>(1993,NZ Statutes,7</i>)

>  I believe that tikanga Maori - or culture - will also evolve. <i>(1993,NZ Parliamentary Debates,533/13741</i>)

>  But tikanga Maori is still at the core of what Mai FM is all about. <i>(May 25, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  Two outstanding features for me were that the Kaupapa Atawhai waka is now fully launched - a sign of the department's commitment to tikanga Maori and its strength for the greater good of conservation. <i>(Mar 1993,What's Up Doc,12 : 1</i>)

### tikanga Moriori/Maori
 
 <i>phr.</i> knowledge of Moriori and Maori culture

>  Experience of tikanga Moriori/Maori and te reo helpful. <i>(Oct 1, 2005,Dominion Post,H17</i>)

### tikanga National
 
 <i>phr.</i> National Party protocol

>  But as with many matters of pressing concern, no one is exactly sure just what Tikanga National would prescribe. <i>(Jan 27, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### tikati
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Jordanidia solandri</i> (Cuv. & Val.). HAKE or SOUTHERN KINGFISH; TIKATI. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,48,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> SOUTHERN KINGFISH or HAKE (TIKATI) <i>Rexea solandri</i> (Cuv. and Val.) .../ This fish is known as Hake in Auckland. In New South Wales it is often caught by trawlers in one hundred and fifty fathoms and is known as King Barracouta. It is a favoured food fish. In Tasmania it is referred to as Tasmanian Kingfish. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,315,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SOUTHERN KINGFISH ... <i>Other names: Jordanidia solandri</i>; kingfish, hake, barraconda; king barracouta, Tasmanian kingfish (Australia); tikati (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,280</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> [The Southern Kingfish is] Called Hake in southern fish shops but bears no resemblance to the traditional English Hake which is far less oily. Tasmanian kingfish, barraconda and `tikati' (Maori) are other names. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,201</i>)

>  <i>Frost-fish. </i>... This is the para of most New Zealand Natives, and the tikati of Taranaki. <i>(1932,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,13/232</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  Tikati, the fish that precedes the frost fish, are [sic] now fairly plentiful on the Otaki beach, and on Sunday several fine specimens were found. <i>(May 2, 1913,Poverty Bay Herald</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

### tike
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka tyke] (See [tyke](../T#tyke))

>  The Marist Old Boys' teams, often called "The Tikes" on account of the grim determination they exhibited in all their games, were called together by Brother Virgilius (now of Wellington) in May, 1905, when the Auckland Secondary Schools Old Boys' Unions played matches for a banner donated by Messrs Smith and Caughey. <i>(Jul 20, 1912,NZ Truth,3</i>)

### Tiki
 
 [In Maori myth Tiki is the first man]

>  And in New Zealand Tiki was the creator of man. <i>(May 10, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  HOW THE CHILDREN OF TIKI MIGRATED FROM THE HIDDEN LAND OF TANE. <i>(Jun 18, 1896,Otago Witness,46</i>)

### tiki
 
 <i>v.</i> elliptical for tiki-tour (See [tiki tour n](../T#tiki-tour-n),[tiki-tour v](../T#tiki-tour-v))

>  I loved watching travel.co hosts Petra Bagust and Dean Cornish tiki-ing about the world. <i>(Dec 29, 2002,Sunday Star Times,D11</i>) [NOTES: [Jo McCarroll]]



 
 <i>n.</i> short for tiki tour n. q.v.; an exploratory look or visit (often as a tiki around a place)

>  (in the context of a story about some female university students tidying up the backyard of their flat, 20-year-old Greta File is quoted as saying: "We'll all attack it at once, and get it all done. The lawns, a couple of car-loads to the tip and a wee tiki around a garden centre to find some plants we like." <i>(Aug 30, 2007,Star, The,3</i>) [NOTES: citation sent in by Brent Southgate]



 
 <i>n.</i> the fish, tarakihi

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>TARAKIHI</i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Cheilodactylus macropterus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Tarakihi. (c) Other common names: Tiki (Kaikoura); Porae <i>(Cheilodactylus douglasi)</i> is normally lumped with Tarakihi and is known as Morwong in Australia and also as Sea Bream. (d) Principal localities where taken: [all except far south] <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,47</i>)

>  tarakihi .. [scientific name] <i>Nemadactylus macropterus</i> .. [other name(s)] morwong, ocean bream, teraki, tiki <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> . [ORIGIN: initialism, a flat carving, often in greenstone, of a grotesque figure representing the first man; short for heitiki. In English contexts used where possibly <i>heitiki</i> more correct, and now (at least in Australian English) for `a lucky charm worn from the neck' [get reference].]

>  ... they had brought many Articles of Trade such as Ahoos, green Images called Tigis, Stone Adzes etc <i>(Feb 24, 1777,Cook's Journals,3/1001</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb 24 Samwell Account in Cook Journals (1967) 3/1001]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Their Tikis or green jade ornaments are becoming so scarce that I could not procure one without paying an exorbitant price, they are only made in the South Island where the stone is found ... <i>(Apr 28, 1839,Journal,2/120</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... she was dressed in the European fashion, ... but retained the <i>mako</i>, or shark's teeth, in her ears, and wore suspended round her neck the <i>tiki</i>i, or household god of green jade, which passes as an heir-loom amongst families. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/308 We can trace in the carvings of the New Zealanders - in their huge <i>tikis</i> or <i>wakapokokos</i>, and in their ornamental houses - a strong analogy to the architectural ornaments of the Mexicans. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/267</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>(1898,Austral English</i>) [NOTES: [see Heitiki]]

>  <b>LYSNAR</b> The eyes of the tiki were filled with rings cut from the parva [sic], or mother-of-pearl, but recently this has been quite superseded by the charm of red sealing-wax. <i>(1924,New Zealand (various authors/titles),62</i>)

>  ... they filled an old canoe with treasures from the pa ... greenstone implements, tikis and carvings, etc., burying it somewhere in the neighbourhood. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,115</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1946,Coming of the Maori, The,449-453</i>)

>  <i>tiki</i>, flat carved greenstone image worn on a cord round the neck, particularly by women: a charm for fertility, being a rough representation of the human embryo. The name in full is heitiki, hei signifying anything worn round the neck; and the tiki being the chief object so worn the hei has been dropped. The tiki is highly valued, and genuine ones can rarely be obtained; fakes are plentiful. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/150</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>tiki</i>: grotesque fiqure of greenstone, etc., used as neck ornament. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>tik</i>i: special NZ carving of small ornament. It represented to the Maoris the unborn child and so good luck <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  A small Maori ornament of greenstone representing a human figure and worn round the neck on a string. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),413</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> A small Maori ornament of greenstone representing a human figure and worn round the neck on a string. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,224</i>)

>  <i>tiki n</i> -s [Maori & Marquesan] <i>1 usu cap</i> : an embodiment of the male principle in Polynesian mythology often depicted as the first man or the superhuman creator of mankind <i>2 a</i> : a Polynesian wood or stone image set up as a temporary abode or embodiment of a god or other supernatural power but not worshiped [sic] as an idol <i>b</i> : a Maori image representing an ancestor that is usu. either large and of wood or small, often in the form of a pendant, and of greenstone <i>Auckland (New Zealand) Star)</i> - compare <i>hei-tiki </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2393</i>)

>  No one has claimed a rare pre-European tiki found in the luggage hold of a plane, two years ago, and given to the Museum of New Zealand, Te Papa, for safekeeping. <i>(Apr 19, 2000,Dominion,14</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>heitiki</i> (or <i>tiki</i>): a greenstone ornament worn suspended from the neck. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,190</i>)

>  <i>tiki n.</i> Maori name for the Creator, thence taken to represent an ancestor; wooden images made by Maoris to represent their ancestor; miniature greenstone neck ornaments [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>tiki ... n</i>. as proper name, Tiki, mythological figure held in some stories as first man; greenstone ornament said to be based on shape of human embryo and once worn as fertility charm. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>JENKIN</b> On another occasion she found a beautiful greenstone<i> tiki, whakakai </i>(ear pendants), and a greenstone <i>mere. </i> <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,101</i>)

>  <i>tiki n.</i> a Maori greenstone neck ornament in the form of a foetus. [ Maori <i>heitiki</i> figure worn round neck] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1225</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 804; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 502]]

>  <b>POLACK</b> Around the neck, similar kinds of ornaments are worn, but the principal favourite is the Tiki. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,II : 178</i>)

>  Wooden Tikis, some of immense size, usually represented the ancestors. <i>(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,81</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> The body is U shaped, humanised below after the manner of a Maori tiki. <i>(1955,Maori Carving Illustrated,12</i>)

>  The beautiful and valuable greenstone from which the Maoris fashion all their..tikis. <i>(1921,Outward Bound,46</i>)

>  His Excellency (the President) with his usual kindly urbanity contributed largely to the enjoyment of the occasion by sending a great number of his rarest curiosities of Native art and manufacture, amongst which we may notice the large Tiki, presented to him by Honi Heke, and the large carved box presented to him by Rangihaeata <i>(Oct 6, 1852,New Zealander, The,2</i>) [NOTES: Reprinted from the Wellington Spectator 18 August]

>  Where possible this [greenstone] would be of sufficient size to produce not only a mere for the chief, but also a tiki for his wife. This latter, which was worn exclusively by women - was fashioned after the form of a human foetus. <i>(Dec 1956,Te Ao Hou,17: 51</i>)

>  At the dinner after the match on Saturday night his Excellency the Governor, on behalf of the New Zealand Rugby Union, presented each member of the British team with a greenstone tiki. <i>(Aug 17, 1904,Otago Witness,59</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of perennial brome grass (Bromus inermis). [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Tiki - winter-dormant and summer-active. Establishes best in warm spring conditions. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,24</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> [blonde child (?)]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Their hair is remarkably black ... Children having flaxen hair, are called `tikis', and [144] are much admired. <i>(1866,Polynesia,143</i>)

### tiki tour
 
 <i>n.</i> originally a tour of [part of] New Zealand; now more generally for a look around, a search [Recently heard applied to a document at meeting where a lengthy document was tabled "Could you give us a tiki tour around this".]

>  His latest exhibition Tiki Tour is a roundabout journey through Taupo. <i>(Jun 12, 2002,Capital Times,15</i>)

>  We have done a quick tiki tour of this small site before finally settling in the main area of the dining room, which is still quite petite. <i>(May 9, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),16</i>)

>  Jeff took a tiki tour of Upper Hutt .. and discovered many a hidden treasure. <i>(Dec 2000,Capital Holiday Guide,4</i>)

>  [Hdg] Tiki tour of the feelgood fiasco <i>(Apr 18, 1999,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)

>  The spores were then.. photographed, developed and stuck on to the glass panes. Visitors are invited to gaze at the topography of "living" air laid over the landscape. It's not the sort of tiki tour most tourists would take, although there are some familiar places... <i>(Dec 1, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F2</i>)

>  Simon Doull's one-day "tiki tour" in the last year looks destined to end today when New Zealand meets Australia in the fifth one-day cricket international at McLean Park in Napier. <i>(Mar 1, 2000,Dominion,48</i>)

>  If it can get [their clients] to Feilding and take them on a tiki tour, they're hooked: "The Americans and the Aussies when they come over here, they're more comfortable in a rural setting." <i>(Oct 19, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  You'll not hear any bad language on this show, unless you count Jim's excruciating wordplay: "Tiki tour? This is a tuk-tuk tour!"... I'd like to have seen and heard a lot more of Mike's work, since they'd gone to all the trouble of a tuk-tuk tiki tour to his studio. <i>(Jun 29, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),78</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tiki-tour</i> unlicensed driving; eg `When I was 12 I had an old BSA Bantam motorbike hidden on the farm, and used to tiki-tour all round the back of Cambridge.' <i>New Zealand Herald,</i> 22 February 1989 .. <i>tiki-tour</i> a look or nosy around. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,101</i>)

>  Concerned Timberlands would only take me on their "tiki-tour", as Brooker calls it, they show me sites where recent "experimental felling" has occurred. <i>(Apr 2000,North & South,84</i>)

>  This Saturday it will also become a performance space for a 15-minute tiki tour of the history of world art... <i>(Feb 15, 2002,Dominion,18</i>)

>  We were responding to a lot of calls received by the communications centre reporting a mystery glow in the hills behind us .. so off we went for a little tiki tour. <i>(Apr 2, 2001,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  These encounters begin a concatenation of events which culminate, after a chaotic Tiki tour around England, in the birth of a baby. <i>(Jul 15, 2000,Dominion,27</i>)

>  There's, likewise, a wee tiki tour of the Med in the skordalia, ratatouille and tapenade which dress the lamb flanks. <i>(Aug 2, 2003,Independent Herald (Wellington),F7</i>)

>  "There's been a few people around. Some of them just were going for rides, doing a bit of a tiki tour," she said. <i>(Dec 26, 2002,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  I went outside and played basketball with the house owner's kids for a while because the bus had gone on some tiki tour round the suburbs to find a place to turn around. <i>(Aug 5, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B1</i>)

>  <b>HUGHES</b> Later that day, I was due to pick up an Australian lieutenant colonel from the airport and was then to host him on a tiki-tour around the South Island for a week while he 'inspected his men'. <i>(1999,Shooting from the Lip,107</i>)

>  [heading] <b>Tiki-touring tutus</b>. The Royal New Zealand Ballet's much-loved tiki-touring institution is on the road. <i>(Jan 10, 2005,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.nzballet.org.nz/news-media-releases.html?news_item_id=34 Accessed 24/03/06]

### tiki-tehetehe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BURTON</b> Many types of fungus were eaten by the Maori. These were known by various names such as hawai, wairuru, tiki-tehetehe, maiheru, tawaka (a species of <i>Agaricus</i>) and hakeka (<i>Himeola</i>). <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,8</i>)

### tiki-tour
 
 <i>v.</i> go on a tour (often tiki-tour around); tour (a place)

>  Thus begins a lengthy literary road trip, as Ritz tiki-tours around England. <i>(Sep 25, 1999,Dominion,20</i>)

>  He had no debilitating illness and was still tiki-touring around the country - much to the dread of some of the parishes he audited. <i>(Oct 2001,Wel-com (Wellington),18</i>)

>  In the first episode of MO' Show.. hosts Mark Williams and Otis Frizzell tiki-tour around LA, shooting guns, looking at cars, daring each other to eat jalapeno peppers and visiting a porn film. <i>(Sep 1, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F7</i>)

>  Changes to navigation rules in the Marlborough Sounds are being rushed through after a cruise liner "tiki-toured" the area without the harbourmaster's knowledge and unnerved at least one boatie. .."This pilot was tiki-touring around the bays as if he was in a 40-foot launch on one of the busiest days of the year". <i>(Jan 13, 2006,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

### tikihemi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ...the tikihemi corresponds with our trout; in shape and appearance it is much like an eel, hence it is named the eel-trout;... <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,499</i>)

>  <i>Vernacular Name</i>.- Powell [TrNZI 2/84] was the first to point out that two fish were referred to as `smelt' in north Canterbury. These were <i>Galaxias attenuatus </i>and <i>Retropinna retropinna</i>. At present Canterbury residents refer to<i> R. retropinna</i> as `silvery,' on account of the silver line running along the side of the half-grown fish. Maoris refer to this `silvery' as `tikihemi,' and to the adult as `paraki.' <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/291</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> In some other localities these young [smelt, <i>Retropinna</i>] are called tuarenga. The half-grown fish is termed tikihemi and the adult paraki. Certain races of fish have a broad silvery band running along the side; hence the term `silvery.' It is to be regretted that in Canterbury the term smelt is still used to apply to the minnow, <i>Galaxias attenuatus. </i> <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,54</i>)

### tikkitak
 
 <i>n.</i> white-fronted tern, <i>Sterna striata</i>

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> WHITE-FRONTED TERN <i>Sterna striata Other names</i>: Sea-swallow, Kahawai Bird, Tara, Blackcap, Grenadier, Swallowtail, Noddy (Chathams), Tikkitak (Stewart Is.) <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,164</i>)

### tiko
 
 <i>n.</i> shit, excrement [ORIGIN: Maori, cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 259.]

>  "You are the most ugliest, nastiest piece of tiko around ..." <i>(Oct 18, 2006,Wairarapa News,24</i>)

### tikumu
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a Celmisia spp cotton plant [used by early rural travellers to make leggings for protection against matagouri and Spaniard spikes.] [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>KERR</b> All these travellers found the going hard, the rough tussock and fern and matagouri and spaniard tearing their clothing and spiking their legs in spite of leggings made of the stripped leaves of tikumu, the cotton plant. <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,44</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tikumu, <i>Col[enso]. Celmisia coriacea. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  Sometimes the snow-white downy fibres from the under side of the leaves of the Kowharawhara, and the Kahakaha, (<i>Astelia Cunninghamii</i>, and<i> Solandri,</i>) and the thin transparent epidermis from the leaves of the mountain Tikumu <i>(Celmisia coriacea)</i>, were also used ... to ornament ... the head. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/37</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  Tikumu, or Cotton plant Celmisia Coriacea, Hook. f. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/44</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Tikumu, the mountain celmisia, is also mentioned [in the c1880 lists]. The white fibrous down was scraped from its under side and manufactured in taupa (leggings). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,65</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 65]

>  <i>tikumu</i>, an alpine plant (Celmisia spectabilis, C. coriaceae). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Celmisia coriacea </i>(Forst.f.) Hook.f. and allied spp. Common names: <i>Cotton plant, horse daisy </i>Maori name: <i>Tikumu ... </i> [W.Lauder Lindsay 1861 applied `cotton plant' to the more familiar sp. of Celmisia] <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,41</i>)

>  Reverting to the right-hand lower corner there is a plaque representing miners at work, surrounded by New Zealand mountain lilies (ranunculus lyalii), tikumu (mountain daisies), celmisia longifolia <i>(Dec 10, 1889,Otago Daily Times,6</i>)

>  It is owing to the thick coating of woolly hairs found on the flower-stalks and under the leaves that the name "cotton plant" is given to it. By the Maoris it was named Tikumu, which it is thought that they gave to all large-flowered celmisias. <i>(Jul 1, 1908,Otago Witness,82</i>)

>  Passing round a spur in the gully I came on a large patch of ground completely covered with the tikumu (<i>Celmisia coriacea). <i>(Mar 13, 1875,Otago Witness,7</i>)

### tilt
 
 <i>n.</i> the canopy of a covered wagon

>  <b>MASTERS</b> I took my primitive dray, now made like a large basket, with bows and a tilt over it. <i>(1871,Autobiography,45</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> In 1867 there were many wagons on the road bearing huge freights from Dunedin to the diggings, but Thomson still adhered to his faithful dray with its tilt over it, and drawn by three horses, one shafter and two leaders. <i>(1956,Pioneer Recollections,2</i>) [NOTES: 1947-1948 Beattie Pioneer Recollections (1956) 2]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> [Caption to picture of 4-wheeled wagon covered by canvas cover on rounded hoops, and 6-horse team, c1900] A `schooner'. ... This is a typical colonial type schooner. Note the five-inch wide iron tyres, and the storm lantern swinging under the hooded canopy, or tilt, as it was called. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,[facing 161]</i>)

### tilter
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a harvesting machine. (See [also back delivery](../A#also-back-delivery))

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> It took a lot of men to work the old back-delivery machines or 'tilters' which looked rather like grass-mowers. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,174</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> Following the scythe as a means of cutting oats and wheat, and preceding the reaper and binder, the tilter filled in the gap as a reaping machine. It consisted of a set of fingers and a knife similar to a modern mower or harvester. <i>(1975,Silver Tussock, The,133</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> The crops were cut with a tilter .. As a schoolboy Benjamin Evans drove the tilter for his father. <i>(1975,Silver Tussock, The,229</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The 'tilter' - also known as the back-delivery because it dropped the sheaves from the back of a ilting hinged platform - was drawn by two horses and was first used in the early 1860s. <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,123</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> When cutting crop with the old tilters (back deliveries) ... [161] The old back-delivery [harvesting] machines or `tilters', which looked rather like grass-mowers. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,157</i>)

>  IMPLEMENTS, ETC. .. McCormick Clover Tilter, McCormick Hayrack <i>(May 8, 1920,Ashburton Guardian,8</i>)

>  WANTED. - A good TILTER, for a Reaping Machine. <i>(Jan 25, 1870,North Otago Times,3</i>)

>  Reid and Gray's Tilter. <i>(Oct 11, 1875,Timaru Herald,2</i>)

>  TENDERS WANTED for TYING, STOOKING and STACKING 233 Acres of Wheat and Oats, near the Cave. To be cut with two Tilters. Tilters, Horses, Drays, and two Stackers found. <i>(Jan 23, 1884,Timaru Herald,1</i>)

>  McCormick mowing machine, rake and tilter. <i>(Jan 15, 1908,Hawera & Normanby Star,8</i>)

>  Disc and chain harrows, s.f. plow, mower and tilter, hayrake, milk cart, 2 cattle dogs. <i>(Jul 23, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,8</i>)

>  Our land is hilly, and before we bought the Buckeye we had only tilters in use. <i>(Nov 13, 1890,Otago Witness,14</i>)

 <i>n.</i> one born, or residing in, Timaru

>  Timaruvians <i>(Oct 28, 1905,NZ Truth,19/6/3</i>)

>  <b>LAWRY</b> <i>(No Lawry and no Timaruvian can tell me who won that contest, so it could ... not have been the Timaru Garrison Band!)</i> [itals sic] <i>(1980,Good Luck & Lavender,50</i>)

>  The Timaruvians are not timorous. <i>(Sep 20, 1924,NZ Truth,14</i>)

>  Thousands of New Zealanders, like the Timaruvian, now pin their faith to this sterling remedy. <i>(Nov 15, 1930,Evening Post,26</i>)

>  We have just as ardent a desire to see a cable laid between Australia and New Zealand as any Timaruvian breathing, and shall hail with delight the day on which the work is begun (which day we believe is not far distant), but we certainly do object in the strongest manner possible to the way in which the railway is spoken of. <i>(Feb 23, 1875,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  <b>MCKAY</b> Loud are the shouts that the far echoes fill, / As the brave Timaruvians answer "We will! " <i>(1889,Spirit of Rangatira,32</i>)

>  We've quite a number of Timaruvians here. <i>(Oct 16, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/4 : 83</i>)

>  Timaruvians rise and bite like trout. <i>(Nov 15, 1916,Century of Sail,I/6 : 134</i>)

>  The song "Good Night to Thee" has a special interest to Timaru people, in the fact that it was written for Miss Stirling in Melbourne by a Timaruvian, Mr H. J. Bilton, son of Mr Bilton, the well known musician here. <i>(Jan 10, 1894,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  Timaru, he had been told, was the most boring place in New Zealand. Who told him that? "A Christchurch girl working in London as a nanny." No doubt a few Timaruvians will want to have a word with that nanny. <i>(Nov 20, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

 <i>n.</i> deer antlers

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> He avoided stepping in the cast antler of a red stag, but noted from habit the number of the points - five well-shaped ones, good timber, a decent length. <i>(1984,Razorback,13</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [drive](../D#drive))

### timber line
 
 <i>n.</i> the highest limit of (timber-)tree growth (cf. bush-, snow-, etc. line) [aka timber-line; ca1900 at least ca1952 Busdriver, Routeburn, Otago `The timber-line as you North Islanders call it - the snow-line as we call it down here.']

>  <b>ODELL</b> Probably the best camping site is on the edge of the timber line, for the bush stops abruptly to give place to tussock and snow grass. <i>(1935,Handbook Arthur Pass National Park,23</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>NOTHOFAGUS MENZIESII, silver beech</i>. A hardy tree, growing from sea level to timber line and often forming pure forests, especially in Nelson and Southland. ...Some stems become malformed through infestations of mistletoe, <i>Elytranthe colensoi</i>, and it is the only known host for our three species of <i>Cyttaria</i>, strawberry fungi which cause large galls. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,80</i>)

>  Mr. D. R. Hoggard, president of the Wellington branch of the Forestry League, said that in the Wellington Conservation Region, which took in practically half of the North Island, the percentage of the total area occupied by permanent State forests was only 5.4, and of that over half was above the timber line. <i>(Mar 19, 1924,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Although thar and chamois kept to the country above the timber line, rarely, if ever, descending into the bush, they were just as much of a menace as the deer, because erosion started on the heights when the vegetation was removed could start a train of erosion extending far down. <i>(Sep 11, 1945,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  This is an enormous area of mountains which rear their summits about 2000 feet above the timber line. <i>(Aug 10, 1895,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  The timber line in Otago is fixed at 3200ft above sea level, and it is here where the shelter hut is now building. <i>(Mar 1, 1900,Otago Witness,63</i>)

>  Most of the way up, until the timber line, young birches, veronicas, and ferns afforded very good handhold. <i>(Feb 25, 1887,Otago Witness,15</i>)

### timber rearing
 
 <i>n.</i> [(mining)]

>  <b>McARA</b> <i>Timber rearing</i>: Usually a wall at the end of a stope partitioning off the ladderway and consisting of 5 ft slabs nailed vertically to stulls wedged between the walls. Also used to make the central `box' in a three-compartment rise. Sometimes cribbing was used instead of building a rearing. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),335</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (obsolete)

 <i>n.</i>

### timber-jack
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka timber jack]

>  On country too steep for bullocks the bushmen themselves moved the logs with tools called 'timber jacks' that they cranked by hand. <i>(Apr 2003,NZ Memories,No. 41/50</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> The principal tools required [by the kauri bushman] were Yankee axes, cross-cut saws, and timber (not screw) jacks. <i>Ibid.</i> 173 (Northland) Timer jacks [are] better than scew -jacks ... The timber-jacks consist of a toothed spear, two pinions, a cogged wheel, all of wrought iron, and properly arranged in a strong piece of wood. A double handle puts in motion the gear, and the spear can be ground out about two feet with power, one man being able to lift at the handle a ton easily. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,171</i>)

>  Flotation is the method most commonly used in the kauri districts ... After the trunks are cut into lengths a `rolling-road' is formed to the nearest creek. All the scrub is closely cut down for a width of from twenty-five to thirty feet ... to obtain an approximately even surface, inclined towards the creek. The logs are forced along this road by timber-jacks, which the bushmen use with remarkable skill, and are impelled with a rapidity which is simply astonishing to persons unused to the process. If the creek contains plenty of water the logs float to the nearest booms without further trouble ... <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,131</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> The [kauri] logs are propelled along these roads by `timber-jacks' until they reach the water. <i>(1889,Forest Flora,150</i>)

>  <b>BARL;OW</b> ... timber jack. <i>(1889,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,112</i>)

>  <b>BODELL</b> This man had taken my 2 timber Jacks and cooking Utensils from the Mill ... <i>(1890,Soldier's View of Empire, A,211,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> TIMBER JACK: See Screwjack. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,189</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 189]

>  <b>MALFROY</b> [caption] TIMBER-JACK MADE IN NEW ZEALAND ... This is a ... well-tried-out jack which is extensively used in logging operations from North Cape to the Bluff <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,21</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> The bushmen that did this work were a tough capable lot - a race apart - whom no difficulty could deter, and who performed amazing feats with a few quite primitive tools. These were axes, maul, and wedges, crosscut saws, mattocks for earth work, pinching bars or crowbars, and the versatile New Zealand-made timber jack. These jacks weighed about 80 pounds and by geared cogs multiplied the man power applied to the revolving handles so that the `spear' with its two chisel points, when applied to a log, could exert a lifting pressure of 5 tons. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,38</i>)

>  We may also mention that Mr. Masefield has on hand a quantity of saw-mill machinery, timber-jacks, &c. <i>(Mar 24, 1869,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  Re the timber-jack: To the best of my knowledge this wonderful instrument of the bushman is solely a New Zealand tool made in the sixties of the last century by that well-known firm of engineers, A. and G. Price, Ltd., of Thames, and later still by Messrs. Judd Bros., also of Thames, for the use of the sawmillers in the Coromandel and North Auckland kauri forests. <i>(Aug 14, 1941,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Receiving no reply to their knocks, they forced open four doors with a timber-jack before gaining entrance to the lower part of the building. <i>(Sep 20, 1933,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  Everything in the shape of staging planks, chains and ropes, crowbars, timber jacks, and other gear is thrown overboard and there is considerable delay and expense in replacing these work tools. <i>(Mar 25, 1914,Grey River Argus,5</i>)

>  <b>HAWKE & SCOTT</b> First timber jacks were used [sc. for stumping] and later a Trewella stumping machine which had a thirteen ton pull on it <i>(1985,Early Farm Machinery in NZ,129</i>)

>  Two ploughs, 2 drays, 1 spring dray .. 4 tons chaff, road scoop, timber jack .. also household furniture of every description too numerous to particularise. <i>(May 26, 1908,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  Everything in the shape of staging planks, chains and ropes, crowbars, timber jacks, and other gear is thrown overboard and there is considerable delay and expense in replacing these work tools. <i>(Mar 25, 1914,Grey River Argus,5</i>)

### timberbelt
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a long and narrow plantation of trees grown for both timber and shelter.

>  <b>WHITE & HODGSON</b> Timberbelt is a term coined in New Zealand to describe a belt of trees designed to emphasise the production of timber; they may also have some sheltering function. Utilisation of timberbelts is not yet widespread but they are increasingly evident in regions where they are being promoted (eg Wairarapa and Canterbury.) <i>(1999,NZ Pasture and Crop Science,275</i>)

>  <b>WHITE & HODGSON</b> Shelter belts and timberbelts should be removed before they become over-mature. <i>(1999,NZ Pasture and Crop Science,276</i>)

>  The Star's Otira correspondent telegraphs that Frank Brosnan, a timber man, employed in the tunnel at Otira, was killed this morning. <i>(Jun 1, 1910,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  All the underground men received an advance of 2/- a day; timbermen, blacksmiths, engine drivers 2/-; surface and battery employees 1/6. <i>(Feb 19, 1920,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  At the time of the accident Love was timberman, and Elie was watching the supports. <i>(Feb 26, 1907,Evening Post,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a `tree' made from the stems and heads of timothy grass

The little `Timothy Tree' being hidden ... . <i>(1933,Farmer's Wife, The,117</i>)

### timuka
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>(Jun 28, 1851,Lyttelton Times,7</i>) [NOTES: There are two plants, the karo (prickly thorn) and taramea (spear grass), which are well worthy of their names, but are almost exclusively confined to the hilly regions. There generally grows near these a plant called the timuka, from the leaf of which the natives make mats and leggings, which are impervious to the sharp points of the former.]

 <i>n.</i> sc loaf (cf double tin, raised tin, split tin)

N.Z.</i> a receptacle for home-baked biscuits, etc. (esp. in <i>to fill her tins</i> to bake a supply of biscuits, etc.) <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1227</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 503]]

### tin billy
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. billy-tin](../B#billy-tin))

>  <b>DOBSON</b> Boiling was done in light tin billies. <i>(1930,Reminiscences,156</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> . [ref to tin-canning; tin can army, tin can party. aka tincan, tin-can]

>  <b>WEBB</b> He ... recalled how he had himself described a man as `a stingy blackguard' who only offered the tin-can party ... twenty-five shillings to be spent in beer. <i>(1925,Miss Peters' Special,62</i>)

>  And is it true that W.G. is going to bring home a mistress for the little cottage, and that L.M. is quite delighted with her costume of grey, also that the officers and men of tin-can army are ordered out in honour of the happy pair? <i>(Aug 19, 1893,Observer (Auckland),21</i>)

>  not milk made, just testing <i>(1900,'Milkmade' Cook Book,5</i>) [NOTES: Year is incorrect]

>  There will be a job for the tin-can army in the good time coming. <i>(Sep 9, 1893,NZ Observer,22</i>)

### tin canoe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GEE</b> So I talked about Charlie: the playground fight, the orchard raiding, the tin canoes. <i>(1972,In My Father's Den,130</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (tinned) fish (See [tin dog](../T#tin-dog))

### tin cow
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering and shepherding] canned milk.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> tin cow: canned milk, usually condensed. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,85</i>)

### tin dish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The only tools we took with us were, a spade, tin dish, and pannikin. We tried a number of places along the bed of the creek, sinking in each from two to four feet; out of the whole we washed not more that six buckets full, ... <i>(Jun 29, 1861,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  We bought a little flour, and borrowed a tin dish from one of the stations in the Manuherikia Valley, and panned out forty ounces [of gold] in about a week. [of gold inserted by JM -OK???] <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  Took a spade and tin dish with us to look for gold, made a pit 6ft. deep and 4 by 3 keenly gossen. <i>(May 29, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,40,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HODDER</b> ... a group of women were round the pool washing the `tailings' in tin dishes. <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,166</i>)

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> ... I saw the swag, and picked it up with the loss of two tin dishes, one long-handled shovel ... <i>(Dec 22, 1863,Diary of a West Coast Prospecting Party,393</i>)

>  In one day they obtained 2oz. of rough gold with a shovel and tin-dish, and immediately reported the circumstance to the Warden at Dunstan. <i>(1887,Handbook NZ Mines,8</i>) [NOTES: 1863 V. Pyke's Report in 1887 Handbook NZ Mines 8]

>  A new rush has taken place over the saddle, six or eight miles from the township, which is turning out well, an ounce being washed out there in a day with a tin dish. <i>(Mar 14, 1865,Lyttelton Times</i>) [NOTES: 1865 Mar 14 Lyttelton Times in 1966 Turner English Language 151]

>  <b>PYKE</b> Borrowing a pan (<i>Anglice</i> : tin-dish) from some neighbourly miners, he progressed up the Gorge trying `prospects' in many places, and at length he was satisfied to `peg out'. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/iv/17,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> ... some few of them had done a little desultory gold getting with shovel and tin dish ... one of whom ... had in his possession in 1862 a fine sample of coarse gold obtained ... up the Kapiti Creek ... <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,94</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The shops supplied ... mostly diggers, and all I saw of their goods were tin dishes and diggers' tools hanging outside. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,166</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> ... all we got for our trouble with the tin dish were a few colours and black sand, which is always an indication that you are in gold-bearing wash. <i>Ibid.</i> 115 So there [in a grave] the good old chap lies besides the remains of his unknown miner of the 1865 tin dish and cradle days. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,69</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> We did not wash any of the gravel, for we had no tin dish, neither did we know how to wash. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iv/56</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Mar 24 in 1863 Butler First Year iv/56]

>  A tailor named McKenzie got 43 ounces in one day, worth GBP3 17s 6d per ounce, with a tin dish on a bar in the Slate River. <i>(Sep 17, 1906,Ohinemuri Gazette,2</i>)

>  In 1862 two miners suffering terrible hardships made their famous gold discovery in the Molyneux River below the junction of the Kawarau and Clutha Rivers, where the town of Cromwell now stands, and after several months of scratching around the crevices and river-bed beaches with pick, shovel, tin dish and cradle, returned to Dunedin with 87lb. weight of gold; these two men were Messrs. Hartly and Riley. <i>(Jun 9, 1927,NZ Truth,14</i>)

>  I washed an ounce and a-half of alluvial gold from a tin dish of dirt, 40 miles farther south than where I sank the first shaft, but on a continuation of the same leading range. <i>(Dec 22, 1871,North Otago Times,4</i>)

>  Mr Evenden of Te Aroha has invented a machine for saving fine gold based upon the principal [sic] of the miners' tin dish. <i>(Mar 28, 1889,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

### tin dog
 
 <i>n.</i> a motor toboggan

>  In recent years the "tin dog" or motor toboggan has become the main mode of transport [sc. in the Antarctic]. <i>(Feb 1992,North & South,23</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [stones in tin to rattle behind herd of sheep in absence of a dog]

>  <b>BARRY DOYLE</b> "... when we got home from school we would get a tin dog and drive the sheep to wherever dad said." What is a "tin dog" I asked. "Motorists, he replied carried petrol in 4 gallon cans in the boot of their car and when they were empty they would throw them in the ditch by the roadside. We would find one, put a fistful of gravel in it and drive the sheep. They were called 'tin dogs'". <i>(Jun 25, 2003,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: original from Ewan McGregor of Hawkes Bay ewan-mac@xtra.co.nz]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rattle made of tin lids and wire [ used to move sheep when a dog is not available or appropriate.]

>  <b>PETER</b> tin dog: rattle made of tin lids. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,5</i>)

>  tin dog - a rattle made of tin lids and wire, used to drive sheep. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to can (meat)

### tin hare
 
 <i>n.</i> nickname for a railcar

>  <b>LEITCH</b> The Wairarapa cars.. were designed to negotiate the Rimutaka Incline under their own power... Of special lightweight construction, these forty-nine-seaters, which sooner earned the affectionate name of the 'tin hares', proved to be fast and reliable machines. <i>(1972,Railways of NZ,194</i>)

### Tin Hat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  `Tin Hat.' - `Thicknut' asks: Can anyone tell me why the `Heads' are called `Tinhats'? Are they any relation to the beings known in mythology as `tin gods'? [The terms `Tin Hat' and `Brass Hat' were current years before the war began, especially `Tin Hat.' Probably the expression had its origin in the metallic headgear worn by some of the military units in pre-war days. There is no evidence (at least no etymological evidence) that `tin-hat' is a corruption of `tin-head,' as one cynic has suggested.] <i>(Nov 1, 1918,Quick March,15</i>)

### tin loaf
 
 <i>n.</i> a square 2lb loaf, baked in a covered tin

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> Digression no 2 - I had to leave this letter - go into the kitchen & cut myself an entire round of bread and bloater paste - tin loaf - <i>(Jun 19, 1908,Letters,1/50,1984 ed.</i>)

### tin mill
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small tractor-led metal threshing mill [widely used on individual farms and requiring seven or eight men to operate.]

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> tin mill: see combine. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,85</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> His main source of income, the cocksfoot seed, is harvested in the old way, in sheaves, with a binder and an old tin mill. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,1</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The direct descendant of the tin mill is the combine harvester, or header, of today, the only real difference between the two being that the mill lacks an engine, cutter bar and self-motivation. Jim's got four tin mills but uses only two, one on each farm. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,10</i>)

>  <b>OGILVIE</b> Next to the header harvester, the `tin' mill did the mill [sc. threshing] men most disservice. This smaller style of mill came in with the tractor by which it was drawn and driven. The `tin' mill only needed seven or eight men to operate it, and its output was no more than half that of the `big' mill. But within a short time these small mills became so popular that many farmers bought their own and could thresh their grain exactly when it suited them and their crops. The `tin' mill and the `Sunshine' header thus put an effective end to the era of the mill men [in South Canterbury]. <i>(1971,Moonshine County,158</i>)

>  <b>NEAVE</b> Then the tin mill and tractor came in, ... and [threshing] work was reduced to a few days only. <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,90</i>)

>  The tin mill is smaller, slower but more portable [sc. than the threshing mill]. <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(7) : 10</i>)

### tin rattle
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rattle made of tin lids [used to move sheep when a dog is not available or appropriate.]

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> It is not unusual to see the tin rattles strung at strategic points along the crush pen with individual cords running from these to within easy reach of the drafting gates. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,76</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> A makeshift tin rattle is good but only if expertly used. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,80</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>
