### Tin Shed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The 'old tin shed,' as the building is irreverently called by the students, was thronged with people throughout the evening, who passed through from room to room, inspecting the assaying plant and all the interesting geological and mining exhibits for which the school is noted. <i>(Jun 5, 1901,Otago Witness,54</i>)

>  OTAGO SCHOOL OF MINES. .. In 1891 a separate building was erected, the material being supplied from the exhibition of 1889-90. At the same time the staff was increased, separate lecturers on metallurgy and geology being appointed. .. This, Mr Given explained, was a very brief and crude history of the Otago institution, or, as it was familiarly called, "The Tin Shed." <i>(Dec 26, 1906,Otago Witness,26</i>)

>  At any rate, the absolute banality of a common "tin shed" does not hinder the students of the Otago School of Mines from feeling and expressing this sentiment. <i>(Jul 8, 1908,Otago Witness,29</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> nickname for the Wellington Fever Hospital at Newtown

>  <b>HYDE</b> When they [the Newtown slum family] took the scarlet fever in a batch, and were sent off in the ambulance to be isolated at the Tin Shed - the fever hospital - Augusta sent them custards ... <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,26,1970 ed.</i>)

>  Her first charge was the building frankly known as "the tin shed," in which diptheria [sic] patients were housed. <i>(Oct 11, 1928,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  In 1939 a 50-bed "emergency" ward was erected, displacing a block of wards, known as the "tin shed," which had previously catered for infectious disease cases. <i>(Dec 10, 1940,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  The isolation ward, termed the "tin shed," was a properly-constructed and lined ward. <i>(Aug 6, 1915,Evening Post,8</i>)

### Tin Town
 
 <i>n.</i> part of Brockenhurst (NZ No 1) Hospital in England in WWI

>  Sister Anderson, of No. 1 N.Z. General Hospital (Tin Town), on Wednesday, 13th, took up the duties of matron here, and is well liked. <i>Ibid.</i> 66 Brockenhurst Hospital is divided into four sections - headquarters, a collection of huts usually known as "Tin Town," and two large hotels and a hall converted. Balmer Lawn and Morant sections I visited that afternoon. <i>(Sep 29, 1916,NZEF Chronicles,60</i>)

>  In January 1916, eighty-seven years ago, the tented and galvanised iron hospital complex, known locally as 'Tin Town' was taken over by No. 1 New Zealand General Hospital. Over 21,000 New Zealand casualties were treated there, including the 93 who died. <i>(Apr 2004,NZ Memories,No. 47/16</i>)

### tin worm
 
 <i>n.</i> rust

>  They [sc. British cars]were invariably slow, handled like corners didn't exist, dribbled oil, were prone to tin worm, required constant maintenance and would likely kill, you if you crashed into something. <i>(Aug 4, 2001,Evening Post,27</i>)

### tin-arse
 
 <i>n.</i> a lucky person [1984 16-17 M E90 Pakuranga Coll. 32 TIN(-)ARSE [luckiness] [M13 F4]]

>  <b>BEATON</b> KATE: Good ol' Ginny [for escaping the consequences]! SANDY: Yeah! KATE: Tin arse! <i>Ibid.</i> Glossary 110 <i>lucky; a lucky person (used ironically). </i> <i>(1984,Outside In,20</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tin arse</i> lucky person; also<i> tinbum;</i> eg `First time he's ever swung a golf club and he scores a hole in one. What a tin arse!' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,113</i>)

### tin-arsed
 
 <i>adj.</i> (informal) 1. (non-derogatory, with perhaps often edge of jealousy, or of jealous non-acceptance) lucky. 2. (derogatory)= tinpot, tinny, cheap in appearance or structure [ca1940 Havelock You don't call that tin-arsed thing a fort, do you?]

### tin-bum
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a lucky person (occas. tin-arse, adj. tin-arsed) cf. tinny

>  <b>BAXTER</b> <i>Song of Tinbum Kelly </i> <i>(1979,Collected Poems,137,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The luckiest player that ever pulled on a jersey, said Old Bert. He's been a bit of a tin-bum. Not like me. I'm always shit out of luck. <i>Ibid.</i> 164 Full as the family poe [sic] - The trouble with the game today - The biggest tin-bum. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,158</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> One of our calls was on a chap who had recently had a good win in the Art Union, and Dan took the opportunity to congratulate him. The result had appeared in the Invercargill paper and the nom-de-plume published was `Tinburn'. Dan of course wanted to know what prompted him to use such a curious nom-de-plume, and the proud winner said that he hadn't used that nom-de-plume at all. `Well what did you use?' asked Dan [57] `Tinbum.' Obviously the press in those days was a little more prudish than it is today. <i>(1975,Men of the Milford Road,56</i>)

>  It's a bizarre and miraculous thing to happen. It's a real tin-bum thing [sc. on winning Lotto]. <i>(Nov 22, 1992,Sunday Times (NZ),1</i>)

### tin-can
 
 <i>v.</i> tin-kettle [aka tin can, tincan]

>  <b>FRAME</b> We tin-canned them and threw rice at them and they gave us a cream bun that was only half cream. <i>(1960,Owls Do Cry,84</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> At that moment the very devil of a noise started up outside, as if a thousand mongrel dogs with two thousand tin cans tied to their tails had been suddenly let loose on a corrugated-iron roof. `What on earth is that?' I gave a jump that nearly sent Norah sprawling. `They must be tin-canning us.' `And what, I'd like to know, is tin-canning?' `Oh, it's a thing they always do round here when a newly married couple comes home.' <i>(1959,No Remittance,96</i>) [NOTES: [Southland]]

>  <b>FRAME</b> Florrie married. We tin-canned her and her new husband and had a feast of fizzy drinks and cakes,... <i>(1982,To the Is-Land,81,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>D E HURLEY</b> Reporting a conversation with Maori woman from Northland who mentioned that over the Christmas/New Year 1989-1990 she and others had gone `tincanning' on Christmas and New Year Eve, meaning going as a group to people's places for drinks etc. making anoise outside until the householder let them in. <i>(Jan 25, 1990,Personal Communication</i>)

>  `My sister married a farmer who lives at Alford Forest [Canterbury] ... and, when she and her husband arrived back from their honeymoon, they were tin-canned.' <i>(Dec 1987,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1987 Dec Letter from Christchurch [re Alford Forest] in RussellJ. Stone folder (ATB MS)]

>  My mother was born and lived in Invercargill up to 1915, and has told me of the custom there to go `tin-canning' newly-weds. <i>(Feb 12, 1988,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1988 Feb 12 Letter from Napier in Russell J. Stone folder (ATB MS)]

>  <b>SCOTT</b> The chaps are coming up tonight. Tin-canning. <i>(1953,Breakfast at Six,19</i>)

>  F.H. has been married, tin-canned, &c., I hear, and is looking better than ever. <i>(Oct 27, 1888,Observer (Auckland),20</i>)

>  Little boys "tincan" the happy pair. <i>(Jun 15, 1918,Wairarapa Daily Times,4</i>)

>  At the Thames Police Court last Monday, four youths were fined L1 each and costs for tincanning a wedding party. <i>(Feb 5, 1891,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  Speaking to a new chum the other day he said he was surprised at people tin-canning newly-married couples. <i>(Jun 22, 1906,Hawera & Normanby Star,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [tin-canning/tin-kettling aka tin can, tincan] (See [tin-canning/tin-kettling](../T#tin-canning/tin-kettling))

>  It is rumoured that several couples are going to run in double harness soon, so get your tin-cans ready, boys. <i>(Nov 22, 1890,NZ Observer,17</i>)

>  Is it a case of get your tin cans ready, boys? <i>(Aug 20, 1904,NZ Observer,22</i>)

>  Our frolicsome boys won't be short of tincans at the next rejoicing if they will only pay a visit to the swamp. <i>(Aug 21, 1880,Bay of Plenty Times,3</i>)

### tin-can band
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "It was quite a usual thing to parade the streets with waving banners, and a tin-can band, and make the night hideous with noise. .." <i>(Aug 4, 1906,Evening Post,14</i>)

### tin-can mail
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Niua-foou is the island famed for its `tin-can mail'. The monthly bills are sealed in a kerosine tin and thrown into the water, a swimmer from the shore picking up the `bag'. <i>(Aug 10, 1921,Quick March,13</i>)

### tin-canner
 
 <i>n.</i> tin-kettler [aka tin canner, tincanner]

>  <b>WEBB</b> I suppose I'll have to part at least two quid of the money I've saved ... for the tin-canners. <i>Ibid.</i> 64 I forgot to warn you. It's the tin-canners, the first lot - youngsters you know.' He dashed out ... and was greeted by shrieks, cheers, and a deafening banging on benzine and kerosine tins. After a brief parley ... the giggling boys departed, and the bridegroom returned to explain to his wife that `It was just a custom - you know. They always do it in this part of the country. The men will be here a bit later, and I'll have to give them something to go and drink our health. <i>(1925,Miss Peters' Special,62</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> Now he was on hand early to ensure that the tin canners did not over-step the mark in decorum. <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,55</i>)

>  That foolish practice of "tin-canning" the bride and bridegroom was indulged in at Whangarei the other evening, and so terrific was the din, that now there is talk of "tin-canners" being asked to explain themselves at the S.M.'s Court. <i>(Nov 9, 1912,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  Why wouldn't E.W. join the tin-canners? <i>(Aug 13, 1904,NZ Observer,21</i>)

>  Of course the inevitable 'tincanners' were in force, and made night hideous for a few minutes. <i>(Aug 29, 1891,Observer (Auckland),16</i>)

>  A.B. to Miss W. and E.N at the Kowhai to R.B. - so I hear - tin canners, get ready ... <i>(Jun 18, 1887,Observer (Auckland),18</i>)

>  JH and FS have taken the decisive step at last. Congratulations. Don't forget the cake and the tin-canners. <i>(Feb 7, 1903,Observer (Auckland),21</i>)

### tin-canning
 
 <i>adj.</i> [attrib. use: tin-canning parties]

>  <b>WEBB</b> He ... had taken his full share in the New Year's Eve pranks and mischief in which colonial youth delights, as well as being a promoter of most of the tin-canning parties when anyone got married. <i>(1925,Miss Peters' Special,62</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> = tin-kettling [aka tincanning, tin canning]

>  This was called "tin-canning" or "tin-kettling," and newly married couples were those who suffered. <i>(Mar 25, 1943,NZ Farmers' Weekly,16</i>)

>  <i>tin-canning NZ </i>serenading of newly-married couple by beating cans. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,806</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tin canning </i>a greeting, often of newly-weds, by tin can bashing, often now for sport or rock music performers; originally and still occasionally aka <i>tin kettling</i>. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,113</i>)

>  `I was growing up in Central Southland in the 1920ies when tincanning was very common.' <i>(Feb 10, 1988,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1988 Feb 10 Letter from Wellington in Russell J. Stone folder (ATB MS)]

>  <b>KEENE</b> In the evening [of our wedding day: ca1898] we prepared for visitors. The number who came to the tin canning was the measure of a couple's popularity. The usual plan was for the guests to approach silently, wait until all were gathered, then to smite their tins with might and main, making a din which could be heard for miles. <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,55</i>)

>  Eight young men were convicted of disturbing an inhabitant [of Hamilton] and fined 5s. and costs each. The magistrate gave them a lecture on the absurdity of the barbarous custom of tin-canning. <i>(Sep 5, 1891,Waikato Times</i>) [NOTES: [1891 Sep 5 Waikato Times] in 1964 Norris Settlers in Depression 197]

>  They say they are going to give me an awful tincanning when I get married. Hope you won't mind, Dora.' <i>(Feb 11, 1911,NZ Truth,294/5/1</i>)

>  ... if I have a dance at someone's tincanning. <i>(Nov 13, 1953,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `You knew this would happen?' I said to her when she came to bed. `Of course. There was bound to be a tin-canning. We'd hardly be married without it. Don't they have them everywhere?' `No, indeed they don't.' <i>(1959,No Remittance,96</i>)

>  They say they are going to give me an awful tincanning when I get married. Hope you won't mind, Dora.' <i>(Nov 13, 1953,NZ Truth,294/5/1</i>)

>  ... if I have a dance at someone's tincanning. <i>(Nov 13, 1953,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `You knew this would happen?' I said to her when she came to bed. `Of course. There was bound to be a tin-canning. We'd hardly be married without it. Don't they have them everywhere?' `No, indeed they don't.' <i>(1954,No Remittance,96</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> ... I suppose our [children's wedding] celebrations [c1880] were rather childish. ... We called them `tin-canning'. We [117] usually started rather late because by then the guests were fairly drunk and their reactions more amusing. Our drums were empty kerosene tins beaten with copper-sticks and the din could be heard for miles. ... Tradition decreed that red-hot pennies be thrown to us when the tin-canning started. <i>(1964,Stamper Battery,116</i>)

>  The usual amount of tin-canning was gone through by the smaller fry, who for their endeavours at instrumentality were rewarded with good things ad infinitum. <i>(Aug 13, 1881,Observer (Auckland),548</i>)

>  In re-examination, Miss Lowen said she had been to a "tin-canning" at Ashburton while on holiday after the return of a honeymoon couple, but that was the only function she attended. <i>(Jun 26, 1902,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  [Caption] "Tin Canning". Bride and groom surprised. <i>(Dec 20, 1944,Franklin Times,2</i>)

>  The OM would like to know if JH gave the boys some wine and cake after the tin-canning last week ... <i>(Aug 23, 1902,Observer (Auckland),22</i>)

### tin-ear
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> An (habitual) eavesdropper: Australian: since ca. 1918. Baker, 1942. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,725</i>)

### tin-filler
 
 <i>n.</i> A plain biscuit or easily made cake,etc, of which a ready supply might easily and cheaply be kept [in airtight tins]

>  Anzac biscuits are a standard tin-filler. <i>(Aug 17, 1999,Radio NZ / National Radio,Morning Report</i>) [NOTES: Alison Gofton & Alison Host during a promotiion of Anzac Biscuits as the Millenium Biscuit.]

### tin-kettle
 
 <i>v.</i> [cf tin-can]

>  <b>WOOD</b> Presently we heard an awful uproar in the ball-room. `Oh! Glory,' said Bridget, `they're tin-kettling them [the newly-weds]; come on,' and she seized an empty kerosine tin and was off. ... There stood Ned and Maggie in the centre, while round marched the rest of the people armed with kerosine tins, billies half filled with stones, and tin dishes, shouting, yelling, and singing ... <i>(1875,Waiting for the Mail,110</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> I was wakened by the din caused by a lot of the diggers tin-kettling the newly-married pair. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,234</i>)

>  <b>BRADSHAW</b> ... the members of the school committee hostile to our popular and excellent teacher were hooted and tin-kettled to their houses. <i>(1888,New Zealand Of Today,323</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> One night, when I had been on the station [newly-married] about a month, Ron ... announced, `We'll be having visitors tomorrow night. They're coming for miles around to tin-kettle us. <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,103</i>)

>  It falls to the lot of very few couples to be tin-kettled after they have been married for a period of two and a half years, but this was the distinction which befell a Rakaia pair (writes a correspondent). The husband had just returned from the front after being absent for practically the whole of his married life. <i>(Feb 7, 1919,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  The larrikins are indanger of losing their time-honored privilege of tin-kettling newly-married couples. <i>(Oct 29, 1894,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

>  They had "tin-kettled" in front of the residence of a newly-wedded couple. <i>(Nov 16, 1908,Taranaki Herald,4</i>)

### tin-kettler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>A Tin-kettling Episode</i>: The detestable practice of tin-kettling a newly-married couple [with kerosine tins] doesn't seem to die out like other barbarities in this country; it is a relic of by-gone days. ... The origin of the custom has never been authoritatively determined and whether it is an English or purely Colonial custom ... doesn't appear to be known. However, the tin-kettlers expect the happy bridegroom to stop the din by handing them cakes and ale. ... It is only the idiotic low-class class of people who would go banging tin-cans opposite a man's house after dark ... . <i>(Sep 23, 1905,NZ Truth,14/8/3</i>)

>  <b>MARKS</b> But it was to the uninvited guests [to the wedding] that we boys paid most attention - the tin-kettlers. <i>(1934,Memories (Mainly Merry),18</i>)

>  I was married in Greymouth in 1937 ... a crowd of children and young adults arrived, armed with noisemakers ... But, six months ago, here in Rotorua, my young neighbour remarried and I was surprised and delighted to see and hear the `tin-kettlers' arrive when the young couple returned from their honeymoon. <i>(Feb 13, 1988,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1988 Feb 13 Letter from Rotorua in Russell J. Stone folder (ATB MS)]

>  `The local farmers put their heads together and planned a tin-kettling party. In the hands of people were carried an assortment of pots and kettles, wooden and metal spoons, kerosene tins containing stones as a rattle, old-fashioned car horns ... The tin-kettling was not complete until the house was circled by the tin-kettlers.' <i>(Feb 8, 1988,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1988 Feb 8 Letter from Christchurch [re Alford Forest] in Russell J. Stone folder (ATB MS)]

>  The "tin-kettlers" are made up by the male friends of those just married, amounting sometimes, if the bridegroom is popular, to fifty persons, the "tin-kettles" used being generally kerosene tins, which are beaten with sticks, sometimes small stones are put in the tin, and the thing is shaken, drums are beaten, pipes played, and everything done which can tend to make a noise. <i>(Jun 26, 1876,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  At a recent wedding, the next-door neighbours of the bridal party provided cakes for the tin-kettlers. <i>(Apr 23, 1904,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  The tin-kettlers reckoned with their host, but not the bull. <i>(Oct 22, 1904,NZ Observer,17</i>)

>  The Missing Link having asserted that it was the muddy roads kept the tin-kettlers from visiting T.B., the latter would be anxious to get an answer to the following question ... <i>(Jul 21, 1883,Observer (Auckland),4</i>)

>  The guardians of the law at the place just named came down on a large force of "tin-kettlers" who were worrying a new-married couple, and secured no less than nineteen of the offenders, who were each fined ten shillings and costs by the magistrate. <i>(Mar 30, 1892,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

>  I'm a stout tin-kettler / For the small, but honest settler. <i>(Jan 1, 1910,NZ Truth,1</i>)

### tin-kettling
 
 <i>n.</i> (formerly) a custom whereby a newly-wed couple are welcomed to their marriage home by noisy banging of kerosene (etc.) tins until they provide the tin-kettlers with refreshments. [also called tin-canning]

>  This was called "tin-canning" or "tin-kettling," and newly married couples were those who suffered. <i>(Mar 25, 1943,NZ Farmers' Weekly,16</i>)

>  <i>A Tin-kettling Episode</i>: The detestable practice of tin-kettling a newly-married couple [with kerosine tins] doesn't seem to die out like other barbarities in this country; it is a relic of by-gone days. ... The origin of the custom has never been authoritatively determined and whether it is an English or purely Colonial custom ... doesn't appear to be known. However, the tin-kettlers expect the happy bridegroom to stop the din by handing them cakes and ale. ... It is only the idiotic low-class class of people who would go banging tin-cans opposite a man's house after dark ... <i>(Sep 23, 1905,NZ Truth,14/8/3</i>)

>  <b>R. MASON</b> `Term must date back 60 years at least judging by the people who are familiar with it as an old expression. A custom (in country districts mostly) of visiting a newly settled-in just married couple in the evening and banging tins and making other noise, etc., outside their house. <i>(1905,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <i>(Aug 1952,Straight Furrow,11 (10)/54</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> After it [sc. the house] had weathered two traditional `tin kettlings' we knew we had nothing to fear. <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,204,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> `Used to be real live-wires when we were first married,' said Mrs Foden philosophically. `Never missed a tin-kettling or first-footing; euchre evenings, sales, duck shoots, dances, send-offs, fires. We always went. ...' <i>(1979,Supper Waltz Wilson,47</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> [On the Chatham Islands] another thing they've dropped now is tin-kettling after a wedding ... thirty or forty of you, all with kerosine tins, you'd make a hell of a din. Then you'd all be called in for drink and tucker. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,30</i>)

>  Mr Buck says he wants to put the record straight `as one who in 1935 was present when Keith Holyoake and his new bride arrrived back in Riwaka from their honeymoon to a traditional "tin-kettling"'. <i>(Dec 24, 1983,Dominion,2</i>)

>  <i> tin-kettling NZ = tin-canning. </i> <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,806</i>)

>  `The local farmers put their heads together and planned a tin-kettling party. In the hands of people were carried an assortment of pots and kettles, wooden and metal spoons, kerosene tins containing stones as a rattle, old-fashioned car horns ... The tin-kettling was not complete until the house was circled by the tin-kettlers.' <i>(Feb 8, 1988,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1988 Feb 8 Letter from Christchurch [re Alford Forest] in Russell J. Stone folder (ATB MS)]

>  I was present at both the tin-kettling and the presentation [to the new bride of a chamber-pot filled with salt and tied about with a wideish ribbon ... with a large bow tied in it ... with the advice that if they used up all of the salt in the chamber-pot they would have Good Luck.] which probably took place between 1925 and 1930. <i>(Feb 10, 1988,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1988 Feb 10 Letter from Nelson in Russell J. Stone folder (ATB MS)]

>  I remember ... being carried to my first `tin-kettling' [at Cobden c1930]. <i>(Feb 12, 1988,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1988 Feb 12 Letter from West Coast in Russell J. Stone folder (ATB MS)]

>  <b>HUNT</b> Another thing they've dropped now is the tin-kettling after a wedding... One bloke might say "How about tin-kettling so and so?" <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,30</i>)

>  The practice of tin-kettling and levying blackmail on people after a wedding was of frequent occurrence, expecially in St Alban's District. ... serenaded a newly wedded happy couple with a tintinnabulation of tin kettles <i>(Nov 13, 1874,Otago Guardian,2</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  The Inspector of Police referred to the common practice of tin-kettling at weddings. <i>(Jun 6, 1868,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  The return of Mr and Mrs Niven from their Honeymoon on Wednesday was taken advantage of a party from here to attend at Cloverleigh, and perform the rites of the ceremony of tin-kettling, without which no marriage in New Zealand is complete. <i>(Mar 26, 1912,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,5</i>)

>  That invigorating and intellectual sport, the tin-kettling of newly-married couples, has been carried on with such tremendous vim lately that several of the kerosine-tin brigade had faced the magistrate. <i>(Oct 22, 1904,NZ Observer,17</i>)

>  The practice of "tinkettling" seems to be carried to extremes in the Wairarapa, judging from the following letter to the Masterton Star:- "A respectable party, as you are aware, has lately joined the bonds of matrimony, and retired to their private residence, some little distance from the town , to spend their honeymoon ..." <i>(Jan 4, 1882,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  To the individual unused to attending such functions, the time-honoured custom of "tin-kettling" a newly-married couple is something dreadful, and must be avoided at all costs. <i>(May 5, 1917,Wairarapa Daily Times,4</i>)

>  Amongst the local news will be found reports in the Magistrate's Court of the Arahura dog case and the Woodstock tinkettling case. <i>(Oct 1, 1891,West Coast Times,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> TIN-KETTLE, TIN-PAN [kettle ?= cauldro, boiler, billy] OED(S <i>tin-kettle</i>: to serenade roughly or opprobriously ... by beating a tin-kettle, [1898 sc an inn-keeper who had beaten his wife; 1900 Lawson diggers for newlyweds; OEDS 1881 Bathgate] also, to (cause swarming bees) to settle [1900 H. Lawson]; also<i> tin pan</i> 1885 English Dialect Dictionary Tin 1 (7) tin-kettle</i> to punish a notorious offender by means of `rough music', i.e., the beating of tin kettles, etc. Hmp; also (10) <i>Tin-pan</i> s.Not. -->SHIVAREE/CHARIVARI cf OED(S<i> charivari </i>the tin-kettling of incongruous marriages. W3 [=Third Webst. Int]<i>Brit</i>. a mock serenade; esp.<i> shivaree </i> cf. Mencken Amer. Lang. Supp 1/232-3 <i>shivaree </i>`... a rowdy serenade of a newly-married couple ...' for a list of US (near-)synonyms and discussion W3 <i>shivaree</i> [f F charivari] A noisy mock serenade to a newly married couple who are expected to furnish refreshments to silence the serenaders. [Had to make new entry to alter verbal noun to noun and was unable to copy in full discussion above (see dataset for remainder) TD]

### tin-kettling party
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARKS</b> There were about fifty boys in the tin-kettling party; ... <i>(1934,Memories (Mainly Merry),18</i>)

### tin-opener wife
 
 <i>phr.</i> often elaborated with AND FRYING-PAN, of one who (it is believed) cannot or will not cook `decent' food for her family [ca1935 Havelock]

### tin-tippy
 
 <i>n.</i> [[New Plymouth schoolboys', early 1920's: a tin canoe made of bent corrugated iron fastened at both ends and made watertight with tar.]]

>  <b>GLOVER</b> We discovered that once a tin-tippy was capsized or rammed it was difficult to get it up again. <i>(1981,Hot Water Sailor,29,1962 ed.</i>)

### tina
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of rape, a forage crop. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Tina swede - The only swede resistant to both club root and dry rot. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,72</i>)

### tinana
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> What I'm trying to say ... is that the conflict was not only over the land, the tinana, but also over the spirit, the wairua. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,69</i>)

### tine harrows
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>EVANS</b> Up to the late 70's tine harrows were popular for preparing land for sowing, but during the 1870's the first Disc Harrows were imported from America. <i>(1956,History of Farm Implements,12</i>)

>  One of my earliest impressions of dairyfarming was the sight of a farmer, complete with sou'-wester oilskin and gumboots, following two horses and a set of tyne harrows around a hillside in the pouring rain on a Saturday afternoon. <i>(Sep 30, 1959,NZ Dairy Exporter,31</i>) [NOTES: (nb: date provided to database was 31/9/1959)]

### tinhorn gambler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Tinhorn gambler</i>. A petty gambler: Canadian since ca. 1912, Australian since ca. 1920, New Zealand since ca. 1922. Adopted from U.S.A.: <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1203</i>)

### tinker grass
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [grass](../G#grass))

### tinker up
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Kaikora calved ... [17] Wintry weather, tried to bail up Kaikora but failed. tinkered up hut Mr Newton still there. <i>(Mar 16, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

### tinker-tailor
 
 <i>n.</i> tinker grass

>  <b>FRAME</b> The cutty grass and the tinkertailor were brushing against her black stockings <i>(1963,Reservoir, The,139</i>) [NOTES: 1966 edition]

### tinned
 
 <i>adj.</i> preferred in NZ to CANNED until about WW2; thence <i>canned (can)</i> became more common in collocations as <i>tinned (canned) food,</i> etc. sc canned (Davin an old-fashioned writer) see also <i>can, canned </i> [ca1935 Havelock general store tinned peaches, salmon, etc. usual; canned seldom or never heard]

>  The Western Packing and Canning Company during 1899 slaughtered 43,000 sheep, averaging 54lb. each; 1,000 cattle, averaging 550lb.; with an output of 9,000 cases of tinned meats, and 640 casks of tallow. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,10</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> There liners and tramp steamers ... tied up at wooden wharves, pouring in machinery ...whisky and tinned salmon ... <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,44</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> We decided to pack enough tinned stores and go ... up the valley a considerable distance. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,128</i>)

>  <b>WINKS</b> ... seldom purchased by Americans in New Zealand, are: clothing, shoes, tinned (we call them `canned') goods, ... <i>(1954,These New Zealanders,68,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>AITKEN</b> Midday passed in this way; we made a meal of bread, tinned Maconochie rations and pork-and-beans, which for attack fare ... was wonderful to have. <i>(1963,Gallipoli to the Somme,160</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> So Jack scratched round in the kitchen and before long I [120] had my entrenching tools into a fine binder of bully fritters and fried tinned potatoes, ... <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,119</i>)

>  Tinned rabbits were fetching more per pound than either tinned or frozen mutton. <i>(Jun 12, 1890,Otago Witness,7</i>)

### tinned cow
 
 <i>n.</i> tinned condensed milk (cf.tin cat, tin dog; armoured cow)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> `Have some tinned cow?' He ... explained that `tinned cow' was a "bushism' for condensed milk. <i>(1916,Wowser, The,90</i>)

### tinned dog
 
 <i>n.</i> bully beef or other tinned meat [aka tin dog]

>  <b>FOSTON</b> The living ... consisted chiefly in some camps [for labourers on the Main Trunk construction] ... of `tin dog' (meat) or `tin cat' (fish). <i>(1921,At The Front,116</i>)

>  He [soldier on Gallipoli] loathed plum-and-apple jam, tinned dog and granite-like biscuit ... <i>(Apr 25, 1918,Quick March,3</i>)

>  Water was always scarce, and food consisted of unappetizing "tinned dog." <i>(Nov 12, 1930,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  Tinned dog, methinks I smell it now, and after having a few British beers to wash the dog down we were rushing along at the rate of 15 miles per hour, arriving at Beverly at 5 am, where we had a good wash in brackish water. <i>(Feb 19, 1895,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  And there is no need to eat 'tinned dog,' as on some of the West Australian gold fields. <i>(Sep 29, 1911,Grey River Argus,7</i>)

>  The dishes bore South African names, but no "damper" or "tinned dog" outraged the palates of these wealthy diners, for the menu was Parisian, and the food was washed down with champagne at GBP1 a bottle, hock at 30s a bottle, and port at 25s, the dinner concluding with costly cigars and fifty-year-old brandy at 3s the liqueur glass. <i>(Apr 25, 1906,Wanganui Herald,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> canned meat [aka tinned dog]

### tinned soldier
 
 <i>n.</i> a tankman, tankie

>  <b>BRUNO</b> Gunboat Nelson, a tinned soldier of the Tank Brigade. <i>(1944,Desert Daze,24</i>)

### tinnie shop
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) (See [tinny house](../T#tinny-house))

>  [He] said he knew a tinnie shop - a house where cannabis is sold in tinfoil packages - in Newtown. <i>(Feb 3, 2001,Dominion,11</i>)

### tinny
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (informal) lucky [1953 18 M A40 Thames DHS 1 TINNY -->1960 16C F B5 Wellington Girls C. 32 TINNY 1984 16-17 F E10 Pakuranga Coll. 32 TINNY [lucky] [M6 F3] -->1984 14 F E125 Wgton Girls C. 32 TINNY -->1984 14 F E127 Wgton Girls C. 32 TINNY [5] ca1935 Havelock `Tinny eh?' was a common remark among children after someone had made a good or lucky shot at marbles.]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Flukes? Destiny? Fate? Or just : How Tinny Can You Be? <i>(1990,So They Began,82</i>)

>  Soon there'll be something worse than reading about some tinny person who's just won Lotto. <i>(Apr 3, 1988,Sunday News,11</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tinny</i> lucky, possibly by association with English `tinny', rich; eg `The bail just hit his racket abd dobbed over the net. How tinny can you get!' NZA. -not very impressive; from tin being regarded as as inferior metal to iron or steel; eg `I tell you that horse is tinny, it'll never amount to anything.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,113</i>)

>  Clark rejects Shipley's view that Labour was tinny to inherit a growing economy. <i>(Mar 18, 2000,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> The truck's in the ditch. We were tinny, though. I no sooner got little Joe out ... but a bloke in an Oakland van came along, brought us right in here. <i>(1981,Hot & Copper Sky,49</i>)

>  <b>BEATON</b> DI: So, what about you? GINNY: Nothin' ... DI: How come? GINNY: They're tryin' to keep it quiet ... DI: You're a tinny bitch. <i>Ibid.</i> 110 TINNY (adj) lucky <i>(1984,Outside In,43</i>)

>  <i>tinny NZ & Austral. sl. </i>lucky. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,806</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1228, sense 5]]

>  <i>tinny NZ & Austral. sl. </i>lucky. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,806</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1228, sense 5]]

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> `I won two houseys. Ten quid. ... Every Friday night regular as Sunday for two years and this is the first time I've got it. <i>And</i> got it straight again after. Tinny, eh?' <i>(1960,Tangahano,151</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... <i>tinny</i>, lucky; <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> When I said `Hooray' he called me back and gave me a few francs; I reckon I was very tinny. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Tinny</i>. 2. ? hence, lucky: Australia and New Zealand: C. 20. Occ.<i> tin-arsed</i>. Cf <i> protected. </i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,888</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> No slouch when it comes to a bit of knuckle - The tinniest player that ever wore the jersey - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,164</i>)

>  Only 100 people a day get to walk along the 2000-year-old cemetery which is the third level under the basilica so we were fairly tinny. <i>(Dec 14, 2003,NZ Catholic,8</i>)

>  There's nothing tinny about two former Wellingtonians behind the consortium that bought Ford's alloy wheel plant in Manukau this week. <i>(Jun 27, 2001,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  Remarks are heard on the "tinny" luck of otherwise of the players while the "stiffs" bemoan their luck. <i>(Jun 7, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/45 : 205</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> (informal) unsubstantial, jerry-built, badly-made (though often, superficially, appearing well-made) (cf. <i>Tin Lizzie, tinpot</i>) [ca1935 e.g. a tinny piece of work, of a job badly done]

>  The shaving cabinets you buy always seem a bit tinny to me. <i>(Apr 12, 1951,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 1951 Apr 12 2YA Wellington Women's Session]

### tinny house/tinnie house
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) retail outlet for tinnies [Chris Fowlie (6/10/1999) says these are usually rented by gangs (so they can't be seized under the Proceeds of Crimes Act), are run by teen-agers (they can't be arrested) and are often open for business 24 hours a day with sales often taking place through a hole in a wall or window - the equivalent of the US "crack house".]

>  A truly fishy "tinny house" has been busted in Foxton. It is alleged that for about three years a man has been selling black-market flounder from his house in an operation the Fisheries Ministry likened to a tinny house. <i>(Jun 27, 2003,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

>  [headline] Tinny house man ordered to sell car <i>(Apr 28, 2006,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  [Hdg] Tinny house worker jailed <i>(Sep 29, 2001,Dominion,11</i>)

>  There are not a lot of tinny houses. The involvement of people here would be as growers and brokers. <i>(Jan 23, 1999,Dominion,19</i>)

>  An 18-year-old found helping to run a tinnie house in Newtown was given a suspended jail term by a Wellington District Court judge yesterday. <i>(May 29, 1999,Evening Post,22</i>)

>  The use of rented properties as "tinny houses" - drug outlets - has increased since the confiscations started. <i>(Jun 6, 1999,Sunday Star Times,A12</i>)

>  It means that if I go to a "tinny house" that is a gang residence and I buy my drugs there, and I am attacked by someone unlawfully on those premises, a much greater penalty will apply to the person whom commits that offence. <i>(1999,Weekly Hansard,77 : 17615</i>)

>  Another man is expected to face similar charges while a number of other people have been charged with possessing cannabis, allegedly bought from a "tinnie house". <i>(Nov 10, 1998,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Armed undercover officer raided tinny houses in the suburbs of Manurewa and Otara and then posed as drug dealers peddling tinnies - foils of cannabis. Tinny houses are set up specifically to sell cannabis to the public. <i>(Apr 17, 1999,Dominion,3</i>)

>  How the thugs' merry peals of laughter must have echoed through the tinny houses and cell blocks. <i>(Dec 16, 1999,Dominion,10</i>)

>  The traditional gang-run tinnie house is now the tinnie and speed house. <i>(Feb 14, 2001,Capital Times,6</i>)

>  The traditional gang-run tinnie house is now the tinnie and speed house. <i>(Apr 2001,Elements,9 : 1</i>)

>  She went looking for a tinnie house, got caught up in the scene. <i>(Oct 9, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),93</i>)

>  They know old people who frequent tinnie houses to access the illegal drug they say assists with everything from epilepsy, muscle spasms, stress, insomnia, headaches, occupational overuse syndrome pain, anxiety, anorexia, cancer, depression, alcohol withdrawal, violent temper control and HIV and Aids-related symptoms. <i>(Jun 20, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

>  A certain amount of P is distributed by the gangs who manufacture it through the well-established, nation-wide tinny-house network. <i>(Apr 5, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  A Waitara pre-school worker denied she was at a tinnie house when cannabis bullets were sold to an undercover cop last year, the New Plymouth District Court heard yesterday... Every adult at the address knew it was a tinnie house, Pue said. <i>(Jun 15, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,3</i>)

>  Justice Ellis called [him] the shopkeeper of a long-time tinnie house operation in Newtown. <i>(Aug 12, 2000,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  If we destroy the power of the illegal market by allowing adults to grow their own, there won't be the number of unscrupulous tinny houses for college kids to buy from. When you go to a tinny house, nobody asks you for ID. <i>(Jul 8, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

### tinny/tinnie
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a small package of cannabis wrapped in tinfoil as sold by drug-vendors. [Worik Stanton (worik@noggon.com)writes that his wife commented "we called them tinnies in Palmerston [North] but when I got to Auckland I found they were called foils."]

>  McFarlane handed over eight tinnies, which he had concealed down his trousers. A ninth was behind his ear. He had been asked to help out with sales and "what was in it for him was one tinny". <i>(Sep 29, 2001,Dominion,11</i>)

>  [Hdg] Tinnie salesman gets year in jail. The salesman at a Newtown tinnie house caught with 52 cannabis tinnies behind the architrave of a door was jailed for one year. <i>(Aug 13, 1999,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  Tinnies of cannabis - about enough of the drug for three cigarettes, wrapped in foil, are sold at such houses. <i>(Nov 10, 1998,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Armed undercover officer raided tinny houses in the suburbs of Manurewa and Otara and then posed as drug dealers peddling tinnies - foils of cannabis. Tinny houses are set up specifically to sell cannabis to the public. <i>(Apr 17, 1999,Dominion,3</i>)

>  A tinny is usually 1-2g of cannabis wrapped in tinfoil. <i>(Jan 28, 2000,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  The judge said the possession of 18 cannabis "tinnies" for sale was too serious a crime for home detention to be considered. <i>(Apr 20, 2000,Dominion,7</i>)

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> I remember a spring day smoking tinnies among the marram dunes at New Brighton when he was shat on twice by seagulls... <i>(2005,Watch of Gryphons,282</i>) [NOTES: Also in Listener 3.4.2004, p.46]

>  ...the mental picture of besuited Rotarians proffering tinnies on street corners had me distracted for a moment. <i>(Mar 29, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

>  Until two years ago the minimum amount a week was five tinnys. One tinny equals a gram of three joints for $20. <i>(Jul 27, 2000,Contact (Wgtn),7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a can of beer

>  "Would you like a beer before you go? I've got some tinnies in the fridge," said my London friend. <i>(Aug 6, 2003,Capital Times,19</i>)

>  JP II and JT I would be doing a damned sight more good it they told the tinnie-and-telly brigade to "return to their paternal vocation". <i>(Aug 8, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

>  When they come back, I'm gonna have to take them to a camp, run a barbecue, have a few tinnies, talk about where we stand as men, what we're supposed to do ... and when those French come back next time, by God they'll be surprised. <i>(Nov 17, 1999,Evening Post,26</i>)

>  A couple of weekends before Norm had invited some of his mates up to his Brooklyn home to watch a couple of videos, have a few tinnies and generally catch ahead of the new season. <i>(Oct 21, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

>  In "Sione-rella", Mr Pepelo demands to know how his "loser sons" manage to track him down yet again as he watches daytime TV sucking on a tinny in their own lounge. <i>(Nov 7, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C4</i>)

>  Hunters in the Orongorongo Ranges near Wellington down tinnies in the huts at nights and talk darkly of the real reason so much 1080 is dropped. <i>(May 19, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

>  Myself, who repaired to the relative sanctuary of a darkened laundry with a tinny and a transistor radio. <i>(Aug 17, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

>  After partying late into the night, they take off back home leaving a litter of tinnies and food scraps to be cleaned up by decent trampers who have just had their backcountry experience ruined. <i>(Oct 2000,NZ Wilderness,8</i>)

>  If patriotism is the last refuge of the scoundrel, at least he can have a few tinnies with his mates and sing along to such great ditties as "The Silver Cap Says It All" and "My Kind of Country, My Kind of Beer." <i>(Feb 5, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a small metal boat [Used also Aust.]

>  More than 30 years ago Parkercraft introduced the first aluminium dinghy to New Zealand boaties. The ubiquitous "tinnies" have since come of age. <i>(Sep 4, 1993,Evening Post,38</i>)

>  Just before lunch.. there is a moment of drama on an aluminium dinghy, lovingly called a "tinnie" or "frying pan" by its owner, local fisherman Pete Pethybridge. <i>(Jul 5, 1999,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  There was one man in a little tinnie in the middle of the Tasman being pulled around by a marlin. <i>(Mar 10, 2004,Otago Daily Times,6</i>)

>  He fished with a brother in a small "tinny" for a few years, but bought his first boat about seven years ago. <i>(Jan 21, 2002,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  The aluminium dinghy makes slow headway against the tidal flow... We beach the "tinnie" on the mudflats of Motuotu Island and make our way through the mangoves taking hold along the shoreline. <i>(May 2002,NZ Geographic,57/84</i>)

>  Kai moana is our heritage and, now that we have wine and supermarkets, we also have mussel farms and savvie, so that you don't even have to own a 12-foot tinnie to indulge. <i>(Oct 30, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

>  The boat, a very basic three metre "tinnie" gets hitched on the back of the wagon, rods strapped in the hull. <i>(Mar 2003,North & South,38</i>)

>  Quietly paddling the Devonport-Takapuna coastline this summer I was nearly run over by an otherwise sane-looking Dad in a tinny pulling his child on an inflatable "biscuit". Only trouble was he was driving fast forward while facing backwards, waving at his child. <i>(Mar 2004,North & South,8</i>)

>  Instead, here, you can get out in a little tinnie in the inner harbour and you're set. <i>(Feb 22, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),4</i>)

>  Another popular occupation was building 'tinnies'. Canoes made from old corrugated iron, pointed to a bow at one end and with a wooden stern nailed in at the other... Before the days of television and 'spacies' this was one of the ways Devonport kids amused themselves, scooting in their trusty tinnies in among Ngataringa Bay's scrubby mangroves. <i>(Sep 1991,Metro,30</i>)

>  New Zealanders are big on fishing: flinging a line off a wharf, from the rocks or over the side of a tinny is a 'regular pastime' for over 400,000 kiwis. <i>(Jul 2004,AA Directions,Spring/21</i>)

>  Whether you have a tinny that barely beats the tide, or a monster which makes your accountant weep, you need a place to either park it or ramp it. <i>(Aug 26, 2005,Dominion Post,B9</i>)

### tino pai
 
 <i>phr.</i> very good [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Tino pai story writer <i>(Oct 20, 2005,Wellingtonian,News 5</i>) [NOTES: headline]

### tino rangatiratanga
 
 <i>n.</i> (Politics) full (Maori) sovereignty or self-determination. [ORIGIN: Maori, from the Maori text of the Treaty of Waitangi (tino indicating a superlative degree, RANGATIRATANGA)]

>  I guess a lot of Kiwis associate the words tino rangatiratanga with protest, aggression and conflict. <i>(Jun 4, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  It is very seductive to say to aggrieved Maori that we must form a political party and go our own way and scream tino rangatiratanga, but, frankly, it isn't a goer. <i>(Oct 21, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  "I was a chain-smoker many years ago. I saw what it was doing for Maori health, for my whanau, so I said `that's it'. Hurrah for tino rangatiratanga." <i>(Apr 20, 2000,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Maori themselves have to put tino rangatiratanga into personal practice and do it for themselves. <i>(Feb 26, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Tino rangatiratanga (chieftainship over land, villages and property was retained by Maori tribal leaders. <i>(Mar 29, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  You can still enjoy our tikanga [customs], you can still have all the drama from the marae, your wives can still practise tino rangatiratanga [being the boss] every day ... <i>(Sep 8, 2004,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

>  <b>Patricia Grace</b> We need politicisation and decolonisation if we're going to claim tino rangatiratanga, otherwise nothing's gunna change, gunna keep on being bad statistics... <i>(2001,Dogside Story,146</i>)

>  .. we are proud to bear the tino rangatiratanga flag ... <i>(Jul 19, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,2</i>)

>  In the Otararua hapu offices on McLean St, below the tino rangatiratanga flags, he explained his comment. <i>(May 20, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

### tino reka tenei kai
 
 <i>phr.</i> the food was delicious [ORIGIN: Maori, code switching not NZE]

>  More of an event than a meal out .. plus tino reka tenei kai (the food was delicious). <i>(Aug 2005,Capital Times,17-23 Aug / 20</i>)

### tino tika
 
 <i>phr.</i> quite correct

>  It's all tino tika, quite correct. <i>(Dec 1912,NZ Free Lance,Xmas Annual : 35</i>)

### tinplate
 
 <i>n.</i> sand flounder

>  Sand Flounder New Zealand Flounder, Patiki, Three corner, Tinplate <i>Rhombosolea plebeia. </i> <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/197</i>) [NOTES: Waite]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> NEW ZEALAND SAND-FLOUNDER (PATIKI) <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i> (Richardson) ... The common Sand-flounder is known from one end of New Zealand to the other, sometimes by other names in different localities. In Auckland it is known as the Dab.../ Some refer to it as Tinplate or Three Corner; the latter name is derived from its fancied trangular shape but why Tinplate I am at a loss to know. To other than fishermen and those intimately connected with the fishing industry it is just a Flounder. Some say it is Tinplate because when cooked it fills a plate. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,191,2nd edn ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SAND FLOUNDER ... <i>Other names: Rhombosolea plebeia</i>; diamond, tinplate, dab, square; patiki (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,216</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SAND FLOUNDER [<i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i>] ... Other names are dab, diamond, tinplate, and square. The Maori name patiki, originally generic, is now loosely used for most flounders. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,32</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>FLOUNDER</i> (four species) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i> (ii)<i> Rhombosolea retiaria</i> (iii) <i>Rhombosolea tapirina </i>(iv) <i>Rhombosolea leporina</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) Flounder (ii) Freshwater Flounder (iii) Flounder (iv) Yellowbelly Flounder. (c) Other common names: (i) Square, Dab, Plaice, Diamond or Diamond-back, Tin-plate, Three Corners. (ii) Blacks, Black Flounder (iii) Greenback [25 `Not common but occasionally taken in southern waters.'] (iv) Yellow Flounder. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,24</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> FLOUNDER SAND ... Other names are `dab', tinplate, square flounder. The Maori name `patiki' is used for all flounder except the sole which is the `patiki rori'. <i>Ibid.</i> 200 TINPLATE <i>see</i> Flounder, sand <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,71</i>)

>  sand flounder .. [scientific name] <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i> .. [other name(s)] dab, diamond back, square, tin-plate, three corners <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### tinplate flounder
 
 <i>n.</i> = sand flounder

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] flounder, tinplate 2284 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### tinpot
 
 <i>adj.</i> (informal) small, insignificant [not exclusively a New Zealand term: OED from 1838]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I'm a cosmopolitan now. I don't stick up for any tin-pot place any more. Ibid. 167 I've never been in the tin-pot place. Never heard of it. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,75</i>)

>  <b>McGill</b> He told Rogers the newspaperman wanted to know if he was interested in a story on a gold strike in some tinpot dump up the coast... <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,163</i>)

>  He added that he and the Transvaal representatives were strongly opposed to wretched tin pot navies, and hoped that in future the British naval programme would be discussed in the Colonial Parliament beforehand. <i>(Oct 30, 1909,Wanganui Herald,5</i>)

>  If they would join one or other, and exercise their vote and influence, I think the desired result would be obtained - that is one respectable association instead of two tinpot affairs, as all the best stock in the Waikato would not make one good show. <i>(Jan 22, 1874,Waikato Times,2</i>)

>  Does he think to impose upon Rororua by his tinpot philanthropy? <i>(May 7, 1887,Observer (Auckland),10</i>)

>  As to tin pot Napier, he's nothing. <i>(Jul 8, 1899,Observer (Auckland),17</i>)

>  Its [sic] a shame that four tinpot mines should be able to disturb the coal industry of N.Z. <i>(Jul 3, 1920,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  It would, he said, be "fatal for New Zealand with only one million of population to start to tinker with a tinpot local navy." <i>(Oct 4, 1912,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> Erin Carter born in March 1932, brought up on his father's farm in Northland, near a tin pot village where there's only the store with the red benzine bowsers in front of it. <i>(1986,Address to a King,59</i>)

### tins
 
 <i>n.</i> cake tins

>  Everyone kept their cake tins full, and visitors would be offered at least a cup of tea and buttered date loaf on arrival. <i>(Jul 17, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  There is morning and afteroon tea as well as three meals a day, and the biscuit tins are always full. <i>(Mar 13, 2004,Dominion Post,E12</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> I don ' t know how she coped. I never knew of her ever having been caught with empty cake tins.... <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,79</i>)

>  These were the staple of her everyday cooking - the delicacies always to be found in the "tins" when we came home from school. <i>(Jan 12, 1993,Dominion,6</i>)

>  <b>KEITH</b> 'Keeping the tins full' was a housewifely virtue for which school girls were trained at an early age. <i>(1991,Lovely Day Tomorrow, A,42</i>)

>  <b>DAISH</b> Not long ago it would have been embarrassing, if not shameful, to be caught with your tins empty. <i>(1989,Good Food,94</i>)

### Tiny
 
 <i>n.</i> familiar NZ Divisional nickname of General Freyberg

>  <b>HAYDON</b> We all thought the world of `Tiny'. He called us his boys. <i>(1941,NZ Soldiers in England,17</i>)

>  <b>SINGLETON-GATES</b> To the men who have followed him battle through two desperate wars he is "Tiny", as he was in New Zealand forty-nine years ago. <i>(1968,General Lord Freyberg,9</i>)

### tio
 
 <i>n.</i> rock-oyster

>  Whilst it was being prepared I strayed along the beach knocking off rock oysters (native name<i> tio</i>). <i>(1940,Establishment of New Plymouth Settlement,119</i>) [NOTES: c1846 Weekes My Island in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 119]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Notable omissions from this list [of food sources compiled by Maori hands c1880] are the kareko (edible sea-weed), tio-kohatu (rock oysters), tio-pati (oysters), and various kinds of shellfish. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>)

### tiora
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> POGNORNIS CINCTA (STITCH-BIRD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Hihi, Tihe, Kotihe, Kotihewera, Tiora, and Tiheora; male and female are someties distinguished as Hihi-paka and Hihi-matakiore or Tihe-kiore. <i>Ibid.</i> 103fn [Stitch-birds] utter that peculiar whistle from which the natives have named them <i>Tiora. </i> <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/101</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> In another place I have already given an account of my search after one of the rarest of New Zealand birds, the ti-ora <i>(Pogonornis cincta). </i> <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,227,1933 ed.</i>)

### Tip
 
 <i>n.</i> chiefly pl., (the) Tips, Tipperary boys; Irish rowdies; larrikins

>  <b>THATCHER</b> To rescue him this rowdie Tip unto his mates he hollered ... . <i>(1862,Canterbury Songster,19</i>)

>  <b>GOLDIE</b> Some of these distant and retired mountain gorges are often the scene of many a wild encounter between the Tipperary or Tips as they are generally termed and the Galway Boys. <i>(Feb 28, 1863,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Feb 28 J. Goldie Third Diary in 1947 Beattie Pioneers Explore Otago 149]

>  <b>MONEY</b> <i>En passant,</i> no class of men ... are less generally known and appreciated than the digger. In saying this, I do not for one moment refer to those curses of a gold-field, the low Irish `tips,' for a more cowardly, ruffianly, or brutal character I have never met with than that lively specimen from the green isle, who seems to flourish with rank luxuriance in the neighbourhood of gold. He is the man who will, as long as he has a mob of his mates to back him, smash up a store, jump a claim, rob a church, ... with equal zest. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,73</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> In those days the diggings were infested by gangs of rowdies, who (whether rightly or wrongly I know not) were designated by the generic name of `Tips'. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,Bk I ch v 19</i>)

### tip
 
 <i>n.</i> a tipping pen q.v.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The tip, a small pen capable of holding ten or twelve sheep, was situated a foot or so above and partly overhanging the dip. Constructed on a large axle ... with the aid of a lever it could be tipped and the sheep sent slithering into the dip below. <i>(1949,High Country Days,102</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a rubbish dump; THE TIP, the usual term for a town or city rubbish dump (most NZ dumps are worked on a tip and fill (often the Bradford tip) system) [ca1900 at least take one's rubbish to the tip is a common expression.]

>  Any increase in tip fees is usually followed by an increase in illegal dumping. <i>(Sep 9, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>tip</i>: (A) The tip is the GARBAGE DUMP. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,104</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Unshorn hoggets, having more `tip' to their wool, are particularly prone to entanglement;... <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,29</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to turn sheep on their backs for inspection or shearing. (See [ram-tipping](../R#ram-tipping))

>  The rams have been tipped, the dog whistles blown, and the caps have long since flown from the tops of giant brown bottles of beer. A bunch of hard-case southern sheilas battling it out for the title of the southern man's Perfect Woman - tipping rams, digging in fence posts, reversing farm bikes and trailers of hay. The 10 finalists followed up on the Sunday with a repeat of those events as well as tipping a ram into the shearing position ... <i>(Nov 2, 2002,Dominion Post,F12</i>)

### tip dip
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a platform sheep dip or plunge dip in which sheep are plunged from above into the dipping fluid.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> This has been fairly good going for a tip dip. <i>(1949,High Country,102</i>)

### tip down
 
 <i>v.</i> rain heavily

>  I surreptitiously unbuttoned my trouser's fly and released a gentle, uncontrolled flow of urine on to the gabardine overcoat of the unsuspecting Scots College boy sitting in front of me. Try doing that at Westpac Stadium, even when it is tipping down. <i>(May 22, 2004,Dominion Post,E6</i>)

### tip grazer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm animal that grazes the top of pasture.

>  Because angoras are tip grazers, they need at least 10 cm of grass in an ideal situation. <i>(2002,Lifestyle Farmer,June/July 25</i>)

### tip it
 
 <i>n.</i> a guessing game

>  [In the Lyttelton Gaol]"Tip it' is something after the style of eggs in the bush. You have one button and some two or three fellows play another two or three. One side guesses which hand of the others contains the button. If they don't guess right it scores one against them. If they do, they take the button and the others guess. <i>(1895,Diary of Jonathan Roberts,32</i>)

### tip tap
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> J.A.Lee in his <i>Children of the Poor</i> (1939) mentions the game of knocking on doors. It was known also as Tip Tap (1885; Waikouaiti). Another widely known trick was Jingle the Penny: `We tied a string to a penny with a hole in it, then hid behind a hedge or on top of a verandah. As a person stooped to pick it up, we would whisk it away' (1895; Ngakawau). <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,90</i>)

### tip-cat
 
 <i>n.</i> 1930-35 West Coast children's game (R. B. McLuskie) A piece of wood with squared sides numbered 1, 2, 3, 4, but tapering to bevelled points at each end, is struck on the bevelled end with a batten so that it spins and lands with one numbered side uppermost. From that number a sequence of ? tasks is given.

>  Tommy Packer was playing tip cat on the pavement, and he hit the cat with his stick, and tipped it, all muddy, straight into the masher's face. <i>(Jun 13, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> Tip-cat's out and hoops are in.' <i>(1983,Springtime in Taranaki,101</i>) [NOTES: 1991 edition]

### tip-dray
 
 <i>v.</i> (See [dray](../D#dray))

### tip-head
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> I got over the hill to Port Lyttleton, and got a job keeping a tip-head on some reclamation work that was going on there. <i>(1889,Rhymes and Rambles,19</i>)

>  Waggons are met at what is known as the tiphead. <i>(1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,4/612</i>)

>  In the Tokatea area the vein is well exposed in the bed of the Whakaroa Creek, just below the `tip-head' of the Harbour View low-level. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 4 (N.S.)/120</i>)

### tipa
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BURTON</b> Definitely a luxury is the queen scallop or <i>tipa</i> of the Maori. ... Scallops occur throughout New Zealand in muddy and sandy flats, the main beds occurring in Tasman Bay and the Marlborough Sounds. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,61</i>)

### tipani
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> She wore a tipani flower in her right ear. <i>(1989,Frigate Bird,42</i>)

### tipare
 
 <i>n.</i> a headband [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>MITCALFE</b> And then ... a whole set of newly-made tipare from Te Hapua. <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey,155</i>)

>  It's a look he seldom varies, except when he looses his hair, puts on tipare (headband), piupiu (flax skirt) and tatua (ceremonial sash) to lead Manutake in haka. <i>(Dec 2004,North & South,64</i>)

>  <b>Grace</b> Another idea was to get some of the young girls to dress in piupiu, bodice and tipare and be available to have photographs taken with tourists. <i>(2001,Dogside Story,252</i>)

### tipau
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> <i>T|ipau, s. A shrub so called. </i> <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,213</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Many other trees abounded in Pamaki [a's-acute], such as the kaikatea [a2-acute], towai, rewa-rewa, totara [a's-acute], puriri, rata [a's-acute], kahika [a2-acute], tipow, tanikaha [a2-acute], rimu, and various kinds of akkas, all of which will be found described in the Appendix (Note 7). <i>Ibid.</i> 2/399 The <i>Tepau</i> is a similar wood to Towai. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/109</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Tipau, although not remarkable yet a stately tree. [<i>Glossary</i> 120 ... <i>Suttonia australis</i> or the larger <i>S. salicina</i>]. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,106,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Suttonia <i>australis</i>, A.Rich. ... Nat. names, `Mapau', `Sipau', and `Matipo', Middle Island, <i> Lyall.</i> `Tipau', north of the Thames river, and `Mapua', south of that river, <i>Colenso. </i>... A small leafy tree, very closely resembling <i>Pittosporum undulatum</i> in appearance. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 172</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tipau, <i>Col[enso]. Myrsine Urvillei</i> and <i>salicina. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  Black Mapau, or Tipau (<i>Pittosporum Colensoi</i>). A shrub tree, very ornamental in contrast with the last [White Mapau], the whole tree very dark coloured... Black Mapau, Tipau (<i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>). <i>Ibid.</i> 39 Tipau. Pittosporum tenuifolium, Banks and Sol. <i>Ibid.</i> 46 Tipau. Myrsine Urvillei, A. D.C. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/33</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth ... (<i>e.g.</i>) the Houhere, <i>Hoheria populnea</i> ...; the Horopito <i>Drimys axillaris</i>.. the Tipau, <i>Myrsine Urvillei</i> and <i>M. salicina</i>.. and, on the sea-coast, ... the Karo, <i>Pittosporum crassifolium</i> and the truly evergreen Ngaio, <i>Myoporum laetum ... </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 1/III/48 The Tipau, or Mapau, (<i>Myrsine australis</i>,) is a small leafy tree ... more plentiful at the north. (<i>Myrsine salicina</i>.) <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/5</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> MAPAU.- (<i>Myrsine australis</i>.) <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,20</i>)

>  [Native name] Tipau .. [Settlers' name] Tipau .. [Family] Myrsineae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>M[yrsine] Urvillei,</i> D.C. ...<i> Mapau; Tipau </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,432</i>)

>  The following is a list of the Auckland contributions towards the Vienna Exhibition .. tepau, myrsine salicina ... <i>(Jan 23, 1873,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

### tipi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> `Ducks and drakes' (<i>tipi</i>) were made [57] by throwing flat stones so as to skip along the surface of the water. <i>(1904,Maori Poetry,56</i>)

### tipo
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [taipo](../T#taipo))

### tiporu
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a variety of farmed native flax, Phormium tenax. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Tiporu was used plaited into plates. It grows 16' high, has very fine fibre. Specimens of this may be seen at Motuiti. <i>(Aug 22, 1903,Farmers' Union Advocate,1</i>)

### tipper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Before preference to unionists was passed the pay [for coal workers in the Wellington Branch of the Waterside Workers' Federation] was per hour for shovellers 1s 6d and for top hands (i.e. winchmen, bullrope-men, trimmers, and tippers), is 3d. <i>(Jun 9, 1911,Maoriland Worker,14/12/3</i>)

### Tipperary kisses
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McCREDIE</b> <i>TIPPERARY KISSES </i>... eggs ... butter, sugar, flour and cornflour ... baking powder, essence ... Drop on cold tray; bake ,,, Press together with raspberry jam. <i>(1915,Bungalow Recipe Book, The</i>)

### Tipperary riot
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It was a good sale. Lots of fellows got drunk and we had a regular Tiperairy riot. They stuck to the rule where ever you see a head, hit it. <i>(Oct 16, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,62</i>)

### tippet
 
 <i>n.</i> part of a fishing line left behind when it snags

>  Tippet: What you leave behind when you snag. Also known as a leader or trace, it's the fine nylon between the hook and the line proper. <i>(Nov 28, 2004,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,12</i>)

### tipping pen
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pen holding six to eight sheep with a floor than can be tipped, spilling the sheep into a pot dip. [also tip]

>  <b>STEVENS</b> The sloping surface which gives a slide in from the side of a bath, the tipping pen in which the floor moves from the horizontal to a steep angle, and the turntable pen, all have the common purpose of getting the sheep into the bath with a minimum of effort and with little risk of injury to the sheep. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,86</i>)

### tippy
 
 <i>adj.</i> wool with a very pointed tip to the staple



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of fleece-wool staple] the projection of thick fibres beyond the fine ones at the tip end.

>  Evenness of fleece is always to be preferred to a fleece which is particularly good on one part of the carcase and weak on another part. Avoid "tippy" wool as much as possible. <i>(Sep 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,238</i>)

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> The hoggets with short-stapled, hairy, very "tippy" fleeces or with poor quality wool, or undesirable wool count, can be marked for culling. <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,151</i>)

>  The end of the staples should be blunt (not tippy). <i>(Dec 1976,NZ Journal of Agriculture,20</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Tippy: Wool with a very pointed tip to the staple. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,60</i>)

>  <b>SQUIRE</b> Tippy - the pointed projection of thicker fibres above the thinner fibres at the tip end of this type of wool staple. Sometimes medullated, weathered, and/or harsh handle. <i>(1991,A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms,39</i>)

>  In a study of `tippy' or unlevel dyeing of loose wool ... he found that ... samples from the back ... of the sheep dye unlevel ... <i>(1938,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,20/250B</i>)

>  Wool with a very pointed tip to the staple. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,60</i>)

>  Tippy: Wool with an extra amount of grease at the tip. <i>(Feb 23, 1920,Ashburton Guardian,2</i>)

### tips
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Here are some [marble] terms from Taranaki around 1880-1890: firsts, lay-up, stakes, tips, sticking-taw, nothings, anythings, fade-it, knuckledown stiff, dubs, finking [sic, ?funking], sips, spanning, placing; and from Dunedin School in 1949: knee high, manyies, tracks, screws, nuts, sleets, kills, forces, dig, dubs, cheats. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,269</i>)

### tipsy cake
 
 <i>n.</i> equivalent to present-day trifle without fruit

>  <b>MURDOCH</b> <i>TIPSY CAKE</i> ... wine over a ... sponge ... sweet almonds, stick them all over the cake; ... ratifias [sic]. Pour ... custard ... brandy <i>(1887,Dainties ... How to Please Our Lords and Masters,63</i>)

>  <i>TIPSY CAKE</i> Soak the cake in brandy ... almonds ... all over ... Pour the custard over and garnish with whipped cream. <i>(1910,Colonial Everyday Cookery,205</i>)

>  <i>TIPSY CAKE</i> ... sponge cake ... sherry ... brandy ... sweet almonds ... rich custard ... Make a small hole in the centre of the cake and pour in and over the cake, sherry and brandy ... Place whipped cream over cake before almonds if desired. <i>(1924,Help the Babies Cookery Book,80</i>)

>  <i>TIPSY CAKE</i> ... Savoy cake in slices ... glass dish ... white wine ... rum over ... served with custard; ... blanched almonds or glace cherries and whipped cream. <i>(1932,St Andrew's Cookery Book,138</i>)

### tipua
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>tipua</i>: goblin. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> ... directly [the Maori] enters the domain of witchcraft, `tipau' (ghouls), `patupaiarahe' (fairy demons), dreams, or tohunga imbecilities, he becomes a fear-haunted, helpless perverter of truth! <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,63</i>)

### tipuna
 
 <i>n.</i> ancestor, grandparent [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>tipuna</b> Ancestor, grandparent.] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 261.] (See [tupuna](../T#tupuna))

>  There were songs for aunties and uncles, tipuna and mokopuna all over the island. <i>(Jul 17, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Tipuna = grandfather/mother <i>(1983,Bone People, The,469 [p] 383</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Tipuna (Tupuna) - are the ancestors and/or the grandparents, and as such the retainers of the language, and the holders of knowledge. Fortunate is the Maori who has had the chance to learn from the experience of their tipuna. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,23</i>)

>  Only his `tipuna' had had the gift of communication, and now he was dead, while Danny's `kuia' thought of nothing but Pakeha mores. <i>(Dec 1986,Landfall,160/492</i>) [NOTES: (Smither)]

>  How will we stand united and with mana, as our tipuna did? <i>(Jul 26, 2005,Wairarapa Midweek,32</i>)

>  He ushers me into his lounge, where the walls are heavy with portraits of his tipuna. <i>(Mar 8, 2003,Dominion Post,F12</i>)

>  .. each hole sports a carved pou that embodies a tipuna of the local tohunga who carved them. <i>(Oct 9, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

### tipuna rights
 
 <i>n.</i> ancestral rights

>  ...our kaumatua were very clear that we draw a distinction between customary rights as defined at common law (which naturally have some affinity with private property rights) and what we call "tipuna rights", which are substantially and substantively different. <i>(Oct 14, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### tipuna whare
 
 <i>n.</i> ancestors' house? [ORIGIN: Maori]

 <i>n.</i> (See [tiraueke](../T#tiraueke))

### tirakara
 
 <i>n.</i> fantail

>  <b>LUSH</b> I have already [?seen]there in numbers the Tui, the Tirakara and the Kingfisher. I have also seen a fine Owl. <i>(Jun 23, 1857,Auckland Journals,189,1971 ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [[check if this is correct???]]

### tirango
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Names - The general name is upokororo ("head full of brains"), derived from the fatness which characterizes it. The small ones are also called rehe, and the large size tirango. <i>(1926,TrNZI,58/638</i>) [NOTES: (Buck)]

### tiraueke
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> <i>33. Creadion carunculatus. G.R.Gray, ...</i>Tieke of the natives of Tasman Bay.<i> Quoy and Gaim.</i> Tiaka or Purourou of the natives. <i>Yate. </i>Tira-oua-ke [e-acute] of the natives.<i> Less. Tierawaki,</i> Cook's Straits. <i>Dieffenbach. ibid</i> 192 - 35. ... Mr Polack also mentions a lark-like bird, of a black colour, under the native name of Purourou, which I do not think belongs to this genus. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/292</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/192]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Another bird, the tierawaki (Jeterus rufisater, Less.), is very common. It is as large as a blackbird, of a jet-black plumage, with red-brown coverlets of the wings and tail. It has two small orange-coloured appendages at the base of the beak. This bird is seen on the lower branches of trees, is very lively, and has a loud penetrating note. It always screams when anything attracts its attention - huei, huei, tierawak, tierawak. It feeds principally on fleshy berries, but also on coleopterous insects. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/147</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> CREADION CARUNCULATUS. (THE SADDLE-BACK.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Tieke, Tiraweke, \{Tirauweke,\} and Purourou. [151] It is naturally a noisy bird, and when excited or alarmed becomes very clamorous, hurrying through the woods with cries of `tiaki-rere', quickly repeated. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,150,{1888 2nd edn 1/18} ed.</i>)

### tireeraka
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Birds seen occasionally [on Three Kings were] kingfishers - kotaretare (<i>Halcyon sanctus</i>); moreporks - koko (<i>Ninox novae-zealandiae</i>); fantails - tireeraka (<i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>); ... <i>(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/154</i>) [NOTES: Fraser]

### Tiri
 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand's first pirate radio ship

>  The actuallest actuality of all is probably the live commentary by a DJ on board the pirate radio ship <i>Tiri</i>, as it ran aground in 1868. <i>(Apr 22, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  Pictures of the <i>Tiri</i>, the boat Hauraki originally broadcast from, clutter the corners along with a <i>Tiri</i> life preserver placed strategically on the wall. <i>(Jul 1, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63</i>)

### tiritea
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of wheat. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Tiritea was bred from the same parental combination that gave rise to Rongotea. Tiritea resembles its sister line, Rongotea, in height and maturity. Tiritea grains are compared with Takahe. <i>(May 1982,NZ Journal of Agriculture,23</i>)

### Tiriti
 
 <i>n.</i> Treaty (of Waitangi) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Mr Chapman also raised the interesting question of whether or not the tiriti was abrogated by the 1845-72 Anglo-Maori wars. If these wars were a case of foreign invasion, then the members of those tribes which resisted did lose the rights conferred by the tiriti. <i>(Aug 9, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  There is, however, nothing in either the Tiriti or in the English translation to suggest that Maori agreed to the hyper-colonisation that occurred during the next 60 years and which saw the settler population jump from around 2000 in 1840 to 1,000,000 in 1900. <i>(Jan 31, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### titi
 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib. uses (and one compound)]

 <i>n.</i> muttonbird [ORIGIN: Maori]

I have not yet been able to make much progress in negotiating, on behalf of the Government, with the Natives for the purchase of Stewart's Island, on account of the absence of many of the Natives interested, who are engaged catching titi (mutton birds), nor shall I be able to do much till they return. <i>(Mar 30, 1864,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Mar 30 N.T.Clarke in 1979 Wilson Titi Heritage 136]
