>  <b>BULLER</b> PUFFINUS BREVICAUDUS \{TENUIROSTRIS\}. BRANDT'S \{BONAPARTE'S\} SHEARWATER. ... <i>Native name\{s\}.</i>- Titi \{Hakoakoa, and Hakuakua\}. <i>Ibid.</i> 317\{1888 2nd edn 2/232\} PUFFINUS TRISTIS \{GRISEUS\}. (SOMBRE SHEARWATER.) ...<i>Native names</i>.- Titi, Hakoakoa, and Totorore: `Mutton-bird' of the colonists. ... when hovering overhead at night [Titi] utter a frequent call-note, like <i>tee-tee-tee</i>, from which the Maori name is derived. [Check that some of this may be 1888 edn only.] <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,315,{1888 2nd edn 2/230} ed.</i>)

>  In that great battle, the two birds, the <i>tiitii (Haladroma urinatrix </i>= petrel), and the<i> taiko</i>, were made prisoners by the river-birds ... [fn] Of this bird, the Taiko, I have formerly heard, particularly at the northern parts of the North Island, but have never seen one. <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/103</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> <i>Native name.</i>- Titi. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/217</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> SOOTY SHEARWATER<i> Puffinus griseus Other names:</i> Muttonbird, Titi. <i>Ibid.</i> 52 COOK'S PETREL <i>Pterodroma cooki Maori name</i>: Titi <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,42</i>)

>  <i>Titi n.</i> New Zealand sea-bird, the diving-petrel [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>titi ... n.</i> N.Z. sea-bird, the diving-petrel [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <i>44. Pterodroma [cookii cookii]<i> (Gray, 1843) <i>Cook's petrel (Titi) </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 27 <i>61. Puffinus griseus </i>(Gmelin, 1789) <i>Sooty Shearwater (Titi) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,24,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>JENKIN</b> There is a Maori saying that when the <i>hakuwai</i> calls, all the <i>titi</i> (muttonbirds) come to the mouth of their burrows and listen. <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,157</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Their next venture, after the muttonbird season was over, ws to run ketes of birds, the titis, up to the Otago heads ... <i>(1970,Stewart Islanders,158</i>)

>  <b>LOCKLEY</b> `Taiko' is the name for any large muttonbird, once abundant on all mountain ranges from the Waitakeres to the Rimutakas of the North Island. In the `good old days', each Maori village had it own taiko colony, which was visited in the autumn to collect the fat `titi' (young birds). <i>(1980,House Above the Sea,188</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> On the southern coasts and in the Bay of Plenty, the greatest delicacy was the muttonbird or sooty shearwater, a type of seabird known to the Maori as<i> titi. </i> <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,9</i>)

>  <i>Mottled Petrel Pterodroma inexpectata</i> (FORSTER, 1844) ... OTHER NAMES:<i> Scaled petrel, Peale's petrel, rainbird, korure, titi. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 80 <i>Cook's Petrel Pterodroma cookii</i> GRAY, 1843) ... OTHER NAME: <i>Titi.</i> ... [81] Many years ago, adult and young Cook's petrels were taken for food by Maoris. <i>Ibid.</i> 96 <i>Sooty Shearwater Puffinus griseus</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... [97] OTHER NAMES: <i>Titi, New Zealand muttonbird.</i> ... Of all the various petrels serving as `muttonbirds' in New Zealand this is the most important. <i>Ibid.</i> 99 <i>Hutton's Shearwater Puffinus huttoni </i>MATHEWS, 1912 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Titi, pakaha, muttonbird</i>. ... Hutton's shearwater breeds only on the South Island. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,76</i>)

>  <i>titi n. NZ </i>= MUTTON-BIRD. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,807</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> It would also be an unhappy thing to cook titi in a hangi. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,13</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Its [the Mangorake's, Taranaki] banks are steep, and from one of them Tangutu dug out a titi: this bird, a Procellaria, or mutton-bird as it is commonly called, has many peculiarities. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/387 Titi - the mutton bird <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/148</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> Eels were still the principal food of the people; but they had imported considerble numbers of titi, or mutton-bird, from the rocks about Stewart's Island. <i>(Jan 10, 1844,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 10 Selwyn Journal in 1851 NZ Part III (Church in the Colonies VIII) 10]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The jibbering of a night-bird (called the titi-porangi) <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/330</i>)

>  <b>TAYL;OR</b> [by Mongurewa river] In the evening there was quite a deafening noise from vast numbers of birds flying about; I found on enquiry it was the titi which is remarkable as being both a sea bird and a night bird as well ... <i>(Dec 1, 1846,Journal,4/145</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> There is also a sort of <i>Puffin</i> or<i> pelicanoides</i>, commonly called the <i>mutton-bird,</i> which the natives take in large quantities by lighting fires on the seaward face of the hills near the coast, into which these birds, called <i>titi</i> by the natives, fly, and are killed. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,165</i>)

>  The petrels - there are nine kinds, and we have names of our own for them, the black burrower, the mutton-bird, the white burrower, the short-billed ti-ti, the long-billed ti-ti, the little storm petrel, and three others that we had no names for - abound on the island.' <i>(Nov 14, 1891,Australasian, The,963, col. I</i>) [NOTES: (`A Lady in the Kermadecs'):]

>  The petrels - there are nine kinds, and we have names of our own for them, the black burrower, the mutton-bird, the white burrower, the short-billed ti-ti, the long-billed ti-ti, the little storm petrel, and three others that we had no names for - abound on the island.' <i>(Nov 14, 1891,Australasian, The,963, col. I</i>) [NOTES: (`A Lady in the Kermadecs'):]

>  <b>COLENSO</b> A peculiar sea-bird, called by the natives <i>tiitii</i>, which often flies irregularly at night, making a noise resembling `tee-tee-tee', rapidly uttered (whence its name), is sometimes taken in this neighbourhood in large numbers. [fn] ... a Petrel ... not identifiable by call only: prob. a mottled petrel (<i>Pterodroma inexpecta</i>). <i>(1894,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1894 Colenso Excursion in Northern Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 23]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Ti-ti, n, </i>Maori name for the sea-bird <i>Pelecanoides urinatrix</i>, Gmel., the Diving-petrel. Spelt also <i>tee-tee. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,471</i>)

>  <i>Ti&#769;.ti</i> (t&emacr.&#769;.t&emacr.), <i>n. 1. (Zoo.l.)</i> The mutton bird (<i>Nectris (?) brevicaudus</i>), so called from its note. 2. (<i>Bot</i>.) See TI. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  <b>SHAND</b> The young of many sea birds ... were used as food [on the Chathams], such as <i>Kuaka </i>(plover), young gulls (<i>Ngoiro</i>), shags (<i>Kuau</i>) ... <i>Ho[o]po</i> (the albatross), <i>Hakoakoa</i> (mutton bird), <i>Taiko</i>[macron] (a smaller-sized mutton bird of slatey blue colour), <i>Titi</i>[i's macron] (a still smaller size) ... <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham Islands, The,6</i>)

>  The tuatara lives in the same burrows as are used by the oii, the titi and the toanui. All three of the petrels are included in the term mutton-birds. <i>(Sep 11, 1922,Quick March,15</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Many of the offshore islands [of Stewart Island] are the breeding-places of the petrel called the mutton-bird (<i>titi</i> or <i>taiko</i>), ... <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 167</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> These are the names used by the southern Maori; in the North Id. Kuaka, Parara and Titi are applied to other birds. Kuaka: <i>Pelicanoides Urinatrix</i>, or Diving Petrel. Black Bill. Wing 4.7 inches. Titi Wainui: <i>Prion Turtur</i> or Dove Petrel. Parara: <i>Prion vittatus</i>, or Broad-billed Petrel. Bill very wide. Wing, 9.5 inches. Titi: <i>Puffinus Griseus</i> or Mutton-Bird. Sooty brown. Bill horn colour. Wing, 12 inches. <i>(1940,Rakiura,205</i>)

>  <b>BAILEY & ROTH</b> I am a gay muttonbirder,/ ... I'm an expert at catching the titi. <i>(1967,Shanties,136</i>) [NOTES: [c1940] 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties 136]

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> We sat down at last, and then it was told me that the <i>titi</i> is a traditional Maori dish, a bird that is known among the Pakeha as mutton bird, because of the richness of its flesh. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,68</i>)

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> We sat down at last, and then it was told me that the <i>titi</i> is a traditional Maori dish, a bird that is known among the Pakeha as mutton bird, because of the richness of its flesh. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,68</i>)

>  <i>ti[i]ti[i],</i> a sea-bird (Puffinus griseus and other spp.), mutton-bird; appear in great numbers and nest in peaty holes on cliffs on unfrequented shores, when great numbers of the young are taken and preserved in their own fat. The tuatara lizard often occupies the same burrow; but the terms of partnership are not known. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>POWELL</b> MUTTON BIRD (<i>Puffinus griseus</i>), Titi or Oi of the Maoris, was and still is in the South an important item in the diet of the Maori people. <i>(1946,Native Animals of NZ,79</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> In the house, big fire of dead tete-a-weka wood going. Two witch's pots (small edition of a whaler's try-pot) and a camp oven swinging above the fire. Titis sizzling in their own kato fat (fat from insides). Piro fat, the fine fat from the intestines, is more precious. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,154</i>) [NOTES: 1952 Apr 20 in 1982 Sansom In the Grip of an Island 154]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> The "titi" or mutton bird, as it is termed by the whalers, is.. a species of puffin. <i>Ibid.</i> 314 TITI, a sort of puffin, called by the whalers mutton-bird. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,225</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Since the spread of rats all over the country the Titi (mutton Bird) has ceased to breed on the summits of inland cliffs and on the rocky bluffs of Lake Taupo to which it regularly resorted of old. <i>(1938,Children of Tane,39</i>)

>  The titi (mutton bird) seeks the land, and flies direct to the one bright object of its desire. <i>(Apr 5, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,16</i>)

>  The taking of Titi seems to the white man like one big picnic for the Maoris. <i>(Mar 1937,NZ Privy Council Cases,23</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Where have all the Titi gone? <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,[8]</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> P. urinatrix Teetee... Procellaria Titi. Ibid. 200 Titi. <i>(1842,Travels in NZ,II/199</i>) [NOTES: Article by Gray]

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> The tables themselves are loaded: hangi food, kaimoana, trout, tuna, titi, puha, karengo. <i>(1992,Marist Messenger,21</i>)

>  As well as the titi, or muttonbirds (sooty shearwaters).. Whenua Hou is home to a variety of petrels, prions and penguins. <i>(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 59/60</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> variant of ti (cabbage tree) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>MONRO</b> The part of the [Canterbury] plain which we crossed ... is uniformly covered with grass ..., mixed with toi-toi and flax in the moister parts, and, in some places, thickly dotted over with the ti-ti. <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 236. Also in 1898 Hocken Contributions 236, 245]

>  Looking up it [Molyneux River] we could trace its course through the large extent of alluvial land, by the thick fringe of ti-ti trees upon its banks, ... producing a most picturesque effect. <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 249]

>  Soft young shoots of the titi or cabbage tree are a taste that one acquires. <i>(Mar 1964,Te Ao Hou,46: 6</i>)

### titi islands
 
 <i>n.</i> muttonbird islands [sometimes Titi Islands]

>  <b>HOWARD</b> ... the Titi Islands following:- Horomamae, Wharepuaitaka, Kaihuka, ... <i>(1940,Rakiura,394</i>) [NOTES: 1864 June 29 Deed of Sale [of Rakiura] in 1940 Howard Rakiura 394]

>  He used to attend the Titi Island meetings, which have always been the cause of bitter whanau quarrelling. <i>(Oct 1998,Mana,No. 24: 68</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> TITI ISLANDS. [heading] ... `... I was about the only old person on the titi islands this year, and do not know whether I will go again.' <i>(1944,Maori Place-Names of Otago,83</i>)

>  There is no difficulty as far as the Chatham Islands or Stewart Island are concerned, although the Census date usually coincides with the mutton-birding season, with the result that a "count" has to be made on the Titi Islands near Stewart Island. <i>(Oct 1, 1935,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  .. with a large settled population, the bottom of the Straight along the coast from Port Pegasus to Rugged Point, and Ruapuke to the Titi Islands, off Patterson's Inlet, will be found to be lined with this popular mollusc. <i>(Jul 13, 1872,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  Most people understand in a general sort of way that the privileges in connection with the mutton bird islands, the Titi Islands, adjoining Stewart Island, are preserved solely for the benefit of the Maori people. <i>(Jan 19, 1910,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

### titi-a-weka
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [teteaweka](../T#teteaweka))

### titi-wainui
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pachyptila turtur, southern fairy prion</i>, a sea bird [also titi wainui]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> We also got four kinds of Petrel in their burrows,- the Mutton Bird, Parara, Titi Wainui, and the Kuaka. <i>(1914,Mutton Birds & Other Birds,16</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> These are the names used by the southern Maori; in the North Id. Kuaka, Parara and Titi are applied to other birds. Kuaka: <i>Pelicanoides Urinatrix</i>, or Diving Petrel. Black Bill. Wing 4.7 inches. Titi Wainui: <i>Prion Turtur</i> or Dove Petrel. Parara: <i>Prion vittatus,</i> or Broad-billed Petrel. Bill very wide. Wing, 9.5 inches. <i>Titi</i>: Puffinus Griseus or <i>Mutton-bird.</i> Sooty brown. Bill horn colour. Wing, 12 inches. <i>(1940,Rakiura,205</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> FAIRY PRION (<i>Pachyptila turtur</i>), Titi Wainui of the Maoris, is the small dove grey petrel which skims the surface of the sea in its energetic quest for food. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,79</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> FAIRY PRION <i>Pachyptila turtur Maori name:</i> Titiwainui (Stewart Island). <i>(1966,Birds of Australia,40</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> There are six species of prions ... of which the likeliest to be seen is the dainty fairy-prion or titi-wainui <i>(P[achyptila] turtur</i> . . ., <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,257</i>)

>  <i>51. Pachyptila turtur </i>(Kuhl, 1820) <i>Fairy Prion (Titi Wainui) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,25,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> With [the titi] were scatterings of the smaller titi-wainui, the Fairy Prions, and a sprinkling of kuaka, the Southern Diving Petrels, which ducked and rose on the outskirts. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,91</i>)

>  <i>Fairy Prion Pachyptila turtur </i>(KUHL, 1820) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Titi-wainui, narrow-billed prion. </i>... Fairy prions patrol the seas in large, loose flocks. ... Fairy prions are one of New Zealand's most abundant petrels ... <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,87</i>)

>  It is known by the vernacular name of fairy dove-petrel and to the Maori as titi-wainui. <i>(Sep 6, 1030,Evening Post,17</i>)

### titiko
 
 <i>n.</i> a univalve shellfish

>  KAIMOANA: Maoris gather a kitful of titiko (a univalve shellfish) from the tidal flats, a traditional right reaffirmed by recent court judgement. <i>(Sep 7, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),A8</i>)

### titimako
 
 <i>n.</i> Williams 425 <i>Ti[i]ti[i]mako ... Anthornis melanura, bell-bird.:</i>

>  <b>BUL.LER</b> ANTHORNIS MELANURA. (BELL-BIRD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Mako, Makomako, Komako, Kokomako, Korimako, Kohimako, Kokorimako, Titimako, and Kopara. Of the above names, Korimako is most generally used by northern and Makomako by southern tribes. The Ngatiawas call this bird Rearea; and the natives of the Bay of Plenty distinguish the male and female as Kokorohimako and Titapu. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,91,{1888 2nd edn 1/85} ed.</i>)

### titiporangi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> FULIGULA NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (NEW-ZEALAND SCAUP.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Papango, Tetepango, Matapouri, Titiporangi, and Raipo; `Black Teal' and `Widgeon' of the Colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,259,{1888 2nd edn 2/273} ed.</i>)

### titipounamu
 
 <i>n.</i> rifleman (bird)

>  <i>titipounamu</i>: rifleman, forest bird. <i>Acanthisitta chloris</i>, bush-wren, rifleman (Williams 425) <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> RIFLEMAN <i>Acanthisitta chloris Other names</i>: Titipounamu, Thumbie (Nelson). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,191</i>)

>  <i>239.b. Acanthisitta chloris chloris</i> (Sparrman, 1787) <i>South Island Rifleman (Titipounamu) </i> <i>(1966,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,63,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Rifleman Acanthisitta chloris</i> (SPARRMAN, 1787) <i>... South Island Rifleman A.c. chloris </i>(SPARRMAN, 1787) <i>North Island Rifleman A.c. granti</i> MATHEWS & IREDALE, 1913. ... OTHER NAME: <i>Titipounamu. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,264</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 13. <i>Acanthisitta longpipes. G.R.Gray, </i>... E[acute] tectee te pomou of the natives of Dusky Bay. <i>Forst. [tectee </i>?misprint for<i> teetee </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/188</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/188]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 18. ACANTHISITTA CHLORIS. Sparrm. Creeper. Titipounamu <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,8</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ACANTHISITTA CHLORIS \{ACANTHIDOSITTA CHLORIS\}. (RIFLEMAN.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Titipounamu, \{Kikimutu,\} Kikirimutu, Pihipihi, Piripiri, Tokepiripiri, and Moutuutu. [(1873) 113] The Rifleman is the smallest of our New-Zealand birds; ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,112,{1888 2nd edn 1/113, 114} ed.</i>)

>  17. <i>Acanthisitta chloris,</i> Sparrm. Wren, Titipounamu, Pi-wau-wau. - Often builds in a small hole in a tree. I know one old konini which is resorted to yearly ... <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,282</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Titipounamu, n. (<i>spelt also </i>Tititipunamu), n. <i>Maori name for the bird Acanthidositta chloris,</i> Sparm., the <i>Rifleman</i> (q.v.). It has many other Maori names. <i>(1898,Austral English,471</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> RIFLEMAN (<i>Acanthisitta chloris</i>). This is a wren and our smallest bird, being only three inches in total length. There are two forms, typical chloris from the South Island and <i>A. chloris granti,</i> the North Island rifleman....The Maori name is Titi-pounamu. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,86</i>)

>  It's a titipounamu, and you often find him in the ti-tree scrub... It is the rifleman, very shy, and a friend of the scrub-cutter. <i>(Feb 5, 1995,NZ Tablet,7</i>)

### titipuke
 
 <i>n.</i> muttonbirds buried underground or deep-frozen with their guts still in them -->ca1974 Chatham Islands (Alf Martin)

### titirangi
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) the indigenous flowering plant, Hebe speciosa

>  Napuka ( <i> Hebe speciosa </i> ), also known as the titirangi shows off its bottle-brush flowers all year round. <i>(Dec 2006,Growing Today,30</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Titirangi (Middle Island), <i>Lyall</i>. Veronica speciosa. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768 ed.</i>)

>  <i>titirangi</i>, a plant (Veronica speciosa), one of a very numerous family. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### titiwai
 
 <i>n.</i> the glow-worm, <i>Archnocampa luminosa</i>

>  The titiwai (stars in wet places) of Waitomo, which means water entering a shaft, have been celebrated since 1887, when they were first explored by local Maori chief Tane Tinorau. The insects feed ravenously as larvae, then briefly emerge as adult fungus gnats to mate, lay eggs and die within three days. <i>(Dec 9, 2003,BLANK ENTRY,A11</i>)

### title
 
 <i>n.</i> an abbreviation of <i>certificate of title</i>, a copy of the entry in the land titles register recording the identity and ownership of a piece of land and any legal encumbrances on it. [Jim Cameron writes : The English equivalent in relation to registered land there is <i>land certificate</i>. The term <i>title deeds</i> is sledom used in NZ. The attached clippings illustrate an aspect of its general use e.g. <i>on separate titles, titles pending</i>.]

>  Some New Zealand land law terms are uncommon or unknown in England. (Conversely the term title deeds is not much used here either by lawyers or laymen. Instead we talk of the title or certificate of title, in the concrete sense of a document which is a copy of the registry entry in the Land Transfer Office. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,1</i>)

### title deeds
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The land transfer system contrasted with the older deeds system, introduced in 1841 as a reformed version of traditional English real property law whereby rights to ownership depended on the existence and validity of a chain of documents. The term title deeds derives from this. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)

### title unit
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The plan was to divide it into two title units and sell it. <i>(Sep 12, 2000,Evening Post,13</i>)

### titmouse
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [great-headed titmouse]

>  <b>POLACK</b> The<i> nirungiru</i> (Parus macrocephalus), or great-headed titmouse, is very common in the country. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/298</i>)

### tito
 
 <i>n.</i> a fib, a lie. [ORIGIN: Maori, ca1900 King Country (J. Morison) ca1935 Havelock in pron. [|ti:tou]]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [The natives] were sure we were telling them tito, `lies'. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,2/43</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> It is a pity [the Maories] do not extend the same condemnation to lying, which is an almost universal failing, and is considered no disgrace whatsoever ... [146] The consequence is, that the natives are generally distrustful and incredulous; and `toe' and `teto', lie and liar, are the words most frequently heard in their conversation, and which they apply to one another and to white men of all ranks without the least hesitation or compunction. <i>(Dec 13, 1847,Sketches in NZ,145</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> `A <i>tito</i> this - a fib, I know; <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/190</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [ca1935 Havelock]

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> ... most natives thought nothing of it, looking upon it as a joke if, finding them out in one, you called them <i>`tito'</i> or liar. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,82</i>)

### titoki
 
 <i>n.</i> a forest tree <i>Alectryon excelsum</i> and its timber; also formerly called New Zealand ash. [[?d] Ed. Fairburn Maharatanga 30 Titoki (this tree sometimes called New Zealand `Ash")]

>  When I saw the stone smash into the glasshouse, images of Sir Robert hiding behind the titoki tree like a miscreant rabbit dodging Elmer Fudd, and groans resounding from the dogged but hapless sharpshooter, leapt up and barked. <i>(Mar 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The hair is often greased with ... an oil pressed from the seeds of the titoki (Aledron [sic] excelsum, belonging to the Sapindaceae). <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/54</i>)

>  <i>(Jul 29, 1844,Report of Select Committee on NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1844 July 29 House of Commons Report of Select Committee on NZ (And)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Amongst the most remarkable Trees is the Teehokee [Titoki] whose berry is of a dark black substance.... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,99</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Alectryon<i> excelsum, </i>DC. ... Nat. name, `Titoki' and `Titongi' of southern parts of North Island. <i>Colenso. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 38</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Titoki, <i>Col[enso]. Alectryon excelsum</i>. Titongi, <i>Col[enso]. Alectryon excelsum. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  The Titoki (<i>Alectryon excelsum</i>), the Akeake (<i>Dodonaea viscosa</i>), the Ngaio (<i>Myoporum laetum</i>), and above all the Rewa Rewa (<i>Knightia excelsa</i>), yield woods, out of which some very beautiful pieces of furniture have been made. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/14</i>) [NOTES: (Monro)]

>  ... the curious red fruit of the Titoki, or Titongi, (<i>Alectryon excelsum</i>); the fruit of the Tutu-papa (<i>Coriaria thymifolia</i>);... and of the Kareao, or Pirita, (<i>Rhipogonum parviflorum</i>). <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  Alectryon excelsum Titoki <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/122</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>DOMETT</b> The youth, with hands beneath his head,/ Against a great titoki's base <i>(1872,Ranolf and Amohia,253</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Alectryon (<i>Titoki</i>) .....<i>O</i>. Coriariae; <i>G </i>Alectryon; <i>S.</i> A. excelsum. A fine large forest tree. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/40 Against a great <i>titoki's </i>[o-acute] base,/ <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/302</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Alectryon excelsum,</i> DC. Titoki.--A beautiful tree with large panicles of reddish flowers trunk 15-20 feet high, and 12-20 inches in diameter. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,131</i>)

>  [Native name] Titoki .. [Settlers' name] Titoki .. [Family] Sapindaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>FEATON</b> ALECTRYON EXCELSUM (<i>D.C.</i>) ... The `TITOKI' or `TITONGI'. Ths very beautiful and valuable tree is indigenous to both Islands, but is most plentiful in the ... North Island.<i>Ibid.</i> 178 Titoki [Maori name] .. Titoki [Settlers' name] .. Alectryon excelsum .. Tree Titongi [Maori name] .. Titoki [Settlers' name] .. Alectryon excelsum .. Tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,97</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> It is sometimes termed `the New Zealand Ash', doubtless on account of its resembling that tree in the shape of its foliage and in the toughness of its wood, but it is most generally known as the `titoki'. <i>(1889,Forest Flora,183</i>)

>  Titoki .. Alectryon excelsum .. Timber strong and very tough; ... bark suitable for tanning <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,15</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Titoki, n</i>. Maori name for the New Zealand tree, <i>Alectryon excelsum</i>, De C., <i>N.O. Sapindaceae</i>. Also called New Zealand Oak and New Zealand Ash. See <i>Alectryon. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,471</i>)

>  <i>Ti&#769;.to-ki </i>(t&emacr.&#769;.t&omacr.-ke.; <i>colloq.</i> t&emacr.-t&omacr.&#769;.ke.), <i>n</i>. [Maori.] <i>(Bot.) A New Zealand sapindaceous tree (Alectryon excelsum)</i>; - called also <i>New Zealand ash. </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  ... titoki or native ash [fn <i>Alectryon excelsum.</i>], and the totara pine [fn <i>Podocarpus totara</i>.] jostle one another in the dense dry bush. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,184</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>A[lectryon] excelsum, Gaertn. ... Titoki. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,103</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Alectryon excelsum [(The Titoki)] </i>... This tree is sometimes called <i>The New Zealand Ash</i>, and its timber is largely used. Maori name, <i>Titoki.</i> perversion of sense of a genuine Maori word. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,225,1940 p. 239 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Oil is employed in beautifying the hair: ... expressed from the seeds of a tree called <i>titoki, ... </i> <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/326</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> 12. Titoki, (<i>name not known.</i>) This tree is also mentioned here, because it bears in great abundance berries from the inner kernel of which the natives extract a very fine oil, samples of which have been pronounced in England as peculiarly suited to the purposes of the watch-maker. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,143</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> The <i>tetoki</i> berry is a fruit resembling a raspberry, but somewhat lighter; .. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),130</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 130]

>  <i>titoki</i>, a tree (Alectryon excelsum), scarlet fruit with shiny black seed at tip, whence the tree (or fruit) is called crayfish-eyes: they fruit yields a scent used with the oil with which chiefs anoint themselves, apparently plentifully once every three years or so, whence the proverb applied to an upstart: "Chief of the titoki year," since anyone can use the scent that year. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  LARGE TREE, NEW ZEALAND ASH. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  `Titoki is the New Zealand ash.' `I always thought it was something else.' [laughter] [see 2. below] <i>(Nov 6, 1952,2ZB</i>) [NOTES: ca1952 Nov 6 2ZB `Taylor's Quiz']

>  <i>A small, handsome New Zealand tree. </i> <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),415</i>)

>  <i>Titoki n.</i> New Zealand tree, kind of ash or oak [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>titoko ... n.</i> N.Z. tree, kind of ash or oak; [Maori] mixed drink, usually lemonade or raspberry essence, and beer. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <i>titoki n</i>. red-flowering NZ tree yielding a tough timber. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,807</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The berry of the titoki tree might be turned to account. The natives extract a very fine oil from it. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,2/317</i>)

>  Upon the hill behind the house is the grove of titoki <i>Alectryon excelsus</i>, the trees from which the garden takes its name. <i>(Apr 1998,North & South,94</i>)

>  <b>HIGGINS in LE PROU</b> We ate bull-a-bulls flour, fuchsia, red pines, tetoke. <i>(1989,Waters Back,17</i>) [NOTES: Citation from 1842]

>  Titoki, better known as New Zealand ash, has poisonous fruit. <i>(Feb 3, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> We ate lots of nikau too, and lawyer berries - they were like raspberries - and miro and titoki berries. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,29</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> There are many natural productions of the country ... One of these is the oil of the <i>Pitoku</i> berry, which is of very fine quality, and much used by the natives in anointing their hair. It is a sweet and pleasantly-tasting oil ... The berry is small, of a brown colour, and very unctuous, with but little taste as a fruit. The tree is wild in every part of New Zealand. <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),43</i>)

>  A surprise find was that the fruit of Syzygium maire or swamp maire had more than 18 times the level of antioxidants while the berries of Alectron excelsis or titoki contained more than 12 times the level. <i>(Feb 28, 2006,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  BOTANICAL DRAWINGS, COLORED. XII. - The Titoki and the Taua in fruit. <i>(May 28, 1853,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,2</i>)

>  In places where they can be established the native karaka is a fine tree, also the koe-koe and titoki - in fact, so are many others. <i>(Apr 20, 1912,Evening Post,12</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shandy or beer and raspberry. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>HUNT</b> Then very often you'd get a Maori ask for a 'titoki' at the pub and that was a stout with a drop of raspberry in it. No one understands what you're talking about if you ask for a titoki in a pub today. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,29</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> titoki: a beer/lemonade/raspberry shandy(gaff) from its similarity to titoki berry juice. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,113</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a beer; a shandy [cf. matai beer cf also used for ?stout and raspberry (?red with a black head, or vice versa)] (See [cf. matai beer](../M#matai-beer))

>  ... this reminiscence reminds me, too, of the variety of bush berries which we children would eat [in Carterton in the 1860s] - the white pine berries, the runi-runi, and ti-toke, these last very much resembling a raspberry, with a black seed on the head of it. <i>(Feb 10, 1921,Quick March,13</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Ti-toki</i>. A mixed drink of beer, lemonade and raspberry: New Zealanders': C. 20. Ex Maori. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,881</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> ... when we call a shandygaff <i>titoki</i> we are giving an authentic native word a meaning it has never had before. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,56</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Titoki</i>. A shandygaff: New Zealand: C. 20. B., 1941. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1204</i>)

>  `Titoki is the New Zealand ash.' `I always thought it was something else.' [laughter] <i>(Nov 2, 1952,2ZB</i>) [NOTES: ca1952 Nov 6 2ZB `Taylor's Quiz']

>  <i>titoko ... n</i>. N.Z. tree, kind of ash or oak; [Maori] mixed drink, usually lemonade or raspberry essence, and beer. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Very often you'd get a Maori ask for a `titoki' at the pub and that was a stout with a drop of raspberry in it. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,29</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>titoki</i> a beer/lemonade/raspberry shandy (gaff), from its similarity to titoki berry juice. <i>[Acland] </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,113</i>) [NOTES: [cf OED]]

### titoki berry
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Around December-January, you might also spot titoki berries on the track. A fleshy scarlet [sic] fruit holds a glossy black seed in a woody capsule. <i>(Jan 5, 2005,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

### titoki fruit borer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> The growing seed is often destroyed by the titoki fruit borer, a caterpillar that feeds only on this species. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,92</i>)

### titongi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [titoki](../T#titoki))

### titri
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Olearia lineata</i>

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> TITRI. - The name given in Otago for <i>Olearia lineata</i>. Not to be confused with tea-tree (manuka) or ti-tree (cabbage-tree palm). <i>(1945,Station Ballads,63</i>) [NOTES: [check 1897]]

### tits on toast
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>HUNT</b> We'd make dumplings, too, fried up in the camp oven. Buggers afloat we called them [c1916] ... And what did we call the poached eggs? A pair of bastards on a raft, and we'd have tits on toast - belly pickled pork on toast. I still use those old names; everyone laughs at me. <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five,29</i>) [NOTES: (Bill Henderson)]

### titted
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of cows] having teats.

>  To identify cows which are treated or three titted paint them on the legs with red tailpaint, stays on a lot longer than spray markers. <i>(Feb 10, 2002,RD1.com,Farm Management</i>)

### titter
 
 <i>n.</i> a young woman, a girl [some NZ specificity, via whalers; in OED from 1819]

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,11</i>)

>  <i>(1953,Landfall,7/3/179</i>)

>  The man who knows when he is well off is the bloke who has spliced a wealthy titter. <i>(Sep 9, 1911,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  Who were those two young titters in white, who do not reside many miles from the police station in Manakau Road, who were playing up such pranks in the moonlight, outside Whitaker's paddock last Sunday evening? <i>(Apr 4, 1891,Observer (Auckland),18</i>)

>  It was my lot to be seated with two titters in front and a seat full behind. <i>(Nov 15, 1890,NZ Observer,9</i>)

### tittie-bottle
 
 <i>n.</i> a baby's feeding bottle

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> Feed'em with a tittie-bottle. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,49</i>)

### tittlebat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The lady [Clara Butt] came to New Zealand, and I followed my invariable plan, and told the truth about her. What the TRIAD had to say (while Mr Tacon and the other tittlebats squealed) went forth. <i>(Jan 10, 1910,Triad, The,3</i>)

### tituriwhatu-pukunui
 
 <i>n.</i> [red breasted plover] (See [also tuturiwhatu-pukunui](../A#also-tuturiwhatu-pukunui))

>  CHARADRIUS OBSCURUS, Gml. Tituriwhatu-pukunui. Red-breasted Plover. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/68</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

### tiutiukata
 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>BULLER</b> TURNAGRA HECTORI. (NORTH ISLAND THRUSH.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Piopio, \{Koropio,\} Korohea, and Tiutiukata. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,135,{1888 2nd edn 1/26} ed.</i>)

### tivaevae
 
 <i>n.</i> [Samoan word now entering NZ vobulary]

>  I made a tivaevae for my son Johann. All the people who loved him would stitch a little bit of their love and affection into the quilt. <i>(Apr 29, 2004,Dominion Post,D2</i>)

>  The most beautiful present, a rare and precious gift indeed, was a red and white tivaevae with a pineapple patttern, which now graces the bed in our spare room in un-tropical Auckland. <i>(Nov 2, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

### tiver
 
 <i>n.</i> potato [NOTE: In view of the meanings and varieties in spelling above, it is not improbable that the word represents the name of one<i> Stivers</i>, who is said to have visited the Bay of Islands before Cook. [See] Lee and Kendall's Grammar p. 107] (See [also taewa](../A#also-taewa),[tawai](../T#tawai))

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> [The old chief] told us he had seen three generations and was in the middle life when the first ship came to New Zealand. The captain's name was Stivers. [fn] <i>Sta[?]ivers</i>, hence another name for the potato, <i>taewa</i>, since he apparently supplied some seed.] <i>(Oct 20, 1819,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,208,1932 ed.</i>)

>  [The chief] expresses grief that his `tivers', potatoes, are all gone. <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/32/92</i>) [NOTES: (John Pierce)]

>  [The natives] boiled some tea and <i>tivers</i> (potatoes). <i>(1843,NZ Journal</i>) [NOTES: (W. Ferguson) 1842 (1843) Letters from Settlers 46]

>  <b>SAM. STEPHENS</b> [A Maori says]<i> iti-iti</i> bit of ground to grow the tawai (potatoes) and the leeks. <i>(1843,Letters & Journals,113</i>)

### tivots
 
 <i>n.</i> new kids [1951 14-16 M 59 Wellington H.S. 25 TIVERPS [?=twerps] -->1951 14-16 M 59 Wellington H.S. 25 TIVOTS]

### tiwai-waka
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tiwakawaka](../T#tiwakawaka))

### tiwaikawaka
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tiwakawaka](../T#tiwakawaka))

### tiwakawaka
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Rhipidura</i> spp., fantail [includes tiwaikawaka, tiwai waka, tiwai-waka; cf similar words with initial p]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|i waka waka; A bird so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,214</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> They had simply decorated their hair ... with the pretty yellow flower of the towai, which hung in heavy clusters from that tree on the banks of the stream ... From their ears were appended the dried feathered skin of the towakawaka-bird [sic : a's-acute; ?error for <i>tiwakawaka</i>], whose body, when living, is scarcely larger than a walnut. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/113</i>)

>  RHIPIDURA FULIGINOSA, Sparrm. Tiwaikawaka. Black Fan-tail. The Black Fan-tail Flycatcher breeds under conditions so very similar to those of the preceding species, that one description will serve for both. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/63</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> RHIPIDURA FLABILLIFERA. (PIED -FANTAIL.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Piwaiwaka, Tiwai-waka, Piwakawaka, \{Tirairaka, Pirairaka\} and Tiwakawaka \{and Pitakataka\}. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,143,{1888 2nd edn 1/69} ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Waka-waka (<i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>) is <i>the</i> robin of our Brighter Britain. It is a fantail, or flycatcher. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/226</i>) [NOTES: [check spg waka-waka]]

>  <i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>, Gml. Pied Fantail, Pied Flycatcher, Ti-waka-waka [36] <i>Rhipidura fuliginosa</i>, Sparrm. Black Fantail, Black Flycatcher, Ti-waka-waka. <i>(Mar 1885,NZ Journal of Science,35</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>55/159</b> <i>ti[i]wakawaka,</i> a bird, the black fantail, see pi[i]wakawaka. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> A fantail flickered across the glade, tiwakawaka, tiwakawaka. <i>(1965,Power of Joy,179</i>)

>  And what said Kupe of you,/O small bird of grace?/He said: "The Tiwai Waka/Kept flitting past my face!" <i>(Jul 11, 1936,Evening Post,8</i>)

### tiz
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>to have/throw a tiz</i>: to show anger <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### tizzie
 
 <i>n.</i> a sixpenny piece (??occas. a shilling) (the 1873, 1984 cits reveal a play on TANNER and TIZZY, both contemp. colloq. for 6d) [also tizzy]

>  <b>DILKE</b> [The Hokitika newsboy put a newspaper] into my hand. `How much?' I asked `Ay-a tizzy.' Understanding this more familiar term, I gave him a shilling. [Is Dilke merely advertising his generosity] <i>(1868,Greater Britain,1/332</i>) [NOTES: [see snapper]]

>  That 'ere eye is aweeping to think that there ain't a tizzy in the [?] to vet the other. <i>(1869,Punch or the Auckland Charivari,140</i>)

>  We are now in the thick of the cross examinatn. by Sheehan of the chiefest of the apostles - Tizzey the Tanner (ko Tanera). ... We have not found anything that can be called a rat; nor even got the scent very strong. But I must not report upon Heret[aunga]. <i>(Apr 6, 1873,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/347</i>) [NOTES: (C.W.Richmond, Napier)]

>  Following are other local money-names...6d. - <i>tizzie, sprat</i> or<i> tanner... </i> <i>(Jan 14, 1899,Sydney Bulletin,(Red Page)</i>) [NOTES: (Letter from <i>Loafer</i>, Tauranga) [Bill Pearson]]

>  <b>WALL</b> A sixpence was a `tizzy', a term not yet quite extinct. <i>(1958,Queen's English, The,97</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The word <i>tray</i> and its variants for `threepence' and zack fpr `sixpence' are said to be common Australianisms but are not known to me in New Zealand. <i>Tizzie</i>, perhaps from<i> tester</i>, is said to have been current in New Zealand, but again I do not know it. On the other hand, a word which Baker says is not current in New Zealand is one I have long known, <i>razoo</i>. ... The word <i>razoo</i> refers to no particular amount of money but is used in the negative `I haven't got a razoo', or, more often, `I haven't got a brass razoo'. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,133</i>)

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> Sixpence [:] tanner, sprat, tizzy, bender, snapper <i>(1972,From Beads to Banknotes,151</i>)

>  The sixpence attracted many slang terms - <i>tizzy, tanner, sprat, bender</i>, and a <i>kick.</i>. <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,15</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> The chief lessee and purchaser was Thomas Tanner, sometimes called Tizzy the Tanner or Tommy Tickles, and by Maori, Ko Tanera. Born in 1830, the son of a Wiltshire landed proprietor, Tanner studied medicine but disliked it. In 1850 he emigrated with his young wife, Julia, ... <i>(1984,City of the Plains,6</i>)

>  Why, it is only half as bad as the tithe pig; and instead of a <i>tizzy</i>, they might as easily have demanded the whole <i>hog</i>. (For the information of those not acquainted with <i>pure</i> English, I may remark , that a tizzy means a sixpence; a hog, or bob, a shilling." [sic] <i>(Dec 1, 1860,Otago Witness,5</i>)

### tizzy up
 
 <i>v.</i> (informal) make (a person or thing) more attractive or showy; (without object) smarten up, dress up. [often as tizzied up adjective]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tizzy up</i> dress up for an outing; probably derived from `titivate'; eg `Well, ladies, are you going to go and tizzy up before we leave?' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,113</i>)

>  <b>McGill</b> And, if Ron was interested, he had rescued all those old tin cigarette and petrol and soap signs from the tip, if he wanted to tizzy up his store frontage. <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,249</i>)

 <i>abbr.</i> three-letter acronym

>  ...longtime Internet users especially will sprinkle written debates with TLAs (three letter acronyms - yes, they can have more than three letters). <i>(Oct 5, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a Pom [ca1978 G. McEwen Auckland and HNZD1. rhyming slang]

>  <i>(Dec 12, 1984,TV2</i>) [NOTES: The Sullivans (Aust TV soap opera)]

### toa
 
 <i>n.</i> warrior

>  A remark of Manuka's was rather good - Maori chiefs (leaders) were toas & went out at the head of their men, Pakeha chiefs stayed in Town and ate biscuit. [fn.] Their notion of luxury. <i>(Dec 25, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/671,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  Tuninia was a celebrated toa ... and made use of the expression as a boastful and defiant speech addressed to some opponents. <i>(Jun 12, 1881,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/486,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C. Wilson)]

>  <b>MANING</b> <i>Toa</i> - A warrior of pree[dieresis]minant[sic] courage; a hero. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,239</i>)

>  <i>(1953,Short Stories (various authors),19</i>) [NOTES: 1901 Grace Tale of D.R. in 1953 Short Stories 19]

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,3/362</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Of great stature and in his prime a <i>toa</i> or warrior of exceptional strength and activity, he was now grown old ... <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,15</i>)

### toatoa
 
 <i>n.</i> an endemic shrub-like bush. [<i>Haloragis erecta</i> a perennial shrubby bush aka Toatoa, fire weed, shrubby haloragis, not to be confused with the three toatoa pine species.]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Toa-toa, <i>D'Urville. Haloragis alata. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  Haloragis alata Toa toa <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/122</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> Toatoa [Maori name] .. Toatoa [Settlers' name] .. Haloragis alata .. Herb <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,178</i>)

>  <i>To&#769;.a-to&#769;.a</i> (t&omacr.&#769;.a.-t&omacr.&#769;.a; <i>colloq</i>. t&omacr.&#769;.a-t&omacr.&#769;.&adot.), <i>n</i>. [Maori.] <i>(Bot.)</i> A New Zealand coniferous tree (<i>Phyllocladus glauca</i>). Another species of <i>(P. alpina)</i> is called <i>celery-topped pine</i>. See <i>Illust</i>. under PINE. <i>[Local, N. Z.] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> TOA TOA (<i>Haloragis alata [erecta]</i>. This is a native to which no English name has become attached, though it is abundant in lowland country from Auckland to Stewart Island. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,129,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Haloragis ... erecta</i> ... It was used as greens by the Maoris. ... TOA-TOA (greens). <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,129</i>)

>  <i>toatoa [=] shrubby haloragis </i>[s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,79</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Haloragis erecta</i> TOATOA ... Perennial bushy herb with 4-angled stems, tough near the base. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,144</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Haloragis <i>alata</i>, Jacq. ... Nat. name, `Toa-Toa', <i>D'Urville.</i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 62</i>)

>  Such are the principal conifers of New Zealand, and those which the largest dimensions; but there are others, although humble specimens enough, which are utilised for various purposes, and amongst these we must cite the toatoa (podocarpus asplenifolius, Taylor), which may be said to take the place of the cedar ... <i>(May 25, 1865,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> indigenous pine distinctive for large leathery leaves. [<i>Phyllocladus toatoa</i>, formerly <i>P. glaucus</i>: the 'celery' pine which grows in lower habitats than <i>P.alpinus</i>, the mountain toatoa.]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Phyllocladus glauca</i> (the toa-toa) is found chiefly in Northern Auckland, and was considered by Hooker to be the `most charming of all the New Zealand pines.' <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,81</i>)

>  <i>toatoa n</i> -s [Maori]: a New Zealand celery-topped pine <i>(Phyllocladus glaucus)</i> having striking whorled branches and often cultivated for ornament <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2402</i>)

>  <i>toatoa n</i>. small New Zealand tree with bright red wood, much prized for walking sticks. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>toatoa ... n.</i> small N.Z. tree with bright red wood, prized for walking sticks. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <i>toa-toa n.</i> small NZ tree related to celery-topped pine. [M] -->OED <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,808</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  [Brown dye] is obtained from a shrub or small tree named toatoa which is said to be rather scarce. <i>(1845,NZ Journal,V/140/119</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Toatoa, n.</i> Maori name of New Zealand tree, <i>Phyllocladus glauca,</i> Carr., <i>N.O. Coniferae.</i> The Mountain Toatoa is <i>P. alpinus</i>, Hook. <i>(1898,Austral English,472</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Phyllocladus</i> spp. (cf. also mountain toatoa)



 
 <i>n.</i> an indigenous pine, the mountain toatoa. [aka toa toa; <i>Phyllocladus alpinus</i> aka mountain toatoa; as one of NZ's three Phyllocladus genera it is sometimes referred to as celery pine, but c.f. celery pine <i>Phyllocladus trichomanoides</i>. [cf. 1940 Laing and Blackwell Plants of NZ 79ff] <i>Ibid.</i> 1/457 ... long poles made of manuka, toa toa or other hard wood. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/120</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Phyllocladus <i>trichomanoides</i>, Don. ... Nat. names, `Tanekaha' north of the Thames, and `Toa Toa' south of that river, <i>Col. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 235</i>)

>  (Colenso Botany North Isd) <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/34</i>) [NOTES: The channelled stems of the Neinei <i>(Dracophyllum latifolium), and the ... saplings of Toatoa, or Tanekaha (Phyllocladus trichomanoides)</i>, made valued walking-sticks.]

### Tobacco Country
 
 <i>n.</i> area on Chatham Islands

>  <b>SKINNER</b> The first part of the track from Waitangi to Owenga lies through well-grassed, hilly country ... a region which the settlers have given the name `Tobacco Country'. <i>(1923,Morioris of the Chatham Islands, The,12</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Whatever the cause, the further and more steeply you rise, the deeper and wetter the bogs become ..., till they reach the foot of the Tobacco Country as the tableland block is locally called <i>(1952,Chatham Islands</i>)

>  .. but there is also a large area of comparatively worthless land - in what is known as the "tobacco country," for instance - so-called, because once bought of the Natives for so much tobacco, though the purchasers never took possession, or, if they did, never made any use of their purchase. <i>(Jan 24, 1902,Star, The,4</i>)

>  ALL THAT LEASEHOLD PROPERTY Known as the "TOBACCO COUNTRY," And estimated to contain 20,000 Acres, more or less, Held at a rental of GBP40 per annum, lease of which expires 12th June 1897. <i>(Jun 20, 1889,Otago Witness,19</i>)

>  We had three glorious days pig-shooting in the "Tobacco Country," a wild waste of bog and bush land lying across the south of the island [sc. in the Chathams] so called because it was bought by a speculative white in the early days from the Maori conquerors for a box or barrel of tobacco. <i>(Dec 28, 1920,Otago Witness,55</i>)

### toby
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Kokopu had been found in stormwater drains, toby holes, and hidden under leaves on moist ground. <i>(May 7, 2001,Evening Post,18</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a raddle stick

>  <b>PROF. DARCY WALKER</b> He did not hear it till c1955 when he came to Wellington cf Macquarie: <i>toby</i> = stick used by owner to raddle badly-shorn sheep with. <i>(Nov 8, 1983,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <i>raddled:</i> This word means `robbed' in the sense of being deprived of one's proper payment. It is an extension of `raddling', carried out by unscrupulous farmers before the Shearers' Union looked after shearers' interests. Any sheep considered unsatisfactorily shorn was marked with red or blue raddle and such a sheep would not be counted in the shearer's tally. The raddle stick was also called a `Toby'. This must not be confused with [27] marking a sheep for other purposes, such as identification of certain sheep for easy selection later. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3.26</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]



 
 <i>n.</i> A stop-cock or valve in a water- or gas-main, gen. in a roadway at which the supply may be turned off, also applied to the covering, lid or box protecting it [Frequently toby box or toby-box; ca1954 Upper Hutt (Val Kiddie)(This was the first time I had heard the word used: I had always called it the `stopcock'. HWO) ; 1955 occas. seen (but not recorded) in the Evening Post --->ca1960 Karori, Wellington]

>  <b>STANDARDS INSTITUTE</b> <i>(1956,Glossary of Building Terminology</i>)

>  Where necessary, street-front tobies [water stopcocks] would be turned off and sealed to prevent firms turning the water back on. <i>(Apr 12, 1999,Dominion,7</i>)

>  Wellington City Council worker Chris Cashen was inspecting a flooded toby in Miramar a few days ago when he saw objects wriggling in the water. <i>(Jan 29, 2001,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  I must correct you as to the origin of 'toby'. It's not Irish for road. It is Gaelic for 'spring'. My ancestors built the navigation canals in the UK. That's why they were called 'navvies' and they took the word toby with them. I'm fluent in the Gaelic, so trust me! <i>(Feb 14, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  I also recommend that all the toby-boxes should be cast in a small block of concrete, as I find that a good many of them get shifted from their positions. <i>(May 2, 1901,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

>  She objected to the toby box on the bounday [sic], but he could not touch it. <i>(Jun 25, 1915,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  All he did afterwards was to fix a new ball tap, do some minor repairs, and supply a toby-box of the value of 4s 6d. <i>(May 2, 1930,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  Toby - surface box generally set flush with paving or other surface to cover the stop-cock or control valve of a water or gas supply. <i>(1979,NZ Standards Council Glossary Building Term.,119</i>)

>  <i>Toby</i> - Surface box generally set flush with paving or other surface to cover the stop-cock or control valve of a water or gas supply. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,119</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>toby</i>: (A) the MASTER WATER VALVE for a building ordinarily located under a metal cover in the sidewalk outside. Who was Toby anyway? `Turn off <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,104</i>)

>  A temporary worker had been engaged to detect unauthorised connections to council tobies (mains taps), and he had found the connections were mostly a normal hose connection to the toby. <i>(Apr 28, 1982,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  Large quantities of water have leaked from a toby in St Francis Grove, Lower Hutt, in the past few days, and the Lower Hutt City Council has done nothing to stop it, an angry woman resident says. <i>(Apr 18, 1985,Evening Post,32</i>) [NOTES: ? here a fire-hydrant valve?]

>  <i>toby n. </i>water stopcock on section boundary <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,504</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> I'll change the washer on that tap ... Go out and turn off the toby, will you dear. <i>(1985,Nest of Cuckoos,107</i>)

>  <b>JAMES M DERMODY, WARKWORTH</b> My parents, and I as a four-year-old, came out from Ireland in 1921. My father became involved in a new water-supply scheme for Blenheim. In the later 1920s the `toby-box' was an item of daily reference: my father had a small supply in his shed at home. They were a cast-iron cylinder of about four inches diameter and not much greater length. <i>(Nov 6, 1989,Personal Communication</i>)

>  What is the origin of the "toby". the tap that turns off water at the street frontage of a house?... I am prepared to wager that it is derived from "toby", which was the gypsy-tinker slang term for "street. There is an early link between tinkers and plumbers, both being experts with the soldering-iron, and I think the plumbers took over "toby" from the tinkers. Consequently, when they turned water off "at the toby" they were turning it off at the street. <i>(Jun 11, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>) [NOTES: Ian Gordon]

 <i>n.</i> [empty headword] (See [toki](../T#toki))

### todea
 
 <i>n.</i> a fern [usually <i>Todea superba</i> but also <i>Todea barbara</i> etc; incl attrib - todea fern.]

>  Though ferns are not absolutely [83] peculiar, ... - from the great arborescent species ..., the lovely todea (<i>Leptopteris superba</i>), down to the exquisite little <i>Hymenophyllum</i> - a most characteristic feature of the forest scenery. <i>(1883,High Alps of NZ,82</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> The Gleichenias [51] are a very distinct kind, with their ladder-like fronds set in umbrella shape. Hymenophyllums, or filmy Ferns, are very curious. Adiantums, or Maiden-hairs, are in infinite variety. The Todeas are very beautiful, especially <i>Todea superba</i>, which is commonly called Prince of Wales' Feather. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,50</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> (And) <i>Todea barbara</i> hard todea <i>(1882,Out in the Open</i>)

>  Some time ago specimens of Todea superba, commonly known as the Crape fern, grew in these grounds, but were removed <i>(May 29, 1920,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  It was composed of plants representing the various Dominions and Colonies - maple leaf for Canada hibiscus for British West Indies, and todea fern for New Zealand. <i>(Jan 20, 1939,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  The ferns, which attain the size of trees, are the splendid long-fronded mamaku with its black trunk, the mauku and tuakura, and the smaller ones are the wheki, the maru, and the tainwhaniwha [sic], the heruheru (todeamedia), and that queen of the New Zealand fern tribe, the tete or todea superba, commonly known as the "Prince of Wales' Feather." <i>(Jun 4, 1910,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  Among the various articles to be sent from this Province to the Exhibition at Otago is a splendid collection of Ferns by Mr. R. H. Huntley, Wellington, consisting of five groups in large frames with glass, each forming a distinct landscape picture. They are classified in the following order .. No.3. Nephrolepsis, Polypodium, Pymatodes, Dictymea, Arthropteris, Grammitis, Gymnogramma, Northolaena, Stenchlaena, Lygodium, Schizaea, Leptopteris, and Todea. <i>(Dec 14, 1864,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,3</i>)

>  The trees growing here were, for the most part, cedars, intermixed with pines, and amongst the ferns the beautiful <i>Todea Superba</i> was very plentiful. <i>(Mar 30, 1872,Otago Witness,10</i>)

>  Apart from the advantage of attracting tourists it will be found a pleasant resort for families and parties desiring to spend a few days during the summer months where they can procure the pure air to be obtained on the mountain, and see the grand views in some of the gorges where grows the mountain cypress, the lovely crape ferns, (todea superba), the toi, and other plants not to be seen elsewhere. <i>(Feb 19, 1890,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Todea barbara</i>

### toe
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) (of rugby players, racehorses, etc.) speed [ca1957 Upper Hutt]

>  <i>(Oct 8, 1963,NZ Truth</i>) [NOTES: [on racing page, check]]

Possessing plenty of toe and being quick off the mark, he frequently found himself away from his supports. Fast as Bert Cooke is, he never appeared able to keep near Page. <i>(Aug 28, 1930,NZ Truth,12</i>)

### toe-biter
 
 <i>n.</i> Dobson fly larva, <i>Archichauliodes</i> sp, black creeper [aka toebiter]

>  insect larva Order 4 (alder-flies) is represented in New Zealand by only a single species <i>Archicauliodes dubitatus</i>, an insect of large size, whose fat, succulent larva is found under rocks in streams, and forms excellent food for trout. This larva is called the `black creper,' or sometimes the `toe-biter'. <i>(Jan 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,3/273</i>) [NOTES: (Tillyard)]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The Dobson fly ... is a speckled orange-winged insect which flits weakly over streams at dusk. The larva (known as the black creeper or toe-biter). . . hides under stones in the creek bed by day, coming out for a night's career with its powerful jaws ready to seize and devour whatever comes its way; <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,151</i>)

>  She made a sudden dip and scooped up a creature she identified as the nymph of a dobsonfly. "It's known as the 'toe-biter,'" she said, "on account of its large jaws and habit of nipping bare feet." <i>(Nov 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 50/51</i>)

>  It is perfectly true that the big bags early in the season are obtained principally by the aid of the "toe-biter," which "hangs out" under stones near the water's edge; but matters adjust themselves later, for the "toe-biters" develop into a fly or something, and they are no longer found in any number. <i>(Nov 2, 1901,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

### toe-kiwi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The tufts of the toe-kiwi - the `cutting-grass' of the bushmen - which are a feature of the kauri bush, are set in a blaze; and as the flames flare up ... the masses of the puwharawhara (Asteliads) which encumber the branches are ignited ... <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/493</i>) [NOTES: (Walsh)]

### toe-rag
 
 <i>n.</i> a handkerchief (cf. SWEAT-RAG) (cf. as a British slang term of abuse: e.g. Eastenders `You're lying toe-rag': and cf. [ca1935 Havelock children]

>  <b>WAINWRIGHT</b> A germ called Frampton gets himself stiffened in Cawdale Rise. A miserable little tow-rag who happens to have been at Saville's party last night.') <i>(1973,High-Class Kill,194</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> Out came my toe rag, as we called our handkerchiefs [in the 1880s in Central Otago], and up went my other hand. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,78</i>)

### toe-ragger
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Toe-ragger, n.</i> In the bush a term of abuse; though curiously in one or two parts of New South Wales the word `toey,' which is derived from it, is a term of praise, a `swell.' The word has been explained as of convict origin, that the rags were used to soothe the galling of fetters; but the explanation is not satisfactory, for the part galled by the irons would not be the toe, but the ankle. A writer in `Truth' has cleared up the word (see quotation). It is of Maori origin. Away from Maoriland "toe-rigger' had no meaning, and a false meaning and origin were given by the change of vowel. <i>(1898,Austral English,472</i>)

>  `The bushie's favorite term of opprobrium `a toe-ragger' is also probably from the Maori. Amongst whom the nastiest term of contempt was that of <i>tau rika rika</i>, or slave. The old whalers on the Maoriland coast in their anger called each other toe-riggers, and to-day the word in the form of toe-ragger has spread through-out the whole of the South Seas.' <i>(Jan 12, 1896,Truth (Sydney)</i>)

>  <i>toe-ragger n.</i> a term of abuse. [corrupt. fr. Maori tau rika rika, a slave] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Toe-ragger, rigger</i>. A term of opprobrium: Australia and New Zealand resp., as in <i>Truth</i> (the Sydney one), Jan. 12, 1896, `"A toe-ragger' is Maori... The nastiest term of contempt was <i>tua rika rika,</i> or slave. The old whalers on the Maoriland coast in their anger called each other <i>toe-riggers</i>, and to-day the word in the form <i>toe-ragger</i> has spead throughout the whole of the South Seas.' Morris. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,893</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> Slang words of Maori origin were once common, but most of them are dead and dying [including] "toe-ragger,' a curse (taurekareka); ... <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Another old whaling term - this time a pejorative - is toe-rigger, which is the original of the still-current slang expression in Australia, <i>toe-ragger.</i> Here is what the Sydney Truth, January 12, 1896 had to say on the subject: "The bushie's favourite term of opprobrium, a toe-ragger, is...probably from the Maori, amongst whom the nastiest term of contempt was that of tau rika rika, or slave. The old whalers on the Maoriland coast in their anger called each other toe-riggers and to-day the word in the form of toe-ragger has spread throughout the whole of the South Seas.' Morris also declares: `It is of Maori origin. Away from Maoriland toe-rigger had no meaning, and a false meaning and origin were given by the change of vowel.' <i>(1941,NZ Slang,14</i>)

>  <i>toe-ragger n.</i> term of contempt for a person down and out [from tramps who tied rags round their feet to prevent blistering]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>The toeragger was not much wealthier [as a swagger] than the battler. His name is from the rags he wore in lieu of socks. </i> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,144</i>)

>  <i>toe-ragger n</i>. whalers' slang for a person to be despised. Also<i> toe-rigger</i> [From Maori <i>taurekareka</i>, slave]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

### toebiter
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish [dubious defn]

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>MONKFISH</i> (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Geniagnus monopterygius</i> (ii) <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (both) Whitefish. (c) Other common names: (both) Monkfish, Monks, Stargazer, Flathead (also applied at times to export gurnard); Churchills, Toebiters, Bulldogs, Spotted Stargazer, Maori Chief (Dunedin); Muddies. (d) Principal localities where taken: Napier, Cook Strait, Nelson, Banks Peninsula, Bluff-Stewart Island. ... Monkfish is a general term for species of the Families Uranoscopidae and Leptoscopidiae. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,37</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (Rural) footrotting or inspecting of sheep's feet.

 <i>n.</i> shellfish <i>Waltonia inconspicua</i>

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>
