### toetoe
 
 <i>n.</i> tussock grass



 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i> a tall showy plumed reed similar in appearance to the South American pampas grass. [Maori] <i>Arundo</i> spp., esp. <i>A. conspicua (kakao)</i>, a pampas grass; cuttygrass [English spellings (and to some extent, pron.) vary around two modes, usually, but not always, reduplicated, and with or without an <i>h</i>. (1)<i> toetoe, tohetohe, tohe </i>(2) <i>toi-toi, to"ito"i</i> [dieresis-o], <i>tohi-tohi, towi-towi, towy-towy;</i> and (?from pron [t it i])<i> tortor</i> (1842) <i>totitoti</i> (1937) is a mistake or misprint. English pron [|toui:toui:], [|touwi:touwi:], [t it I] The form is occasionally found unreduplicated, espec in combination with <i>-grass</i> (cf. <i>tui-grass</i>, probably an analogous Canterbury hearing influenced by <i>tui</i>: cf.<i>poe</i>, pui)<i> plurals usu. with</i> -s]

>  The only buildings were the New Zealand Company's store;.. a whare of toetoes, occupied by Lethbridge, the surveyor, and another occupied by Peter Crow in the bush... <i>(1948,Port Chalmers,16</i>)

>  Native toetoes can be recognised by their white, waxy leaf-sheaths plus the veins or tramlines on the leaves between the midribs and the leaf margins. <i>(Aug 27, 1999,Dominion,14</i>)

>  <b>1/III/34</b> ... the outer thatch [was] Toetoe, (<i>Cyperus ustulatus</i>), or <i>Rautahi (Carex ternaria,)</i> or Ririwaka (<i>Scirpus maritimus</i>), or of two kinds of Wiwi, or Rushes (<i>Juncus maritimus</i>, and <i>effusus</i>); ... <i>(1869,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> (grass) ... the huts were always well constructed ..., the thatch of toe-toe being securely fastened ... with the wire-like stems of the ake or metrosideros scandens. The interior was lined with reeds of the toe-toe, [88] kakapo or arundo conspicua. <i>(1876,East Coast Historical Records,87</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Toe-toe ..... (See Sword-grass) The term `toe!' alluding to the light large flower of this grass is used metaphorically precisely as we use the word `chaff.' ... Sword-grass (Toe-toe) [es-dieresis] .... <i>O.</i> Gramineae;<i> G</i>. Arundo; <i>S.</i> A. conspicua. "The largest New Zealand grass; confined to these islands; culms three to eight (ten) feet high; used for thatch and lining houses with reed-work. Flowers very like those of Panama grass.' <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/303</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Toe-toe (toy-toy)</i>: A tall plumed grass, very much like the Pampas. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,191</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> I am told [toetoe] is the correct spelling, not toi toi which is more usual. <i>(1933,Press, The</i>)

>  <i>toetoe: </i>large grass with white and yellow plumes. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <i>toe-toe</i>: The Maori name of a very large New Zealand grass something like the `pampas' grass grown in gardens. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),415</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> <i>(1960,Maori Girl,215</i>) [NOTES: [see raupo]]

>  <i>(1966,Short Stories (various authors),311</i>) [NOTES: 1963 Shadbolt in 1966 Short Stories]

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> It seemed impossible that this ... estuary, with its toe toe and flax-covered banks, was only half-an-hour's fast drive from the city. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,47</i>) [NOTES: (Day by Itself)]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Toetoe (plume grass) .. .. toetoe, toetoekakaho .. .. <i>Arundo conspicua </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>toetoe</i> [s] <i>Cortaderia fulvida</i> (Buchan.) Zotov <i>Cortaderia richardii</i> (Endl.) Zotov <i>Cortaderia toetoe</i> Zotov <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,79</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Cyperus ustulatus </i>A. Rich. Common name: <i>Cutty grass </i>Maori names: <i>Toetoe, upokotangata ... </i>The name `toetoe' was applied to various graases and sedges. ... Cheeseman's name for it was <i>Mariscus ustulatus</i> (A. Rich.) Cheesem. <i>Ibid.</i>58 <i>Cortaderia fulvida</i> (Buchan.) Zotov <i>Cortaderia splendens</i> Connor and allied species Maori names: <i>Toetoe, kakaho</i> ...The second has been known as <i>Arundo kakaho</i> Steud., <i>A. conspicua </i>Forst.f., and <i>Cortaderia toetoe</i> Zotov. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,49</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Cortaderia richardii</i> TOETOE ... Native to: S[outh and] St[ewart Islands]. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,372</i>)

>  Clumps of dead toetoe and marram, grass seem to have been resting places for them [sc. gulls]. <i>(Jul 12, 2003,Dominion Post,F9</i>)

>  All along the miles of the straight stretch of road from Waitakaruru to the Pipiroa ferry are to be seen hedges of toetoe, mostly marking boundaries or the margins of Government drains. <i>(Apr 27, 1929,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  I stuck a toetoe branch down the back of my neck. <i>(Apr 3, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  It is not quite clear how pampas came originally to the Piako Swamp - invading the domain of the indigenous toetoe and flax - but probably pampas just wandered there, for it is classed, among other exotics, as garden escape. <i>(Dec 14, 1932,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  ...nothing else was familiar: the mountains of dark steaming bush, the huge braided river, the clumps of flax and toetoe, the stark promontories of rock, amd all around the house boggy pasture and wet and rotting trees. <i>(May 7, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  <b>35/22</b> The roof was .. covered with raupo - sometimes with an inner sheeting of totara-bark - while an upper layer of toetoe-grass, secured by ropes of mangemange .. preserved the raupo from wet. <i>(1903,TrNZI</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> To|e to|e; A long rushy grass so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,215</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> They cut a few sticks, ... tie them together with a flax plant, and thus form the frame of their shed, and cover it with <i>toetoe,</i> a kind of long grass, four to five feet long ... . <i>(Jan 4, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,212,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The most disgusting insects in nature exist among the spear-grass, called toi toi, of the swamps and plains; it is called the kikararu [a's-acute]. Its odour is disgustingly offensive, and it is often found in rush or other dwelling-houses. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/320</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> This day's journey was over a level grassy plain diversified with patches of flax and toetoe. <i>(Mar 17, 1853,Traveller under Concern,88,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> It happened that as Tuhuihuia was going one day to gather a species of grass, called <i>toetoe</i>, to thatch his hut, ... <i>(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,18</i>)

>  ... from the consecrated grove, flax and toetoe have been gathered; ... <i>(Aug 15, 1857,Lyttelton Times,3</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Toe-toe. <i>Arundo conspicua</i>. ... Toe-toe, <i>Col[enso]. Cyperus ustulatus. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> the rays of the rising sun first struck the glistening leaves of the flax, toetoe, raupo and karamu shrubs in the swamps adjoining the river ... <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,139</i>) [NOTES: 1935 J.W.Stack More Maoriland Adventures in Wily and Maunsell Robert Maunsell (1938) 139]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> They dwelt in whares [11] (huts) built in the traditional shape of an inverted V, roofed with grass, and lined inside with the reeds of the toetoe or New Zealand pampas grass. The orthodox Maori name for these reeds is pukakaho, but the white men called them `bukaka'. <i>(1839,First White Boy Born in Otago,10</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> And along the river bank the <i>kakaho</i>, the <i>toetoe</i> or pampas grass, once waved its plumes abundantly; ... <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,146</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> Earp spells the word tohitohi, and even to the present day it is spelt toi toi as often as the correct toe toe. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,277</i>)

>  <i>toetoe</i>, giant ornamental grass (Arundo conspicua), the flower-culms used for reed-work between the carved slabs of house-walls, cross-laced in a variety of patterns with narrow strips of flax [147] or pingao, white and black or bright orange (the tukutuku-work): very often mis-spelt toitoi or toi toi, or tohi tohi. Though the name toetoe is by the Pakeha confined to Arundo, by the Maori it is applied to various species of rush and grass with a qualifying suffix: Arundo is toetoe-kakaho, the latter word referring to the valued culms. Some people object to the reed tukutuku-work as being flimsy and perishable: in reality it is as durable as galvanized-iron and far more handome and artistic: the interior of a good Maori house is a thing of beauty. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  Coastal shrub species such as flax, toetoe, hebe and manuka were planted. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 7</i>)

>  Whatipu is a wilderness of slick sands, a soft, broad headland laced on one side by toetoe (swamp grass) and on the other, by foaming sea. <i>(Jul 2006,Air New Zealand inflight magazine,33</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> Beautiful clumps of the giant flax, formium tenax [sic], or the tall pampas-like toe-toe grass [sc. <i>Leptospermum scoparium</i> (sic), footnote confused with <i>Arundo conspicua</i>] with its drooping plumes, lined the shore. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,144</i>)

>  There is a good cart road up to this pa, but it traverses country covered with flax, toetoe, and light bush. <i>(May 25, 1860,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  <b>DREAVER</b> The land turned out to be a waste of raupo and toetoe by the Tokomaru Stream. <i>(1984,Horowhenua County and its People,146</i>)

>  Toetoe (Cortaderia) is excellent for the holiday home, but avoid pampas grass, which is a pest plant... <i>(Jan 24, 2004,Dominion Post,E14</i>)

>  Wooden buildings, mostly built after the Maori style, are rapidly taking the place of the old houses of toetoe and raupo. <i>(Dec 30, 1898,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  The hall was neatly decorated with the foliage of toetoe, nikau, keikei, and other native plants. <i>(May 5, 1874,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Here, however, we doubt if there is even one so enamoured of the beauties and luxuries of those good old times when poles and bundles of toetoe were deemed a comfort, and if to these were superadded a few baskets of <i>tiwa's</i> and a <i>spare</i> rib of native pork to grace the festive board; language was too poor wherewith to express our delight. <i>(Jan 21, 1843,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,183</i>)

>  Of the above-mentioned plants, only a few can be recommended as likely to resist the local gales - viz., koromiko, rewarewa, manuka, karamu, taupata, akeraho, akepiro, houhou, kaikaia, tikapu (?), the tawhais, tawhera, toetoe, and, in damp and fairly sheltered and shaded places, the ferns - ponga, mamaku, tuakura, korokio, piupiu, rarauhe, Pteris tremula. <i>(Aug 29, 1912,Evening Post,8</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Environment) [ORIGIN: Maori]

### toetoe-upoko-tangata
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. Maorihead](../M#maorihead))

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>M[ariscus] ustulatus, C.B.Clarke, </i>... [767] Abundant in lowland districts throughout. ... <i>Toetoe-upoko-tangata; Toetoe-whatu-manu</i>. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,766</i>)

### toetoewhatumanu
 
 <i>n.</i> =toetoewhatumu q.v.

>  The displacement of the ... coarse sedge known as toe-toe-whatumanu (<i>Cyperus ustulatus</i>, A. Rich.), ... by European grasses ... is so striking ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/18</i>)

### toetoewhatumu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Toetoewhatumu. Cyperus ustulatus, A. Rich. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/50</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

### toey
 
 <i>adj.</i> bad-tempered, aggressive

>  He agreed he became "toey" at times when his payments stopped because ACC said it had not received his medical certificates. <i>(Nov 24, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A13</i>)

>  Party members are a bit toey about getting rid of the monarchy. <i>(Apr 14, 1996,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

>  Maureen was getting all toey as all Brian wanted to do was stay and drink more grog. <i>(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,45</i>)

>  Anyway, since I'bve been crook, it's a fact that I've been getting all ratty and toey ... <i>(Jun 11, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  He's [sc. a stalker] been sending letters and is getting really toey. This guy is quite serious about what he's doing. <i>(Oct 21, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  Understandably, US exporters get toey competing against their own cheaper goods, in essence, arguing that they face too much competition. <i>(Nov 20, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> This was our third child so I wasn't as `toey' as John had been .... <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,86</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Unfortunately, [the dog] had one fault - his temperament. He got toey on the trial ground and Bain had difficulty in holding him. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,86</i>)

>  West Coasters sometimes get toey. In fact they probably coined the phrase. But the toey-ist people in the South Island at present are the voters of Otago Central. <i>(Oct 30, 1972,Dominion,4/3</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> By about the third day cooped up in that little box I began feeling really toey ... .<i>Ibid..</I< 255 TOEY (v) Agitated, excited, ill-at-ease. It has been suggested that this derives from the racecourse, where a horse that is nervous and fidgetty at the starting gate is said to be toey. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,145</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> All this made me a bit toey to get out but the doctor wasn't having any. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,102</i>)

>  [Hdg] Tow operators toey over confiscated cars. <i>(Jan 17, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,1</i>)

>  [Hdg] Getting toey. Environment Waikato is considering selling millions of dollars worth of overseas shares in the face of faltering investment returns. <i>(Sep 22, 2001,Evening Post,15</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> of race-horses, rugby footballers, sports, fast, ready to move

>  Don't let them down. You've got to be toey and eager to get after them; tough but not dirty. <i>(Jul 4, 1983,National Business Review,32</i>)

>  A toey field which included Olympic Games aspirant Richard Lockart lined up for the men's 100 metres breaststroke final. <i>(Mar 19, 1988,Dominion,38</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>toey</i> anxious, randy, ready, excitable; originally of a keen sprinter in horse racing; eg `Am I toey tonight! I must score or I'll go apeshit.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,114</i>)

>  He's a stronger horse now and is not as toey as he used to be. <i>(Sep 26, 2003,Dominion Post RaceForm,20</i>)

 <i>n.</i> person of stature or integrity

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> TOFF (n) Person of stature or integrity. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,255</i>)

### togs
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [togs](../T#togs))



 
 <i>n.</i> bathing, swimming costume [aka bathing togs, swimming togs; ca1932 GT Diary (Dannevirke) ...<i>togs</i> always meant <i>bathing togs.</i>]

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> ... We tore down to a quiet beach, stripped off our clothes, and plunged in ... We didn't bother about togs. <i>(1935,Little Country, The,216,1937 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> ... `Roll your stockings in your bloomers and hide them under the seat [of the dressing-shed]. A girl last week lost simply everything, and had to go home in her togs.' <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,117,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>togs</i>, used of bathing suits rather than of clothes in general; <i>hippies,</i> bathing trunks. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,92</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> Inside his pants Gibert's togs chafed his skin, and he was impatient for the water to swish about his body. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,203,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> You want to see her in swimming togs. <i>(1956,Sweet White Wine,92</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> You want to see her in swimming togs. <i>(1956,Sweet White Wine,92</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Who would care that I had swum at Waikiki? Not from the Royal Hawaiian but changing into togs in a public lavatory. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,38</i>)

>  <b>HARVEY</b> Old Pimply Face was starting to take off her bathing togs.<i>Ibid.</i> 67 67 He had his togs underneath. `You start cheering and I'll do the swimming.' <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,64</i>)

>  <b>JEFFREY</b> I didn't bring any t-togs,' I said. `I have no intention of swimming. I detest fresh water. ...' <i>(1964,Mairangi,147</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> As for the children, they soon adjust to change. They soon learn for instance that `togs' are bathing clothes, that `skite' means to show off ... <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,42</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> Our bathing suits, rolled in their towels (there was a special way of parceling up your `togs")., were forever wet,... <i>(1979,Living in the Maniatoto,42</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>togs</i> Not all kinds of clothing, but only one kind. Bathing togs, or a bathing suit. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,104</i>)

>  <i>togs </i> <i>(1981,Auckland Secondary Teachers College</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>GILDERDALE</b> <i>togs</i> Bathing costume. In T.T. Garland's <i>Old Joe </i>(1937) used first as `old togs', meaning `old clothes' in English sense, but later in the same book as `bathing togs'. <i>(1982,Sea Change,242</i>)

>  <b>OLIVER</b> He always had a large old suitcase with him; when it was opened it revealed little more than togs and a towel, ... <i>(1983,James K. Baxter,31</i>)

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> On the hottest days she takes her togs when she goes to the shop or the phone booth. <i>(1986,Then Again,57</i>)

>  <i> tog sl. 1 n.</i> (usu. in pl.) garment, esp. (<i>NZ & Austral</i>.) swimming costume. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,808</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1231; cf. 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 505]]

>  [Hdg] Don't throw out your togs yet.... But don't throw out the togs in despair - summer is (still) on its way. <i>(Jan 6, 2000,Dominion,1</i>)

>  Tarts' Swimming Togs, the Trouble. <i>(Feb 4, 1911,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Boys of all sizes swirled and yelled and grinned, some dressed in swimming togs or footie jerseys. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,147</i>)

>  When I was a kid I kept a pair of togs under my pillow in case of the Big One. <i>(May 8, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  Most of the children didn't even have togs. <i>(Apr 1992,Charivari (Auckland),3</i>)

>  He'd arrive each summer with togs - we'd say, what the hell are they for? - and soft feet! <i>(Jul 29, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  <b>COLE</b> Or was it at the creek where you used to swim without togs? <i>(1949,It Was So Late,11</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> The first thing Mum does when we arrive at the cottage, before she sweeps up mouse droppings or once a dead one, or unpacks anything, is to open all the windows wide, then take off her corsets quick as anything, put on her togs and head for the sea. <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,18</i>)

>  Waikato had a decidedly male-in-male- togs-looking woman in their goal. <i>(May 3, 1993,Salient,15</i>)

>  At least one of the teachers.. should be in their swimming togs and stay with the children. <i>(Jan 28, 1928,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,33</i>)

>  Sam was in bathing togs, and all ready should occasion occur to go overboard. <i>(Nov 11, 1908,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> So I put on my swimming-togs, and went down to the creek. <i>(1937,Wednesday's Children,49</i>) [NOTES: 1989 edition]

>  Bring my bathing togs, Mum? <i>(Feb 16, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,62</i>)

>  ... Mum's bag ... was bulging with bathing togs and things to change <i>(1987,In Fifteen Minutes,162</i>) [NOTES: 1944 Texidor in 1987 In Fifteen Minutes 162]

>  It was with great reluctance that wet togs are donned on a raw and rainy morning. <i>(1934,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,III/45</i>)

>  We haunted the Marton baths.. lining up long before opening time with our bundles of togs and towel. <i>(Apr 1992,North & South,40</i>)

>  Two New Zealanders walk by, like schoolboys on their way home, swinging their bathing togs in their hands. <i>(May 25, 1942,NZEF Times,I/48 : 4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> sports clothing

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... he knew very well what [the coach] would say when his boys were changed out of their cadet uniforms into their football togs ready to practice. <i>Ibid.</i> 119 ... boys hanging up their clothes on the pegs and pulling on their practice togs. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,88</i>)

>  <b>GLOVER</b> Once you have your togs on/ There is no chance of any escape/ From life's or love's or rugby's battle./ <i>(1971,Diary to a Woman,1</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>togs </i>swimming trunks for [sic] football gear; contracting English meaning of any clothes. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,114</i>)

>  Time to see about FOOTBALL TOGS With the Season not far ahead, all intending Players should get in touch with us right away. <i>(Apr 2, 1921,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  The girls were dressed in black football togs, even to the shorts. <i>(Jul 30, 1932,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  In his football togs Todd is 14st 12lb, and Gillespie is 13st 11lb. <i>(Sep 21, 1940,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  Cricketing togs galore on Saturday; they got a proper christening ... <i>(Dec 8, 1888,Observer (Auckland),17</i>)

>  P L is quite a masher with his tennis togs on ... <i>(Mar 5, 1898,Observer (Auckland),26</i>)

>  Q is for Quinlan, most charitable Paddy, / In striped football "togs," begad he's a laddie. <i>(Dec 3, 1904,Observer (Auckland),12</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> clothes generally [universal use - archive]

>  TOGS. - Clothes - in war sometimes unrecognisable as such. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiv</i>)

>  On her return home she took off her Sunday "bests" and donned her milking "togs." <i>(Jul 23, 1927,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,44</i>)

>  This "capping" occasion was Sir James Hector's first appearance before an Otago audience in his Chancellor's togs. Wonderful togs they are - gorgeous to a quite unspeakable degree. <i>(Sep 4, 1890,Otago Witness,24</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [toetoe](../T#toetoe))

### toheroa
 
 <i>n.</i> [also attrib]

>  <i>toheroa n</i>, pl<i> toheroa</i> or <i>toheroas</i> [Maori] : a large marine bivalve mollusk (<i>Amphidesma ventricosum</i>) of New Zealand used extensively for food <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2404</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Toheroa .. .. toheroa .. .. <i>Amphidesma ventricosum </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>toheroa n.</i> a New Zealand edible mollusc [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),169</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,169</i>)

>  <b>20</b> <i>toheroa ... n.</i> New Zealand edible mollusc [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins)</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Toheroa Paphies ventricosa</i> ... A species unique to New Zealand. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,161</i>)

>  We were sure that the toheroa inspector would arrest her and put her in jail. <i>(Apr 1998,North & South,31</i>)

>  Bubbles in the wet sand tease anyone who remembers toheroa soup or fritters - but, if you see toheroa, don't touch. This succulent fish is protected by law now to allow regeneration. <i>(Nov 1999,AA Directions,30</i>)

>  The only canning company, at present operating in a small way.., packed a very limited number because of the number of small toheroa in the beds. <i>(1955,AJHR,H.-15/46</i>)

>  Toheroa diggers were present at west coast beaches, North Island, in large numbers during the weekend for the reopening of the season. <i>(Dec 4, 1945,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> On the north bank were placed, among the bushes, three raouis, or carved monuments, painted with red earth. These had been erected here to prevent native travellers or strangers from grubbing in the sand for a favourite large cockle, called <i>toi-roa</i>, which are steamed and dried by the natives, and taken as portable food for a journey. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/71</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> ... she sent us a basket of tairoas, a large white shellfish from the coast, which is considered a delicacy .. <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,66</i>)

>  <b>STALLWORTHY</b> Sufficient ... was done ... to show that the toheroa could be successfully tinned ... <i>(1916,Early Northern Wairoa,59</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> Along the coast I came to the dunes, and here I piched camp and roasted some tohiroa. This is a huge shellfish, which in these parts lives in colonies under the sand. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,68,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> The toheroa taste has spread among members of the Royal Family. <i>(1934,Journalese,124</i>)

>  THE TOHEROA (<i>Amphidesma ventricosum</i>). <i>(1934,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,15/396</i>)

>  <b>BASHAM</b> <i>SCALLOPED TOHEROAS </i>... Mince toheroas with onion. Mix in ... breadcrumbs. Stir in 1 beaten egg, pepper and salt. Put a spoonful in each cleaned toheroa shell and ... piece of butter on top. Bake ... <i>(1939,Aunt Daisy's Cookery Book,29</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> From the sea, the principal foods obtained are the toheroa shellfish (local [Otaki] variety, tohemanga) and flounders. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,99</i>)

>  <i>toheroa</i>: edible shellfish. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  The Maori name of an edible shellfish found on New Zealand beaches, a great delicacy. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),415</i>)

>  The Maori name of an edible shellfish found on New Zealand beaches, a great delicacy. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),415</i>)

>  It is said that like caviare and olives the Toheroa is an acquired taste. <i>(Jun 1936,NZ Railways Magazine,54</i>)

>  Toheroa fritters for breakfast are scrumptious. <i>(Mar 9, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,95</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> Kuku and tohemanga or toheroa are also very nice cooked this way. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,39</i>)

>  Sale for one month only and strictly for cash. A few quotations .. Toheroas, 6d tin ... <i>(May 14, 1906,Wanganui Herald,7</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to gather toheroa

>  <b>McLEOD</b> The four of them had been toheroa-ing, hunting the large, slithery-tongued shell-fish delicacy with wooden spades, and a sack. <i>(1967,Dorinda,64</i>)

### toheroa bed
 
 <i>n.</i>

### toheroa fritter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BLACKMORE</b> <i>TOHEROA FRITTERS AND BACON </i>... Egg ... Butter ... Milk and Water ... Flour ... Salt and Pepper ... Toheroas or other shell-fish ... Baking Powder ... Fry in spoonfuls in smoking hot fat. <i>(1928,Cooking  of NZ Fish,34</i>)

>  <i>TOHEROA FRITTERS</i> ... toheroas ... egg-white ... butter ... warm water ... flour ... pepper and salt ... cook tablespoonful in smoking fat ... garnish with lemon. <i>(1936,Recipe Book [St Mary's],21</i>)

### toheroa soup
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... we could look over half the restaurant and watch the waitresses ... hurrying between the tables with wooden trays filled with plates of sausages-and-mash, baked beans on toast, or toheroa soup. <i>(1956,Sweet White Wine,54</i>)

>  Our familiar emblem is a rare bird - the kiwi. ... Almost a rare as the kiwi is the toheroa soup we talk about but seldom sip. <i>(Oct 24, 1959,Dominion,14</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> None of us dreamed that the day was coming when toheroa soup and toheroa fritters would be featured on menus at home and abroad; that they would be popularly recognised as being the most distinctive of our national delicacies ... <i>(1970,Way Up North,61</i>)

>  <b>DRYLAND</b> `Toheroa soup?' The waitress laughed outright. In 1957 this most blessed of shellfish had not yet proved its ability to survive. `You won't get no Toheroa soup. Hasn'y been any for years. But there's Kontiki.' <i>(1973,Multiple Texture, A,38</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> Toheroa soup is canned and sold overseas, but the only place you can get it in New Zealand is in the larger tourist hotels. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,104</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> It is in the area of soups that we find New Zealand's greatest contribution to international cuisine - the legendary toheroa soup. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,40</i>)

>  <b>LATHAM</b> The lisle stockings were a ghastly greenish-khaki colour that the girls called `toheroa soup'. <i>(1986,WAAC Story, The,20</i>)

>  <b>OVENDEN</b> I'll bet the entire Trentham yearling stock sale to a tin of Toheroa soup that the money from Rialto is the key. <i>(1986,O.E.,198</i>)

>  TOHEROA SOUP ... toheroas ... stodk ... onion ... celery ... milk ... cornflour ... curry powder ... seasoning ... cream ... lemon juice ... <i>(1936,Recipe Book [St Mary's],13</i>)

>  <i>TOHEROA SOUP</i> ... toheroas ... milk ... onions ... simmer gently for 2 hours. <i>(1938,'Milkmade' Cook Book,5</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... tinned fruits purchased with our own money, and a great can of toheroa soup (bought at the New Zealand Club, Cairo) to be quaffed in triumph at Benghazi on Christmas Day. <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,18,1974 ed.</i>)

>  Each year during the season she produced great bowlfuls of marvellous toheroa soup, richly green, spiked with a touch of cayenne, a little mace. <i>(Sep 1999,Cuisine,No. 76 :</i>)

>  Toheroa soup is just a memory for most of us now and whitebait fritters for Sunday breakfast an almost unheard-of luxury. <i>(Mar 2, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  Toheroa soup is such a delicacy (says the Lyttelton Times) that is deserves more fame than it enjoys in New Zealand, where the curious burrowing shellfish from which it is made flourishes in beaches from Dargaville to the Bluff. <i>(Aug 18, 1916,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  She recalled her visit to New Zealand in 1920, and said her memories included toheroa soup and blue cod. <i>(Dec 24, 1942,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  We came upon a notice I could not resist photographing - "The famous New Zealand toheroa (oyster) soup." <i>(Jan 5, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  "Toheroa soup!" sighed Lisbeth blissfully over the menu, "and not out of a tin, either." <i>(Mar 9, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,95</i>)

>  A fine menu, including toheroa soup, was quietly settled behind the varying depths of waistcoat, and the King was duly toasted. <i>(Oct 29, 1907,Observer (Auckland),16</i>)

### toheroa stakes
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THOMAS</b> But my friends in the far north tell tales of parties going to the beach to fish, and spending their time engrossed in a game known as the Toheroa Stakes. The game is based on the shellfish's remarkable ability to bury itself and disappear in the solid sand of the wet beach. ... The game is, or was, played by drawing a circle on the beach. Each player selects a toheroa. The bets are made. On the word go each toheroa is placed on its side within the circle. The first to disappear is the winner. <i>(1970,Way Up North,100</i>)

### tohetohe
 
 <i>n.</i> toetoe [toetoe aka tohitohi, tohi tohi, tohi, tohetohe, tohi grass; see toetoe] (See [toetoe](../T#toetoe))

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Close to Pipitea is a whare karakia, or chapel,belonging to the Christian natives, which is built of <i>raupo</i> and <i>tohi-tohi</i> grass, according to the native fashion. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/289 The New Zealand flax grows in every direction ... and the <i>tohi tohi</i>, a species of long rush-like sedge, is abundant on the margin of the water, and in marshy and damp situations. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/235</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The indigenous grasses are not numerous in their varieties, or remarkable, with the exception of the <i>tohe-tohe</i>, [at 240 toitoi] a reed whose leaves are sometimes four feet in length, and its cane-coloured seed-stems six feet in height. <i>(1848,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),148</i>)

>  <b>6</b> This is a rich tract of land: some of it dry, and clothed with luxuriant grass; other portions of it more or less swampy, with a vegetation of flax, <i>tohe-tohe</i>, (a long riband-grass with graceful feathery seed-tufts,) ... <i>(Jan 18, 1851,Lyttelton Times</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> About 200,000 acres consist of open land covered with grass, anise, fern, flax, and tohitohi. <i>Arundo conspicua</i> tohe-tohe <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),113</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> The banks of the ... Avon ... are thickly fringed with weeping willows, interspersed with ... clumps of tohi, which is exactly like the Pampas grass you know so well in English shrubberies. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,47,1870 ed.</i>)

>  Then ... amongst the ... tohe tohe reeds, and saw-edged grass, a pair of Harriers had built their rough, flat-topped home or ... alarmed the pyebald Redbill, that circles round ... screeching its clamorous note; or we might watch the pied Stilt with long pink legs ... making for the rush-fringed lagoon ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/47</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>TINNE</b> There was some difficulty in picking one's way ... through the tall raupo (rush) and tohi-grass; ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,28</i>)

>  <b>ANSON</b> ... all the open ground surrounding the settlement [at Piraki] ... being covered with tussac-grass, raupo, or tohitohi (N.Z. pampas-grass). <i>(1911,Piraki Log,155</i>)

>  The valley is covered with a high crop of fern [,] flax, tohi tohi, tutu, and other shrubs. <i>(May 16, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POTTS</b> <i>(1882,Out in the Open</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

### tohi-palm
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cordyline indivisa</i> (See [toi-palm](../T#toi-palm))

 <i>n.</i> [?any connection with TOI n2]

### tohora
 
 <i>n.</i> the southern right whale [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>COLLINS</b> Eto-ho-ro-ha[macron], <i>A whale. </i> <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,561</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Toh|ora, <i>s</i>. A sperm-whale. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,215</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the leviathans, who sport in shoals around these shores, the para paraua [a's-acute], or <i>sperm-whale</i> (Physeter macrocephalus); the tohora, or <i>right-whale</i> (Balaena bysticoetus); the mungu nui, or <i>black physeter</i>; the<i> sun-fish</i> (Diodonmola); <i>fin-back</i> (Balaena physalis); the <i>musculus,</i> or large-lipped whale; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/323</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the larger tribes that sport in shoals in the vicinity of the shore, are, sperm-whales, called <i>para</i>[as-acute]<i> paraua</i>[a3-acute] (physeter macrocephalus); black whale, <i>mungu nui </i>(black physeter); right whale, <i>tohora</i> (balaena mysticetus); finback, (balaena physalis)... <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202fn</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Tohora, or Right Whale. - <i>Polack</i> , N.Z. i. 323; ii.401. <i>Balaena Antipodum, Gray.</i> N.S.t1. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/183</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/183]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,136</i>)

>  `Looking at it as it lay extended, it resembled a very large whale (nui tohora).' <i>(1878,TrNZI,11/90</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> `In the open sea, and to the south, the most prized whale next to the sperm is the black whale, or tohora (<i>Eubalaena Australis</i>), which is like the right whale of the North Sea, but with baleen of less value.' <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,21</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tohora, n, </i>Maori name for a whale. [1855, 1878, 1883] <i>(1898,Austral English,473</i>)

>  <b>REID</b> <i>Tohora, n, </i>Maori name for a whale. [1855, 1878, 1883] <i>(1961,New Zealand (various authors/titles),97</i>) [NOTES: 1930 Cowan Tales of the M. Coast in 1961 Reid NZ 97]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Balaena australis </i>... [94] Southern Right Whale. TOHORA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,93</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>tohora</i> or<i> tohoraha</i>: whale. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,195</i>)

>  <i>tohora n.</i> Maori name for the whale. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>tohora ... n. </i>Maori name for whale. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  Orca the Killer and his mate, in their patrol of the seas, never went far from a certain bay, where a whaling station was; and the sea they searched for food was in the track that is followed by Tohora, the Right Whale of the Southern Seas, in his journeyings north. <i>(Jan 21, 1911,Marlborough Express,6</i>)

### tohu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HULME</b> You chew absently ... and bring your other hand down to fiddle ... with that rattler, tohu, cloak pin, long dagger of pounamu you wear by the silver cross round your neck ... <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,10</i>)

### tohunga
 
 <i>n.</i> a (Maori) priest or gifted or learned person; a teacher, faith-healer, doctor, or wise man (often in English contexts, `medicine man') [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [also priest](../A#also-priest),[wizard](../W#wizard))

>  <b>COLLINS</b> ?explanation Etang-a-te[acute]da Eti-ket-ti-ca, means a principal chief, or a man in very great authority. ... Etanga-roa, or E-ta-hon-ga, is a priest; whose authority in many cases is equal, and in some superior to the Etiketica. <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,344</i>)

>  The tohangha (<i>tohunga</i>) Rakoo (Rakau) paid great attention to Mr. Hall; ... <i>(Sep 6, 1814,Marsden's Lieutenants,59,1934 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1814 Sep 6 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 59]

>  The <i>tohunga</i> or medicine-man who revealed these secrets thus concluded his narrative. <i>(1905,TrNZI,37/95</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> attrib ... directly [the Maori] enters the domain of witchcraft, `tipau' (ghouls), `patupaiarahe' (fairy demons), dreams, or tohunga imbecilities, he becomes a fear-haunted, helpless perverter of truth! <i>Ibid.</i> 130 When the next measle, or other epidemics, come along, you send for that hideous monstrosity, your tohunga, who knows no more of treatment than that shag in the river there, who brings his stinking lotions and baths, his idiotic prescriptions, ... until the sufferer dies, and then stalks away to do murder elsewhere. <i>Ibid.</i> 262 In my recent outback explorations I have met with scenes which horrified ... and brought home to me ... the imperative necessity ... that it be a criminal offence, punishable with hard labour imprisonment, to practise tohungaism, boast of tohungaistic reputation, apply to a tohunga for illegal operations, and learning the incantations, ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,14, 63</i>)

>  Preamble WHEREAS designing persons, commonly known as <i>tohungas</i>, practise on the superstition and credulity of the Maori people by pretending to possess supernatural powers in the treatment and cure of disease, the foretelling of future events, and otherwise, and thereby induce the Maoris to neglect their proper occupations and gather into meetings where their substance is consumed and their minds unsettled, to the injury of themselves and to the evil example of the Maori people generally: ...[check if cap initial req for tohungas???] <i>(1907,NZ Statutes,No. 13</i>) [NOTES: 1907, No. 13 <i>Title</i> An Act to suppress <i>Tohungas</i> (24th September, 1907)]

>  The other party applied to a 'tohunga, or seer, for information. <i>(Dec 7, 1872,Auckland Weekly News,5</i>)

>  Ethnic statistics are fleshed out, the Tohunga Suppression Act is revisited and Maori genesis is show to be a way of healing the fragmentation of modern life. <i>(Nov 28, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> Something like the story you wrote about the wily Maori tohunga who puts a spell on a red-headed Irish woman? <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,7</i>)

>  We shall probably next hear of a tohunga applying to the Supreme Court for a writ of mandamus to some Maori Council. <i>(Sep 3, 1904,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  Yes, it is better to be a tohunga than a seller of sly grog <i>(Oct 1, 1904,NZ Observer,23</i>)

>  The Maori Tohunga is a kind of bush Baptist who dips the community in a creek for every kind of ill. A girl named Kake was immersed up Hukerenui way, and died shortly after from inflammation of the lungs. A charge of murder occasionally would discourage Tohungaism. <i>(Mar 7, 1908,NZ Truth,142/1/4</i>)

>  It is not many years since a tohunga in the North Island materially reduced the Maori population by applying the same treatment to every patient I brought to him. ...Naturally, the cemetery began to assume a busy appearance, and the practice of his profession by the tohunga was cut short by a charge of manslaughter. <i>(Jan 1913,NZ Truth,397/5/6</i>)

>  When a party goes out eel-fishing at night, the old man of the band, the kaumatua or tohunga, divests himself of his clothes and goes first to the river-bank. <i>(May 10, 1920,Quick March,38</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> T.W. Ratana was a direct descendent of a <i>tohunga</i> and although he opposed tohungaism ... . <i>(1972,Ratana,48</i>) [NOTES: 1925 July 23 United Press in 1963 Henderson Ratana (1972) 48]

>  tohunga <i>n</i>. a priest; a prophet; a wise man. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,42</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> All this became a bit of a nuisance for Tupara, the local tohunga, that old cure-all and fortune-teller. <i>(1944,Brown Man's Burden,14,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <i>tohunga</i> , expert: hence, tohunga-karakia, priest; tohunga-moko, tattooing expert; tohunga-whakairo, carving-expert; tohunga-makutu, devil-doctor. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/147</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> <i>tohunga</i> an expert in folk medicine or magic <i>Ibid.</i> 236 These [Otaki Maori] people tend to make a distinction along these lines between the semi-respectable curing-tohunga and the completely unrespectable sorcerer-tohunga. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,xxii</i>)

>  PRIEST. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  The Maori name for the priest, prophet or `medicine-man' of the tribe. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),415</i>)

>  The Maori name for the priest, prophet or `medicine-man' of the tribe. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),415</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The ministers of religion were the <i>tohunga</i>, who were usually chiefs, though they did not form a hereditary or distinct caste. <i>Ibid.</i> 302 [Maori glossary] <i>tohunga</i> Wizard; priest; skilled person. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,22</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>tohunga</i>: priest, expert <i>(1963,O Te Raki,195</i>)

>  <i>tohunga n.</i> Maori name for wise man, magician. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> <i>(1967,Evil Day,382</i>)

>  <i>tohunga ... n</i>. Maori word for wise man, magician [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> tohunga: traditionally an expert, learned man or priest; in recent time a faith-healer. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,142</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>Tohunga</i>: Maori WITCHDOCTOR, priest, wise man, scholar, etc. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,104</i>)

>  <i>tohunga</i> n. N.Z. a Maori priest, the repository of traditional lore. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1231</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 808; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 505]]

>  TOHUNGA Priest. Mr Manihera held one of the most prestigious positions in Maoridom as tohunga (high priest of knowledge) in the Mataatua canoe tribes <i>(Jul 6, 1983,Dominion,4</i>)

>  The young Maori who stole the sacred pouwhenua from outside the Tauranga Town Hall on Friday night is risking ostracism and death, says respected Maori leader Sir Graham Latimer. Sir Graham has issued a special plea to the young man to return the pouwhenua (a sacred post) and to consult a tohunga (Maori spiritual advisor). <i>(Sep 20, 1987,Sunday News,1</i>)

>  Educated Maoris favour the new order, and impute the national decay [in health] to modern tohungas, who are but imposters; ... <i>(Jul 1910,Kai Tiaki,103</i>)

>  The taiaha Barney wields in the challenge has been ritually blessed by tohunga Oho Brown. <i>(Jun 4, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> The tohunga is a very wairua person, trained.. to know what sort of bush plants to look for, and how to give it to a person. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,56</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> Kawiti, who is himself a tohunga, throws a rakau for his own path. <i>(1973,Old New Zealand,255</i>) [NOTES: Original edition 1863]

>  I have met with scares which.. brought home to me.. that it be a criminal offence, punishable with hard-labour imprisonment, to practice tohungaism, boast of a tohungaistic reputation, apply to a tohunga for illegal operations, and learning the incantations, for the latter is the tap root of the upas tree. <i>(Feb 11, 1905,NZ Herald,Suppl. 1</i>)

>  A CENTENARIAN TOHUNGA FOUND ALIVE AT WAIROA. <i>(Jun 15, 1886,Waikato Times,2</i>)

>  <b>ELDER</b> <i>Atua</i> had then, as it was conceived, entered into him. Hence he was taboo (<i>tapu</i>) himself, or a sacred person. No New Zealander was permitted to come near him except the togungha (<i>tohunga</i>) or priest and those of his own family. <i>(Mar 1815,Marsden's Lieutenants,77,1934 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> ... he was told by the <i>tohunga</i>, or priest, that if he presumed to put one finger to his mouth before he had completed the work ... the Etua would certainly punish his impious contempt, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/192 ... I who was not a <i>tohungha ... </i> <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/174</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Toh|unga, <i>s</i>. A priest, a mechanic, a skilful man <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,215</i>)

>  <b>CRAIK</b> ... he was told by the <i>tohunga</i>, or priest, that if he presumed [153] to put one finger in his mouth ... the Atua (divinity) would certainly punish his impious contempt ... <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,152</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> When in want of my breakfast I was told that fire and water were<i> tapu'</i>d and that none must eat or drink until ... the <i>Tohunga</i> or priest was in preparation for the ceremony at a short distance. <i>(Mar 4, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,228,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... he informed me that both fire and water was tapued by the tohunga, or priest, who was busy at preparing an incantation near the Wai-tapu [a2-acute], situated among a grove of cabbage palm-trees without the fence. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/130</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> A Priest (<i>Tohunga</i>) once [248] established, becomes a person of much importance among his countrymen. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/247</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> An old Tohunga, or priest, was therefore persuaded to show me the way ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/141</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The New Zealanders are a superstitious people and many amongst them (Tohungas) or wise men can foretell events.... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,95,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> He is the tohunga, or priest, of the family; ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/247</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> It required the intervention of a `tohunga' or priest, to remove this interdict. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,49</i>)

>  But the <i>Daedalus</i> [in 17??] succeeded in taking two Maoris on board, a warrior and a tohunga [to teach flax-dressing] - rather unfortunate choices when it is considered that flax-dresssing was neither toa nor tohunga work but wahine work. <i>(1971,NZ Heritage,1/277</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> ... our great Chief, who is also a `Tohunga' (Priest). As the tohunga had slept and eaten in this dwelling, it was considered very `tapu.' <i>(Dec 31, 1856,Report</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Dec 31 T.S.Grace Report of Taupo District in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 67]

>  The Tohunga (priest) predicts in doleful accents the ultimate extinction of the Maori race, ... <i>(Aug 15, 1857,Lyttelton Times,3</i>)

>  A <i>tohunga</i> has been at work exorcising evil spirits from the <i>wahi tapu. </i> <i>(Sep 20, 1857,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/309</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  <b>MANING</b> I sat in the doorway, and soon perceived that my visitor was a famous <i>tohunga</i>, or priest, and who also had the reputation of [142] being a witch [sic] of no ordinary dimensions. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,141</i>)

>  A Tohunga, a priest, prophet, or sorcerer, had told the Maories that they should make no stand at Mere-Mere - ... <i>(1864,Von Tempsky Memoranda,69</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> <i>(1872,Ranolf and Amohia,102</i>)

>  <b>NORRIS</b> The old<i> tohunga </i>still kept his evil eye on them. <i>(1874,Among the Maoris,125</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> Spiritism is a very old institution in Maori-land, and conversations with departed spirits, through the medium of the `tohunga,' were held ... prior to the arrival of the Pakeha. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,45</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> ... Kangapo the `Tohunga'[o-acute] - a Priest/ And fell magician famous far and near;/ <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/151</i>)

>  All the witnesses on one side declare the testatrix was `porangi' (mad). The other side say she was a `tohunga' and a prophet and had gifts of healing ... <i>(Jun 18, 1891,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/583</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maori word for a wise man. `Perhaps from Maori verb tohu,</i> to think.' (Tregear's `Polynesian Dictionary.') <i>Tohu</i>, a sign or omen; hence <i>Tohunga</i>, a dealer in omens, an augur. <i>(1898,Austral English,473</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> ... it was for another, the wizard or priest <i>(tohunga)</i> to prescribe the remedy. <i>Ibid. 124 After these [sc. the chiefs] ranked the ordinary `professional' <i>(tohunga)</i> who though generally a priest or wizard needed not always to be a priest, but must be a `skilled person' either in witchcraft, canoe-making, house-building, tattooing, etc. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,20</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a reputedly wise person or intellectual leader (transferred use)

>  [poem]<i>The Rival Tohungas</i>. Tohungas white, Tohungas brown ... . <i>(Dec 30, 1905,NZ Truth,28/5/4</i>)

>  It was not so very long ago that I heard these two gentlemen characterized in this Chamber as political tohungas. That term was applied to them by the late Premier, and I have not read in <i>Hansard </i>any contradiction by either of these two gentlemen of that term so applied to them, so I suppose it sticks down to the present time. <i>(Jul 19, 1907,NZ Parliamentary Debates,139/518</i>) [NOTES: (Apirana Ngata on the Tohunga Suppression Bill)]

>  Tawhiao, who appeared, as did the other natives present, greatly impressed with the method adopted, said if the Pakeha "Tohunga" had proposed to rectify the cast in his eye, or to change his hair from grey to a dark color, he would not have agreed, as people knew him by these signs, but that it was good for the "Tohunga" to take away the pains of his limbs. <i>(Oct 30, 1884,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

### tohungaess
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Some years ago a famous tohunga makutu ... opposed his craft to that of an equally famous tohungaess of the Maniapoto ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,262</i>)

>  From casual enquiries I find that a young woman, believing that the makutu spell had been laid upon her, came in to consult a noted tohungaess to have a malison removed, but recrimination ensuing the hocus pocus ended in the afflicted fiercely attacking the diviner and becoming insane, which insanity then spread to the remainder of the family of four, including the father and son-in-law. <i>(Jan 24, 1905,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

### tohungaism
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical, derogatory) sorcery or faith healing as formerly practised by (or alleged against) tohunga. [also as tohunga-ism; apparently a cause for concern among Pakeha c.1900, the word being frequently used in newspapers, etc.; a Tohunga Suppression Act was passed in 1908.]

>  One of Ratana's ambitions is to destroy the tohungaism prevalent among the native people. <i>(Jan 5, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,42</i>)

>  I have met with scares which.. brought home to me.. that it be a criminal offence, punishable with hard-labour imprisonment, to practice tohungaism, boast of a tohungaistic reputation, apply to a tohunga for illegal operations, and learning the incantations, for the latter is the tap root of the upas tree. <i>(Feb 11, 1905,NZ Herald,Suppl. 1</i>)

>  The Tohunga Suppression Act of 1908 is now to be repealed although some Maoris say that tohungaism still exists. <i>(Nov 1, 1962,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: ca1962 Nov 1 NZBS News (9.00 a.m.)]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> [Canon Williams] said the decline of the [Ratana] Movement had produced a marked increase of <i>tohunga</i>-ism. <i>(1963,Ratana,72,1972 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WEBSTER</b> At [the Anglican Synod, Napier, 1907] the Rev. F.W. Chatterton moved "That this Synod views with satisfaction the recent action of the Government in the direction of the suppression of <i>Tohungaism, ... </i> <i>(1979,Rua,225</i>)

>  Tohungaism still flourishes among Maoris in Taranaki, according to evidence given at an inquest into the death of a Maori child, Marlene Davis, aged 17 months, at Hawera, states a New Plymouth correspondent. <i>(Jul 24, 1942,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Tohungaism is not a lawful means of subsistence. <i>(Oct 1, 1904,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  <b>Michael King</b> Although emphatically Maori in his use of language and metaphor, [Ratana] rejected many traditional practices and values such as tribalism, tangihanga, tapu, tohunga-ism and carving. <i>(2003,Penguin History of New Zealand,291</i>)

>  <b>14/4/6</b> Some of the Pow Wows, in their anxiety for the welfare of the Maoris, rushed in with a proposal to stamp out Tohungaism from amongst them. <i>(Sep 23, 1905,NZ Truth</i>)

>  ... convert [Maoris] ... from heathenism and tohungaism. <i>(Dec 9, 1905,NZ Truth,25/5/6</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> In my recent outback explorations I have met with scenes which horrified ... and brought home to me ... the imperative necessity ... that it be a criminal offence, punishable with hard labour imprisonment, to practise tohungaism, boast of tohungaistic reputation, apply to a tohunga for illegal operations, and learning the incantations, ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,262</i>)

>  I am giving honourable members a definition of genuine tohungaism as it was known in the old days. <i>(Jul 19, 1907,NZ Parliamentary Debates,139/518</i>) [NOTES: (Apirana Ngata on the Tohunga Suppression Bill)]

>  The Maori Tohunga is a kind of bush Baptist who dips the community in a creek for every kind of ill. A girl named Kake was immersed up Hukerenui way, and died shortly after from inflammation of the lungs. A charge of murder occasionally would discourage Tohungaism. <i>(Mar 7, 1908,NZ Truth,142/1/4</i>)

>  Our readers will remember a most interesting article from her pen on `Maori Customs and Tohungaism.' <i>(Jan 1911,Kai Tiaki,38</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> Tohungaism, and the practice of <i>makutu</i>, ... are all practically dead ... <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,77</i>)

>  It was the voice of prophecy! Tohungaism! / With a cry of surprise the girl let the tiki slip from her hand. <i>(1939,Silhouettes of the Past,170</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> <i> tohungaism</i>, derivative from tohunga.[ital. req last word???] <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  A man who said he was a Roman Catholic by faith claimed that he had been instantly cured of chest trouble.. by [accused], charged.. with practising the cult of tohungaism. <i>(Nov 9, 1955,NZ Truth,13</i>)

>  At the recent Te Aute College Conference at Gisborne (writes a correspondent of the Hastings <i>Standard</i>) a paper was read from an old Te Aute student, T. Halbert, pointing out the different barriers a Maori meets in his career of life. They are: (1) Tangihangas or "wakes," (2) huis, (3) Tohungaism, (4) drunkenness. <i>(Dec 31, 1897,Timaru Herald,4</i>)

### tohungaistic
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  I have met with scares which.. brought home to me.. that it be a criminal offence, punishable with hard-labour imprisonment, to practice tohungaism, boast of a tohungaistic reputation, apply to a tohunga for illegal operations, and learning the incantations, for the latter is the tap root of the upas tree. <i>(Feb 11, 1905,NZ Herald,Suppl. 1</i>)

### toi
 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>TOI E</b>conomic <b>D</b>evelopment <b>A</b>gency toi-EDA <i>Wanted: Personal Associate to CEO</i> If you .. love the <b>Toi - Eastern Bay of Plenty</b> sunshine, and have trusted track record, mauria mai - please apply. <i>(Aug 26, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,38</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [toetoe](../T#toetoe))

>  This arrangement of the toi clumps ... seemed to have disturbed all the insects ... <i>(1898,TrNZI,30/416</i>) [NOTES: Listed in databse under headword "toe" - check]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Barbarea <i>australis,</i> Hook. fil. ... Nat. name, `Toi', <i>Colenso. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 14</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Toi, <i>Col[enso]. Barbarea vulgaris. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  Also, the cooked leaves and herbaceous tops of [32] the Toi <i>(Barbarea Australis)</i>, and of the Poroporo, or Raupeti <i>(Solanum nigrum)</i>; and the baked inner stems and sago-like pith of the large black fern tree, Korau, or Mamaku <i>(Cyathea medullaris). </i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Toi (<i>Cordyline indivisa</i>) is a more ornamental, rarer, and smaller species of `cabbage-tree'; ... The name of Toi likewise belongs to a herb (<i>Barbarea vulgaris</i>), the leaves of which are eaten like cabbage or spinach. <i>Ibid.</i>2/211 To them [greens] may be added the Toi (<i>Barbarea vulgaris</i>), a herb which served the ancient Maori as cabbage. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/192</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> BARBAREA VULGARIS (<i>Linn</i>.) The Common Herb of St. Barbara. ... This herb is indigenous to the Northern Island, and was formerly used by the natives as food. Upon the authority of Mr. Collenso, the native name of this plant is `Toii'. <i>Ibid.</i> 178 Toii [Maori name] .. Cress [Settlers' name] .. Barbarea Australis .. Herb <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,17</i>)

### toi iho
 
 <i>n.</i> (trademark) (more fully toi iho Maori Made) a registered design mark used to identify and promote authentic and quality Maori arts and crafts. [ORIGIN: Maori, Ryan toi 'art' iho 'heart, essence, etc./ see www.toiiho.com]

### toi moko
 
 <i>n.</i> a preserved tattooed head
