>  A preserved tattooed head has come home after at least a century overseas. The toi moko had been part of a collection in the Ethnographic Museum in Buenos Aires, Argentina. <i>(May 25, 2003,BLANK ENTRY,A3</i>)

### Toi Whakaari
 
 <i>n.</i> The New Zealand Drama School [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  He graduated with an English degree from the University of Otago in 1988 and from Toi Whakari: New Zealand Drama School in 1990. <i>(Feb 7, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),66</i>)

>  100 students from the New Zealand School of Dance and Toi Whakaari: New Zealand Drama School will begin classes there in May. <i>(Apr 1998,North & South,24</i>)

>  Storytelling, warmth and humour combine as students of the Toi Whakaari: NZ Drama School acting and enternainment technology courses present the New Zealand premiere of Lanford Wilson's <i>Book of Days" at Te Whaea in Newtown at 8pm tonight. <i>(Oct 28, 2003,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  [Advt] Toi Whakaari new zealand drama school congratulates our grads on their nominations <i>(Dec 4, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### toi-grass
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [TOI GRASS] The outside walls and roof are closely ... thatched with Toi grass. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/174</i>) [NOTES: (Travers) Listed in database under "toe-grass"]

>  By means of a green blade of the toi grass, held between the teeth, a weka's cry of distress is imitated almost to perfection. <i>(Jul 9, 1910,Evening Post,13</i>)

### toi-palm
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> In spite of this warning I climbed down a steep bank to the cave, but found no bones, only a beautiful group of dragon-palms - a rare coincidence. The leaves of these <i>Dracaenae</i> - called by the Maoris tohi-palms - possess flexible fibres out of which the natives weave their waterproof rain-mats (<i>pureke</i>). <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,176,1933 ed.</i>)

### toi-roa
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [toheroa](../T#toheroa))

### toi-toi
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) long fibre from the native toi-toi, used as a thatch. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>SEWELL</b> Wooden slabs and clay - Toi toi grass for thatch are the materials. The result is a second rate kind of farm outhouse. <i>(Mar 12, 1856,Sewell Journal 1853-1857,208</i>) [NOTES: in McIntyre (1980) Sewell Journal 1853-1857]

>  <b>HEWETT</b> .. twenty of them were thatching the roof of a building close to our house with toi-toi (like pampas grass). <i>(1910,Looking Back,19</i>)

>  <b>JAMIESON</b> Then they built [in 1850] two primitive toi toi tents or huts and another on piles, called the whata, a place for stores. For several months they lived in those toit toi tents. In April, 1951, my second brother, John Donald was born in mother's toi toi home. [toi-toi] <i>(1916,Old Memories,40</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>To&#769;.i-to&#769;.i</i> (t&omacr.&#769;.e.-t&omacr.e.;<i> colloq.</i> toi&#769;.toi&#769;.), <i>n. (Bot.) </i>[Written also <i>toe-toe</i>.] Either pf two species of a tall reedy grass of New Zealand (<i>Arundo conspicua</i> and <i>A. fulvida</i>); - called also <i>toi-toi grass. [Local, N. Z.] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a native grass, Cortaderia spp, with fluffy plumed flowers. [toe-toe]

>  <b>FULLER</b> In breaking up the wild waste land, bullocks are generally preferred, particularly if it should be strong land which is being broken up, because the flax, toi-toi and tutu roots check horses too much ... <i>(1859,Five Years in NZ,130</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS & TURNER</b> When high country settlement began in the mid-nineteenth century flax, toi-toi and snow tussock of enormous size grew in profusion ... <i>(1983,NZ High Country Four Seasons,19</i>)

### toi/toii
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) broad-leaved or mountain cabbage tree

>  Toii prefers cool, moist conditions and can be difficult in areas with hot, dry summer days or prolonged humidity with heat. <i>(Dec 2006,Growing Today,30</i>)

>  We had travelled on till dark, and there was neither <i>toi</i>, flax, nor sticks to build a shelter; ... <i>(Mar 8, 1851,Lyttelton Times,7</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> In mountainous parts of the country where flax was scarce the <i>toi</i> (Cordyline indivisa) was found extremely useful. <i>Ibid.</i> 238 The Tuhoe tribe found a substitute for flax in the leaves of the mountain cabbage-tree (<i>toi</i>: C. indivisa). <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,225</i>)

>  <b>BINNEY et al.</b> Toi-tree (<i>Cordyline indivisa</i>) <i>(1979,Mihaia,202</i>)

>  <i>(1871,AJHR,F-I/43</i>) [NOTES: 1871 AJHR F-I, 43 Gilbert Mair in 1979 Webster Rua 82]

>  The tap-root of the <i>toi (Cordyline indivisa)</i> was eaten formerly, as also the young undeveloped leaves. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/110</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Cordyline indivisa, Steud.</i>] ... <i>Toii. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,707</i>)

>  <b>WEBSTER</b> A feature of the high ranges of the Huiarau is the toii (<i>Cordyline indivisa</i>), a variety of cabbage tree which grows at a much higher altitude than the well known <i>Cordyline australis.</i> <i>(1979,Rua,81</i>)

### toiki
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  When a tiger-shark was hooked, or a large toiki (a much larger species than the dogfish), the lines and anchor were quickly hauled in. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/601</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> bronze whaler, whaler shark [toiki]

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BRONZEWHALER [<i>Carcharinus brachyurus</i>] ... Other names are whaler, New Zealand whaler, and toiki (Maori). <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,12</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> NEW ZEALAND WHALER <i>Galeolamna brachyurus</i>: A peculiarly New Zealand variety of the Whaler shark frequently taken up north. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,219</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BRONZEWHALER SHARK ... <i>Other names: Carcharinus brachyurus</i>; New Zealand whaler, whaler shark, toiki (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,177</i>)

### toiler
 
 <i>n.</i> a hard, persistent or conscientious worker; grafter (often qualified by great, hard, real) [ca1935 Havelock e.g.: He drinks, never pays his bills, but I'll say this for him, he's a great toiler]

>  `Truth' is the toilers' paper. <i>(Jan 1906,NZ Truth,29/5/1</i>)

>  `Truth' is the toilers' paper. <i>(Jan 6, 1906,NZ Truth,29/5/1</i>)

>  His irreverance [sic] opened by smooging to the toiler with the remark: "Whatever is said of the labah [sic] and Socialistic movements, there is in the heart of them a devine [sic] fire.' which is unadulterated flapdoodle and a saintly method of expressing an obvious platitude. <i>(Jul 25, 1908,NZ Truth,162/4/5</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> The only reason they do it is because Peter is a toiler, and puts in solid permanent improvements in the way of stumping and draining. <i>(1924,Follow the Call,12,1975 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> A <i>toiler </i>or<i> battler</i> is a hard, conscientious worker - both are used with a shade of condescension; <i>battler</i> is also found in Glasgow slang, but there it has the signification of a gangster who fights with his fists. <i>(1943,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,88</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A great toiler ... <i>(1949,Wayleggo,120</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> He was a great toiler, strong as an ox, heaving huge carcases around as if they were bags of paper. <i>(1969,Awatea,110,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> As a toiler Jack Hutley could be classed with Scut Cheeseman, but in a different field. He had been an oyster picker and commercial [40] fisherman working out of Auckland. <i>(1970,Way Up North,39</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> I heard of one helluva tough bushman, Charlie Moore - he and his brother Bill were the two of greatest toilers I have ever seen .. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,64</i>) [NOTES: (Bob Edwards, bushman)]

>  Whene'er you see amid the throng/A fellow toiler slip,/Just give him, as you pass along,/A brave and kindly grip. <i>(Aug 4, 1877,Otago Witness,19</i>)

>  To the toiler for bread, by necessity led,/Life wends its monotonous way <i>(Apr 12, 1918,Thames Star,4</i>)

>  The average Australian was a great toiler. He did not want the dole or charity; all he wanted was work. <i>(Aug 2, 1932,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  The Otago digger, under every phase of his own clause, is allowed nearly twice the area of ground for mining on that is allotted to his brother toiler, whose fate it is to delve on the ancient terraces of the wide renowned Wakamarina. <i>(Sep 17, 1864,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  Variety is the order of the day, and often the tired toiler requires something tasty to induce him to eat, and at the same time enjoy his food. <i>(Sep 4, 1890,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  So I threw out with nicest discerning / (Proof, the toiler was worthy of his wages) / The choicest tit-bits of my learning, / Choicely culled from the classical pages ... <i>(Apr 19, 1856,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  To get away from the din of city life, from the perpetual tax and possible worry of office or business cares, and go into camp away up among the mountains, where the scenery is grand, the air the purest, with no wire or mail to remind one of what the grinding world is doing, is no small bliss in itself to the real toiler. <i>(Apr 1, 1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,498</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport) a hard `grafting' forward [rugby]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> You'll beat Tuarau without him. ... Besides MacFarlane is a real toiler. He'll be a good substitute. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,114</i>)

>  Mr Smith, a vascular surgeon in Auckland, played in the same University teams as Mr Tapsell in Dunedin. `He was as hard as anyone,' he said, `but was a very fair player. Certainly not vicious. He was a good honest toiler.' <i>(Aug 12, 1986,Dominion,5</i>)

>  Huia are happy-go-luckys [sic] of the competition and play brilliant to bad football. Their average age must be about 30. "Rangi" Hopa and Murphy will hook for the reps. Whaki is a great toiler. <i>(Jun 5, 1930,NZ Truth,19</i>)

>  A great toiler in the tight, he is never far from the ball, and in the open he has the pace and ability of a back. <i>(Jul 20, 1940,Evening Post,15</i>)

### toitoi
 
 <i>n.</i> a univalve (See [also Cook's turban shell](../A#also-cook's-turban-shell))

>  <b>POLACK</b> This repast consisted of three different kinds of shellfish, the large muscle, or uru roa [a-acute]; cockles, or the toi; and iwirou; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/93</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [includes all toe/toi/tohi/tohe etc as attrib] (See [toetoe](../T#toetoe))

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... an enormous fire was kindled in the centre of a <i>tohi-tohi</i> house that constituted the chapel, ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/103</i>)

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> The house was what is called a toi-toi whare; the toi-toi is a reed which the natives use for the walls and roofs of their huts; these reeds are plaited together with flax; ... The New Zealanders will build a house in this manner for a white man for five pounds. [fn] Whare is the native name for house. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,22</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> But here, a little stream meandered under cream-plumed shimmer of huge toi-toi bushes, crimson-billed pukekos with thoughtful expressions waded downstream, and a dart of wings meant riri-riro, the Maori's little bird of laughter, adventuring in the sunlit spaces. <i>(1934,Journalese,109</i>)

>  The church had been beautifully decorated with belladonna lilies, small white clematis, and toitoi grass by Mrs. Mathew Holmes, sister of the bridegroom, and the wedding music was played by Mrs. Whitehouse. <i>(Sep 24, 1921,Evening Post,9</i>) [NOTES: (nb: date entered in database was 224/9/1921)]

>  The vestibule was arranged with comfortable chairs and sofas for sitting out, and decorated with leaves and clumps of toitoi grass. <i>(Jun 8, 1934,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  The first adventure of the married couple was an attempt at farming on some poor land at Whiteman's Valley, where they supplemented a meager income by charcoal burning, selling toitoi grass for thatching, etc. <i>(Apr 17, 1943,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  .. about 9 o'clock I went on shore at a potatoe [sic] garden called Karaka, from which I had a fine view of the toi toi flat, which extends from Riwa Riwa to near the foot of the Taraua ... The natives informed me that when the heavy Waipuki or freshlet comes, the water leaves the river at some distance up and rushes across this flat towards kare Kare, but at present it is quite dry and not of a swampy nature as the generality of the toi toi land about this part of the country. <i>(Jun 29, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  A three-year-old child of Henry Collins, at Hukeranui, died from injuries sustained through falling into a burning toitoi bush. <i>(Nov 4, 1891,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Miss Barber has secured some really fine genuine ancient Maori carvings for her Maori whare, the walls of which are toitoi reeds, laced together in the geniune old manner. <i>(Jun 15, 1901,Evening Post,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a bird

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|oi t|oi; A bird so called; also a spattle. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,215</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [toi-toi, toi toi, toie toie]

>  <b>FRAME</b> They came to the rubbish dump, the stink and filth of it, with the toi-toi like a fringe of shawl. <i>Ibid.</i> Mrs Harlow, who was a light woman, curved like a feather, with hair yellow like toi-toi, looked in the door at them. <i>(1960,Owls Do Cry,48</i>)

>  He sat on an upright chair beneath the vase of toitoi provided by Aunt... <i>(Aug 25, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),98</i>)

>  There are five native species [sc. of tussock grass], commonly known by their Maori name, toetoe, often misspelt "toitoi". <i>(Aug 27, 1999,Dominion,14</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Toe-toe</i> and <i>Toi-toi</i>, Maori name of several species of native grass of the genus <i>Arundo,</i> especially <i>Arundo conspicua</i>, A. Cunn. <i>Toe-toe</i> is the right spelling in Maori, given in Williams' `Maori Dictionary.' In English, however, the word is frequently spelt <i>toi-toi</i>. It is also called<i> Prince of Wales feather. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,472</i>)

>  <b>ALEXANDER & CURRIE</b> <i>Toi</i>: a New Zealand pampas grass. <i>(1906,Treasury of NZ Verse,288</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> Below us lay a shivering mass of white native blossom - a little tree touched with scarlet - a clump of toi-toi waving in the wind - and looking [35] for all the world like a family of little girls drying their hair - <i>(1907,Urewera Notebook,34</i>) [NOTES: 1907 Mansfield Urewera Notebook (1978 Gordon) 34-35]

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> Comfortable barracks had been erected of toi-toi and flax, so, on the arrival of the colonists in 1848 [in Otago] they were agreeably surprised at such preparations. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,118</i>)

>  <i>Toe toe</i>.- I am told this is the correct spelling, not<i> toi toi</i>, which is more usual. <i>Toi toi</i> means a cockabully. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland)]

>  <b>WIGLEY</b> The waving wheat, the nor'west heat,/ .../ The toi-toi's quiver, the swollen river,/ <i>(1934,Some Southern Songs,11</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> Earp spells the word tohitohi, and even to the present day it is spelt toi toi as often as the correct toe toe. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,277</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> ... below the clearing is a sea of flax and toi-toi where long-legged pukeko wade in scummy pools. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,90,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> toitoi: generally toetoe, plumed grass similar to pampas grass. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,273,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> Sometimes the whare is built of large `Slabs";[sic cap] sometimes of toi-toi (a sort of bulrush), sometimes of wattled clay; and sometimes of brushwood. <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,140</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> He soon drew far enough ahead of his pursuers to double back on his own tracks to an area of toi toi, and take off at a different angle along a seemingly unused track, ... <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,192</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> toi-toi: a grass, with tall flower heads, that grows in big clumps. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,304</i>)

>  <i>toe-toe, toi-toi n.</i> several species of tall New Zealand grass [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>toe-toe, toi-toi ... n</i>. several species of tall N.Z. grass [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> toi-toi <i>tall tussocky grass with whitish-yellow plumes </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,189</i>)

>  <i>toi-toi n.</i> a tall New Zealand grass with feathery seed-heads. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1231</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 809; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 505]]

>  <b>SEWELL</b> We are on open common with Flax, Tu-tu, Toi-toi and coarse grass. <i>(1853,Journal,1/423,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>(1867,Rambles with a Philosopher,111</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  Arundo conspicua Toi toi <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/127</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>MONEY</b> The work was ... removing clumps of toi-toi and Spaniards with sharp adzes, and cutting the scrub, flax, etc., with bill-hooks, ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,70</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> The whole [of the temporary Maori shelter] was then covered by layers of <i>toi-toi</i>, a tall fibrous rush-plant resembling pampas grass, tied in position with the ubiquitous <i>harakiki</i> (New Zealand flax) <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,89,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WALLACE</b> The two most objectionable WEEDS [bold] ... on [reclaimed Hawkes Bay swamp] land are (1) the Sedge Toi-toi (<i>Cyperus ustulatus</i>), a tufty or tussocky plant with very sharp leaves, which naturally grows on the rich dry land surrounding swamps; ... <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,243</i>)

>  Where Christmas lilies wave and blow,/Where fan-tails tumbling glance,/ And plumed toi-toi heads the dance. <i>(Dec 19, 1895,Otago Witness,6</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  We think of Mansfield's clump of toi-toi "waving in the wind and looking for all the world like a family of little girls drying their hair". <i>(Oct 23, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

>  .. there shall be levied in respect of every building constructed wholly, or in part, of raupo, nikau, toitoi, wiwi, kakaho, straw, or thatch of any description, and situate within the boundaries so defined, the sum of twenty pounds. <i>(May 19, 1843,NZ Colonist,2</i>)

>  On the south-western shores of Tarawera there was a sparse growth of tutu and toitoi. <i>(Dec 22, 1927,Evening Post,13</i>) [NOTES: (nb: date originally entered into database was 22/13/1927)]

>  There are few plants which are suitable for hedges in such exposed positions, but native flax and toitoi make quite good shelter where space permits their use. <i>(Oct 9, 1941,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  ".. the Towi-towi, a reed, whose cane-coloured seed-stem frequently rises to the height of six feet, from a whirl of leaves four feet in length " <i>(Feb 16, 1929,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: Reprinted from a newspaper of 1853]

>  There was no sound, just the sight of this dark figure pushing out of that yellowish dawn fog, marching the empty asphalt past clumps of jaded toitoi at the side of the road. <i>(Dec 28, 2003,Sunday Star Times,D12</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> ... the Thatch is the Toie Toie [toetoe] a kind of Flags that is used for he Huts, and an other one more like what our Coopers use for putting between the seams of a Cask, called Rappoo [<i>raupo</i>]. <i>(1834,New Zealanders at Gallipoli,35,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... small uneven-shaped sticks, fastened with flax withes against the inverted poles, are then covered with rushes, over which a spear-grass (toitoi) is plentifully added, which ensures the place as water-proof. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/208</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... fern, flax, toi toi and tutu. There is plenty of birch on the hillsides. <i>(Apr 8, 1840,Diary,1839 ed.</i>)

>  `Section 2 .. .. there shall be levied in respect of every building constructed wholly or in part of <i>raupo, nikau, toitoi, wiwi, kakaho</i>, straw or thatch of any description [ .. .. [pounds]20].' [1861, 1867 tohi, 1892, 1895] [a number of quotations] <i>(1843,NZ Legislative Council</i>) [NOTES: 1843. `An Ordinance for imposing a tax on Raupo Houses, Session II. No. xvii. of the former Legislative Council of New Zealand': [From A. Domett's collection of Ordinances, 1850.]]

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> Wherever I observed the Tea-Tree frequent ... the land appeared superior; its growth a mixed one of <i>Phormium tenax</i>, fern, Toi-Toi[sic], and a coarse sedge-grass. <i>(Apr 1844,Diary,205</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Apr Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 205]

>  Walked over low swampy land with creeks, etc., fine good soil - flax, toi-toi, grass, etc. <i>(Dec 17, 1848,Torlesse Papers,42,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... very broken ground, covered with fern, toitoi, grass, and tall manuka. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,240</i>)

>  [Request for tenders for the supply of bedding to the Colonial Hospital] ... 10 Mattresses, 10 Bolsters - Raupo or Toitoi. <i>(Jun 7, 1851,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

>  [The Waikato] runs through ... deep fern fringed with raupo (a bullrush) ...[sic] and the magnificent toi-toi, a gigantic grass whose flower stalk is from 8 to 12 feet and has an exquisite feather of two feet long playing in every air. This grass is ... with the phormium tenax the most remarkable of the open country vegetable products. ... You see many native dwellings & many canoes for they are a most locomotive people, few as ignorant of all but their own kainga as our British Hodge. There are troops of wild ducks & shags, some hawks of large size, bitterns & smaller waterfowl unknown to me. <i>(Mar 25, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/80</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  There were.. swamps to be drained and thickets of flax and toitoi to be cleared. <i>(May 18, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,21</i>)

>  A man has been found after wandering in the bush for three weeks, during which time he subsisted on toitoi, reeds, and leaves. <i>(Jun 22, 1874,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  The schooner anchored in Denham Bay, and the men of the party commenced erecting temporary dwellings, whares of wood with nikau and toitoi. <i>(Oct 29, 1889,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  As a result of observations and feeding tests, Mr. W. Wakeman, of Pahiatua, claims that toitoi and cabmage [sic] tree leaves chaffed provide a fine fodder for stock. <i>(Sep 5, 1913,Grey River Argus,7</i>)

>  I watched a royal wedding scene. There were waving ferns, waving tussock and waving toitoi. Under the sea there was waving kelp. <i>(Sep 19, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),100</i>)

>  We reach shortly a long stretch of toi toi bordering both river banks. <i>(Jan 29, 1924,Otago Witness,69</i>)

>  My father has a large 'toi-toi' estate in the north. <i>(Jan 17, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,I/10 : 235</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <i> ... large numbers of aua or kataha</i> (Agonostoma forsteri); <i>about 60 lb. ... weight of pilchard or mohimohi (Clupea sagax)</i>, two varieties; ... hundreds of young red-cod, rarii <i>(Lotella bacchus)</i>, and what I believe are young rock-cod, or kokopu or rawaru<i> (Percis colias).</i> The red-cod were from 3 in. to 4 in. in length, and the rawaru, or, as the natives here call them, `toitoi' or `panepane', from 2 in. to 6 in. long <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/319</i>) [NOTES: Mair Fish in Piako River]

>  The bulley (Gobiomorphus gobioides Cuv. & Val.) is unknown outside New Zealand. <i>Ibid.</i> 8/296 -The bulley, or toitoi, is known from the Bay of Islands to the Bluff ... <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/294</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Gobiomorphus gobioides</i> (Cuv. & Val). BULLY; TOITOI or KOKOPU. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,49,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Toe toe.</i>-I am told this is the correct spelling, not<i> toi toi, </i>which is more usual. <i>Toi toi</i> means a cockabully. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland)]

>  <i>Gobiomorphus gobioides:</i> [European Name] Bully [Maori Name] Toitoi. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,73</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>Cockabully.</i> Often shortened to <i>bully</i>, especially by fishermen; <i>Gobiomorphus gobioides,</i> a small freshwater fish. The word is probably derived from kokopu, one of the Maori names for it. Another Maori name is toitoi. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,362</i>) [NOTES: [This entry not in Acland's gloss; added by HWO.]]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> BULLEY (TOITOI) <i>Gobiomorphus gobioides </i>(Cuv. and Val.) The bulley is one of the most abundant of our native freshwater fish. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,318</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 18. <i>Certhiparus Novae Seelandiae . Lafr. ...</i>Toe Toe of the natives of Dusky Bay.<i> Forst. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/189</i>) [NOTES: Gray 1842]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 27. CERTHIPARUS NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. Gml. Brown Creeper. Brown Linnet. Toitoi. <i>C. Maculicaudus.</i> Gray. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,11</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> CERTHIPARUS NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (NEW-ZEALAND CREEPER.) ... <i>Native names</i>.- Pipipi and Toitoi. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,105</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> ?a sea-fish

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> The toitoi is decribed by Captain Mair as a small blue fish, similar to those caught in lakes, but larger. They are fair eating, but full of bones - quite unlike the papanoko, ... <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,143</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 28. <i>Miro toitoi, ... </i>Nirungiru [sic] of the natives. <i>Polack. </i>Ngirungiru of the natives. <i>Yate, Dieffenbach. </i>To-i-toe of the natives. <i>Less. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/191</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/191 28]

>  <b>BULLER</b> CERTHIPARUS NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (NEW-ZEALAND CREEPER.) ... <i>Native names.</i>- Pipipi and Toitoi. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/51</i>)

### toity
 
 <i>n.</i> toilet

>  <i>toity</i> toilet, often <i>down the toity</i> to lose something, or fail; eg `There goes another Lotto ticket down the toity.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,McGILL,114 ed.</i>)

### tokahaere
 
 <i>n.</i> rock

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> TOKAHAERE MONUMENTS [heading] About Arohena in the King Country tall pillars stand like high monuments. Geologists term them residual columns of rhyolite rock. ... From 15 to 50 feet high ... the Maori calls them `tokahaere' <i>(1955,By Reef and Range,27</i>)

### toke
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|oke, s. ... a fish so called; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,215</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an earthworm

>  <b>FULLER</b> Toke or earthworms were considered a delicacy, eight different worms being known. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,10</i>)

### Toke
 
 <i>n.</i> a Tokelauan islander

>  In Auckland, for example, you can take one look at someone and say to yourself: Oh yeah, she's a Sa, he's a Raro, he's a Toke. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 11 : 67</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Tokoroa

>  Not bad for a girl from Toke who didn't really know what writers were until she was billeted with Marilyn Duckworth's daughter on a trip to Wellington in form two. <i>(Dec 23, 2000,Evening Post,20</i>)

### toke-tipa
 
 <i>n.</i> an earthworm

>  <b>BULLER</b> [In a Kiwi's gut was found] ... a round object, nearly as large as an ordinary marble, which proved to be the egg of the great earthworm toke-tipa. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/319</i>)

### tokee
 
 <i>n.</i> [empty headword] (See [toki](../T#toki))

### Tokelauan
 
 <i>n.</i> one born or resident in the Pacific islands of the Tokelaus. Tokelauan, adjective

### token
 
 <i>n.</i> the ears attached to a strip of skin taken to the tail as a voucher for payment --

>  The Government last year introduced a bounty of 2s. 6d. for every token representing an opossum killed. <i>(Dec 1952,Tararua Tramper,7</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,32</i>) [NOTES: [see LINE]]

>  <b>WATSON</b> They took the bounty off possum tokens while we were at Manarua - <i>(1965,Stand in the Rain,148</i>)

>  Hundreds of opossum tokens- the ears and a strip of the back - were hanging on the washing lines in front of a wharepuni. This old style Maori sleeping house ...seldom used since the `old people' died ... <i>(1978,Manawatu Tramping Club Jubilee,84</i>) [NOTES: [reference 1961]]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PINNEY</b> From June to the end of 1892, 87,696 rabbit tokens were counted at the [Benmore] station. <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,29</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a milk token

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>milk token</i>: ... each milman coins his own PLASTIC MONEY; good only for buying milk from him. You purchase this money from your local corner dairy ... and put it in the milk and cream bottles. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,66</i>)

>  [Hdg] Milk Tokens. The Department.. cannot deliver without milk tokens. <i>(Oct 22, 1945,NZEF Times,IV/225 : 5</i>)

>  Grocers' organisations in Wellington say that most of the city's grocery shops will stop selling milk tokens after the end of the month. <i>(Oct 11, 1952,Dominion,10</i>)

### tokepiripiri
 
 <i>n.</i> a wren

>  <b>BULLER</b> ACANTHISITTA CHLORIS \{ACANTHIDOSITTA CHLORIS\}. (RIFLEMAN.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Titipounamu, \{Kikimutu,\} Kikirimutu, Pihipihi, Piripiri, Tokepiripiri, and Moutuutu. [(1873) 113] The Rifleman is the smallest of our New-Zealand birds; ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,112,{1888 2nd edn 1/113, 114} ed.</i>)

### tokey
 
 <i>n.</i> [empty headword] (See [toki](../T#toki))

### toki
 
 <i>n.</i> . [trade axes later were iron heads only, seldom with handles; an adze; aka tochee, toghee, tokee, tokey]



 
 <i>n.</i> the Maori adze or axe, originally of stone; an axe or tomahawk. [Maori] [Williams 433 Toki (i) ... <i>Adze or axe </i>generally made of stone, possibly sometimes of shell.: early spg <i>e-to-ki, tochee, toghee, togie, tokee tokey, </i>]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the Isles of the South-Sea ... [English]<i> A Hatchet</i> ... [New-Zeeland] Toghee [o-grave] <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284</i>)

>  <b>COLLINS</b> E-to-ki, <i>An axe. </i> <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,561</i>)

>  <b>ELDER</b> Toki =adze; here used quite generally <i>(1819,Marsden's Lieutenants,158</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  We are much more likely to die of despair in Town at not being taken to Waitara than by bullet or `toki' if we are taken. [fn] Toki, an axe. <i>(Mar 1, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/530</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  [The old Maori's gun] was probably intended not to hit us but to frighten us into a fit state for nobbling with the toki. <i>(Apr 9, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/559</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> They used a variety of manual weapons, such as the mere, the hani, the hoeroa, the patu, the taiaha, the toki, etc. ... <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,249</i>)

>  When one of the guides told me that this toki was cermonial, to be used by the tohunga to make the first cut in a carved panel or a waka, I experienced a "he tangata" moment - it's all about people. <i>(Jul 10, 2011,Sunday Star Times,Arts F6</i>)

>  <b>BAYLY</b> The Indians are fond of green stone they have among them which they call Poanamo. With this they make their Togie, or ads of & chissles to carve with, & bobs for their years[ears] & gorgets to hang at the breast. <i>(Jun 4, 1773,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1773 June 4 Bayly's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/209]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|oki, <i>s</i>. An axe. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,216</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The name for the [stone] axe was toki, which is another name for rock or stone, and the adze was known as toki pu tangata [a2,a3-acute], or a warrior's stone battle axe. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/25</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The most valuable of Maori tools was the stone axe (<i>toki</i>) in some shape or dimension, for with its aid trees were felled ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,319</i>)

>  <b>JOSEPH</b> `The jade, the pounamu ... It's a beautiful stone ... Look,' he said, holding up a long hooked ear-drop of pearly translucence, `that's tau tau and the stone is called inanga. And this, the pekapeka - they say the shape comes from the bat. This is for the ear, too - the mako, like a shark's tooth ... And these,' - he ran his fingers along a row of ... nephrite adzes - `toki. See this one' - he held the heavy red-mottled adze in his palm - `that's the red kind, totoweka, woodhen's blood. The deep green, that's kawakawa.' <i>(1962,Pound of Saffron, A,113</i>)

>  <i>toki n</i>. Maori stone adze, hence Maori for steel axe. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> He [Marsden] observed ... that if he [the NZ chief] would order his people to supply them with pigs and flax, they should be regularly paid either in axes or tokees. <i>(Dec 17, 1814,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/82,1817 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> A Vocabulary of the Language of New Zealand. [...] Tochee, <i>A hatchet, or adze.</i> <i>(1770,Journal,126</i>)

>  Maori made a whole range of woodworking and wood and bone-carving tools - including adzes (toki), chisels (whao) and gouges - from pounamu. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/8</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> by transference from an iron axe (head), iron or an object of iron or steel, such as a nail or fish-hook (note that most early trade `axes' or `tomahawks' were the iron heads only without the handles [aka tochee, toghee, tokee, tokey]

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> Tokee ... Iron. <i>(1807,Some Account of NZ,77</i>)

>  <b>ELDER</b> An axe or a good tokee (<i>toki</i>) will purchase one and sometimes two good-sized pigs. <i>(Sep 6, 1814,Marsden's Lieutenants,59,1934 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1814 Sep 6 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 59]

>  <b>ELDER</b> He has exerted himself well since the <i>Active</i> has been in the Bay, and made in one week fifty-seven axes and tomahawks and ten flat tokees (<i>tokis</i>). <i>(Jul 6, 1815,Marsden's Lieutenants,102,1934 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> He [the chief] appeared quite tha man of business, all his ideas being completely absorbed in plans of procuring tokees and axes. <i>Ibid.</i> 243 =?iron nail (though wow is used of a nail 1/280 see <i>picaninny</i>) ... the chief, in allusion to our having no tokees to purchase his pigs with, cried out after me, `no tokee, no tokee, no porkee, no porkee,' laughing heartily at his own wit; ... <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/241</i>)

>  <b>ELDER</b> I made the chiefs a few presents of small tokeys (<i>toki</i>), axes, fish-hooks, etc., . <i>(1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,223,1934 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1819 Aug 2 William Hall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934)]

>  <b>CRUISE</b> Having got from one of the officers a small bit of iron, called by the natives a tokee, he threw it into his canoe.[which lay alongside???] <i>(May 20, 1820,Journal,101,1823, 1957 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [check May 20 or 21???]]

>  <b>BAILEY & ROTH</b> For toki, pigs, and murphies we exchange our traps, you know. <i>(1967,Shanties,19</i>) [NOTES: [?whalers' pidgin] (1836) 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties 19]

### TOKM
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Te Ohu Kai Moana, a Maori Fishing Statutory body

>  A new statutory body,Te Ohu Kai Moana, will hold the cash, capital shares with voting rights and 20% of the income shares... [The Treaty of Waitangi Commission] has done a good job of growing the Maori fishing industry but is now proposing to turn itself into a permanent body, TOKM. <i>(Aug 18, 2002,Sunday Star Times,E2</i>)

>  At last there's going to be a result, whether it's TOKM [Te Ohu Kai Moana, the commission] or the Iwi Forum's model. <i>(Sep 28, 2002,Dominion Post,A16</i>)

### toko
 
 <i>n.</i> punishment possibly here f. (or reinforced by) Maori <i>toko</i> `rod, staff' (Williams 434 (ii)), hence <i>the cane, the stick </i>

>  <b>COMBS</b> [The children] take what I gather they call `toko' with a stoicism bordering upon effrontery. <i>(1939,Harrowed Toad,27</i>)

### Toko Toko gold
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> The greengrocers in Newtown in Wellington groan with foreign produce - the rough red-brown taro root, the Toko Toko gold sweet potato, sugar cane, green bananas. <i>(1982,Other New Zealanders, The,10</i>)

### tokoeka
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i> Newly defined southern species of kiwi found in Haast, Fiordland, Stewart Island [also tokoweka]

>  <i>1.b. Apteryx australis australis</i> Shaw and Nodder, 1813<i> South Island Brown Kiwi (Tokoeka) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,15,1980 ed.</i>)

>  What has been your best moment as a DOC ranger? It has to be capturing a Haast tokoeka (kiwi). <i>(Mar 2002,Thin Green Line, The,13</i>)

>  Groups of tokoeka regularly forage for sandhoppers and other arthropods on beaches around the island, stabbing their beaks into the sand in pursuit of prey. <i>(Jan 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 45/70</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> APTERYX AUSTRALIS. (SOUTH-ISLAND KIWI.) ... <i>Native names</i>.- Kiwi and Tokoeka. ... The first example of the <i>Apteryx </i>of which there is any record was obtained in New Zealand about the year 1813, by Captain Barclay, of the ship `Providence' ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,364,{1888 2nd edn 2/322} ed.</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> SOUTH ISLAND KIWI, (<i>Apteryx australis</i>), Tokoeka of the Maoris is larger and more robust than the North Island species.... <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,76</i>)

>  Ornithologists have separated the brown kiwi into three subspecies: the North Island brown kiwi <i>Apteryx australis mantelli;</i> the South Island brown kiwi <i>A. a. australis</i>; and the Stewart Island brown kiwi <i>A. a. lawryi.</i> ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Rowi, Tokoeka</i>. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,38</i>)

>  The Conservation department had consulted Ngai Tahu and proposed to adopt nationally the tribe's traditional name of "tokoeka" for the southern species, he said. Tokoeka, literally translated as weka with a rod or walking stick, will be the name of the Haast, Stewart Island and Fiordland varieties of brown kiwi and the other [sic] will be the North Island and Okarito. <i>(Apr 23, 1993,Dominion,3</i>)

>  It's also the place many trampers report seeing the southern tokoeka - otherwise known as the brown kiwi. <i>(Mar 12, 2006,Sunday Star Times,Travel, p1</i>)

>  The Southern Tokoeka kiwis of this island [sc. Stewart Is] are famous for being active both day and night. <i>(Jun 8, 2002,Dominion,13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> South Island brown kiwi or the Stewart Island kiwi [Difference is unclear between these two headwords except that this one is more general and used before the specific naming of the newly defined species of the other headword. Also tokoweka.]

>  Another remarkable flightless bird ... is the member of the Apteryx family commonly called by naturalists the <i>`roa'.</i> The Maoris say that the proper name of this bird is the<i> `tokoweka'. </i> <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/338</i>)

>  All kiwi will put up a fight when cornered, but Stewart Island tokoeka - as large as great spotted kiwi - are particularly notorious. <i>(Jan 1, 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 45/91</i>)

>  And may we not therefore conclude that if the Maoris had known anything of the Dinornis, the present representatives of the genus, which in appearance, form, and plumage most probably closely resemble some of the extinct gigantic forms, would have in preference been named by them Moa-iti, or some similar appellation, instead of calling the Apterix owenii kiwi from its peculiar call, and the apteryx Australis, Tokoeka and Roa? <i>(Apr 8, 1871,Star, The,3</i>)

>  This kiwi, known as Tokoweka or Apteryx Maxima, is the largest of the kiwi family, and occupies the high country at the back. <i>(Nov 28, 1934,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  Indeed, it is now recognised that a number of related but genetically divergent varieties are clustered in the south-western corner of the country - the Stewart Island tokoeka, the Southern Fiordland tokoeka (found from Wilmot's Pass south), the Northern Fiordland tokoeka and the Haast tokoeka. <i>(Mar 2005,NZ Geographic,No. 72, p. 11</i>)

>  For less talk and more sense we might begin with the far south of this country, where fishermen see themselves as Stewart Islanders - not roa or tokoeka! <i>(Feb 25, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  <i>1.c. Apteryx australis lawryi </i>Rothschild, 1893 <i>Stewart Island [Brown] Kiwi (Tokoeka) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,15,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> The tokoeka, the Stewart Island kiwi, is an unusual case ... <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five,260</i>)

>  A very interesting little show has passed through Gore this last week - viz., five specimens of the rapidly disappearing "tokoweka," a sort of cousin, so to speak, of th more celebrated "kiwi." <i>(Jul 25, 1889,Otago Witness,17</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

### tokohea
 
 <i>n.</i> the Notornis

>  <b>BULLER</b> NOTORNIS MANTELLI. (MANTELL'S NOTORNIS.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Moho, Takahe, and Tokohea. [191] Mr. Walter Mantell states that ... It was known to the North-Islanders by the name of `Moho', and to the South-Islanders by that of `Takahe'. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,189,{1888 2nd edn 2/85} ed.</i>)

### tokotoko
 
 <i>n.</i> a walkingstick, a staff [incl took took]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> [He] showed me how to carve an ornate <i>toko toko</i>, or Maori orator's stick. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,69</i>)

>  There is an award of $5000, some cases of the sponsor's fine product, and (distinctively) a tokotoko or carved orator's stick, as a kind of staff of office. <i>(Jun 26, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  Thumping his tokotoko on the floor he shouted that if I betrayed his trust: "You'll go down there - six feet under." <i>(Oct 1998,Mana,55</i>)

>  His speech included an impromptu song in the old style, and gestures with a tokotoko (carved stick). With a foot-shaped handle, the tokotoko represents the god of forests Tane Mahuta pushing against the heavens. <i>(May 7, 2002,Evening Post,25</i>)

>  Purchases made in London and now displayed in the Maori Hall of the Otago University Museum include a very fine toki routangata, or fighting adze, with carved handle and blade of semi-translucent mottled greenstone, a wooden marionette, a finely-carved wooden fish-hook, the top of a staff or took took, and two ancient and well-carved weapons, patu and rakau. <i>(Aug 14, 1924,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The Arms of the men consist of a long wooden pointed Spear, sometimes 20 feet long, which they call an <i>Ahooat</i> [o2-grave, a2-macron]. They have short Darts about 5 or 6 feet long called <i>Togho-togho</i> [o2, o4-grave]. <i>(Jun 8, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/300</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|oko t|oko; A walking-stick. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,216</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Clad in our `bush' costume, but without weapons, and each with a <i>toko toko</i> or long walking-staff in our hands, my fellow traveller and myself set off in excellent spirits, accompanied by five Maori lads, who carried our baggage ... in their <i>pikau</i> or knapsacks, strapped over their shoulders with the leaves of flax. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/3</i>)

>  Ahipene came into the office with a tokotoko in his hand whereupon White said, Is it not for me? <i>(Sep 18, 1861,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/719</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> They, like Wiremu Kingi ..., were not armed with guns; each carried a <i>tokotoko</i> or walking-staff and directed his men. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,238</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> The elder walked slowly out in front of the meeting house. He had a tokotoko in his hand, a walking stick, which he raised suddenly ... <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,75</i>)

>  We learn that Mr E. S. Willocks, jun., who performed the duties of Clerk to the Native Lands Court, during the whole of its late session at Maketu, was, on his leaving that place, presented by the various hapus of the Arawa tribe with the following articles of native manufacture, vis.:- A "Taiaha" called "Te Ruamitimiti," a "Patuparaoa" called "Te Aruhe," a "Pekerange" mat, a "Whakatupu" mat, a carved "Paoi," and a carved "Tokotoko." <i>(Sep 7, 1878,Bay of Plenty Times,3</i>)

### toku pepeha
 
 <i>phr.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, pepeha = idiom, motto, proverb, quotation, saying toku = my, mine http://www.learningmedia.co.nz/nz/online/ngata/m2edictionary] (See [pepeha](../P#pepeha))

>  "..They will have the chance to learn their kaupapa and toku pepeha - who they are and who and what they represent." <i>(Jan 11, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,12 (back page)</i>)

### Tokyo scrum
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This formation was first used by a Japanese team against the NZ University team in Japan, all players - except the half-back - packing into the scrum when a scrum is ordered near the goal-line and pushing the defenders off the ball to score a certain try. <i>(Oct 8, 1952,Standard, The,4</i>)

### tolc
 
 <i>n.</i> a clot (back-slang) [1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 2 TOLC [REVERSE CLOT]]

### toll booth
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  What then was United States ambassador Paul Cleveland doing emerging from a public telephone toll booth at one of Wellington's post offices. Handing over a large sum of cash to pay for what the teller announced was a 15-minute toll call, he seemed sheepish on discovering he had been observed. <i>(Jan 23, 1987,National Business Review,10</i>)

### toll call
 
 <i>n.</i> a telephone call to a place beyond the free-calling area for residential subscribers. [Jim Cameron writes: DNZE refers to this as <i>formerly</i>, but it remains in use. This dictionary refers to an OED entry, but Collins would suggest that any such British use is obsolete. (R. Mason) NZ `collect': England `reverse charge call' NZ `toll': England `toll' =local charge call NZ `toll': England `trunk' =a distant toll call]

>  <b>SCOTT</b> As for toll calls ... I'll leave you the little forms you fill in. <i>(1944,Life with Barbara,88</i>)

### toll operator
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  For toll service the subscriber calls a certain number, which connects him to one of a group of lines running to the toll operator, who then handles his call as in manual equipment. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/339</i>)

>  Miss Haybittle, toll operator, has been transferred to Te Kuiti, and is succeeded by Miss Smith, of Auckland. <i>(May 23, 1923,Evening Post,6</i>)

### toll service
 
 <i>n.</i> [provision of telephone calls to another area, toll calls]

>  Mr Chapman remarks that nearly every country has begun its telephone service on the "flat rate" system, which means a fixed rate, irrespective of the number of calls made, such as is in force in New Zealand, but that in all "progressive countries" this has been superseded by the toll service. <i>(Oct 30, 1906,Wanganui Chronicle,4</i>)

### toll-free
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of a telephone call) made to a special number (a <i>toll-free number</i>) without toll charge to the caller, the recipient footing the bill

### tollroom
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>(Jun 8, 1968,Wanganui Photo News,76</i>)

### tolls
 
 <i>n.</i> the toll exchange [<i>Telephone conversations </i>...Tolls here : Hello, could I have Napier eight oh seven five eight three]

### tolly
 
 <i>v.</i> to defecate [ca1934 Alistair Campbell, quoting a Dunedin schoolchildren's parody of "Mary Had a Little Lamb": She took it up to school one day/ It tollied on the floor./ Mary had to lick it up/ And hoick it out the door.]

### tolpis
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>tolpis </i>[s] <i>Tolpis barbata</i> (L.) Gaertn. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,79</i>)

### tom
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Tom (post, prop)-</i> A length of timber erected for the temporary support of part of a structure of formwork usually firmed up by driven folding wedges. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,119</i>)

>  Billet was up the range with a gang of men erecting stout bush "toms" under the stringers of each bridge. <i>(Feb 1, 1956,Weekly News,44</i>)

>  In tapping under the rafter, the tom slipped, lifted the catch, and opened the dam. <i>(May 7, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  The trips [of the driving dam] were held in place by a tom... The knocker tripped the trigger which in turn released the tom. <i>(Sep 1991,Historic Places,29</i>)

>  If wrought from the surface down, and washed by a Tom or sluice,wages might be made. <i>(Jan 28, 1855,Nelson Examiner,2</i>)

### Tom Dodds
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [shearing] alcohol.

>  <b>GORDON</b> Shearers are human and occasionally - especially after a night on Tom Dodds - the booze - "drag the chain." <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,ii 2</i>)

### Tom Noddy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Sterna ... frontalis [? striata] </i>... It pursues schools of small fish close to the coasts of New Zealand, Tasmania and Australia, and as the kahawai fish also feed on the same class of food, the tern is a useful indicator to fishermen of their presence. Hence its name of kahawai bird. Kahawai Bird. Sea Swallow, Tom Noddy. TARA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,82</i>)

### Tom Pudding
 
 <i>n.</i> a bird

>  <i>Hoary-headed Grebe Podiceps poliocephalus</i> JARDINE & SELBY, 1827 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Hoary-headed dabchick, Tom Pudding.</i> ... Breeds mainly in southern Australia and Tasmania <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,56</i>)

### Tom Thumb
 
 <i>n.</i> a small (approx. half-inch) fire-cracker (smaller than a DOUBLE-HAPPY (q.v.) -->ca1960 at least, Wellington children

>  <b>JOSEPH</b> Exhausted as he was, he began to see ... a whole city burning for Guy Fawkes' night (children shouting in the garden for rocket and roman candle, mount vesuvius, jumping jack, bridal veil and bengal light, hellzapoppin, flying saucer, tom thumb and catherine wheel). <i>(1977,Time of Achamoth,40</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> It was quite good, but didn't compare with a Mighty Cannon in a tin can. It was round the store [in the ?1960s] we would have our first late October battle with lighted Tom Thumbs and Double Happys. <i>(1985,G'day Country,62</i>)

### Tom Thumb bird
 
 <i>n.</i> a bush wren

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BUSH WREN <i>Xenicus longpipes Other names</i>: Matuhi, Tom Thumb Bird (Stewart Is.). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,192</i>)

>  <i>Bush Wren Xenicus longipes</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... This tiny, inconspicuous bird may be extinct; ... <i>South Island Bush Wren X.l. longpipes</i> (GMELIN, 1789) <i>North Island Bush Wren X.l. stokesii</i> GRAY, 1862. <i>Stead's Bush Wren X.l. variabilis</i> STEAD, 1936. OTHER NAMES: <i>Matuhituhi, green wren, Tom Thumb bird</i>. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,265</i>)

### tom-bobbler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own... Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates,.. tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> Marbles were the currency of childhood and there were an astonishing number of names for them, some referring to the material or colour, others to value or function in the game. There were agates, tom-bobblers and whities. The march of technology added ball bearings - ballies or steelies - which were unbeatable when it came to smashing an opponent's miserable glassie. World War II[roman] brought `clayeys' often imperfectly spherical they were pathetic terracotta substitutes for real marbles. <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)

### tomahawk
 
 <i>n.</i> in Australia and New Zealand, a small axe or hatchet [also tommyhawk; possibly the most quoted reference is in the verses often attributed to Southey but written By Thomas Campbell in the <i>Pleasures of Memory</i> (lines ...), with <i>Erie's </i>altered in c1839-1840 by persons unknown to <i>Zealand's</i>: [quote]. The words is still used for a small belt axe (DEH: 2004)]

>  One of our party proceeded to chop fork-sticks.. and immediately around came a family of bob-robins within measurable reach of his tomahawk. <i>(Sep 14, 1927,NZ Tablet,37</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> ... in return [for her gift] I gave her a small Tomahawk. <i>(1820,Journal,23,1928 ed.</i>)

>  Hiroki... was got up in regular fighting costume, having only a cloth round his loins, a scarf across his heart, and a tomahawk stuck in his belt. <i>(Mar 22, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  They struck at her twice with tomahawks as she closed the door. <i>(Jul 26, 1900,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  The face reader told him that if anyone sent him outside for the tomahawk he would bring in the frying-pan. <i>(Jan 15, 1924,Otago Witness,1</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> There were one or two solitary settlers, undoubtedly living in dread of a Maori tomahawk in the wishbone, for the country was afire with guerilla attacks and ambushes. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,48</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> They were all crammed into huts, ... the principal chief, executioner, with a sharp tomahawk in his hand, ready to receive them. <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,44</i>) [NOTES: 1833 Sheridan Letter from<i> Sydney Monitor</i> in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 44]

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> ... the rest with tomahawks and spears. <i>(1836,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>) [NOTES: 1836 Marshall New Zealand in 1893 TrNZI 25/498 (Colenso)]

>  Thomas Spicer, `Korareka Beach', announced that he had for sale such articles as `duck frocks and trousers, ... tomahawks, ... and crockery'. <i>(1842,Bay of Islands Observer</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Bay of Islands Observer in 1956 J. Cowan The New Zealand Wars (2 edn) 12]

>  <b>1/79</b> We were 6 in party, ourselves, young Stark & 3 Maoris with `pikau' burdens. ... After tiring of [singing] they set off with burning brand & tomahawk to catch crawfish & tuna, the eels of the country, in a little brook near our encampment. <i>(Mar 25, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1960</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  [On equipping soldiers for the bush] ... each man will carry a hatchet, a tomahawk, a strong jack-knife, .. <i>(Jan 19, 1861,Otago Witness,Supplement 2</i>) [NOTES: distinguished from hatchet]

>  ... one man ... discovered a long range of rifle pits, and the pah of which till then we knew nothing. ... He returned in less than two minutes with a long-handled tomahawk. <i>(Jan 26, 1861,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  Note accompanying a Tomahawk formerly belonging to the Cannibal Chief Taraia ... <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/396</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> The father took down his tomahawk, and creeping up cautiously, lest he himself should be makutu-ed by a look, brained his parent ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,34</i>)

>  <b>CRAIK</b> First, the chief threw off the mat which he wore as a cloak, and, brandishing a tomahawk in his hand, began a war-song ... <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,92</i>)

>  <i>Tom&#769;.a-hawk</i> (t&obreve.m&#769;.&adot.-hak), <i>n. [Add.] 2</i>. An ordinary hatchet. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  He flourished his tomahawk, but did not offer to strike anyone. <i>(Apr 17, 1972,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  Jepson, the constable continued, said he killed them with a tomahawk. <i>(Sep 30, 1932,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  About a similar distance in the rear came the guard. It consisted of long, lanky, biped, with a "tommyhawk," having a handle in it as long as himself. <i>(Sep 17, 1863,Wellington Independent,3</i>)

>  'Bring me the life-preserver and the little tommyhawk.' <i>(Dec 6, 1887,Mataura Ensign,4</i>)

>  ARMY Ground Sheets, Haversacks, Pails, Axes, Tommyhawks, Tools. Come, inspect. <i>(Mar 29, 1930,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  .. HEDGE CLIPPERS, barrow wheel, WHEELBARROW (good order), grubber, spanners, garden hose, chisels, nails, levels, sprayer, axe and tommyhawk <i>(Oct 6, 1945,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  The spear might be long. his "mere' heavy, and his tomahawk of the best polished greenstone, but unless he possessed the wonderful shooting stick of the pakeha he might as well send in his cheeks [sic] at once. <i>(Aug 28, 1890,Otago Witness,36</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> We called at the town [for] ... that most useful article when travelling, viz. a tomahawk - nothing so handy: you cut your firewood with it ... , also if you have to build a hut, a Yankee tomahawk is the best tool possible for the purpose. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,131</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Sam drew a small tomahawk from his belt, and stepping up to the pig, [276] struck her a sharp blow with the hatchet ... and enabled him to quickly despatch her. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,275</i>)

>  ... an old axe or tomahawk answers the purpose. <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/198</i>) [NOTES: (Brown)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> a word of North-American Indian origin, applied in English to the similarly shaped short one-handed axe or hatchet. The word is not frequent in England, but in Australia the word <i>hatchet</i> has practically disappeared, and the word <i>Tomahawk</i> to describe it is in to the stone hatchet of the Aboriginals. A popular corruption of it is <i>Tommy-axe</i>. [1802; NZ 1888] <i>(1898,Austral English,473</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>(1931,Sheep-station Glossary</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> <i>(1938,Brown Bread,44</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> When clad in a shoulder mat, Native fashion, or when stripped to the waist with the haft of a tomahawk in his flax-cord belt, which supported his kilt-like<i> piupiu ... </i> <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,37</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the kaka, <i>Nestor meridionalis</i>

>  <b>PASCOE</b> <i>The Kaka.</i> Or as it used to be called in parts of Otago, the tomahawk, from its habit of digging into rotton [sic] trees for the large white grub. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,251</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> [strike or kill with a tomahawk]

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> He remembered well how he himself had once tomahawked a Pakeha who broke the tapu of a burial ground. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,36</i>) [NOTES: 1973 edition]

>  .. I told Austin sneaks like him would deserve tommyhawking for impounding people's horses at this time of night <i>(Jul 21, 1900,Manawatu Herald,2</i>)

>  The first man was tomahawked from behind. <i>(Aug 28, 1890,Otago Witness,36</i>)

>  The danger of being shot by wild Irishmen may be set off against that of being tomahawked by the Maoris. <i>(Jul 15, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/100</i>) [NOTES: (H.C. Richmond)]

>  <b>KINGSLEY</b> <i>(1859,Geoffrey Hamlin,147</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> tomahawked a boy dreadfully tomahawked <i>(1879,Waikato War,55</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> [shearing context]

>  (Wallace Shearing Glossary) <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/28</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> to cut sheep when shearing them. <i>(1898,Austral English,474</i>)

>  <i>v.tr (slang)</i>. to cut sheep when shearing them. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>tomahawk</i> ... v.t. (Sl.) <i>cut sheep while shearing. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <i>tomahawk ... 2 v. (NZ & Austral.) </i>cut (a sheep) while shearing it. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,809</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> as a currency of land purchase

>  <b>CAPT. GROVE BEADON</b> [Payment for land claimed from Maoris] Tomahawks 2-1/2 dozen ... Boarding Tomawaks [sic] 2-1/2 dozen. <i>(1876,Piako,16</i>)

### tomahawking
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) rough shearing so that sheep are cut.

>  'Tomahawking' will in no case be allowed, neither will clipping or chopping be tolerated; the wool must be taken off at first blow. <i>(Sep 18, 1875,Otago Witness,08</i>)
