### tomato blonde
 
 <i>n.</i> WW2 ?an Egyptian with European blood

>  But they drew no laughter, all soldier eyes on female nakedness itself. The dark girl, much the younger, appeared to be Nubian, though with mix of European blood to leaven swart African, so giving to velvet skin a rich midnight bronze matching densely crimped black curls tinged with deepest red. In soldier dialect tomato blondes were tuppence a dozen in the Cairo melting pot. Here in startling relief Port Said offered all-over tomato brunette. <i>(1986,Islands,37/35</i>) [NOTES: WWII]

### Tomato House
 
 <i>n.</i> usually for temporary buildings [ca1930 at least (J. Winchester) the `temporary' buildings erected as offices during WW1 in the vicinity of the wooden Government Building, Wellington (f. their shape and appearance)]

>  <b>SMITH</b> In an endeavour to sidetrack the innocent, a big new building called the Kremlin or Tomato House was erected in the middle of one side of the triangle [of Government buildings in Wellington]. <i>(1947,From N to Z,24</i>)

>  "If the people of Wellington had had any backbone," he said, "they would have gone along to the tomato house" - Mr. Browne's own name for the Base Records office - "and they would have pulled it down." <i>(Nov 27, 1919,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  THE SELF-HELF [sic] CO-OP GROCERY, LTD., HAS OPENED AT 74, LAMBTON QUAY (Opp. the "Tomato House"). <i>(Jun 17, 1926,Evening Post,2</i>)

### tomato sauce
 
 <i>n.</i> a horse [ORIGIN: rhyming slang]

>  We wander over to the bar for a pen and ink, where the talk gets round to tomato sauces. tomato sauces....horses <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)

### tombstone
 
 <i>n.</i> white pastry square with mincemeat filling; =fly cemetery [ca1938 Father Matthew Durning]

### Tominaki
 
 <i>n.</i> coined for Taranaki after Tom Cruise's visit

>  [Hdg] tom loves taranaki / 'Tominaki' loves tom... More than 1200 locals had stood in a field to spell out "Tominaki" for the aerial shot - to say thanks to their big-hearted and gracious guest. <i>(Jan 2004,Ephemera</i>)

>  tominaki: He came. He conquered. While in the region filming "The Last Samurai" Tom Cruise fell in love with Taranaki, New Zealand. <i>(Sep 8, 2003,Dominion Post,1</i>) [NOTES: Advertisng Supplement "Taranaki why it's hot"]

### tomming
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There is every reason to believe that many other gullies that have not paid for tomming, will yield well to sluicing parties, especially for a ground sluice. <i>(Jul 4, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  The other operations were confined to tomming and cradling, at which fair wages were made. <i>(Sep 17, 1906,Ohinemuri Gazette,2</i>)

>  Proposed by Mr. CARTER, seconded by Mr. MYERS, and carried unanimously - "That every digger shall be entitled to 20 feet square of land for sinking, and 30 feet square for surface digging, tomming, sluicing, cradling, &c." <i>(Mar 18, 1857,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  Five men, who have been working at the head of Otorongo stream, have obtained one ounce of good gold as the result of two days tomming, which was brought into town yesterday. <i>(Jul 14, 1869,Evening Post,2</i>)

### tommy
 
 <i>n.</i> a tomahawk, hatchet

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> ... there was nothing else for it [sc. a hard loaf] but the tomahawk. With this I managed to hack a hunk off one side; but I had to `mellow it' with [32] the back of the tommy before it was soft enough to bite. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,31,1935 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1872 Oct in c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 31-32]

>  <b>BARKER</b> [A shepherd says] I had to get the tommy (anglice [e-acute] - tomahawk -), and chop his boots off. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,148</i>)

>  <i>Tommy</i>.- A tomahawk, hatchet. A much-used tool on stations. (C.) <i>(Dec 23, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
 <i>n.</i> [a currant loaf] (See [spotted Tommy](../S#spotted-tommy))

>  <i>Brownie </i>.- Bread baked with currants and sugar. Sometimes called <i> Tommy </i>.up <i>and now, usually </i>, currant loaf.<i> ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) </i> <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>HARPER</b> [Shearers' rations in the 1890s included] once a week, usually on Wednesdays, bread made with currants, which the men called `tommy'. <i>(1934,Windy Island,221</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> ... <i>tommy,</i> a country term for bread baked with currants and sugar ... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,57</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [lead solder]

>  <b>HISLOP</b> ... these good tradesmen ... can call me a crackerjack on lead soldering, known to the [jewellers'] trade as tommying. Of course, I cannot understand anyone using tommy on a watch repair, ... <i>(1943,Pure Gold,80</i>)

### Tommy
 
 <i>n.</i> ?collocation ENGLISH TOMMY, cf. (ENGLISH) JOHNNY

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> ... all the men he phoned were fixd [sic] up except this young chap, just out from England who was staying at the Club. ... He seemed a decent sort of chap - quiet - a bit stand offish like most of these English `Tommies'. [This passage is crossed out.] [fn 164] `English Tommies": like the phrase `English Johnny' which Mansfield used in her story <i>Millie</i> in 1912, this is presumably a contemporary New Zealand expression for a recent immigrant. <i>(1916,Aloe, The,159,(1982 O'Sullivan) ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a bookmaker

>  In the strict letter of the law the bookmaker can refuse to change a wager as it takes two to make a bet and two to cry it off, but I do not remember a similar case where a `Tommy' refused to accommodate a client. <i>(Mar 30, 1907,NZ Truth,93/2/4</i>)

>  They were punched [?punted check] in through the front door of their old urinal - which is their present office - by that delightful organisation of talkative tommies known as the Burgesses' Association. <i>(Sep 7, 1907,NZ Truth,116/5/7</i>)

>  Wonder how bookmakers came to be known as `Tommies"? `Tommy [sic] is short for Tommy Rook, and probably it was owing to `rook' sounding something like "book' that caused some wag to term him as such. A rook would apply to some bookmakers, but where does the `Tommy' come in? The bookmakers were never named Tommies after soldiers, who are so dubbed, for when the latter lie on the field they've generally been shot at, whereas the bookie when he lays on the field, he's not been shot at, and, moreover, he never lays himself open to be shot at, either by marksmen or punters. Tommy is certainly a misnomer, and it should have been applied to a backer, who is ever the target for the unerring aim of the Bar Von. <i>(May 6, 1911,NZ Truth,306/2/4</i>)

>  <b>TUCKER</b> By the same token some of the bookmakers that I have known have been fearless fielders. There is a fascinating story of one of New Zealand's `tommies' who went through a very bad patch. <i>(1978,Thoroughbreds Are My Life,167</i>)

>  The Tommies got `rats' over the Napier Park meeting. The Hutt trained Moata's successes depleting the ring of a lot of money. <i>(May 23, 1907,NZ Truth,92/2/3</i>)

### tommy cod
 
 <i>n.</i> cockabully

>  His own exploits were simple enough, such as "myself and another young fellow" shooting fifty rabbits and a dozen pigeons after dinner; fishing where the waters abound with Tommy cod and 100 foot whales; and next day getting "nothing but sharks and a few blue cod - about a dozen sharks. <i>(Jul 23, 1897,Timaru Herald,2</i>)

>  There were tommy cods, silver bellies, a kind of whitebait, I suppose you'd call them inanga; we'd cook them in tin lids ... <i>(Aug 24, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  ...we'd spend the remainder of the afternoon swimming and shouting and splashing and attempting to catch the cockabullies - only we called them "tommycods" - that hid under the stones. <i>(Dec 2002,NZ Memories,No. 39/52</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> Then to pause in mid-stream to chase Tommy cod among the rocks. <i>(1914,Greenstone Door, The,47</i>)

>  But it [sc a reef in Auckland harbour] was also alive, with mussels, oysters, crabs, pools full of shrimp and tommy-cod. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>) [NOTES: (Sinclair `At the Point')]

### Tommy Dodd
 
 <i>n.</i> an alcoholic drink

>  <b>BAKER</b> The following New Zealand terms, recorded in the closing twenty years of the last century, are also worthy of comment: ... <i>white Maori,</i> tungstate of lime (New Zealand miners' use); <i>Tommy Dodd,</i> an alcoholic drink; ... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,46</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Tommy Dodd</i>. 4. A small glass of beer: New Zealand and Australian: late C. 19-20. B., 1941, 1942. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1205</i>)

### Tommy hat
 
 <i>n.</i> a British-army type of headgear issued to NZ troops at the start of WW1 (later replaced by slouch hats, then by LEMON-SQUEEZERS

>  <b>BURTON</b> The `Tommy hats' in which the New Zealanders were landed were soon thrown away and replaced by Australian felts. <i>(1935,Silent Division, The,81</i>)

### tommy shop
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Westport Railway Works] a contractor who keeps a `tommy shop' - as it is called - at which they [the gangers] are forced to buy the goods they require <i>(Feb 9, 1880,Evening Post,2</i>)

### tommy stick
 
 <i>n.</i> a pit-candle holder [(mining)]

>  `The consensus seems to be that the object is a pit-candle holder. There are different names for them in Australia and New Zealand where they're known as pit spiders or in New Zealand only as tommy sticks,' Mrs Hall said. She said many old miners or children of miners had rung her to identify the object. Pit-candle holders were used with carbide lamps in mines. The pointed part was put into the pit prop with the hook hanging down. The candle was put through the hoop. <i>(Oct 13, 1984,Evening Post,48</i>)

### tommy-axe
 
 <i>n.</i> tomahawk [ca1920 W.H.B. Orsman -->Morris: a popular corruption of tomahawk; EDD Warw; P: Aust coll; W3 Aust]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>a popular corruption of the word Tomahawk</i> (q.v.); it is an instance of the law of Hobson-Jobson. <i>(1898,Austral English,474</i>)

>  A popular name in Australia and New Zealand for a small axe or chopper, a `tomahawk'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),416</i>)

>  <i>tommy axe n</i>. Australian corruption of American Indian word tomahawk. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>tommy axe n</i>. literally, half-axe, in size between axe and tomahawk; any axe-type implement. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <i>tommy-axe n. NZ & Austral. colloq</i>. tomahawk. [abbr.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,809</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <i> tommy-axe n</i>. corrupt. fr. tomahawk. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12</i>)

>  That Tomahawk's latest nickname is "Tommy-axe." <i>(Nov 15, 1904,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  His experience of independent members was that they were all Liberals, and that they might be divided into three classes those who sat upon a rail with a little "tommy-axe" to grind and were ready to jump down on any side they could get it sharpened <i>(Nov 18, 1896,Otago Daily Times,2</i>)

### tommyfish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Tommyfish <i>Limnichthys fasciatus<./i> Waite <i>(1955,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,410</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 410]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] tommyfish 1798 TOMMYFISHES 1798-9 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  The biggest paddock in my [King Country] section is chock-full of `tomos'. A `tomo', let me explain, is a deep natural well in the limestone formation, formed in the course of centuries, maybe by surface drainage. <i>(May 10, 1920,Quick March,39</i>)

### tommyhawk
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> A List of Articles Wanted for the Use of the <i>Active</i> and the Settlement in New Zealand, 1815 [heading] ... [138] ... twenty dozen tommy hawkes, twenty dozen of sickles, eight pit and four crosscut saws (two pit saws as long as can be procured), ... <i>(1815,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,137</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Two Sawyers present saying don't strike him, for your life Sir; he also drew his tomy hawk which I caught hold of. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),59,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> He mangled his body with a tomyhawk. <i>(1845,Journal,3/187</i>)

>  <b>MEURANT</b> ... tomyhawk <i>(1845,Diary,30</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> I would rather cut up a three-ply tea chest for kindling with a blunt tommyhawk ... <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,17</i>)

>  The Hapuka Hoisters' Union followed, led by Mr S. D. Hanna, J.P., eating oysters, closely followed by Mr Ernest Oswald, carrying a tommyhawk. <i>(Oct 24, 1908,Observer (Auckland),19</i>)

>  The tommyhawk was identified as belonging to the prison works. <i>(Nov 6, 1897,Marlborough Express,2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... anyone who shd. try to sell within these boundaries shd. be tomyhawked <i>(May 8, 1854,Journal,8/179</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLL</b> None of them [the dead officers in the pa] or the men were tommyhawked [by the Maoris] which is a great wonder. <i>(Apr 30, 1864,Journal,216</i>)

>  ~ <i>v.t</i>. to cut a sheep when shearing. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12</i>)

### tommying
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HISLOP</b> ... these good tradesmen ... can call me a crackerjack on lead soldering, known to the [jewellers'] trade as tommying. Of course, I cannot understand anyone using tommy on a watch repair, . <i>(1943,Pure Gold,80</i>)

### tomo
 
 <i>n.</i> a cave or hole; a chute-like hole made by water in especially volcanic material. [Maori ?]; a water-worn hole; a sink-hole in limestone country f. ?Maori <i>tomoau</i>, abyss (Williams 435), or the noun f.

>  A "tomo", for the benefit of novices and overseas readers, is from the Maori language, a generic term for any sort of pot-hole or sink-hole. <i>(Mar 1953,NZ Speleological Bulletin,I/5 : 10</i>)

>  I look back on the Tomo Thyme expeditions as times of really good fun. <i>(Sep 1992,NZ Speleological Bulletin,IX/163 : 80</i>)

>  The drainage of this limestone dip-slope area is mostly underground by means of sink-holes or `tomos,' which are so common as to give the name `Tomo Country' to the district. <i>(1946,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.41 (NS) 20</i>) [NOTES: (Marwick, `The Geology of the Te Kuiti Subdivision') see also p. 37]

>  Te Kuiti, April 14 (P.A.) - A slip into a tomo (A water-worn hole) over 100 feet deep resulted in the death yesterday of ... of Oparue, Te Kuiti. ... A search resulted in the finding today of his rifle on the tomo edge ... <i>(Apr 14, 1952,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: Apr 14 (?15 or 20th?)]

>  <i>(1952,Arena,31/3</i>)

>  <i>(1953,Short Stories (various authors),188</i>) [NOTES: 1953 Middleton in 1966 Short Stories 188]

>  <b>MASON</b> tomos with [Limestone hole] in brackets as explanation. Some way further north many tomos noted <i>(1954,Personal Communication,2</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> `Coming to look for tomos?' he used to sing out ... <i>(1959,Stone, The,32</i>) [NOTES: (The Stone)]

>  <i>(1959,Tararua,xiii/47</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> <i>(1961,Hole in the Hill,76</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> He wouldn't have had a show if the horse hadn't swerved off the road and gone straight through the crust of a small <i>tomo,</i> a volcanic cavity in the ground, and wasn't able to clamber out again. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,72</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> ... a lot of new things came into our life when we were at Taupo. ... And there weren't any fears in our life then, either; except for tomos that might be there covered with bracken and you wouldn't see them and you might fall in ... <i>(1965,Stand in the Rain,122</i>)

>  See him [sc. a shepherd] drag a gully full of tomos and patches of scrub and guarantee ... you wouldn't find one straggler. <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,127,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton `Saving the Breed')]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A characteristic of this limestone country [near Te Kuiti] are its caves and underground tunnels or holes - `tomos'. One of the Puketi tomos has been measured to run for two miles.... <i>(1972,Wake of The Axe,155</i>)

>  <b>S H WILSON</b> Completely enclosed cavity found in volcanic ash or pumice beds, sometimes filled with steam or gas. <i>(Sep 14, 1980,Letter</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> He seemed to think that the dog was stuck down a tomo (deep chute-like hole) somewhere [Waione-Galaxy area of Rotorua.] <i>(1982,Wild Pig,168</i>)

>  <i>tomo n. NZ </i>depression or hole in limestone terrain. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,809</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  The plateau is covered with a splendid growth of grass, and would form an excellent cattle run but for the deep funnel-shaped holes which everywhere abound. The Natives call them "tomo." <i>(Jul 8, 1859,Auckland Provincial Gazette,VIII (14) : 94</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The limestone formation.. forms a plateau.. remarkable for subterraneous water-courses, caves and deep funnel-shaped holes, called by the natives Tomo. [Fn] Tomo signifies crumbling, sinking down, and is therefore very appropriate <i>(1867,New Zealand  (Polack),340</i>)

>  A fortnight later she turned a flip into a tomo - (a miniature cave) - the King Country is riddled with them. <i>(Feb 26, 1927,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,39</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> Occasionally the ground fell in, creating an oubliette of raw earth out of which rose frightening plinks and gurgles and squishing sighs. These pits were called tomo, an abstruse word used sometimes of caves, such as the celebrated Waitomo Caves. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,14</i>)

>  The underlying caves are mainly horizontal, often with steep entrance shafts known as tomos (<i>wai</i> means water, <i>tomo</i> a hole in the ground). <i>(Apr 1997,NZ Geographic,No. 34/43</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> betrothal

>  <b>HUTT</b> At a 'tomo' (betrothal) ceremony, "Ratu's father then spoke... [later] the tomo proceedings had merged into that of the beer party." <i>(1991,Maori & Alcohol,79</i>) [NOTES: See Hohepa <i> A Maori Community in Northland</i> 1970, pp. 99-100]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a sink-hole or under-runner, down which sheep and cows can be trapped and left to die

>  <b>MOWAT</b> This afternoon filling in tomos and burning on front face. <i>(Aug 18, 1936,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> See him drag a gully full of tomos and patches of scrub and guarantee if you went over it all on foot afterwards you wouldn't find one straggler. <i>(1953,Short Stories (various authors),26</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> One of the Puketiti tomos has been measured to run for two miles, and some of them are a death trap for stock. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,155</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> tomo: name adopted from the Maori for an underrunner. 'we'll have to fence that tomo.' <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,85</i>)

### tomo group
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There are now quite a number of Tomo Groups in New Zealand. In what places do they practise their rather chilly sport? <i>(Jul 1972,Affairs,23</i>)

### Tomorrow's Manukau - Manukau Apopo
 
 <i>phr.</i> Manukau City Council's long term strategic plan

>  John's valuable insight into community issues and his work on the community consultation for Council's long term strategic plan, Tomorrow's Manukau - Manukau Apopo, will be of lasting benefit to all of Manukau's residents. <i>(Dec 6, 2005,NZ Herald,C8</i>)

### Tomorrow's Schools
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  The Tomorrow's Schools policy provided an innovative and sound model of educational administration which achieved a consensus among parents and teachers. However, education policy in the last 18 months has substantially departed from Tomorrow's Schools as market-oriented policies have been introduced. <i>(May 11, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  The Education Ministry had been deliberately allowed to become a spectator of school failure in the 10 years since the introduction of the Tomorrow's Schools model... The original scheme of Tomorrow's Schools did not anticipate that the Ministry of Education would become a spectator. <i>Ibid.</i> 11 The Tomorrow's Schools reforms saw the abolition of the Department of Education and thousands of elected parents taking over the governing of their local schools... "It's important to bear in mind what Tomorrow's Schools was and what it wasn't." <i>(Sep 29, 1999,Dominion,8</i>)

>  Northern suburbs educators are looking back on 10 years of Tomorrow's Schools with a mixture of opinions... "The co-operative ideals of Tomorrow's Schools have been betrayed by the very agencies that are supposed to serve schools." <i>(Oct 12, 1999,Independent Herald (Wellington),11</i>)

>  The Education Act 1989 brought in Tomorrow's Schools and radically overhauled an education administration system that had changed little in concept for over a century. <i>(Nov 5, 1999,NZ Education Review,14</i>)

>  Is Tomorrow's Schools communication between school and community, the democratic process in action, <i>or</i> a fruitless round of meetings to put ideals on paper? <i>(Dec 4, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),131</i>)

>  Rees-Thomas dislikes the notion, central to Tomorrow's Schools, of open-slather competition between state schools. <i>(May 20, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  The whole thing began, really, with Tomorrow's Schools ... teaching had always been about teachers sharing resources and working co-operatively, and suddenly it became individual schools working on their own. <i>(Jul 6, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  Under the Tomorrow's Schools system, each school gets a block grant, from which they have to pay for everything from chalk and school books to toilet taxes. <i>(Oct 14, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  The Tomorrow's Schools reforms introduced in 1988 gave every school its own governing body and the school structures in existence in October 1989 are the ones that are still in place today. <i>(Jun 12, 2006,NZ Herald,A11</i>)

### tomtit
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a small bird found in both the native bush and exotic forest, also called miromiro or ngirungiru; occas. used for esp <i>Petroica toitoi</i>, pied tit, white-breasted tit (tomtit preferred for euphemistic reasons to TIT simplex) (See [tit](../T#tit))

>  The wren and the tomtit and the thrush all are represented quite as nearly or even more so than the robin save that the tomtit is black headed with a yellow breast. <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,44,1960 ed.</i>)

>  PETROICA MACROCEPHALA, Gml. Ngirungiru. Piro piro. Tomtit. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/59</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  MYIOMOIRA TOITOI, <i>Reich</i>. Pied Tit. This familiar little bird, the `Tomtit' of the Colonists, is far less plentiful than it formerly was ... <i>(1878,TrNZI,10/203</i>) [NOTES: (Buller)]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> The robins, riflemen and tomtits tamed very soon, the robins being an easy first in this respect. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,108,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> Bush robins, parakeets, tomtits, grey warblers, riflemen, pigeons and the bush hawk are still to be seen. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,129</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> TOMTIT <i>Petroica macrocephala</i> Other names: Miromiro (North Island), Ngirungiru (South Island), Wheedler, Butcher-bird (Taranaki). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,200</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Tomtit .. miromiro (North Island), ngiru-ngiru (South Island) .. <i>Petroica macrocephala</i> ... <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> There are five races of tomtits (<i>Petroica macrocephala</i>): the pied tit (<i>toitoi</i>) of the North Island and outlying islands; the yellow-breasted (<i>macrocephala</i>) from the South Island, Stewart Island and neighbouring islands; the Snares black (<i>dannefaerdi</i>); and the Auckland Islander (<i>marrineri</i>). <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,279</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> .. their way still led through bush-land, and .. tiny birds began to appear: perky tomtits in immaculate suits of black and white .. grey warblers trilling their lilting arias. <i>(1968,Turn Back the Clock,69</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> No, my father didn't mind us shanghaiing thrushes and that, but he always said don't touch the natives. We did, though - pigeons and tomtits and tuis. <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five,261</i>) [NOTES: (Wattie, Stewart I.)]

>  <i>tomtit n.</i> tit; small NZ bird, esp. miromiro. [TOM, TIT] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,810</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>BULLER</b> .. to say nothing of Korimako and Pitoitoi .. we never saw or heard the notes of either the Piopio, the Tieke, or the Hihi, all of which birds were at one time more numerous even than the Huia. The <i>Zosterops</i> was everywhere abundant, also the Grey Warbler and Rifleman, and .. we found the Tomtit comparatively plentiful; the Parakeet chased its mate through the tree-tops with sharp cries of `twenty-eight'; .. and among the tangle of the underwood the ever-present Flycatcher displayed its pretty fan-like tail. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/14</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tomtit, n</i>. name applied in New Zealand to two New Zealand birds of the genus <i>Myiomoira</i>, the species being <i>M. toitoi</i>, Garnot, in North Island; <i>M. macrocephala</i>, Gmel., in South Island. <i>[1888 Buller] </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,475</i>)

>  <i>Tom&#769;.tit&#769;.</i> (t&obreve.m&#769;.t&ibreve.t&#769;. <i>or</i> t~om&#769;.t&ibreve.t&#769;.), <i>n. (Zoo.l.) [Add.] (c) </i>Either of two species of miro. See MIRO. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  I have paid special attention to the nests of three birds- the Native Canary (<i>Orthonyx ochrocephala</i>), the Robin (<i>Myiomoira albifrons</i>), and the Tomtit (<i>Myiomoira macropcephala</i>). Sir Walter Buller says .. that the Blue Crow (<i>Glaucopis cinerea</i>) lays a single egg, and .. leaves the care of the chick to the to the Popokatea, or Canary (<i>Orthonyx albicilla</i>). <i>(1904,TrNZI,36/119</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Homiromiro .. = miromiro</i>, n. <i>Petroeca toitoi, tomtit.</i> [so 1957/58] <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,68</i>)

>  Born under another state of things, it [Governor Grey's Appeal Bill] slumbered for a term of years, and has been only now woke up, to find itself old, without having really ever lived - emerging from its roosting hole like an owl into daylight, perplexed and dismayed by the strange company in which it finds itself, buffeted, like that venerable bird, by every tomtit that comes across it. <i>(Apr 21, 1857,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Numbers of native birds such as tomtits, white heads, grey warblers and fantails are expected to increase in the Otaki catchment of the Tararua Forest Park ... <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> shit [rhyming slang]

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> `And what of Kruschen?' `To help him towards his daily tom-tit, and for the same reason, ...' <i>(1976,Send Somebody Nice,17</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> `First time I saw him nearly had tomtits. Thought he'd fall for certain.' <i>(1978,Murder in Fancydress,123</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [Williams nf: is this a nominalisation of 436 <i>Tonga</i> (i) South wind. > cold > protection????; or is it an anglicisation of TAONGA (see TAONGA)]

 <i>n.</i> handshears

Also known as `sheep shears', `shears', `tongs', `swords', `b-bows', `bows', `daggers', and `Jingling Johnnies'. <i>Ibid.</i> 3/29 <i>tongs</i>: Hand shears. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/25</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

>  <i>Tongs</i>.-Nickname for blade shears. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> We find shears called jingling johnnies and tongs... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,39</i>)

>  <b>MILLS</b> For the benefit of the non-farming reader, blade shearing is done by twin-bladed sheep shears [41] (called `tongs') ... <i>(1960,Sheep-O,40</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A top blade-man in his day . . . He's past using a pair of tongs now but I noticed he's still got a dog tied up in his backyard. <i>(1973,Big Country,20</i>)

>  I can handle a pair of tongs. <i>(Oct 29, 1984,TV1</i>) [NOTES: 1984 Oct 29 Country GP TV1]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>(Dec 1958,NZ Timber Journal,73/1</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [shearing] hand or blade shears.

>  Tongs.- Nickname for blade shears. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> We find shears called jingling johnnies and tongs. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,39</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A top blade-man in his day, he claims that his 164 ewes, at Molesworth, is the blade record for that shed. He's past using a pair of tongs now. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,20</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> They used - and still do in parts of the high country - shears, exactly the same as those a down on his knees edge trimmer uses. But slightly adapted and much sharper and liable to be called "Tongs" or "Jingling Johnies." <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,2</i>)

### Tongue and Meat Club
 
 <i>phr.</i> Wellington Tramping AND Mountaineering Club [ca1952 R. Mason A contemptuous nickname for this club which assumed the all-inclusive provincial title though only the fourth or fifth oldest in Wellington. Now (1952) used by W.T.M.C. members themselves. At a meeting of the Wellington Association of Mountain Clubs a member of the W.T.M.C. can be a `Tongue and Meat'.]

>  <i>Tongue and Meat Club</i>, that name bestowed on the Wellington Tramping and Mountaineering Club by the scarcely sympathetic members of fellow clubs when it first adopted its present name, has become so established that a W.T.M.C member may now introduce himself by saying `I'm a Tongue and Meat'. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,30</i>)

[and] was used during the hearing of an assault case at Lyttelton. ... using some kerbstone language with much emphasis ... `You've tongue-murdered me ... <i>(Nov 11, 1905,NZ Truth,21/5/8</i>)

### tonguer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> The now obsolete term <i>tonguer</i> was also in use in the early 1830's, and very likely before it although I have discovered no written records of it prior to 1836. A tonguer was a native or white living in New Zealand who assisted a whaling crew to cut up whales and who also acted in the capacity of an interpreter. These men earned their name not from the act of interpreting, but from the fact that they were given the whale's carcase and tongue to dispose of as they wished. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,13</i>)

>  <b>CAPT JOY</b> ... it was told to me by our toungers [sic, at Cloudy Bay] that the cost [of a shore-hut] was trifling. ... I left them [sc Maoris] Came [sic] on board and despatched the boat the other side for a linguist our tounger. (The Ships all employ Tonguers [sic] and it is a general custom I have acceded on the same terms as others) On his arrival we opened the Subject but they [sc Maoris] were ashamed and finally settled that they would build the house. ... I must not omit here Some mention of what are called tonguers. they [sic] are here 2 and 3 white people who have a boat and some natives on a ship arriving they repair on board to solicit the Job for the ship The terms are that they occasionally furnish a crew to help tow whales they furnish a boats crew to help cut the whale in and do any talking for the captain whose ignorance of the language requires their aid in any matters with the natives. When the whale is cut in they are entitled to the carcass and the tongue which in plentiful Whaling is always left on the carcass and they contrive to get for [sic] 6 to 8 barrels of oil from each carcass but they are in general Blackguards and no dependence can be put in them - Runaways from Ships Mostly. [McNab's ed. fn] The Americans had ... to rely to a great extent upon Maori labour and to be indebted to the services of interpreters. These men were locally [?Cloudy Bay] called `tonguers'. ... In `The Piraki Log' [1911] the word `tonguer' is suggested as a corruption of the Maori `tonga'. This is quite wrong. The tonguer was a man who interpreted and assisted in cutting up and who was paid with the tongue of the whale.[choose between this and following citation???] <i>(Apr 28, 1835,Log of Mary Mitchell</i>) [NOTES: 1836 Apr 28 Capt. Joy Log of Mary Mitchell in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 435, 436]

>  <b>CAPT JOY</b> The Principal Chief (Te Rauparaha) is absent on one of his marauding traverses like the Ancient bands (of) Pirates and his authority delegated to 3 chiefs 1 on the western shore the other 2 here ... I despatched the boat the other side for a linguist or tonguer ... I must not omit here Some[sic] mention of what are called touguers. They are here 2 or 3 white people who have a boat [40] and some natives. On a ship arriving they repair on board to solicit the Job for the ship. The terms are that they occasionally furnish a crew to help tow whales, they furnish a boats crew to help cut the whale in and do any talking for the Captain whose ignorance of the language requires their aid in any matters with the natives. When the whale is cut in they are entitled to the carcass & the tongue which in plentiful Whaling is always left on the carcass and they contrive to get from 6 to 8 barrels of oil from each carcass but they are in general Blackguards and no dependence can be put in them - Runaways from Ships Mostly. <i>(Apr 28, 1836,Log of Mary Mitchell,39</i>)

>  [He] found it to be James Robinson, the <i>Tonguer</i> from Wongooloa [ed Anson note Glossary 166: The word is obviously a corruption of the Maori `tonga' meaning `from the South', or `Southerner'; ... ] <i>(Apr 10, 1839,Piraki Log,82</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> After the ships had cut off the blubber ... the carcase was towed on shore by a crew of men, who call themselves `tonguers'. These men take off what little blubber the ship's crew have passed over, and also take out the tongue and try it all out. <i>(1840,Plain Truths,60</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The tongue was of a white or ash colour, blackish towards the root. This organ gave several barrels of oil, and is a monopoly of the `tonguer,' or `cutter-in.' The latter operation is performed in Te-awa-iti near the shores, where by means of a windlass the whale is raised to the surface of the water under a scaffold called the `shears.' <i>Ibid.</i> 1/109 To this quantity must be added the tonguer's oil, so called from the man who `cuts-in the whale,' for which he is allowed the oil of the tongue, of the heart, and of the intestines, for his own benefit. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/51</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The ["tonguer"]takes his name from having an exclusive right to the oil obtained from the tongue and other interior parts of the whale in payment for his duty of `cutting in', or dissecting the whale. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/323</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> The Americans had ... to rely to a greater extent on Maori labour, and to be indebted to the services of interpreters. These men were locally [at Cloudy Bay] called `tonguers'. <i>Ibid.</i> 322 The Robinsons, so their names were given to the Doctor [Thiercelin in January 1840 at Akaroa], acted as `carcassiers', as the French called them, collecting stray floating whales, or the intestines of whales ... blown into the Bay, and melting them down to produce an inferior [323] class of oil which they sold to the whalers. Among the English-speaking whalers these men were called `tonguers'. <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,194</i>) [NOTES: [see also 1836 citations???]]

>  <b>BAKER</b> The now-obs word tonguer was also in use in the early 1830's... These men earned their name not from the ... interpreting, but from the fact that they were given whale's carcass and tonguer to dispose of as they wished,' B., 1945: New Zealand and whalers': C. 19. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,13</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Tonguer.</i> `A native or white living in New Zealand who assisted a whaling crew to cut up whales and who also acted ... [as] an interpreter. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1205</i>)

>  <i>tonguer n</i>. interpreter in whaling gang, given whale's tongue and carcase to dispose of. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the worker who takes out the tongue and weasands [ca1951 May 17 Feilding freezing Works p.c. Colin Gordon <i>Tonguer</i>: one who cuts out tongue and who also `pulls the weasands'. [wiz ndz]]

### too right
 
 <i>exclam.</i> (informal) yes indeed! certainly! [Very common expression as far back as 1940s]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He's a beaut. Too right he is. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,146</i>)

>  <i>(1966,Short Stories (various authors),163</i>) [NOTES: 1963 Ballantyne in 1966 Short Stories 163]

>  <i>(slang phrase) n.</i> mild ejaculation of approval or approbation. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> `Bloody oath, Mat what good luck!' ... `Shout you eh?' `Too right you can. Double whisky.' <i>(1984,Some Other Country,182</i>) [NOTES: 1976 Hulme `Hooks and Feelers' in 1984 Some Other Country 182]

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>too right</i>: `That's too right mate'. That's a correct statement (opinion), and furthermore I AGREE WHOLEHEARTEDLY with my friend. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,105</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> `I suppose so. Oh yes. Too right it was.' ... `Are you thinking of Maria?' `Too bloody right I am, mate.' <i>(1980,Photo-Finish,201</i>)

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> `He can be tiresome, I imagine.' `Too right he can, and I'm exhausted.' <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,49</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Missus Wayne's a bit of a beaut, isn't she. She's a hard case all right. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,159</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> `Too right I could'. `You're not allowed to say too right.' <i>(1934,Winds of Heaven</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> What is it they say in this country? <i>Too right.</i> Too right I would darling. <i>Ibid.</i> 94 I'll have something to say to young Packer about that. Too right, I will ... <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,34</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> The expressions `too true', `too right', in which too has its old intensive value, are in common use. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,90</i>)

>  <i>Too right </i>- certainly, sure. <i>(1944,US War Dept. Short Guide to NZ,39</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Following are examples of colloquialisms common to New Zealand and Australian English, with their English or American equivalent ... <i>too right</i>: certainly <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),115</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>too right </i> <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  "Oh I don't know," objected Irene. "We get on very well without one here, don't we, Belinda?" "Too right," assented Belinda promptly <i>(1933,Guardians of Tony, The,p19</i>)

### Tooley Street
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We have the three tailors of Tooley Street with us still. <i>(May 4, 1912,NZ Free Lance,8</i>)

>  Who are we to think we make Tooley Street toe the mark? <i>(Nov 10, 1926,NZ Observer,2</i>)

>  She has been.. working in the famous Tooley Street. <i>(Aug 18, 1938,NZ Observer,16</i>)

>  The officers report that the best houses in Tooley Street have.. co-operated with them... Let me say at once that we have received nothing but co-operation, so far, from the Tooley Street people. <i>(1936,NZ Parliamentary Debates,244/682</i>)

>  Our officers in London have been asked to work with Tooley Street for the purposes of implementing the new policy. <i>(1936,NZ Parliamentary Debates,244/682</i>)

>  From the worried gentlemen in Tooley Street to the humblest cow-spanker, we all look to the cow. <i>(Dec 18, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,35</i>)

>  No knight ever learned more from a fall in the lists than New Zealand learned from many years of hard effort to share equally with Denmark in the popularity of the British butter consumer; and it is almost certainly a fact that both New Zealand and Denmark, and also the consumer, profited by the daily contest at the Round Table of non-romantic Tooley Street. <i>(May 11, 1944,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Cold stores, capable of storing two thousand tons of butter and cheese, are being erected in Tooley Street. <i>(May 21, 1894,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  [Caption] Tooley Street Control... The Prime Minister stated.. that.. the butter market in London was controlled by a committee of Tooley Street merchants who met daily and fixed a price. <i>(Aug 24, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,23</i>)

>  Captain FitzRoy said that he had never admitted that the Government had acted on any such principle as the one described; and that he requested that gentlemen would not attribute sentiments to him which were not his; and that he did not concur in the opinion expressed of the position of Maories [sic] and the settlers, although it might be the real opinion of some few persons who thought they represented the public, just as "the three tailors in Tooley Street" imagined that they were the people of England. <i>(Nov 16, 1844,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,1</i>)

>  Four merchants of Liverpool have rivalled the fame of "the three tailors of Tooley street" by addressing a letter to the Emperor of the French, enquiring if he intended to invade England. <i>(Feb 21, 1860,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  A Nominee Council, worse than a Tooley street oligarchy, aping a House of Lords in New Zealand! <i>(Nov 30, 1852,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  <b>Hursthouse</b> The Free Kirk of Scotland, were it twice as numerous and twice as "emigrationary" as it is, could no more colonise and fructify the noble province of Otago, than the "three Tailors of Tooley Street" could represent the people of England. <i>(1857,New Zealand, or Zealandia,I: 251</i>)

>  Mr Singleton, the Government cheese expert, states that "he found in Tooley Street, London, that merchants, sore over their losses of last year, are disinclined to contract for a whole season's butter supply, but prefer to purchase in London as consignments arrive ..." <i>(Oct 21, 1907,Bay of Plenty Times,4</i>)

The hermits were kept in a special paddock, and supplied the station mutton. <i>(1925,Random Recollections,20</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> ... one or two [cattle] drowned at Molyneux and two `tooted' at Teschemaker's ... <i>Ibid.</i> 152 I have had little experience of sheep being `tooted' [tutu-ed] [sic] or of scab. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,140</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> After camping the night there, we had to cross Kerr's Hill which was covered with tutu, a poisonous plant for sheep, and the curse of drovers. ... Odd sheep started to get `tooted' and instead of pushing on the men started bleeding the sheep. This was done by cutting a vein under the eye. It was supposed to help the sheep, although I never thought it did any good. <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,19</i>)

>  <b>McNEISH</b> `Tutu?' Polson inquired. `We opened is stomik an there was the weed lying like clover.' `Tooted. I'm sorry, Droo.' <i>(1970,Mackenzie,129</i>)

### tooth-leaved beech
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Nothofagus fusca</i> [[So Kirk, Botany Thames Goldfields TrNZI 1869 90]] (See [beech](../B#beech))

>  The tooth-leaved beech (<i>Fagus fusca</i>) occurs in considerable quantity at the sea level ... <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/445</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk Hauraki Gulf)]

>  [<i>Notho]fagus fusca</i>,Hook. f. ... Tooth-leaved Beech. Black Birch of Auckland and in part of Otago and Southland. Black or Bull Birch of Lake Wakatipu. Red Birch of Wellington, Nelson, and in part of Otago and Southland. It is not so easy to see why any difficulty should have occurred in the identification of this fine timber-tree apart from the misleading tendency of the common names generally applied. The thin yet firm texture of the leaf, the prominent veins, the sharply-toothed margins, are characters that can only be confused with those afforded by a careless observer. Yet merely owing to the use of common names based upon colour, and applied or rather misapplied to the leaves, bark, or wood at the fancy of the bushman, no species has been more misunderstood. <i>Ibid.</i> 302 All the men engaged at the sawmills [of West Oxford] had heard of `tooth-leaved beech,' but only one or two of them had seen it growing, and no one had seen it converted. TOOTH-LEAVED B. <i>F. fusca</i>, Hook f. All these, however, are commonly called<i> Birches. </i> <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/300</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk, NZ Beeches),]

>  <b>KIRK</b> With the view of obviating the confusion caused by the misuse of the common names hitherto applied to the New Zealand beeches, I would suggest the adoption of new names based on the obvious characteristics of their foliage:- For <i>Fagus fusca</i>, tooth-leaved beech; for <i>Fagus solandri</i>, entire-leaved beech; and for <i>Fagus menziesii</i>, round-leaved beech. <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,17</i>)

>  The utmost confusion prevails among the common names of the birches. There are scarcely two districts, a few miles apart, in which the same name is applied to the same tree, and a similar result may be obtained by consulting two bushmen in the same bush. With a view to obviating this difficulty Mr Kirk suggests `the adoption of new names based of the obvious characteristics of foliage [e.g.] for Fagus fusca `tooth-leaved beech'; for Fagus solandri `entire-leaved beech' etc. [This is unsuitable as foliage varies] I would suggest the retention of the common Otago names Fagus fusca - Red birch ; F. solandri - Black-heart birch ; F Menziesii - Silver birch. <i>(1876,TrNZI,64/</i>) [NOTES: (Blair),]

>  <i>(1880,TrNZI,12/</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>Beech</i> ... In New Zealand, there are six species of true beeches, which according to Kirk are as follows - ... <i>(1908,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Cockayne: Report on a Botanical Survey of the Tongariro National Park (And)]

### tooth-leaved birch
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Nothofagus fusca</i>

>  [Native name] Tawhai-raunui .. [Settlers' name] Tooth-leaved birch .. [Family] Cupuliferae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

### toothed lancewood
 
 <i>n.</i> Pseudopanax ferox, aka savage lancewood

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> lancewood, toothed. <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>)

>  [Native name] Horoeka .. [Settlers' name] Toothed lancewood .. [Family] Araliaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>)

>  Lancewood, toothed .. Pseudopanax ferox .. Wood very elastic and strong, suitable for cabinet making and walking sticks <i> Pseudopanax ferox <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers</i>)

>  The chameleon nature of the ferocious-looking toothed lancewood (Pseudopanax ferox) fascinates him for the same reason. As a juvenile, this unusual tree resembles an anorexic stick insect with thin, leathery leaves drooping pitifully from a single puny stem. <i>(Sep 15, 2002,Sunday Star Times,D9</i>)

>  The spindly, prehistoric-looking toothed lancewood <i>Pseudopanax ferox</i> is always a talking point. It undergoes a radical transformation as an adolescent, shedding its stiff, jagged, juvenile leaves to become a fairly ordinary dark green tree. <i>(Oct 31, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),47</i>)

### toothfish
 
 <i>n.</i> a desirable but ugly Antarctic fish

>  The Government is to seek international agreement for up to three New Zealand ships to catch endangered toothfish in the Ross Sea near Antarctica this summer. <i>(Jul 19, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  There's big money in toothfish... In posh restaurants they know ir as Chilean sea bass. To the Japanese, it is mero. <i>(Aug 7, 2000,Dominion,8</i>)

>  Toothfish has quite a unique flavour. Orange roughy is more opaque and white with a fleshy feel. This is a way lot more firmer. It's damn ugly to look at too. <i>(Aug 13, 2003,Dominion Post,A7</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Ross Sea toothfish are a separate species from the Patagonian variety. "The research we've gathered suggests this Antarctic variety grows faster than the Patagonian. They seem to mature in five to seven years and probably live from 30 to 409 years." <i>(Aug 7, 2000,Dominion,8</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  It's sweet and delicate on the palate, and heavy on the wallet, but scarce supplies of Antarctic toothfish are now available in Wellington. <i>(Aug 13, 2003,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

### toothpaste tube principle
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Certainly it is not difficult to find places where the simple law of superposition appears to be no longer obeyed, and other places where the rock architect appears to have made an over-enthusiastic use of the tooth-paste tube principle in moving rocks. <i>(Dec 3, 1960,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,9/7</i>)

### TOP
 
<b>1</b> <i>abbr.</i> (Rural) Traits other than Production [a dairy breeding score of 17 criteria or traits.]

>  The information that can be gleaned from TOP, if the time is taken to use it, is of immense value. <i>(May 31, 2002,Dominion,27</i>)

>  The inspection of cattle and the official scoring by Jersey New Zealand through the Traits Other than Production (TOP) system is another service offered. Animals are scored for 17 traits on a 1 to 9 basis, which gets entered on the LIC database. <i>(May 31, 2002,Dominion,29</i>)

### top
 
 <i>n.</i> the deck (of a ship) or wharf (distinct from the hold `down below')

>  ... a top job. <i>Ibid.</i> 104 You might get a job `on top'. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,65</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a best quality farm-produced animal.

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Brought sheep in, picked out the tops, then drafted up some odds and ends. <i>(Feb 26, 1931,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Stan, June and I to the yards & drafted off 60 wethers, the tops of the mob which I hope to fatten. <i>(Jan 31, 1941,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> With both the steer calves and the wether lambs, the tail-enders plus a few of the tops are sold at weaning time and the balance are held over. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,156</i>)

>  Jersey diary heifers - Tops, BW 112. <i>(May 4, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,29</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> A `top' is a ball of sorted, scoured, and combed wool. As the sovereign is the recognised standard monetary coin so is `top' the recognised basis of manufactured wool dealings. <i>(1920,Australian & NZ Sheep Farming,145</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> They led me along a tunnel (level, as they called it), which ran at right-angles from the main drive. At the coal-face two men were working up a ladder ... `They're blowing out tops,' Charlie explained. `Lumps of coal hanging from the ceiling are a danger to men working below'. <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,45</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the head, esp. in phrs off one's top [ca1938]



 
 <i>n.</i> high point, ridge of mountain on musterer's beat (See [tops](../T#tops))

>  <b>HARPER</b> [Is he] sleeping out top. <i>(1937,Windy Island,22</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Brownie was to do the top and the head shepherd was to be `under the top'. <i>(1949,High Country Days</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Get up on a high top with a musterer and he will gaze out across the far ranges and ask you what this peak is... <i>(1951,High Country,44</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> sc. a saw

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> And while I `topped', and `set,' and filed [the old saw], the mother looked from them to me ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,178</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a highest paddock or mountain pasture [grazing sheep in the summer, and from where the top-man takes his mustering beat.]

>  <b>EVANS</b> .. cannot give a full account until the tops have been mustered for shearing. <i>(Nov 25, 1867,Letter,242</i>) [NOTES: in Vance (1965) High Endeavour]

>  Fixed up crossing onto rocky staircase .. rode home up Stuart's paddock mustering the sheep up to the tops. <i>(Apr 24, 1889,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Higher up the valley they [sandflies] weren't so bad, and out on the open tops they ceased to exist ... <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,169</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. we managed to get out on to a top from which was a good view of much of the country. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,222</i>)

>  <b>PORTER</b> .. the slow drive back to the safe country, where the sheep spent the months of winter, here we had to watch them carefully because if left alone the stupid beasts would try to return to the tops. <i>(1970,Under the Nor'West Arch,33</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> tops: the high country. Hence the phrase - for working or grazing - 'on the tops.' <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,86</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> .. the typical 'back country' sheep run, where the sheep were let loose across the tops, grazing on fern regrowth. <i>(1985,Farming in the Mohaka State Forest,4</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> You still miss it, nothing like it - getting up on those tops at daylight. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,179</i>)

>  <b>BARNES</b> Originally Ian used the chopper to actually muster the sheep off the tops down to 1,200 metres ready for the men to take them home. [open tops] <i>(1987,Country Calendar (TVNZ),116</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [mustering] to reach a vantage point on the top of a hill or ridge.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Before I topped the ridge on my way out the next morning ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,196</i>)

### top (on)
 
 <i>v.</i> to inform on

>  Detective Denis O'Rourke said Spence denied knowledge of the alleged rapes, and said he would `do time rather than top on his mates'. <i>(Oct 9, 1973,NZ Truth,51</i>)

>  And he claimed that he was told that he would be `looking down the end of a 12 gauge' if he `topped' about the alleged rape of his girl-friend. <i>(Jan 24, 1973,NZ Truth,3</i>)

>  <b>JUSTIN</b> One morning three of us were sitting behind some boxes having smoko. One said, `One thing about our [prison] librarian friend, he's improved. He doesn't top any on us all so everyone can hear.' <i>(1973,Prisoner,33</i>)

>  <b>MACKENZIE</b> <i>top, to</i> to inform on someone. To hang <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,99</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> `That flaming tally-clerk must have topped me, I heard Gaint groan, ... <i>(1964,Watersiders,31</i>)

### top and tail
 
 <i>v.</i> to sleep (or fit) head to foot (e.g., of children in a bed, of people in a bunk or small tent) (cf HEADS AND TAILS) [ca1952 R. Mason citing N. T. Moar used of trampers on a hut mattress]

### top beat
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a highest altitude mustering area in a muster.

>  <b>STRONACH</b> The man on the top beat, in all but exceptional circumstances, keeps a little in front [in a sidling operation]. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,23</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE & WOODALL</b> Usually one man and his dogs will climb well above the sheep to what is known as the 'top beat.' <i>(1958,NZ Sheep Station,22</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> top beat: though it comes from musterers' parlance the expression is not used by them alone. It is the 'plum' job of a day though it ensures the longest climb. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,85</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> It's lonely working the top beat on a muster. The next shepherd may be a kilometre away and you've got to think ahead as you move along, to ensure you keep in line with the others and cover the country. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,52</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> There's no place I'd rather be,/ Than the top beat/ With a snow-line boundary./ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,35</i>)

### top beat man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a musterer who works in a mustering team at highest altitude. (See [high beat man; top man](../H#high-beat-man;-top-man))

>  <b>MOMATIUK & EASTCOTT</b> The top beat man will go for the ridge, others for their assigned positions further down the slope. <i>(1980,High Country,69</i>)

### top hand
 
 <i>n.</i>

If he wasn't a good man on the end of a saw - some blokes were `jockeys', they rode it - well, he wouldn't last long in the bush as far as his work was concerned ... <i>(1986,Speaking a Silence,62</i>) [NOTES: (Bob Edwards, bushman)]

### top lip
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BOWEN</b> The present-day Romney, with wool to the toe and even on the `top lip' is much different from the clear open-pointed Romney of fifty years ago. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,9</i>)

### top man
 
 <i>n.</i> attrib use of TOP n1, of the highest level of a musterer's beat; or phs as a collocation TOP MAN, the musterer working the higher (or highest) beat (See [also top beat man](../A#also-top-beat-man))

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... the top man <i>(1937,Windy Island,232</i>)

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> Starting at dawn, we climbed up the Clayton side of the range, leaving men at intervals to muster along the side, and the top man waited while others went through the saddle to muster the southern side. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,18</i>) [NOTES: [f. c1869]]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a musterer in a mustering team who has the beat at highest altitude. (See [top beat man; high beat man](../T#top-beat-man;-high-beat-man))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Although their instructions were to 'just scratch the bottoms' it was inevitable that the top man would have some long hard 'heads' catching sheep up in the basins. <i>(1949,High Country Days,70</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> On the third spur I stopped to await the top man. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,53</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The top man in this case was following a narrow ridge straight into a cold southerly. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,105</i>)

>  It was probably another quarter of an hour or so before the topman showed ... <i>(Mar 23, 1967,NZ Farmer,35</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> If there is any extra work at the end of the day such as killing a sheep or driving the mob on round the hill, the bottom man gets the job, not the top man. <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,92</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Breakfast is timed so the top man - the one with the highest beat - can reach his starting position by daybreak. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,44</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> The top man moves off with others slightly behind to stop sheep breaking back behind them. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,45</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (See [top](../T#top))

>  Before preference to unionists was passed the pay [for coal workers in the Wellington Branch of the Waterside Workers' Federation] was per hour for shovellers 1s 6d and for top hands (i.e. winchmen, bullrope-men, trimmers, and tippers), is 3d. .. A gang of men consists of eight - four shovellers - and four top men - with an aggregate of 11s per hour, before preference. <i>(Jun 9, 1911,Maoriland Worker,14/12/3</i>)

 <i>n.</i> the creamy top of a bottle of milk; aka top of the milk [ca1950 Wellington 1980 p.c. Ross Clark (Auckland)]

### top-hamper
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka top hamper, tophamper]

>  When examining the plan for a county bridge submitted to him during the course of a deputation this afternoon, the Minister of Public Works (the Hon. J. G. Coates) remarked that it seemed to have a good deal of "top-hamper" about it. <i>(Dec 17, 1923,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Heavy scaffolding is being erected round the Town Hall in preparation for the removal of parapets and other unnecessary top-hamper. <i>(Jul 31, 1934,Evening Post,7</i>)

I was indignant to find that they had been weighed with great bunches of tops, which added somewhat to their weight, particularly as they had gone to seed. When I asked that the carrots should be weighed without this top-hamper, I was told that they had been bought from the Internal Marketing Department in that condition so they were obliged to sell them with the tops on to avoid loss to themselves. <i>(Sep 29, 1945,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> ... superphosphate was hand-sown on top of areas where good cocksfoot and clover had been established by serial [?aerial] sowing ... but with the first heavy frosts in March and April, the top-hamper was killed and there was no extra feed remaining for the winter. <i>(1969,Molesworth,210</i>)

### Top-knot

 <i>v.</i>

>  You know what they say about country folk. If they're not top-dressing, they're cross-dressing. <i>(Apr 22, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

### topdresser
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a portable topdressing machine carried on a tractor, truck or trailer [known as a 'blower'].

>  <b>MACNICOL & TROTTER</b> Then with new modern machinery and topdressing all the farmers were able to buy topdressers, first the small ones with the manure put in and spun out on the grass behind the tractors. Then came the Munro topdressers which were fitted on to the back of a trailer ... [top-dresser] <i>(1987,I, Duncan from Skippers,98</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... a <i>topdresser<</i> is an implement for spreading fertilizer. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,141</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pilot of a topdressing plane or a topdressing plane.

### topdressing
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fertiliser used for topdressing.

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> Forty or fifty tons of top-dressing are brought in by truck, then loaded on to a plane and spread from the air. <i>(1976,High Country Sheep Station,21</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the applying of fertiliser to farm land.

>  Another method of effecting economy in topdressing is by the use of the grass harrow in the regular spreading of animal excreta. <i>(Jul 15, 1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,11</i>)

>  On fat lamb farms topdressing now will improve the feed position and ensure that ewes are better fed before and during lambing. <i>(Apr 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,349</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Burning, oversowing and topdressing, followed by severe grazing, has worked on a few blocks. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,179</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the fertilising of land with superphosphate, etc., in farming spread from machines or aeroplanes. -->see also TARANAKI TOPDRESSING the application of fertiliser to the surface of the soil (cf also AERIAL TOPDRESSING)

>  A top-dressing plane flies round us. <i>(Feb 1989,North & South,45</i>)

>  `Topdressing' in England is used only of nitrogenous manures and on a growing crop. In New Zealand a much wider use. [The restricted meaning given by Walker would seem to be that of the OED vb and vbl sb.] <i>(Jul 24, 1953,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 1953 July 24 Professor T.W. Walker, Lincoln Agricultural College :talk on 2YA Wellington (8.00 pm)]

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The</i>)

>  The first commercial topdressing flight, made ... in May 1949, pointed the way to a new industry ... <i>(Nov 24, 1979,Listener (New Zealand Listener),365/4</i>) [NOTES: (Duncan Campbell)]

>  [<i>Country Calendar's</i>] special on topdressing pilots was highly therapeutic. <i>(Feb 8, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The claims of the National Party are similar to an offence in the fruit industry which is termed `top-dressing'. This consists of deceiving a buyer by placing high-quality fruit on the top layer of the case and inferior quality fruit underneath it. <i>(Mar 31, 1954,Standard, The,2</i>)

>  <b>TULLET</b> `I want the top-dressing pilot.' `They're both top-dressing pilots.' <i>(1964,Red Abbott,9</i>)

### tope
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also shark](../A#also-shark),[school shark](../S#school-shark))

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 130. GALEUS CANIS. L. Tope. <i>(1872,Fishes,81</i>)

>  <b>SENIOR</b> [Maoris] have a sweet tooth for sharks, especially the smooth-hound dog-fish and tope <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,298</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i>Galeus canis</i> .. Tope. <i>Ibid..</i> 116 TOPE (<i>Galeus canis</i>). ... It is the `school-shark' of the Sydney fishermen. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,115</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tope, N</i>. an Australasian Shark, <i>Galeus australis</i>, Macl. It differs somewhat from <i>Galeus canis</i>, the <i>Tope</i> of Britain. Called also the <i>School-Shark</i>, in Australia. <i>(1898,Austral English,475</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tope, N</i>. an Australasian Shark, <i>Galeus australis</i>, Macl. It differs somewhat from <i>Galeus canis</i>, the <i>Tope</i> of Britain. Called also the <i>School-Shark</i>, in Australia. <i>(1898,Austral English,475</i>)

>  <i>Galeus australis</i> Macleay. The tope is common along the east coast of Otago. <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/235</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The Tope (<i>Galeorhinus australis</i>) is a medium sized shark,... but neither it nor the ten foot whaler (<i>Carcharinus brachyurus</i>) seem to be dangerous. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,192</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 192]

>  4. <i>Galeorhinus australis</i> Macleay. Tope. Maori: Mangoreremai <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/130</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> a kind of shark. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> SCHOOL SHARK or TOPE (<i>Notogaleus australis</i>). This is very similar in shape to the Dog-fish, ... but it ... is much larger. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,61</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> TOPE OR SCHOOL SHARK <i>Notogaleus rhinophanes</i> (Peron) The origin of the vernacular name Tope is not certain, but local fishermen who followed this occupation in Britain, said this name is a corruption of `top' in allusion to its well-known habit of following the lines to the top of the water and then snatching off Whiting or other hooked fish in full view of the helpless fisherman. Other New Zealand common names are Southern Tope and School Shark. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,63</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SCHOOLSHARK [<i>Galaeorhinus australis</i>] ... Sand shark and tope are alternative names <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,12</i>)

>  <b>SOR</b> <i>SHARK, SCHOOL </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Galeorhinus australis</i> (principally). (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Flake Fillets. (c) Other common names: B.B., Big Boys; Soupfin; Grey Boys; Tope or Topi. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,42</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> SCHOOLFISH The flesh, like that of the gummies, is quite edible and regularly marketed, usually as Lemonfish. The Schoolshark is usually dark-grey-green above and white below. Known also as Tope and Sand shark they are the only fish who close their eyes when they sleep. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,224</i>)

>  Mrs Heather Morgan, of New Plymouth, caught a 21.77kg tope on an 8kg line in Northland's Parengarenga Harbour in January last year. <i>(Jul 11, 1986,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  [species name] school shark .. [scientific name] <i>Galeorhinus australis</i> .. [other name(s)] tope, big boys, greyboys, flake, grey shark, soupfin, sandshark <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>School shark Galeorhinus australis</i> ... Other names include tope and grey shark. Also occurs around southern Australia, and is similar to the Californian soupfin shark. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,29</i>)

>  It must be remembered that this bird, having for its habitat the tops of alpine ranges, is seen by few others than shepherds ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/.278</i>) [NOTES: ?transitional use]

>  Several tope sharks were hooked on his line. <i>(Mar 9, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  Some anglers have been catching good sized ling and tope sharks out at Nicholson's Trench. <i>(Sep 16, 1992,Evening Post,42</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The southern tope, or school shark, <i>Galeorhinus australis</i>, is one of the small so-called dogfish commonly taken by trawlers around New Zealand. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/259</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Galeorhinus australis</i> Macleay. SOUTHERN TOPE or SCHOOL SHARK. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,7,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Galeorhinus australis</i> Macleay. Southern tope. <i>(1938,TrNZI,68/400</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]
