 <i>n.</i> (See [tope](../T#tope))

>  4. <i>Galeorhinus australis</i> Macleay. Tope.Mangoreremai <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/139</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] shark, tope 201-4, 208-9, 218, 234 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### topi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tope](../T#tope))

### topitopi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Topitopi, <i>Mantell. Alectryon excelsum.</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

### topknot
 
 <i>n.</i> a bump near spout-hole of a whale

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The whalers can distinguish ... a bull from a cow; - the elevation near the spout-hole(s), called the top-knot, being much higher in the bulls, and this part is always above the water; <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/54</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the kempy wool growing on a sheep's head that may cause wool-blindness. [top knot, top-knot]

>  The odd bits, such as the 'top knot,' cheeks, and little odds and ends of broken wool, should be packed separately, but the filthy wool should be thrown out. <i>(Aug 1887,NZ Farmer,229</i>)

>  Top-knot, consisting of very light kempy tender wool, often having a bluish tinge. <i>(1919,Wool in NZ,20</i>)

>  Pick out stained wool, face pieces, leggings and top-knots. <i>(Oct 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,313</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> The wool on the forehead known as the top-knot is the wool that hangs down over the eye, blinding the sheep. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,64</i>)

>  <b>BLACK & DYSON</b> If possible keep top knots out of all specialty carpet wool. <i>(1978,Carpetmakers: Specialty Carpet Wool breeds of NZ,15</i>)

>  <b>FISK</b> If you have a bigger mob or are pooling wool with neighbours then you can divide the bellies and pieces into crutchings (from the back end) bellies, leg pieces and topknots (the short hairy pieces from the top of the head and cheeks.) <i>(1996,Practical Smallfarming,84</i>)

>  <b>WOOLPRO</b> Topknot: short, fuzzy, and usually kempy wool growing on the poll of a sheep (top of head.) [top-knot, top knot] <i>(2001,A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms,38</i>)

>  The points are: Large head, on a strong neck, well covered with wool, and having a top-knot <i>(Oct 9, 1906,Nelson Evening Mail,1</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> A top-knot of true wool, not down, should cover the forehead [of the Leicester]. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,37</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> He ... removes the top-knot - a small cap of fuzzy oily wool which grows on the top of the sheep's head. <i>(1927,Sheep & Wool Industry,77</i>)

>  Pick over the lambs' wool. Pick out stained wool, face pieces, leggings, and top-knots, as these wools make the buyer suspicious ... <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,313</i>)

>  <i>Top knot</i>: The wool grown on the top of the head. <i>(Feb 26, 1965,NZ Journal of Agriculture,15</i>) [NOTES: (H.A.L. Laing)]

>  <b>166</b> `What's 2nd Pcs H?' `Grade 2, pieces - that's skirtings - hogget wool.' `And Bls E?' `That's belly wool. E grade bellies.' `Lox?' `Oh, that's floor sweepings.' `What about Lbs Wigs?' `Lamb top-knots - off the head.' <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,FERNANDEZ</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> .. inside the hind legs, off with the topknot, lean the sheep over to the left,. . <i>(1981,Sunrise,45</i>)

>  <i>Topknot:</i> Wool shorn from the top of a sheep's head. <i>Ibid.</i> <i>Wigging:</i> Shearing wool from the head of sheep. Normally carried out at crutching. The trade term for this wool is eye clips. Also known as topknots or wigs. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,60</i>)

>  <i>topknot ... NZ & Austra</i>l. wool shorn from top of sheep's head. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,811</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Sheep of the following description may be safely rejected .. 2. Sheep that strip at the points, and lose the belly-wool, having a clean head without top-knot. <i>(May 3, 1871,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  The head should have a good topknot, the face wool meeting under the eyes, and ending abruptly, yellow down, not white hairs on the nose: ears small, and covered with down or wool; wool to the toes is desirable. <i>(Jan 28, 1882,Otago Witness,7</i>)

### topknot/topnot
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>TOPKNOT Notoclinus fenestratus</i> ... It also has a distinctive high first dorsal fin set well forward toward the head that prompted the name topknot. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,283,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] topknot, topnot 1926, 1932, 1984, 870 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <i>Notoclinus fenestratus</i> (Forster). Topnot. <i>(1938,TrNZI,68/416</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Topnot <i>Notoclinus fenestratus</i> (Bloch and Schn.) (Syn. <i>Tripterygion compressum</i> Hutton) ... Topnot <i>Zeablennius laticlavius</i> (Griffen) ... Topnot <i>Sticharium venustum</i> (Griffen) ... Topnot <i>Cynophidium punctatum</i> Regan <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,4111</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 411]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> TOPNOT <i>Notoclinus fenestratus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,335</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> TOPNOT [<i>Notoclinus fenestratus</i> ] ... No other names are in common use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,18</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The Blenny goes by several other names too. In his various forms he is the Cockabully, Threepenny, Twister, and Topknot. All are slightly different in looks but come under the collective name of Blenny for easy grouping. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,28</i>)

### topknotting
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the cutting of headwool from sheep. (See [eye-wigging; eye-clipping](../E#eye-wigging;-eye-clipping))

>  Crutching rates are 10/- per 100 plus an extra 2/- per 100 for topknotting or eyewigging, plus 15 per cent. <i>(May 24, 1951,NZ Farmer,26</i>)

### topmaker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BREMNER</b> <i>Topmaker</i> manufacturer who straightens and thins wool into a continuous, untwisted, thumb-thick sliver, ready for the spinning machine. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,10</i>)

### topmaking
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>(Nov 5, 1969,NZ News,5/5</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of fleece wool] suitable for worsted processing.

>  Crossbred descriptions were mainly light conditioned good average topmaking wools, though a proportion lacked full length ... Good average topmaking, however, compared the bulk of the offering. <i>(Nov 6, 1963,Straight Furrow,5</i>)

>  Prices for poor topmaking sorts and fairly faulty lines of oddments were very erratic. <i>(Jan 8, 1964,Straight Furrow,5</i>)

### topper
 
 <i>n.</i> informer

>  GLOSSARY OF TECHNICAL TERMS: A boobhead is a prisoner; a screw is a warder; a topper is a toady or an informer. The Act or the Collar - The Habitual Criminals Act, under which persons declared habitual criminals can have their sentences increased by the Prisons Board for offences against prison discipline during their incarceration. <i>(Nov 1950,Here & Now,14</i>) [NOTES: (Ian Hamilton `NZd's Last Flogging')]

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> Some screw had been earwigging, I like to think, though there were quite a few toppers and crawlers at the [detention] camp. <i>Ibid.</i>53 Toppers are what used to be called copper's narks in England and there's always about thirty per cent of them in jails. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,22</i>)

>  Truck drivers were `staff men' and nearly all toppers and crawlers. <i>(Nov 1953,Here & Now</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton) [Prison]]

>  <b>WATSON</b> ... Detective Crome came round. He wanted a statement about Jerry. ... `I'm not a topper,' he said after Detective Crome had gone. <i>(1965,Stand in the Rain,109</i>)

>  <b>JUSTIN</b> `Yeah! Here's my slot. Twenty-two. Come on in. ...' So, a slot was a cell. A topper was a kind of pimp. I was learning. <i>(1973,Prisoner,23</i>)

>  To Sergeant A. Carter, prosecuting, the witness alleged gang members had accused her friend of being a `topper'. <i>(Dec 24, 1973,NZ Truth,3</i>)

>  <b>AGNEW</b> I learned later that a topper was at the dance. He betrayed my presence to the Whataroa constable. <i>(1974,Loner, The,176</i>)

>  The Prisoner's Code: I can't crawl to screws (cops, prison officers, etc), snyone who does is a `Nark', `topper', or has `Hands on his stomach' or `Brown Ankles' or is `Pushing a third' remission. <i>(Jul 12, 1976,Salient,6</i>)

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> Prisoners who did converse [with officers] were accused of `sucking up' to officers, and were held in contempt by their fellows. They were known as `brown noses', `toppers' or `canaries'. <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,16</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> TOPPER (n) Informer. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,255</i>)

>  The phone rang twenty times a day, with accusations of topper, pakeha topper ... <i>(1987,Metro Fiction,10</i>) [NOTES: (Keogh, Chiaoscuro)]

### topping
 
 <i>n.</i> informing (See [cf. top (on)](../T#top-(on)),[top off v](../T#top-off-v))

>  The complainant's fiance said `topping' was a term the gang used to describe reporting a matter to the police. <i>(Dec 24, 1973,NZ Truth,3</i>)

### topping and tailing
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) ear-marking, castrating and tailing of lambs.

>  A & P Conference iv topping and tailing were best done on grass or in temporary yards and low, swampy ground was best avoided. <i>(Aug 1900,NZ Farmer,Supplement</i>)

### topping off
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the finishing or fattening of farm stock for sale or exhibition. [1956 citation is of the verb to top off]

>  <b>TAIT</b> Crops of swedes are grown for feeding out in winter and for 'topping off' the stock. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,88</i>)

>  TAIT Farms & Stations of NZ II All wether lambs are fattened, a crop of turnips being grown for topping off. <i>(1958</i>)

### tops
 
 <i>n.</i> usually pl with def art THE TOPS, the top of a ridge; a peak; (collectively) the tops of hills, ridges, or mountains, the highest country (usually above the bushline) occas. in phrs ON TOP (cp BOTTOMS) cf also TOP MAN - [used regularly in weather forecasts]

>  <i>(1958,Tararua,12/29</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Faintly he heard Mac on the top above him yelling and barking his dogs. <i>(1959,Tall Tussock,22</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> <i>(1961,Good Keen Man, A,22</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> On the sheep station they called me Cloudburst ... I was on the tops while you were still on the titty. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,174</i>)

>  The Forest Service has placed huts at short intervals along the tops, ... marked tracks ... <i>(1971,VUWTC'71,7</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> I'll push them [sc sheep] off right there, towards the tops. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,44</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> Beyond Top Hut the track is benched for maybe 20 minutes before large poles take over to mark the easy, well-worn path across the tops. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,65</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> Beyond Top Hut the track is benched for maybe 20 minutes before large poles take over to mark the easy, well-worn path across the tops. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,65</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> But treacherous bogs ... abound in the flat tops, and roadmaking would be difficult. <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations of NZ,5</i>)

>  The agreement freeholds the tops of the Hector Mountains on the lake side of the range... Under their agreement, the Taylors can graze the tops on their property only with a limited number of a specified kind of sheep for the first three months of each year. <i>(Jul 12, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  We gaze towards the tawny peaks - the areas thart are likely to go to DoC through tenure review. Bridget Mead declares that her daughter has the right to muster along those tops, as she has done - it's a part of her heritage. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/41</i>)

>  The ridge walk had become impassable and they had to bush-bash through the leatherwood on the tops. <i>(Jan 9, 1994,Sunday Times (NZ),19</i>)

>  At the end of the second day, I'm bluffed on the tops and have to set up a fly-camp surrounded by an arena of windthrow-covered ridges, cliffs and waterfalls... Compared with the tops on which I had been stranded, the podocarp-clothed Waiau River provides easy travelling and a relative abundance of bush-foods... <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  It must be remembered that this bird, having for its habitat the tops of alpine ranges, is seen by few others than shepherds ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/278</i>) [NOTES: ?transitional use]

>  I went out to the tops: in a small basin under the top, .... facing a rocky country that we called `skay', there was a mob of sheep snowed in ... <i>(1907,TrNZI,39/73</i>)

>  Most of the ridges are rounded on the tops, and many of them are covered with extensive peat bogs, dotted over with numerous lagoons. <i>(1915,TrNZI,47/121</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> ... there was a wedge-shaped block of ninety thousand acres of high tops, mostly bush-bound, in the middle ... <i>(1946,Early Canterbury Runs,210</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> Cloud on the tops always keeps trampers on their toes. Navigation becomes important and the likelihood of some whacking directional error increases sharply. <i>(1988,Hills, The,48</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> Basic tops travel involves little more than following a track out of the bushline and onto the crest of a tussock ridge, ... Even more challenging than this is camping on the tops, perhaps by a pretty tarn with a long-range view over the mountains and a brilliant sunset. ... <i>(1988,Hills, The,46</i>)

>  [Caption] Tops camp at head of Karangarua Valley, South Westland. <i>(1988,Hills, The</i>)

>  It is a fine sight you see the river flowing down between mountains 3 and 4000 ft., the [70] lofty back ranges looking blue and the tops covered with snow. <i>(Dec 13, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,69,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Abner and I took the top of the range and had a great run after the sheep. There were eight fellows spread about the face of the range below us but we did not make a very clean muster notwithstanding. <i>(Oct 24, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,202,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> We lost very few sheep; [217] they were all up at the tops of the high hills, their favourite summer pasture. <i>(1868,Station Life in NZ,216</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> We were so late in the season we could not tackle the high tops, but had to dodge a ray-trace [check] across the depression abreast the Ramsay Glacier. <i>(1893,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1893 Roberts letter to A.P. Harper in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 28]

>  ... the tops, however, often carry tussock and other herbage, affording admirable pasture for sheep in summer; but stock have to be removed from April to October, during which period this country is generally covered with snow. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,31</i>)

>  I went out to the tops. <i>(1907,TrNZI,39.73</i>) [NOTES: (Benham)]

>  ... I only managed to botanize the sea-face and the tops ... <i>(1907,TrNZI,39/382</i>) [NOTES: (Townson)]

>  Dawn finds the leader [of the musterers] on the tops, six thousand feet up, the wet wind in his face and his shirt ringing wet with sweat. <i>(Apr 10, 1917,Triad, The,66</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> On the right below the high top called Pou-nui-a-Hine is another small cave beneath a limestone projection, in ancient times the home of a <i>kumi. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 170 The `burn' of 1902 ... was not what is technically known as a `clean' fire. ... this fire left unburnt ridge-caps, the tops, sometimes even the upper slopes. <i>(1921,Tutira,65</i>)

>  <i>Tops</i>.- Often called <i>high tops.</i> (1) High summer country. (C.) (2) In North Canterbury, country above the bush. (C.) <i>(Dec 23, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  On the tops he could keep pace with men young enough to be his sons. <i>(1945,NZ Geographer,29</i>) [NOTES: 1945 Pascoe Canterbury High Country in NZ Geographer 29]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... shingle tops ... Mount White's beckoning tops. [check] <i>(1947,Wayleggo,33 et passim</i>)

>  Next morning we had to be up early to reach the tops before the merinos broke camp and started back for the country off which they had been mustered. <i>Ibid.</> 31 Merino ewes always camp just below the highest top on what they regard as their own piece of country, ... <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,18</i>) [NOTES: [f. c1869]]

>  <b>28</b> The end came when, coming down off the tops with a fair-sized load of skins, the frame wedged in some stout branches of scrub. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,THOMSON</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> On the high open tops shooting was also at reasonably close range. <i>(1952,Deer Shooter,44</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> If sheep there sometimes grew fat it would be as deer occasionally grow fat on the tops and basins above our beech forests; ... <i>(Mar 19, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,26,1961 ed.</i>)

>  ...set out under tall mahoe and fuchsia, till the track breaks out of the bush on to scrubby tops beaten flat by the wind. <i>(Jan 9, 2004,Dominion Post</i>)

>  Beyond here the track ascends the open tops of Judd Ridge, which can be very exposed to the elements. <i>(2008,NZ Geographic,94/30</i>)

>  Another area sought for wilderness designation is the 'Paparoa tops', along the rugged ridges inland from Paparoa National Park. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,No. 304/4</i>)

>  Generations of high-country run-holders, for example, speak of the tops, the tussock and the tarns with something akin to awe, as do the Tuhoe, the people of the mist, of their land. <i>(Aug 13, 2003,Dominion Post,B8</i>)

>  There are some groves of fine timber on the sides of the valley; the hills which bound it are high, but on their tops lies a table land only partially wooded. <i>(May 16, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  Little two-seater Robinsons, which some of the cockies have, were ferrying snow rakers up to the tops. <i>(Oct 1992,North & South,17</i>)

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> Diederich and Scanlon followed the usual route to the tops. <i>(1994,Tararua : the Story of a Mountain Range,144</i>)

>  The map showed that the far side of the valley would soon have farmland in behind the bushed tops. <i>(Nov 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

>  They knew which way each gate opened, how to saddle up, whether it was a stray sheep on the Tops or that plant thing. <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

### topside run
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an approach or position of a dog, while working sheep on a hill.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> topside run: the more usual, and opposite of, bottomside run. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,86</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Top-side run</i>: This refers to a run where the dog works above or on the top side of the sheep. The reverse to bottom-side work. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,169</i>)

### topside work
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [high or hill country mustering] the driving of sheep downhill from above.

>  The 'Lake' country is mainly open shingle and it's mostly 'topside' work - slewing sheep downhill. <i>(Apr 11, 1968,NZ Farmer,45</i>)

### topuni
 
 <i>n.</i> a mat [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  In his hand [Pomare] usually carries a short cloak of dog-skin, called <i>topuni, shupuni</i>, or <i>patutu. </i> <i>(1852,U.S. Exploring Expedition,1/303</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar in 1852 Wilkes U.S. Exploring Expedition 1/303]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> These two distinguished chiefs sat to me ... wearing the <i>topuni</i>, or war-mat of dog's skin. <i>Ibid.</i> 156 The `topuni,' or war mat, belongs exclusively to the man, and is only possessed by chiefs ... The `topuni' consists of a large flax cloak, into which is fastened, with every thread, a portion of dog's hair, assorted into various colours, having the exact appearance of a beautiful fur. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/297</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The `topuni,' or war mat, belongs exclusively to the man, and is only possessed by chiefs ... The `topuni' consists of a large flax cloak, into which is fastened, with every thread, a portion of dog's hair, assorted into various colours, having the exact appearance of a beautiful fur. <i>(1866,Polynesia,156</i>)

>  There is a splendid show of greenstone .. genuine topuni or fighting gown made out of the skin and hair of dog tails; and a host of other things, a careful examination of which would be a liberal education in the achievements of the natives. <i>(May 25, 1893,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

### topuni reserve
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Also included were statutory acknowledgements of [nohoanga] (traditional camping sites), topuni sites (significant sites)and place names. <i>(Sep 2000,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://knowledge-basket.co.nz/waitangi/temanu/celebrate.html]

>  It was the announcement of the creation of nohoanga (exclusive riverside campsites facilitating access to customary fishing sites) and topuni reserves (the granting of additional status to areas of special significance) within the Ngai Tahu settlement that resulted in Mr Graham's recent comments that there were some different laws for Maori and pakeha. <i>(Jun 15, 1997,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

### tor
 
 <i>n.</i> a marble (See [also taw](../A#also-taw))

>  Tor - the one you used for all shooting - a marble never to be given away <i>(1987,Alfredton,75</i>) [NOTES: Quote from c1935]

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> Gus had prayed every prayer he could think of that Knocker would do it so that he could give back to Knocker the seven marbles he had won from him by cheating. ... That afternoon he did a boy's sums for seven allys and a tor. <i>(1960,Tangahano,10</i>)

 <i>n.</i>



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  They included 345 pingao, 600 spinifex and 350 <i>Muehlenbeckia astonii</i> (shrubby toraro) planted as part of a dune restoration project at Castlepoint. <i>(Oct 2003,Footnotes,No. 20/7</i>)

### torch
 
 <i>v.</i> to seek out and take fish (esp. flounders and eels, by spear), or muttonbirds, by dazzling them with torchlight [ca1935 Havelock, for flatfish The torchlight provided by strong torches, car-battery lamps, or flaxstick and tow, dipped in tar or oil and fired]

>  <b>JENKINS</b> A writer in the <i>Southland Times</i> in the early part of the century stated that he had heard the <i>hakuwai</i> on 10 May 1913. He and his companions were on one of the hills of Poutama Island, torching, when they heard an unusual sound. <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,157</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> [The flounders are a splendid fish, the finest of their tribe; spearing them by torchlight is an excellent sport.] <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,77</i>)

### torchie
 
 <i>n.</i> sc. in eel-fishing

>  <i>(Apr 6, 1966,Weekly Herald,32</i>)

### torching
 
 <i>n.</i> catching fish, or muttonbirds, by dazzling them with torchlight [ca1935 Havelock e.g. Go torching on the mudflats.] (See [cf. spotlighting](../S#spotlighting))

>  <b>BAILEY & ROTH</b> We catch all our birds in the torching,/ And after the cleaning we're scorching;/ <i>(1967,Shanties,136</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> It is during this period that mutton-birders go out at night, `torching', catching the young birds which are often found at the entrance to their burrows. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,60</i>)

>  ... the hunting system [for muttonbirds] known as `torching' which takes place right at the end of the season ... <i>(Mar 5, 1966,Dominion,6</i>)

>  <b>JENKINS</b> This would account for the fact that it is heard only at torching time, after most of the adult birds have left the island, and when muttonbirders search out the young birds by torchlight. <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,156</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Ever since the beginning, to `take' meant journeying to the islands and living there for the season and using hands or any device to catch the birds. ... to `take' also includes an `attempt to take'. During the torching season the birds run down the paths to the sea at night. Paths are used as boundaries by the [56] supervisors and in some cases the dividing line is the middle of the path. If one pursues a bird and grabs it over the other edge of the path one can be prosecuted, even although the bird came from the pursuer's manu. <i>Ibid.</i> 68 The `torching' season became known as such because the birders used flaming torches thick enough to be held aloft comfortably grasped in the hand. They were about three feet long and well soaked in muttonbird fat, before being set alight and were made from dry tree branches. Piles of these `torches' were prepared and set aside awaiting [69] the start of the `torching' and each family had their torch bearers. In most cases this duty was carried out by the children. Gradually this primitive method of `lighting the way' gave place to the European kerosene lantern and when supplies of kerosene were exhausted a lighted candle was sometimes used inside the glass globe of the lantern. <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,55</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> First part of the work over except for scrapings. Coming up to torching time. Birds excellent in quality and quantity. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,153</i>)

>  About April 20 the birds start coming out of their burrows and torching begins. <i>(Oct 14, 1953,NZ Observer,16</i>)

>  The 'torching' season become known as such because the birders, used flaming torches thick enough to be held aloft comfortably grasped in the hand. <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,68</i>)

### torea
 
 <i>n.</i> the oystercatcher, <i>Haematopus</i> spp. or red-bill

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Tor|ea; A bird with a long red bill. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,216</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 51. <i>Haemotopus picatus, Vigors's King's ... </i>Scarcely different from this species, and very common in New Zealand. Toria of the natives. <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/196</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/196]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Word list] Torea a sea bird <i>(1843,Journal,52</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> We continued our route along this beach, ... endeavouring in the meantime to shoot some toreas (redbills), in order to add a little to our [51] small stock of provisions. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,50</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> ... the most numerous bird along the coast is the torea (Hematopus picatus). <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,140</i>)

>  <b>WEKEY</b> Among gulls, albatrosses, and other sea birds ..., the<i> toria</i>, or oyster eater, and the <i>korora</i>, also living on a species of shell-fish, called<i> pipi</i>, is met with on the sea shore. <i>(1862,Otago As It Is,66</i>)

>  Haematopus unicolor. Torea. Red-bill. East Coast. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  HAEMATOPUS LONGIROSTRIS, Vieil. Torea. Oyster-catcher, Red-bill. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/69</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 68. HAEMATOPUS LONGIROSTRIS. Vieill. Pied Oyster-catcher. Red-bill. Torea. 69. HAEMATOPUS UNICOLOR. Forst. Black Oyster-catcher. Red-bill. Torea. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,26</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> HAEMOTOPUS LONGIROSTRIS. (PIED OYSTER-CATCHER.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Torea. ... This fine species ... is generally dispersed round the southern coast of Australia ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,273,1888 2nd edn 2/16 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Torea, or oyster-catcher (<i>Haematopus longirostris</i>) is one of the sea-coast birds, and is often to be seen about our tidal rivers. It is a black bird. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/225</i>)

>  <i>Haematopus longirostris</i>, Vieill. Pied Oyster-catcher, Red-bill, Torea. <i>iBID.</i> 67 <i>Haematopus unicolor</i>, Forst. Black Oyster-catcher, Black Red-bill, Torea. - Breeds out on `the plains' in riverbeds ... <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,510</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Maori name for all the New Zealand species of the Oyster-catchers (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,475</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The large sea-gull (<i>karoro:</i> Larus dominicanus) and another gull, the Oyster-catcher (<i>torea</i>: Haemotopus sp.), were domesticated, but merely as pets ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,180</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> An island further up the river is known as Torea (redbill) because these birds nest there. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,18</i>)

>  <i>to[o]rea</i>, a shore-bird (Haematopus ostralegus), oyster-catcher. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>torea:</i> oystercatcher, bird. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Haematopus ... longirostris </i>... The bill is shorter and stouter than the New Zealand type, ... Oyster Catchers are found in New Zealand, Australia and the Moluccas west of New Guinea. Oyster Catcher. Torea. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,79</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> SOUTH ISLAND PIED OYSTERCATCHER <i>Haemotopus finschi Other names</i>: Redbill, Torea. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,118</i>)

>  <i>torea n.</i> Maori name for the New Zealand oyster-catchers [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  There's so much outdoors,.. the Ahuriri River atrtracting dotterels, plovers, terns, oystercatchers, pied stilts, paradise ducks. <i>(Jan 6, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  <i>torea ... n.</i> Maori name for N.Z. oyster-catchers [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <i>142. Haematopus ostralegus finschi</i> Martens, 1897 <i>South Island Pied Oystercatcher (Torea)</i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,43,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LOCKLEY</b> There is also the plain, all-black torea (known as the `variable oystercatcher') locally [Hauraki Gulf] called `mussel-picker,' a solitary bird which haunts the rocky shore where mussels grow. <i>(1980,House Above the Sea,180</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> <i>South Island Pied Oystercatcher Haematopus ostralegus finschi </i>MARTENS,1897 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Torea, redbill. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,174</i>)

>  Along the uncovered mudflats may be seen the torea or oyster catcher (haematopus longirostris), a bird of pied plumage. <i>(Nov 5, 1887,Auckland Weekly News</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> HIMANTOPUS LEUCOCEPHALUS. (WHITE-HEADED STILT.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Tutumata \{and Tuturipourewa\}; Torea (of Arawa tribe). ...The White-headed Stilt, which appears to be also widely distributed over ... Australia, is a comparatively common bird in the middle and southern portions of New Zealand; ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,203,{1888 2nd edn 2/21} ed.</i>)

[ed. fn] This name is applied to both the Oystercatcher ... and the Pied Stilt (<i>Himantopus leucocephalus</i>). The latter is more likely to be seen about these lakes, and is known today in the area as Torea. <i>(Jan 21, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 21 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 368]

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> PIED STILT <i>Himantopus leucocephalus Other names:</i> Stilt, Barker, Poaka, White-headed Stilt, Daddy Long-legs, Torea (Rotorua), Pip (Kaipara). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,147</i>)

(RED-FRONTED PARRAKEET.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Kakariki, Kakawariki, Powhaitere, Porere, and Torete ...[59] The Red-fronted Parrakeet is very generally dispersed over the whole country - but is more plentiful in the southern portion of the North Island than in the far north, where the yellow-fronted species predominates. <i>Ibid.</i> 62 PLATYCERCUS AURICEPS. (YELLOW-FRONTED PARRAKEET.) ... [follows preceding cit.] <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,58,1888 2nd edn 1/137, 142 ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> We ... lived upon ... <i>toretore</i> or sea anemonies [sic], making the latter into what we termed a `soup', flavoured with salt water and the wild parsley which grows among the rocks of that coast. <i>(1846,Notes of an Expedition to Kawatiri and Araura</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Expedition to Kawatiri in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 244]

>  <i>Tori tori,</i> and <i>ko toretore</i>, are sea anemone. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,415</i>)

### tori line
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ...New Zealand's challenge in Hawaii is to convince other countries to use techniques such as: setting hooks at night to reduce bird numbers;.. flying streamers (tori lines) astern to distract birds; firing cannons to scare birds; avoiding areas where birds are known to congregate. <i>(Nov 23, 2002,Dominion Post,F12</i>)

>  Marine investigations have shown that tori lines (bird-scaring devices) dramatically reduce the by-catch of sea-birds. <i>(Aug 24, 1997,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

### toria
 
 <i>n.</i> early variant of torea (stilt)

>  <b>66</b> BIRDS MET WITH IN PELORUS ... Oysterpickers - two kinds, called Toria by the natives... BIRDS MET WITH IN PELORUS ... Stilts - two kinds, called Toria by natives. Flycatchers and Fantails. <i>(1855,Sailing Directions</i>)

### Torlesse
 
 <i>adj.</i> . [Geol. classification]

>  As far as I know, there is no adequate definition of the upper limit of the Torlesse. There is a default Definition:the Torlesse is the rocks of the accretionary complex below a line, commonly a discordant contact, that are more deformed, less fossiliferous and generally more geologiacally intractable than those above. <i>(Mar 1998,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,115/53</i>)

>  The name "Torlesse" was introduced by Haast (1865) for the "greywackes" of Canterbury, based on the Torlesse Range in the foothills of the Southern Alps. <i>(Jul 1998,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,116/21</i>)

>  In contrast to the Torlesse Group the Haast Schist is represented with considerable structural detail, mainly derived from Grindley (1963). <i>(Nov 1968,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,Vol 26</i>) [NOTES: No page numbers in Newsletter 26]

>  The revival of Haast's term "Torlesse", proposed informally by Brian Mason and formally by Suggate (1961), has been found convenient and practicable for mapping purposes. <i>(May 1971,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,30/9</i>)

>  The great bulk of Canterbury greywacke remains Torlesse undifferentiated: Perhaps Wilson's units defined in S62 may be shown to have wider application. <i>(Jun 1974,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,37/31</i>) [NOTES: [That capital on 'perhaps' is as written.]]

### toro
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Myrsine salicina</i>

>  Of the nine Myrsine native to New Zealand, toro is one of the lesser known. <i>(Aug 12, 2006,Dominion Post Indulgence,19</i>)

>  The leaves of toro (<i>Myrsine salicina</i>, mamangi (<i>Coprosoma arborea</i>) amd makomako (wineberry) have a rich pink undersurface, yet these species thrive in sunny, exposed positions at the forest margins. <i>(Nov 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 60/61</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Persoonia</i>, a member of the Proteaceae, [Auck/N Auck to 38 lat]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Of the <i>Proteaceae</i>, the Tora (Persoonia tora)[toru] and Rewa-rewa (Knightia excelsa) are the only known species. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> [=? Suttonia; ? Persoonia] The Toro, this tree grows naturally into a perfect oval, and the leafs [sic] at the extremity of each branch are pink, the others quite green and printed. It seldom grows larger than 40 feet and its flower has a pleasing and odoriferous smell. [<i>glossary</i> 120 ...<i> Suttonia salicina</i>. The reference to flower odour suggests <i>Persoonia toru</i>, also sometimes known as toro]. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,105,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Persoonia <i>toro</i>, A.Cunn. ... HAB. Northern Island. Woods, from Auckland northward, ... Nat. name, `Toro', <i>Col. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 219</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Toro, <i>Col[enso]. Persoonia Toro. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>1/iii/5</b> Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth ... the Toro, <i>Persoonia Toro;</i>... the Karo, <i>Pittosporum crassifolium</i>; and the truly evergreen Ngaio, <i>Myoporum laetum ..</i>. <i>(1869,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>2/90</b> The slopes of the hills are usually covered with a dense forest ... the forms being ... Toro (<i>Persoonia Toro</i>, A Cunn.) ... and immense tussocks of an undescribed Astelia, and cutting grasses (<i>Gahnia lacera</i>, Stend., <i>G. arenaria</i>, Hook. f.) ... and Mange-mange (<i>Lygodium articulatum</i>, Swartz.), ... render all progress tedious ... <i>(1870,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> TORO.- <i>(Persoonia toro.) </i>A small tree, 20 to 30 feet high, confined to the Province of Auckland. <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,23</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> [Native name] Toro .. [Settlers' name] Toro .. [Family] Proteaceae <i>(1886,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Persoonia toru (The Toru or Toro).</i> A small tree. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,148,1940, p. 153 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> The development of the kauri to dominance was preceded by scrub and low-forest phases, the chief species being mingimingi, <i>Gaultheria</i>, manoao, kumarahou, tauhinu, <i>Lycopodium</i>, umbrella fern, manuka, kanuka, tree ferns, tanekaha, toro, and rewarewa. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,3,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BRASCH</b> He had collected too a number of North Island trees and shrubs, at that time rareties in Dunedin, ...-a kauri, a whau, a puriri, a toro, a tawa and others. <i>(1980,Indirections,53</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>P[ersoonia] Toru,</i> A.Cunn. ... <i>North Cape to Rotorua and East Cape. ... Toru; Toro.</i> ... The specific name was given as `Toru' in Cunningham's original description ... and according to Mr Colenso this is the proper spelling of the Maori name. It was, however, changed to `Tora' ..., and was again altered to `Toro' by Sir J.D. Hooker. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,605</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Persoonia toru (The Toru or Toro).</i> A small tree. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,148,1940, p. 153 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. >  <b>DENNIS</b> Most prominent among the broadleaf species here [in Westland] are toro (<i>Myrsine salicina</i>), Westland quintinia (<i>Q. acutifolia</i>) ... stinkwood (<i>C. foetidissima</i>) ... and pate (<i>Schefflera digitata</i>) with hutu <i>Ascarina</i> [158] <i>lucida</i>) ... <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,157</i>)

### toroa
 
 <i>n.</i> the albatross. [Maori]

>  They spent their time in fishing, shooting, and planting for the settlers and in catching the Toroa, a bird which they prized for its flesh. <i>(Feb 1, 1933,NZ Tablet,15</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Tor|oa, <i>s</i>. An albatross. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,217</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 72. <i>Diomedea exulans, Linn., Lath</i>. ... `Not immediately near the shores ... but in the New Zealand seas, exist several kinds of albatrosses, which the natives call Toroa.' - <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/200</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/200]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Word list] Toro[?a] a sea bird a large bird hakokoa do <i>(Aug 1843,Journal,52</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Six species of the <i>Procellaridae </i>are found on the sea-coasts. The Titi, or mutton bird, and the Toroa, or albatross, are the most celebrated birds of this family. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/28</i>)

>  Diomedea exulans. Toroa. Albatross. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/161</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 118. DIOMEDEA EXULANS. L. Albatross. Torea.[sic: error for Toroa] <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,43</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> DIOMEDEA EXULANS. (WANDERING ALBATROSS.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Toroa. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,289</i>)

>  DIOMEDEA MELANOPHRYS. (BLACK-EYEBROWED ALBATROSS.) ...<i> Native name</i>.- Toroa. ... This species of Albatross is far more common in our seas than <i>Diomedea exulans, ... </i> <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,292,1888 2nd edn 2/198 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1928,Where the White Man Treads,61</i>)

>  <b>55/159</b> <i>toroa</i>, a sea-bird (Diomedea exulans), albatross: tuft of down much valued as an ear-ornament. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>toroa</i>: albatross <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> Feathers of the albatross (<i>toroa</i>), long-tailed cuckoo (<i>koekoea</i>), and heron (<i>kotuku</i>) were also valued. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,284</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Diomedes ... regia</i> ... [88] Royal Albatross. TOROA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,87</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>toroa</i>: albatross. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,195</i>)

>  <i>toroa n.</i> the wandering albatross [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>toroa ... n.</i> the wandering albatross [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <i>18. a. Diomedea exulans exulans</i> Linnaeus, 1758 <i>Wandering Albatross (Toroa) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,18,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Wandering Albatross Diomedea exulans</i>LINNAEUS, 1758 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Wanderer, toroa. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 60 <i>Royal Albatross Diomedea epomophora</i> LESSON, 1825. ... OTHER NAME: <i>Toroa</i>. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,58</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HULME</b> It was the kind of bird Kerewin called a mollymawk, and Joe, toroa. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,247</i>)

>  <i>New Zealand Black-browed Mollymawk D[iomedea] m[elanophrys] impavida </i> MATHEWS, 1912. OTHER NAME: <i>Toroa </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,61</i>)

### toroa-ruru
 
 <i>n.</i> the nelly

If a birder comes near the nest the old birds move away a little and the young ones squirt a horrible smelling oil out of their mouths for a distance of 8 ft. Nellies are filthy birds that range the whole of the Southern Hemisphere and deserve their name of stinkpot. Nelly, Stinkpot. TOROA-RURU. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,86</i>)

### toroi
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Toromiro. <i>Podocarpus ferruginea. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

### toropapa
 
 <i>n.</i> one of several native shrub species distinguished by highly fragrant flowers and crimson berries

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Alseuosmia macrophylla (The Large-leaved Alseuosmia).</i> A shrub, 4 ft.- 10 ft. in height. ... Maori name, <i>Toropapa. </i>... <i>Alseuosmia quercifolia (The Oak-leaved Alseuosmia)</i> ... Maori name, <i>Toropapa. </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,428</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>ALSEUOSMIA MACROPHYLLA.</i> Toropapa ... is common in the northern half of the North Island, less so at its southern limit about Nelson. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,428</i>)

>  <b>SALE</b> The Maoris call it toropapa, and the botanists alseuosmia - perfume of the grove. <i>(1982,Four Seasons,33</i>)

>  The alseuosmia macrophylla, the sweet-scented toropapa, or bush daphne, to give it yet a third name, is also very early this year, as it has been out for at least a week on the top of the Waitakeres. <i>(Sep 3, 1930,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  Toropapa was discovered by Europeans when Alan Cunningham found it growing in Hokianga in 1826. ... Toropapa can be found growing in the Wild Garden and the Banks Entrance Garden at Otari-Wilton's Bush. <i>(Oct 14, 2006,Dominion Post,17</i>)

### tororaro
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The shrubby tororaro (Muehlenbeckia astonii) is found only on Wairarapa and Wellington-Cook Strait coasts and inland in Marlborough and Canterbury. <i>(Sep 24, 1998,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  They include <i>Carex cirrhosa</i> (a sedge which was growing at the edge of a boat ramp at Lake Wairarapa), the Castlepoint daisy, not found anywhere else in the world, and shrubby tororaro or <i>Meuhlenbeckia astonii</i>, a coastal shrub ranked as nationally vulnerable. <i>(May 2004,Footnotes,No. 21/7</i>)

>  At the far end of a flat triangular peninsula, you'll find an exclosure plot built by the Conservation Department to protect the last few shrubby tororaro in the area. <i>(Dec 30, 2003,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  Muehlenbeckia astonii, the shrubby tororaro, suffered habitat disturbance, unseasonal chills, roading, quarrying, fire, and predation everything from horses, goats and possums to rabbits and invertebrates. Today there are just 44 wild plants in DOC's Wellington conservancy, and its conservation status is "highest priority threatened species". <i>(Sep 1, 2001,Dominion,10</i>)

>  Among the species being grown in the new reserves are <i>Muehlenbackia astonii</i> (shrubby tororaro), <i>Euphorbia glauca</i> (shore spurge, thought to have been extinct in the Wellington region for 20 years, then rediscovered in 1996/96 on a rocky stack off Kapiti Island), <i>Discaria tomentosa</i> (a species of anchor plant, one of Wellington's - and the North Island's - rarest plants), <i>Hebe speciosa</i> (showy hebe) and <i>Tetragonia tetragonioides</i> (New Zealand spinach). <i>(Jan 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 67/11</i>)

### torore
 
 <i>n.</i> = torori

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> That [tobacco] which they grow universally obtains the name of Torore which signifies intoxicating. <i>(Apr 28, 1851,Journal,7/198</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> (fn) This song has a great deal of meaning : the being bereft of pipe refers to the very heavy duty which had been put on tobacco, to make the native, who was the chief consumer, pay as much as possible; but this over-grasping defeated itself. The native, unable to purchase tobacco to the extent he formerly did, began to grow it himself, and from instructions he has contrived to pick up, he now manufactures it; this he calls <i>torore</i>; some of it is so well made that there are great smokers who will give equal weight of our best tobacco for it. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,134</i>)

>  A stranger - a white man - passes through and stops to give his horse a feed; at once some twenty or thirty people turn out; and squat down in all dignity, wrapping their blankets around them like so many Roman consuls, smoke their vile burning torore (why they prefer that to our Cavendish at 8s. the lb. I can't understand), and take notes of every gesture. <i>(Jan 19, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

### torori
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Torori - Tobacco of Maori manufacture; smokes like Boer tobacco but has an odour peculiarly its own. <i>(May 16, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,II/18 : 126</i>)

>  Maori youths under fifteen are not to smoke tobacco, 'torori' (the powerful native-grown 'baccy).. on pain of fine. <i>(Jun 15, 1912,NZ Free Lance,9</i>)

>  My native acquaintance also took advantage of the spell, for ... she inquired if I objected to the smell of `torori' (native tobacco); ... `My torori; I cured it; take some, try it.' I shuddered. I remembered one day when the tobacco hunger pinched me sore - torori or suffer; ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,144</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1928,Where the White Man Treads,31</i>)

>  <i>torori n.</i> Maori tobacco, prepared from plants cultivated over generations from seed obtained from early settlers and seamen. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> torori: home-grown tobacco <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,142</i>) [NOTES: (1938 p.42 get cit]]

>  <b>PARK</b> It had.. an interior atmosphere thick with the woeful fumes of torori, the black native pigtail tobacco. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,37</i>)

### torotoro
 
 <i>n.</i> ?a cloak ?a climber

>  <b>HYDE</b> Soak the lithe toro-toro,and best yarn/ Was not so strong for tying up stockades,/ <i>(1937,Selected Poems,37,1984 ed.</i>)

>  T|oro t|oro; ... the root of a shrub so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,217</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> In tying the Rafters to the Ridge Pole, and in making their Parrs, the same Creeper is used, but I forget its name Toro Toro [torotoro [inserted by E.M. later]]. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),35,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> The minor fence [of a pa] ... formed of large poles and stakes tightly knotted or woven together with <i>torotoro</i>, the fibrous roots of a plant which abounds in the woods; ... <i>(1835,New Zealanders at Gallipoli,122</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> .. I was in the thick of it, and worked all day lashing the fence; the fence being of course not nailed, but lashed with <i>toro-toro,</i> a kind of tough creeping plant, like a small rope, which was very strong and well adapted for the purpose. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(iii) 41</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Torotoro, <i>Lindsay. Metrosideros scandens. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> ... stout pieces of timber were securely fastened horizontally, by a good lashing of `Toro-Toro' vine ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,85</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> The spot fixed upon was ... under the shadow of a ... tawhero (<i>Weinmannia racemosa</i>), sheltered all round by close-growing porokaiwiria, torotoro, and other shrubby trees ... <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/12</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> Torotoro [Maori name] .. Myrtle [Settlers' name] .. Metrosideros scandens ..Climber <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,178</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> <i>(1952,Houses by the Sea,63</i>)

>  One hundred and sixty Kauri spars, raised perpendicularly, form the frame-work of this singular pile, several of them are squared, and five of them from 90 to 100 feet high, to which smaller spars of from 10 to 15 feet are again added, bound firmly together by the strong vine Torotoro, making the total height of the turret in the centre from 115 to 120 feet, from which other 8 turrets, ranging from 70 to 80 feet, run the length of the erection. <i>(Sep 4, 1849,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

### torpedo
 
 <i>n.</i> a shark (See [ray](../R#ray))

>  <i>Narcobatus fairchildi</i> (Hutton). SOUTHERN NUMBFISH, OR WHAI REPO. ... The species is also called `torpedo' or `electric ray.' Hutton states that the Natives (presumably of Napier) call it whai-ngenge. <i>(1930,Selected Poems,11/198</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps]



 
 <i>n.</i> a torpedo-shaped bottle

>  All that was whole were [sic] unembossed Mace and Dixon torpedoes and plain ginger beers. <i>(May 1980,NZ Antique Bottle Collector,4(3)/19</i>)

>  I have been hearing a lot about the Hamilton patent controversy, lately, namely Walter Hamilton patented the torpedo (or egg shape bottle). I believe I have indisputable proof that he did not. <i>(1980,NZ Antique Bottle Collector,5(1)/9</i>)

>  [Caption] Early Australian impressed ginger beers and Schweppes torpedo from the Avon <i>(Feb 1978,NZ Antique Bottle Collector,No. 2(2)/11</i>)

>  <b>NEAME</b> Now we were into the top class bottles, torpedos, Maughans patents or aerated water bottles and two bluish-green Townsends both in one camp. <i>Ibid.</i> 37 ...we found a lot of Torpedos and also a few Australian Torpedos. <i>(1985,Gold, Coins, Greenstone, & Bottles,29</i>)

### torpedo fish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MANNERING</b> I was told [during a visit to Te Anau in December 1923] that poachers had been taking them [Alantic Salmon <i>Salmo salar</i>] for some years previous to our visit and that local people called them `Torpedo fish', as distinct from trout. <i>(1943,Eighty Years,206</i>)

### torpedo pass
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... Wilkinson ... suddenly flings a torpedo pass right out to his backs ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,84</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a mixture of beer and anti-freeze; (any (home-brewed) strong liquor [ca1943 (Ken Griffiths); in common use postwar (DEH)]

>  He and 13 others.. drank a mixture of beer and glycol, an anti-freeze liquid. The resultant brew is generally known as torpedo-juice. <i>(Aug 20, 1947,NZ Observer,12</i>)

### Torrens title
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I feel I should correct [Easton's] throwaway comment on "outrageous overcharging" by government for transfers under the Land Transfer Act, commonly referred to as the Torrens Title system... However, the Torrens Title system as originally established in 1871 was from the outset intended to be self-funding and not a cost on government. <i>(Mar 19, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),115</i>)

### torrentfish
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cheimarrichthys fosteri</i>

>  <b>McDOWALL</b> A very distinctive and unusual looking fish occasionally caught by whitebaiters is the torrentfish <i>Cheimarrichthys fosteri</i>... [23] Adult torrentfish are found in similar habitats to the bluegilled bully - swift gravelly rapids, from sea level to 50 or more kilometres inland. <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,23</i>)

>  "Rare species such as giant, banded and short-jawed kokopu have been found or relocated in streams and rivers on Council land, as well as koaro, red-finned bullies and torrentfish." <i>(Jun 2002,Elements,17/3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cheimarrichthys fosteri</i> [aka torrent fish; also shark-bully, [Maori Name] papanoko.]

>  <i>Cheimarrichthys fosteri</i> , Haast <i>(1929,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/166</i>) [NOTES: (Phillips)]

>  <i>Cheimarrichthys fosteri</i> : [European Name] Torrent-fish or shark-bully [Maori Name] Papanoko. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,73</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> TORRENT FISH .. .. papanoko .. .. <i>Cheimarrichthys fosteri</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family CHEIMARRICHTHYIDAE. The torrent fish, <i>Cheimarrichthys fosteri</i>... spends part of its life in salt or brackish water, and part in rapids of lower stretches of shingly rivers. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,222</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> The curious-looking torrentfish (<i>Cheimarrichthys fosteri</i>) also comes out of the sea in spring. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,189</i>)

>  The torrentfish is found in very swiftly flowing, tumbling rapids and so is not likely to be seen ... The blue-gilled bully may share its habitat, and both species have sea-living juveniles. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,73</i>)

>  [caption] <b>Under water:</b> Native fish found in the region's [Wellington] rivers and streams are, clockwise from top left, inanga, redfin bully, torrent fish, banded kokopu, giant kokopu and kaoro. <i>(May 7, 2005,Dominion Post,A21</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The Stargazers or `Catfishes' are related to the Rock Cod and the Torrent fish of the rivers;... <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury</i>)

>  Being so rare, it has not received a popular name, and I cannot therefore do better than chisten [sic] it 'Torrent fish.' <i>(Jan 22, 1908,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

### toru
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of toro (See [toro](../T#toro))

>  Some interesting botanical associations - such as toru, kauri and tanekaha - occur in the 85 hectares of coastal modified primary and secondary forest at Taupiri Bay. ... <i>Toronia toru</i>, the only endemic species of the <i>Toronia</i> genus, has fragrant flowers, leaves that can be red-tinged, and corky spots on the bark. <i>(Nov 2005,Open Space,5</i>)

### torua
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> This evening, Huruhuru made me a pair of sandals, such as are in common use among these natives. They are called `paraerae', and are made either with the leaves of the flax-[210]plant, or of the `ti' <i>(cordyline Australis)</i>. ... Mine were a pair with double soles, called `torua', ... <i>(Jan 13, 1844,Southern Districts of NZ,209</i>)

### toss a reverse lunch
 
 <i>phr.</i> to vomit [toss also as noun]

>  He claimed incident in Eastern Moon had arisen when local person had `tossed a reverse lunch' of prawns, sweetcorn, and tomato skins over his person ... <i>(1979,Loosehead Len's Gluepot Greats in $1,154</i>)

### toss in
 
 <i>v.</i> to throw in esp. toss it in, to admit defeat

>  <b>THOMSON</b> And though a few had stuck it out, gradually gaining experience and learning to love the life, others had decided out of hand that deer shooting was not for them and had tossed it in. <i>Ibid.</i> 22 In fact, over the years one could find men from practically every occupation tossing in what are generally considered `good' jobs, and spending the rest of their working lives in the mountains. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,21</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> <i>(1956,Sullen Bell,128</i>)

### toss one's lollies
 
 <i>phr.</i> to vomit [also chuck or lose one's lollies; cf. ODE toss one's cookies N.Amer.]

>  They were seen ... tossing back Tequila Slammers and then heard together in the <i>pissoir </i>on their knees tossing their lollies long and loud. <i>(Mar 1986,Metro,27</i>)

>  Blah! The Ferret's about to toss her lollies. <i>(Jul 1987,Metro,22</i>)

>  <b>McGill</b> lolly any sweets, short for lollipop. NZA. - chuck or lose your lollies to spew. - do one's lolly lose one's temper. NZA <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,70</i>)

### toss the coin
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Gilbert said that he had plenty of money as he played pool, toss the coin and won quinellas at the races. He refused to `point the finger' at where Mr Maddox got the dope. `... this has spoilt a good day. I have been raging all day. I punched him over because he tried to punch me over, and put me in my place. He reckoned he was a Liverpudlian seaman who had been here for 20 years, a big toughie,' Gilbert had told him. <i>(Dec 7, 1984,Press, The,4</i>)

### toss the tiger
 
 <i>phr.</i> an elaboration of toss (up) to vomit f. striped appearance of vomit: cf Techicolour yawn [ca1960 Auckland (Vincent O'Sullivan)]

### toss up
 
 <i>v.</i> to vomit: a variant of the general English throw up (cf TOSS THE TIGER) [ca1935 at least]

### toss-about
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  `Euclid Junior' writes: I notice that diagrams, graphs, charts, etc., are now much used to explain things. After a toss-about among Government departments, I tried to think of a suitable diagram for the Official Mind. <i>(Sep 2, 1918,Quick March,11</i>)

### tossel
 
 <i>n.</i> a (child's) penis [or tassel [ca1935 (but possibly from ca1900) Havelock Jane Morison]

### tossing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THATCHER</b> Algebra is all my eye, / For mathematics do not sigh, / But at tossing learn to be quite fly. / And you'll be a leary boy. <i>(1875,NZ Songster,No. 5/62</i>) [NOTES: The Leary Boy]

### tot
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) totara [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  <b>McGILL</b> Jack Wilson of Orepuki had his first job with the sawmill at Port Craig. He remembers the twenty-four-metre-high derrick used to winch the timber on a pulley out to the ships. In his day about 130 men cut rimu, tot (his word for totara) and birch on the beach. <i>(1980,Ghost Towns of NZ,195</i>)

### totalisator
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  From the birdcage, the people would walk to the totalisator. <i>(Nov 18, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,3</i>)

>  <b>BRIDGES & DOWNS</b> The invention of the automatic totalisator is as much a New Zealand one as it is Australian. <i>(2000,No 8 Wire: The Best of Kiwi Ingenuity,54</i>)

>  In fan tan palaces 10 per cent goes to the house. Seems almost as respectable as the totalisators, doesn't it? <i>(May 25, 1912,NZ Free Lance,30</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> By the late 1870s a monster in the form of the totalisator (primitive as it was at that stage) raised its head to leer in the direction of the bookies. The warning became louder in 1879 when the Wellingtonc orrespondent of the <i>Lyttelton Times</i> telegraphed:'... that it is proposed to amend the Lotteries Act so as to legalise the totalisator"... In 1880 a Mr Ekberg patented an elementary type of totalisator which was first used at the Canterbury Jockey Club in that year. <i>(2000,First Past the Post,89</i>) [NOTES: Miriam Redwood "I lay the Field" in <Proud Silk</i> 1979]

>  <b>[WICKHAM]</b> Another social pest that might very well be handed on to the local option tribunal, is the Totalizator. <i>(1897,Casual Ramblings,80</i>)

>  There are suggestions that there were some early primitive totalisator machines in operation in Auckland and Australia, but the credit for the first mechanized totalisator in the world goes to a Mr. Ekberg of Christchurch who invented and patented a mechanical totalisator in 1880. ... It was first used in Riccarton by [47] the Canterbury Jockey Club. ... Public opposition continued to flourish and in 1896 a Bill to abolish the totalisator was narrowly defeated. <i>(Jun 2005,NZ Memories,No 54, p. 46</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> Nine-tenths of the betting is done through the Totalisater, ... <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,114</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> A sort of betting machine called the `Totalisator' has indeed been legalised by the New Zealand Government; but may only be used at race meetings where prizes of thirty pounds or upwards are given. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,98</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The `totalizator' provides for many the excitement that may sometimes be lacking in the race; but the dividends dispensed therefrom never seem to be adequate ... <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,43</i>)

>  The Totalisator, at the late Sydney meeting, seems to have taken greatly with the public. <i>(May 24, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  Every village has its race course, its grandstand, and its totalisator. <i>(Jan 1, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  An electric machine has a tremendous advantage over the old manual totalisator. <i>(Aug 23, 1939,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

### Totalisator Agency Board
 
 <i>n.</i> the government sponsored betting agency (See [also TAB](../A#also-tab))

>  This was soon followed by the establishment of private bookmakers, known as 'bookies' who were to remain on the New Zealand scene, albeit illegally, until the Totalisator Agency Board was established in 1950. <i>(Jun 2005,NZ Memories,No. 54, p. 46</i>)

>  <b>JOURNET</b> Your betting shop will have a notice in its window <i>announcing</i> that it is a Branch of the Totalisator Agency Board. <i>(1954,Take My Tip,69</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> `Stop off at the TAB., will you, Jim.' ...the converted shop which now carried the big sign `Totalistor Angency Board', the open sesame for off-course betting. <i>(1964,Footsteps in the Sea,64</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> A Totalisator Agency Board was then set up under a Gaming Amendment Act (15 December 1949) to operate the scheme, ... <i>(1966,Encyclopedia Britannica,3/22</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> [spg -z-] The introduction of off-course betting in 1949 brought the doubles totalizator and the TAB (`there shall be a board [175] to be known as the Totalizator Agency Board' - Gaming Amendment Act, 1949). <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,174</i>)

>  <i>Totalizator Agency Board NZ </i>official board, with local offices, for off-course betting on horses. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,813</i>) [NOTES: [.nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.] [spg with -z- ]]

### totalla
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [totara](../T#totara))

### totalled
 
 <i>adj.</i> drunk

>  Getting "pissed", "off your face". Trashed", "boozed" or "totalled" is still seen as a rite of passage, and no alteration of the legal drinking age seems to be changing that. <i>(Jun 3, 2000,Dominion,19</i>)

### totara
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a fish

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Of the Fam. Plectognathi there are three species: Tetraodon Hamiltoni, the Monacanthus parika, and the Diadona globulas. Kopuawai, <i>Koputaratara, Papati </ik>and<i> Totara</i>, all these are descriptive names, implying that it is covered with sharp spines, and is inflated; this singular fish is frequently cast up on the shore, and sometimes of a large size. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,625</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> = patotara

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Totara,<i>Lindsay. Cyathodes oxycedrus</i>. ... 768 Totara, <i>Col[enso]. Leucopogon Frazeri. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>L[eucopogon] Fraseri, A.Cunn.</i> ... A small shrubby plant 2-6 in. high, ... branching from the base; ... [415] <i>Totara.</i> ... The drupe is juicy, sweetish, and edible. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,414</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) <i>Podocarpus totara</i>, a forest tree and its timber, mahogany(-pine); a large native forest tree prized for its timber. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  N.Z. Rail is the totara in the rain forest of inter-island communication. <i>(Jun 1987,Landfall,162/208</i>) [NOTES: (Coker)]

>  I am not sending my totara tree [sketch] this time because I want to make another foreground. <i>(Jun 18, 1884,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/522</i>) [NOTES: (Dorothy K. Richmond)]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The totara is one of the finest trees in the forest. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/221</i>)

>  4. Totara, (<i>Podocarpus totara</i>.) This tree grows to a large size, especially in the Hutt, Porirua, and Karori districts. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,141</i>)

>  <b>BRADEY</b> totarer <i>(1849,Papers</i>)

>  FOR SALE, a few 2-inch Totara COUNTER TOPS, 2 feet wide. <i>(Feb 1, 1851,Lyttelton Times,4</i>)

>  The timber in [the groves] is chiefly Kaikatea, (white pine), Mahi, (black pine), Remu, (red pine), Pokaka, Kowai, and Totara .. That in the other forest [ie. not Harewood or Alford] is principally tawai, (black birch), the best timber for boat and ship building, but sometimes unsound ... <i>(Jun 28, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  Totaras, white and black pines, koas etc <i>(1851,Otago Journal,8/126,1852 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> Of the [Wairarapa] plain, about 100,000 acres is covered with fine timber, chiefly totara, matai, hiora, kahikatea, manuka &c., all highly valuable for .. cabinet work and carpentry. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),113</i>)

>  Podocarpus <i>totara, Cunn.</i> .. Nat. name, `Totara', <i>Cunn. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 233</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> There are traces all over the hills of vast forests having once existed; chiefly of totara, a sort of red pine ... <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,95,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Totara, <i>Col[enso]. Podocarpus totara.</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  Totara (<i>Podocarpus Totara</i>), a most valuable timber tree, being very durable and easily worked. <i>Ibid.</i> 32 Totara (<i>Podocarpus nivalis</i>). A mountain shrub resembling Totara, of no economic value. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Podocarpus Totara Totara <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/122</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>PRENTICE</b> Black, white and red pines, totara .. <i>(1870,Tale of NZ,33</i>) [NOTES: (Otago)]

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Totara [a1 acute] .. <i>O</i>. Coniferae; <i>G</i>. Podocarpus; <i>S</i> P. totara. `The Swamp-totara, P. dacrydiodes, grows up to 150 feet in height. P. totara, from which canoes are made,' to a less height. `Bark used for roofing.' <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/302</i>)

>  English Name Native name Botanical Name Totara Totara .. <i>(1889,Catalogue, Canterbury Court Exhibition,35</i>)

>  Totara .. Podocarpus totara .. Timber of a reddish colour, soft, easily worked, and of great durability ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,15</i>)

>  <b>DOUGLAS</b> .. Red and Black pine, Rata and Totara .. the Cedars and Totaras .. young Totara, however large they may be [in Westland]. <i>(1892,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1892 Douglas letter to Strauchan in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 175]

>  <b>HARPER</b> There is also the rata tree, the prince of firewoods .. miki-miki, kamahi, or so-called red-birch, white and black birches (which are really beeches), the mountain broom, ribbon wood, broad leaf, totara, and many others .. and always available in the Ranges [sc. Alps]. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,35</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Totara, n. </i>Maori name for a lofty-spreading New Zealand tree, <i>Podpcarpus totara,</i> A. Cunn., <i>N.O. Coniferae</i>. In Maori, the accent falls on the first syllable; but in English use it is often placed on the second, and from Mr. Polack's spelling it must have been so as early as 1840. Called also <i>Mahogany-pine</i>. There are several other species, e.g. <i>P. vivalis</i>, Hook, the <i>Mountain Totara</i>; called also <i>Mahogany Pine</i>. <i>(1898,Austral English,475</i>) [NOTES: see <i>Mahogany</i>, and <i>Pine.</i> [1832 on various cits]]

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Totara</i>: A native timber. <i>Ibid.</i> 54 <i>Kauri</i> and <i>Totara, Rimu</i>, and <i>Matai and Maire,</i>/ Red-as-blood <i>Rata</i>, and bright-as-blood <i>Pohutukawa,/ Manuka </i>dark-ey'd, Convolvulus star-ey'd - / The glittering of you that morning! <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,12</i>) [NOTES: [fn2 attached to the italicised names] 2. <i>Kauri</i>, etc. (<i>Kow-ree</i>, To[acute]-ta-ra, Ree[e acute]-moo, <i>Mah[a acute]-ti,</i> Mi-reh, Rah[a acute]-tah, Po-hoo-too-kah[a acute]-wah, <i>Mah[a acute]-noo-kah</i>): New Zealand Bush trees.]

>  <i>~ n</i>. a large coniferous tree giving valuable timber; the timber of the tree [Maori]. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12</i>)

>  The chief indigenous trees of commercial value are: Kauri (<i>Agathis Australis</i>) .. totara (<i>Podocarpus totara </i>and <i>Podocarpus Hallii</i>), Miro (<i>Podocarpus ferrugineus</i>) .. northern and southern rata (<i>Metrosideros robusta</i> and <i>Metrosideros lucida</i>). <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,578</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> Totara posts were put in at intervals, leaving spaces for doors and windows according to the plan ... <i>(1937,Thomas,64</i>)

>  <i>totara</i>, a most valued timber-tree (Podocarpus totara), so useful to the Maori that he considered it the ariki of all trees; the best timber for canoes, house-timbers, slabs for carving, manuka and like heavy woods being used for weapons - taiaha, tewhatewha, etc. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/147</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>totara</i>: tall timber tree. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> (gloss.) totara: a New Zealand tree. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,273,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> In addition the boys have cut down rimu, totara, matai, and pinus growing on the property and built a 4-stand woolshed without help from outside. <i>(Dec 22, 1954,Shepherd's Calendar,202,1961 ed.</i>)

>  The Maori name of a large timber tree, much used by the Maoris for canoes, housebuilding, etc. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),417</i>)

>  <i>totara n </i>-s [Maori] : a tall tree (<i>Podocarpus totara</i>) of New Zealand having hard reddish wood used for furniture and construction (as of bridges and wharves) and being the country's most valuable timber tree next to the kauri. <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2415</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Totara .. totara .. <i>Podocarpus totara </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>totara n</i>. lofty New Zealand tree, a red pine [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>totara ... n.</i> lofty N.Z. tree with fine and easily worked reddish wood [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <i>totara</i> [s] <i>Podocarpus totara</i> G. Benn. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,79</i>)

>  The Duke of Edinburgh Chest of 8 Drawers, centre drawer richly panelled with totara burr and rewarewa - - GBP6 10s. <i>(Apr 8, 1882,Bay of Plenty Times,3</i>)

>  The principal samples sent by the company were a figured rimu door with mottled puriri panels, and a series of veneers of the following timbers:- Mottled and plain rimu, totara burr, mottled and wavy kauri, honeysuckle, pohutukawa, maungaeo, silver pine, and towai. <i>(Feb 27, 1905,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Successional planting of such species as totara and miro is so labour intensive. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 1</i>)

>  The totara piles without sheathing were, he added, all sound. <i>(Dec 24, 1884,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  The organ front is of a very chaste design, the uprights being of rimu, the cross rails kauri, the open fret-work in this being backed by totara burr, and further relieved by totara mouldings; the desk is of rimu, and the whole being polished up shows up the beautiful contrast of the woods to perfection. <i>(Sep 29, 1896,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> A species of pine, called by the natives <i>totarra</i>, excited our astonishment, from the bulk and height to which it grew. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/329</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|otara,<i> s</i>. A species of pine ... <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,217</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> Here we found the trees .. of that kind called by the natives Demo and Totara. The former resembles the pine and the latter has a leaf like the yew tree ... <i>(1820,Journal,20,1928 ed.</i>)

>  .. the wood with which they make a light - cow|atti and to'talla [kauati and totara] <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,1986,109 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> <i>(1832,Genus Pinus,2/190</i>) [NOTES: 1832. G. Bennett, in Lambert's `Genus Pinus,' vol. ii. p. 190: [Mor]]

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> A great variety of hard-wood grows at New Zealand, admirably adapted for the timbering of any sized ships; among them is the boride[e-acute], rattar[as-acute], taraide[ase-acute], mai[a-acute], totara[a1-acute], koi katoa[a2-acute], toa[a-acute] toa[a-acute], tani raha[as-acute], to wai[a-acute], reiva[a-acute] reiva[a-acute], tana[as-acute], and many others. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,19,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> Tota[acute]ra <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal</i>)

>  <i>totara n.</i> a tall coniferous forest tree of New Zealand with durable wood. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1237</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 813; COM 506]]

>  <b>1/III/32</b> .. the small berries of the Makomako (.us; Aristotelia racemosa), [and] of the heath-like Totara (<i>Leucopogon Fraseri</i>) .. were eagerly sought after .. by [incomplete citation?] <i>(1869,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>YATE</b> Totara (<i>Taxus</i>) - This tree .. is about twenty feet in circumference, and from fifty to sixty feet high in the trunk. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),39</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> There is scarcely a more splendid sight to behold than a New Zealand forest. In the one we now entered, we did not perceive any kouri, or yellow pine-trees; but the totara [a's-acute], or red pine, grew in vast abundance .. The rito, and innumerable other palm-trees, were in great quantity. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/286 .. tota[acute]rra <i>Ibid.</i> 2/391 <i>Totara</i> [a's-acute] (<i>taxus australis</i>) g. coniferae .. is known as red pine. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/105</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> .. the most superior [canoes] are to be found at Wairoa, (Hawkes' Bay) the tribes at that place employ the <i>totarra</i> or red pine, the <i>kouri</i> or white pine being rarely found beyond the Thames (Horake[a-e-acute]). <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/227</i>)

>  I was informed that about 15 miles from Petoni, there is an extensive forest of toteroa, which timber ranks next in value to kauri. <i>(Oct 10, 1840,GBPP,1841 (No.311) 120</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Oct 10 Shortland to Hobson in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 120]

>  <b>HODGSKIN</b> Tatatarra [sic], another tree, growing between 60 and 70 feet in high [sic] ... <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,27</i>)

>  .. forests of <i>totorra. </i> <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/28/47</i>)

>  totarra <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/29/51</i>) [NOTES: (Dieffenbach)]

>  <b>BRIGHT</b> to-ta-ra <i>(1841,Handbook for Emigrants,54</i>)

>  Totara is one native that, along with manuka and tauhinu, can establish itself into pasture even under moderate grazing pressure. <i>(Oct 2000,Growing Today,69</i>)

>  TOTARA, MATAI, AND RIMU - SUPPLIED AT CURRENT RATES WHOLESALE AND RETAIL. <i>(Apr 6, 1907,New Zealand Free Lance,15</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> It was an open forest of Totara [fn <i>Podocarpus totara</i>] and strongly resembled the pine-woods of Canada. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,72,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The red pines are the <i>Totara, Towa, Mahi</i>, and <i>Rata</i>, all of which are close-grained, and moderately hard. <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),42</i>)

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> The prevailing species of tree are remo, totara, and tall manuka, all indicative of an inferior soil. <i>(May 24, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 May 24 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 223]

>  <i>Podocarpus totara</i> spg TOTARRO <i>(Jul 29, 1844,Report of Select Committee on NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  [tatare] Bark of Tatare [Totara] Tree .. <i>Ibid.</i> 105 [tohara] The Tohara [totara] is handsome, with small sharp leafs [sic] of a dark, green color ... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,49,195t6 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  The roof was thatched with raupo and strips of totara bark called "flags." <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,205</i>) [NOTES: Writing of 1940]

>  <b>DOMETT</b> One lone totara-tree that grew/ Beneath the hill-side. <i>Ibid., </i> 1/157 Made .../ Fast to a single lightsome yew,/ One lone <i>totara</i>-tree [a1-acute] that grew/ Beneath the hillside rising high .../ <i>(1872,Ranolf and Amohia,107</i>)

>  25 Totara slab. <i>Ibid.</i> 20 269 Totara knot slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,16</i>)

>  When they got to a fence overlooking a deep gully, prisoner and Sergeant Moore dismounted, and the former said: `You see that totara tree and a fern. The body is there.' <i>(Jun 3, 1911,NZ Truth,310/5/2</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> It appeared as a small inverted totara canoe. <i>(1951,Confessions,38</i>)

>  They used the totara stakes of the stockyards to construct the frame of the house and stripped iron from the slip-torn houses of the wealthy suburbs for the roof. <i>(Jan 21, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)

>  Amongst the articles on view were .. [a] chess table of kauri, with kauri and cedar top, border of Queensland oak and totara burr. <i>(Dec 4, 1920,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> It lays seven purely white eggs, in a compact nest, in the lower branches of the Totara-tree. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),56</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... the natives have a legend that [the Manawatu] was formed by an Atua, or `Evil Spirit', who was in the form of a large Totara-tree ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,118</i>)

>  [Chemist and Druggist's advertisement] N.B. A few Scantling and well-seasoned Totara Boards. <i>(Feb 15, 1851,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

>  <b>GOLDER</b> Upon Te Aro Tot'ra Line <i>(1854,Pigeon's Parliament</i>) [NOTES: [get more context and page no]]

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Eight feet tall and totara proud, he steps forwards, speaks for a few minutes in Maori, then switches to English. <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,107</i>)
