>  <b>FORDYCE & MACLEHN</b> In 1890 Mariano built wharves at Paremata and Mansa Island from totara posts washed down from floods in the Manawatu and Rangitikei. <i>(2000,Bay, The : A History of Community at Titahi Bay,31</i>)

### totara bark
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> already incorporated into ordinary text huts made of totara bark ... <i>(May 2, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)1844 May 2 Tuckett's Diary in Hocken Contributions 214]

>  <b>SMITH</b> Most of the houses are built of slabs of totara bark which is taken from the tree in sheets, often 20 feet long by 10 broad, ... <i>(Jan 14, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 14 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 362]

>  [At the abandoned Maori camp was] a pannikin of native manufacture made of totara bark ...[sic] <i>(Feb 12, 1864,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/87,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  ... we may divide colonial architecture into periods or ages. First, the wattle-and-dab period, with its contemporaneous, but more advanced, varieties of fern tree and totara bark <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/166</i>) [NOTES: (Blair Building Materials Otago)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> The camp ... contained thirty thatched houses roofed with totara-bark. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/282,1983 ed.</i>)

>  The roof was ... covered with raupo - sometimes with an inner sheeting of totara-bark - while an upper layer of toetoe-grass, secured by ropes of mangemange ..., preserved the raupo from wet. ->1940 Howard Rakiura 208 The tightly packed [kelp bags of mutton-birds] are then cased in longitudinal strips of totara bark and placed in flax bags, the whole being laced with fibre or twine. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/22</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> It was still raining fast ..., but we continued on till two, [385] when we halted, and commenced building a totara bark hut. <i>(Feb 16, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Feb 16 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 384-385]

>  The scene, he writes, is one of those bush hamlets you may see in some of the less-frequented parts of the Urewera Country, a kainga of three or four houses of the olden type, roofed with totara bark; a camp nearly surrounded by forest ranges. <i>(Oct 10, 1931,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  The bag is protected with strips of totara bark lashed around it, and the mutton-birds are then ready to be sent to market, to be consumed by those who like this dainty. <i>(May 11, 1910,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  The best house here was built of reeds, wattled together and bound with flax, roofed and lined with totara bark and coarse grass. <i>(Oct 11, 1842,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,4</i>)

>  Totara bark, it may be explained to the uninitiated, can be pulled from the trees in great sheets, and makes a capital roof when properly laid. <i>(Apr 26, 1888,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  No raupo thatch is seen here, but the whares are roofed instead with totara bark. <i>(Mar 16, 1899,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

### totara berry
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Totara berries, glowing like tiny orange beacons in the morning mist, send a succulent signal to passing birds that breakfast is served. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,82</i>)

### totara birch
 
 <i>n.</i> ?=hard beech, <i>Nothofagus fusca</i> [ca1935 Marlborough like TOTARA, resistant to decay in the ground, and wood of a reddish colour: used especially for fence-posts] (See [totara birch](../T#totara-birch))

### totara knot
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I finished a candlestick for Mrs Cox I have been making out of two totara notts. <i>(Mar 23, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,169,1950 ed.</i>)

>  The centre [of the table] is a beautifully figured totara knot with a border of tetoki, the whole being highly polished. <i>(Mar 20, 1869,Wellington Independent,4</i>)

>  The top of the desk is of heart of kauri, beautifully polished, and is inlaid with the guide trefoil in totara knot and kahikatea. <i>(Dec 21, 1926,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  It took the form of an old brass helmet badge, which had been recovered in the course of excavations at Wanganui, mounted on a handsome piece of polished totara knot, and carrying a suitable inscription. <i>(Nov 6, 1930,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  .. large honeysuckle and totara knot chest <i>(Aug 29, 1944,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  We hope Mr Berger will repeat the experiment: we imagine totara knot would soon become a more general favourite even than walnut for piano cases. <i>(Sep 15, 1873,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  The whole will be enclosed in a casket made of indigenous woods, mounted on carved legs, with a top of totara knot, on which the Duke's arms will be carved. <i>(Jun 5, 1901,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  After an exciting contest, prizes were presented as follows .. Gentlemen - First, Mr. J. Davis (pipe made from totara knot) ... <i>(Aug 29, 1918,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  The Wellington specimens before alluded to as exhibited by Mr Vogel were kowai, honey-suckle, totara and knot, aki rotangi, rimu knot, black macri, and aki aki. <i>(Jan 3, 1863,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  In addition, five other valuable Presents will be distributed, viz. .. Totara Knot Looking-glass[,] Totara Knot Book-shelf. <i>(Oct 17, 1884,Evening Post,3</i>)

### totara pine
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAAST</b> Along the rather wet track appeared, besides the Totara pine, white pines (Kahikatea, <i>Podocarpus dacrydioides</i>), and black pines (<i>Rimu</i> or <i>Dacrydium cupressinum</i>), which ... towered high above the other forest foliage; although here and there black beeches (<i>Fagus fusca</i>), 100 feet high ... were also to be seen. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,81</i>)

>  Broadleaf [fn <i>Griselinia littoralis.</i>], five-fingered Jack [fn <i>Panax arboreum</i>.], ..and the totara pine [fn <i>Podocarpus totara</i>.] jostle one another in the dense dry bush. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,184</i>)

>  BASTINGS, LEARY, AND CO, on Jerry street Wharf - Red, black, and totara pine. <i>(Jun 28, 1884,Otago Daily Times,2</i>)

### totara post/batten
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fencepost or batten made from the hard native timber totara.

>  .. with good fences of totara posts and wire, 4 pig-proof paddocks ... <i>(Aug 13, 1908,Weekly News,2</i>)

>  In the long run, totara posts are cheaper. <i>(Apr 1, 1944,NZ Dairy Exporter,02</i>)

>  Sheep farm: Good sheep-proof fences, ring-fenced with concrete and totara posts. <i>(Sep 29, 1961,NZ Farmer,69</i>)

>  I strung a couple of extra snarly 3 in barbs atop the new 8-wire boundary line and stayed the barbs up fairly liberally with totara battens. <i>(Aug 1972,NZ Farmer,10</i>)

>  In the mid 90s he started collecting old totara fenceposts. <i>(Jun 16, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  Clearing sale: deer strainers ... deer gates; quantity totara posts. <i>(Jun 22, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,22</i>)

### totara sponge
 
 <i>n.</i> (recipe)

>  <b>BASHAM</b> <i>TOTARA SPONGE </i>... eggs ... beat yolks ... with ... sugar ... whites to a stiff froth and add to yolks ... and beat ... Sift ... flour ... cream of tartar ... soda ... fold into mixture and add grated rind of 1 orange ... orange juice ... Bake ... <i>(1939,Aunt Daisy's Cookery Book,155</i>)

### totara-kiri-kotukutuku
 
 <i>n.</i> fuchsia barked totara

>  Most frequently the kauri will be found mixed with rimu, kahikatea, tanekaha, miro, totara, totara-kiri-kotukutuku, northern rata, puriri, . <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,116</i>)

### totaratara
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Word list] totaratara a small shrub like a heath <i>(Aug 1843,Journal,51</i>)

### Totarol
 
 <i>n.</i> a natural preservative made from totara

>  [He] has been working for six years to develop and patent Totarol, a natural anti-bacterial and preservative made from totara wood. <i>(Aug 5, 2004,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

### totarra
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [see totara](../S#see-totara))

### tote
 
 <i>n.</i> a shrub (=? toke)

>  <b>SAUNDERS</b> Fighting above Anzac Cove in late April 1915 would be similar to fighting today on the gorse and scrub-covered slopes around Wellington Harbour and the Hutt. It `was very thick and stood about 5ft high and was of a laurel leaf type very like `Tote' that is found in New Zealand.' It was such scrub that the Anzacs laid in their coffin trenches. <i>(Apr 27, 1915,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1915 Apr 27 Saunders Diary in 1984 Pugsley Gallipoli 160]



 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) totalisator [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  <b>BRIDGES & DOWNS</b> Is it fair to call the automatic tote a New Zealand invention? <i>(2000,No 8 Wire: The Best of Kiwi Ingenuity,56</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> This side-of-the-mouth coot says for me to touch the pants first, so I say, `O.K. Five beers please'. <i>Ibid.</i> 75 There is all sorts of plonk, and the little joker tells me to touch my kick [to shout] ... I think all the others have death adders in their pockets, 'cause not one of them touches his tweeds. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,23</i>)

>  <i>(1952,Here & Now,2(4) : 3</i>) [NOTES: Not found]

>  <b>[WICKHAM]</b> Confronted with a plebiscite the exit of the 'tote' would be a hurried one., <i>(1897,Casual Ramblings,80</i>)

>  That skinny little bloke standing next to me down by the tote said there was nothing in it. <i>(Dec 21, 1914,Public Service Journal,17</i>)

>  <b>11/1/1</b> If it were not for the `tote' ... <i>(Sep 2, 1905,NZ Truth</i>)

>  `What you've got to do is to keep up agin the tote and when I signal, bung in and get the div. quick,' a youth was explaining to his `cobber'. <i>(Jan 10, 1916,Triad, The,43</i>)

>  <i>(1952,Here & Now,2/4/3</i>)

>  <i>the tote</i>: each way <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  The tote, says Grant, accurately reflected the amount of discretionary spending money in the wider economy. <i>(Aug 12, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

### tote betting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Three `tote-betting' cases ... <i>(Jan 1899,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  ... procuring the offence of `tote-betting'. <i>(May 1, 1899,Evening Post,6</i>)

### tote odds
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Prior to that date a very considerable amount of 'tote odds' betting had been done away from racecourses. <i>(Jan 3, 1914,Great Days in NZ Mountaineering,3</i>)

>  [Hdg] Laying "Tote" Odds... So said Mr. Poynton S.M.. when urged to increase by one day the sentence passed.. for having laid totalisator odds. <i>(Feb 19, 1924,Otago Witness,66</i>)

### tote ticket
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He had in his possession a large number of tickets known as "tote tickets," in packets of 50 ready for distribution to agents. To the person holding the ticket of which the last three figures corresponded to the last three figures of the totalisator turnover at a certain race-meeting a prize was paid. <i>(Aug 2, 1943,NZEF Times,III/110 : 3</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> Why, even your wives ... wouldn't sit out two [racing] events if they couldn't invest in a tote ticket or a half-crown sweep. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,118</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> Fivers and singles in every conceivable pocket, tucked in with undecipherable notes, tote tickets, everything you could think of. <i>(1967,Ink on My Fingers,106</i>)

### totera
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Totera, <i>Cunn. Fuchsia procumbens. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

### tothersider
 
 <i>n.</i> an Australian, an Australian immigrant (See [also the other side](../A#also-the-other-side),[othersider](../O#othersider))

>  In those early years the whaling vessels often brought doubtful characters from Australia, most of them ticket-of-leave men or ex-convicts, commonly called `T'othersiders', or `Van Demonians', etc. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,33</i>)

>  T'Othersiders are still calling Maoriland legislation `experimental'. <i>(Sep 9, 1905,NZ Truth,12/1/4</i>)

### toti
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BASS ... <i>Other names: Polyprion moeone</i>; bass-groper; moeone, toti (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,226</i>)

### totitoti
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [toetoe](../T#toetoe))

### totoa
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Phyllocladus alpinus</i>, Hook. Totoa.[check = toatoa] --A small ornamental and densely-branched tree sometimes 2 feet in diameter. Bark used for dyeing and making tar. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,125</i>)

### totoara
 
 <i>n.</i> the robin

>  <b>HAAST</b> Amongst the small inhabitants ... is the Totoara, the New Zealand robin, which is the first to welcome the explorer in the wilderness, and which remains steadily near the camp. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,140</i>)

>  PETROICA ALBIFRONS, Gml. Totoara. Robin. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/60</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> MIRO LONGPIPES \{MIRO AUSTRALIS\}. (NORTH ISLAND ROBIN.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Pitoitoi, Toutou, Toutouwai, and Totoara. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,121,{1888 2nd edn 1/33} ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> MIRO ALBIFRONS. (SOUTH-ISLAND ROBIN.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Pitoitoi, Toutou, Toutouwai,and Totoara. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,122,1888 2nd edn 1/36 ed.</i>)

>  30. <i>Petroeca albifrons,</i> Gml. Robin, Toto-ara. - This delightful warbler is decreasing in numbers very rapidly ... <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,287</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts) [spg toto-ara]]

### totoeka
 
 <i>n.</i> Streaked variety of greenstone.: spg also totoweka

>  <b>JOSEPH</b> `The jade, the pounamu ... It's a beautiful stone ... Look,' he said, holding up a long hooked ear-drop of pearly translucence, `that's tau tau and the stone is called inanga. And this, the pekapeka - they say the shape comes from the bat. This is for the ear, too - the mako, like a shark's tooth ... And these,' - he ran his fingers along a row of ... nephrite adzes - `toki. See this one' - he held the heavy red-mottled adze in his palm - `that's the red kind, totoweka, woodhen's blood. The deep green, that's kawakawa.' <i>(1962,Pound of Saffron, A,113</i>)

### totokipio
 
 <i>n.</i> the dabchick

>  <b>BULLER</b> PODICEPS RUFIPECTUS. (NEW-ZEALAND DABCHICK.) ... <i>Native names</i>.- Weweia, Totokipio \{, Taihoropi (Hokianga), and Taratimoho (Waikato)\}. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,350,1888 2nd edn 2/280 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Totokepio is the `little diver' of the settler to distinguish it from the `big diver' (the crested grebe). The Canterbury compiler spells it as given, but in Southland the name is Totokipio, and if taken in sections To-toki-pio it is easy to pronounce. The kamana or crested grebe is not mentioned. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 64]

>  <i>Podiceps rufipectus</i>. - Totokipio, Dabchick. Both Islands. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/113</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  PODICEPS RUFIPECTUS, Gray. Totokipio. Dab-chick, Little Grebe. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/73</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 105. PODICEPS RUFIPECTUS. Gray. Dab-chick. Totokipio. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,39</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [Maori tobacco aka totori, torore, torori] (See [torore](../T#torore))

### totorore
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> PUFFINUS TRISTIS \{GRISEUS\}. (SOMBRE SHEARWATER.) ...<i>Native names</i>.- Titi, Hakoakoa, and Totorore: `Mutton-bird' of the colonists. ... when hovering overhead at night [Titi] utter a frequent call-note, like <i>tee-tee-tee</i>, from which the Maori name is derived. [Check that some of this may be 1888 edn only.] <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,317,{1888 2nd edn 2/232} ed.</i>)

>  <b>18/95</b> <i>Observations on</i> Puffinus assimilis (<i>Gould</i>), Totorore, <i>their habits and Habitats. </i> <i>(1886,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  2/209PRION TURTUR. (DOVE PETREL.) ... <i>Native name\{s\}</i>.- Whiroia and Totorore. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand</i>)

 <i>n.</i>
